### [1] [Ultra-fast Neural Inference for Stochastic Gaussian Splatting Denoising](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25604)
*Chenxiao Hu,Hao Zhang,Yanchen Zhang,Meng Gai,Guoping Wang,Sheng Li*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper presents a temporal neural denoiser for stochastic 2D Gaussian Splatting rendering that eliminates the need for sorting and alpha blending while maintaining temporal stability. The method combines dual-path EMA accumulation, per-pixel learned trust prediction, anisotropic spatial filtering, and variance-gated composition to suppress rendering noise during free camera navigation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Stochastic rendering in Gaussian splatting eliminates the computationally expensive sorting and alpha blending processes, but introduces spatial noise. The authors aimed to develop a denoising approach that preserves the performance benefits of sort-free rendering while achieving visually compelling, temporally stable outputs.

Method: The proposed temporal neural denoiser operates on pixel streams from view-consistent stochastic splatting renderers. It incorporates four key components: (1) dual-path exponential moving average (EMA) accumulation for temporal data, (2) per-pixel learned trust prediction for history validation, (3) a fixed anisotropic spatial filter, and (4) variance-gated composition with stabilization.

Result: The denoiser successfully suppresses spatial noise while maintaining temporal stability during free camera navigation. The pipeline retains the sort-free, blend-free rasterization performance. However, there remains a PSNR gap compared to sorted alpha-blending renderers, though the denoiser's computational overhead is offset by the time saved from eliminating sorting and blending operations.

Conclusion: The combined pipeline achieves a balance between rendering performance and visual quality. While there is still a PSNR gap to traditional sorted alpha-blending renderers, the approach is practical because the denoiser overhead is compensated by the time savings from removing sorting and blending processes, making stochastic rendering with temporal denoising a viable alternative for real-time applications.

Abstract: Stochastic rendering eliminates the sorting and alpha blending process in Gaussian splatting, at the cost of introducing spatial noise. Formulating temporal denoising over the pixel stream shared by view-consistent stochastic splatting renderers, we propose a temporal neural denoiser validated on stochastic 2D Gaussian Splatting rendering, combining dual-path exponential moving average accumulation, per-pixel learned trust prediction for history validation, a fixed anisotropic spatial filter and a variance-gated composition with stabilization. The denoiser suppresses the noise, achieving temporally stable, visually compelling outputs during free camera navigation, all while retaining the sort-free, blend-free rasterization performance. The combined pipeline retains a PSNR gap to sorted alpha-blending renderers, but the denoiser's overhead stays below the time saved by removing sorting and blending.

</details>


### [2] [Deepfakes and Synthetic Media: Generation, Detection, and Governance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25017)
*Alexandros Gazis,Efstathios Karypidis,Kleanthi Santamouri,Theodoros Vavouras,Nikos E. Mastorakis,Stylianos Pappas*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A comprehensive survey on deepfake detection using visual intelligence and computer vision techniques, covering generation architectures (GANs, latent diffusion, neural rendering, video synthesis), detection methods (CNN, transformer, frequency-based), artifact analysis, evaluation benchmarks, and governance frameworks including cryptographic provenance and regulatory approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The proliferation of deepfakes—synthetic audiovisual content generated by deep learning models—poses critical threats across civilian and military domains, enabling identity fraud, disinformation, and evidence fabrication. This has severe consequences in journalism, finance, healthcare, and legal contexts, necessitating effective detection mechanisms to combat misinformation and maintain institutional trust.

Method: The survey systematically reviews: (1) Generation architectures including GANs, autoencoders, latent diffusion models, neural rendering, and video synthesis systems; (2) Detection approaches leveraging spatial, temporal, frequency-domain, and physiological artifacts; (3) Detector families including CNN-based, transformer-based, and frequency-based methods; (4) Evaluation benchmarks and cross-generator generalization protocols; (5) Governance solutions including cryptographic provenance standards, watermarking, and regulatory frameworks (EU AI Act, DSA, GDPR).

Result: The survey identifies key visual artifacts, digital patterns, and physiological cues exploited by detectors, reviews major detection architectures, and highlights cross-generator generalization as the field's central challenge. It also examines the effectiveness of defense-in-depth strategies integrating forensic detection, verifiable provenance, and institutional accountability.

Conclusion: Effective deepfake governance requires a multi-layered defense approach combining forensic detection capabilities, verifiable cryptographic provenance standards, and institutional accountability mechanisms. The field faces significant challenges in achieving robust cross-generator generalization, while emerging regulatory frameworks (EU AI Act, DSA, GDPR) provide initial governance structures but require continued development to address evolving threats.

Abstract: Deepfakes, synthetic audiovisual content produced by deep generative models, have escalated into a critical threat across civilian and military domains, enabling identity fraud, disinformation campaigns, and evidence fabrication. In high-stakes environments, ranging from journalism and finance to healthcare and legal contexts, the consequences extend to severe misinformation, market manipulation, identity fraud, and the erosion of institutional trust. This entry explores how modern visual intelligence and computer vision techniques are used to detect deepfakes. It outlines key deepfake generation models, such as GANs, autoencoders, neural rendering, and diffusion systems, while also explaining how adversarial methods enhance realism and challenge existing detectors. The overview highlights visual artifacts, digital patterns, and physiological cues commonly leveraged in detection and reviews major CNN, transformer, and frequency-based approaches. It also summarizes evaluation practices and the difficulty of achieving strong generalization. Finally, it identifies emerging directions, including modern intelligence techniques for civilian and military content verification. This survey covers generation architectures (GANs, latent diffusion, neural rendering, video synthesis), the spatial, temporal, frequency-domain, and physiological artifacts they produce, and the detector families that exploit them. We examine evaluation benchmarks and protocols, highlighting cross-generator generalization as the field's central open challenge. Beyond detection, we discuss cryptographic provenance standards, watermarking, and regulatory frameworks (EU AI Act, DSA, GDPR). We conclude that effective deepfake governance requires defense-in-depth integrating forensic detection, verifiable provenance, and institutional accountability.

</details>


### [3] [PartLLM: A Unified Multimodal Foundation for 3D Part Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25832)
*Zhe Zhu,Yiheng Zhang,Peng Li,Zixing Zhao,Honghua Chen,Yaqing Zhang,Le Wan,Zhiyang Dou,Cheng Lin,Yuan Liu,Mingqiang Wei,Wenping Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: PartLLM is a unified multimodal model that formulates 3D part segmentation as an intent-conditioned generative problem, supporting text-guided, interactive, and full-shape semantic decomposition within a single model by using autoregressive semantic decomposition and a decomposition-aware decoder.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing 3D part segmentation approaches typically address specific settings in isolation (e.g., text-guided or point-based interaction), but the authors argue these settings can be unified under an intent-conditioned generative framework where different prompts specify the desired part decomposition.

Method: PartLLM uses a two-stage approach: (1) conditioned on an input 3D shape and a user prompt, it autoregressively generates semantic part hypotheses as queries, and (2) feeds these queries to a decomposition-aware decoder that jointly predicts coherent part masks. This unified design supports multiple task settings within a single model.

Result: PartLLM consistently outperforms task-specific baselines across extensive experiments conducted across different task settings (text-guided part segmentation, interactive segmentation, and full-shape semantic decomposition with controllable granularity).

Conclusion: The work demonstrates the effectiveness of unifying 3D part segmentation under an intent-conditioned generative formulation, achieving superior performance over task-specific approaches while providing a more flexible and unified solution.

Abstract: Part segmentation is a fundamental problem in computer graphics and 3D vision. Recent works have expanded 3D part segmentation beyond fixed taxonomies, but existing approaches typically only address a specific setting, such as text-guided part segmentation or point-based interaction. In this work, we argue that these settings can be unified as an intent-conditioned generative problem, where different prompts specify the desired part decomposition. To this end, we introduce PartLLM, a unified multimodal model that formulates 3D part segmentation as autoregressive semantic decomposition. Conditioned on an input shape and a user prompt, PartLLM autoregressively generates semantic part hypotheses as queries for mask prediction and feeds them to a decomposition-aware decoder that jointly predicts coherent part masks. This unified design supports text-guided part segmentation, interactive segmentation, and full-shape semantic decomposition with controllable granularity within a single model. Extensive experiments across these task settings show that PartLLM consistently outperforms task-specific baselines, demonstrating the effectiveness of unifying 3D part segmentation under an intent-conditioned generative formulation.

</details>


### [4] [SPARC: SuperPixel-Aware Region Contrastive Learning for Self-Supervised Dense Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25067)
*David Szczecina,Yuanpei Xiang,Jitao Hu,David Clausi,Yuhao Chen,Jason Deglint,Paul Fieguth*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SPARC is a region-level contrastive learning framework for self-supervised visual representation learning that uses superpixels to establish explicit correspondence between augmented image views, outperforming previous methods like MoCo-v2 and DenseCL on dense prediction tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing contrastive self-supervised learning methods focus primarily on image-level or pixel-level representation learning, while region-level representation learning remains less explored despite its potential for improving dense prediction tasks like semantic segmentation and object detection.

Method: SPARC introduces a region contrastive branch that performs superpixel-based feature pooling and optimizes a region-level contrastive objective jointly with a global image-level objective. The framework leverages superpixels to establish explicit correspondence between augmented image views.

Result: Under identical settings, SPARC consistently outperforms previous methods such as MoCo-v2 and DenseCL, achieving improvements of up to +9.79 mIoU for semantic segmentation and +4.88 AP for object detection. Ablation studies demonstrate that region-level objectives produce the strongest performance.

Conclusion: Region-level contrastive learning is an effective approach for improving self-supervised visual pretraining for dense prediction tasks, providing significant gains over image-level and pixel-level approaches.

Abstract: Self-supervised learning (SSL) has become an effective approach for learning visual representations without manual annotations. Among SSL approaches, contrastive learning has been widely used for visual representation learning. However, existing contrastive SSL methods have focused primarily on image-level or pixel-level representation learning, while region-level representation learning remains less explored. We propose SPARC, a region-level contrastive learning framework that leverages superpixels to establish explicit correspondence between augmented image views. SPARC introduces a region contrastive branch that performs superpixel-based feature pooling and optimizes a region-level contrastive objective jointly with a global image-level objective. Under identical settings, SPARC consistently outperforms previous methods such as MoCo-v2 and DenseCL, achieving improvements of up to +9.79 mIoU for semantic segmentation and +4.88 AP for object detection. Ablation studies further demonstrate that region-level objectives produce the strongest performance. Thus, region-level contrastive learning is an effective approach for improving self-supervised visual pretraining for dense prediction tasks. Code repository can be accessed at https://github.com/xRIPEIx/SPARC.

</details>


### [5] [You've Seen Enough: Quality-Constrained Image Coding for Machines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25108)
*Khoa Pham-Dinh,Sanaz Nami,Hamed Rezazadegan Tavakoli,Moncef Gabbouj,Farhad Pakdaman*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A method for Image Coding for Machines (ICM) that balances human-acceptable visual quality with machine vision task performance by formulating joint compression-segmentation as a constrained optimization problem, using penalty functions to guide quality to a target level.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Machine-vision systems increasingly consume visual data instead of human observers. Existing methods either optimize for machine tasks or human quality, but this paper aims to cap human-observed quality at a just-acceptable level while using remaining capacity to improve machine performance, inspired by just-noticeable distortion concepts.

Method: Recasts joint compression-segmentation training as constrained optimization where the codec meets a predefined acceptable target visual quality while a task term consumes remaining coding capacity. Two penalty functions are proposed: an absolute function and a bilinear function (with steeper slope when target quality is exceeded).

Result: Under quality constraint, the proposed method achieves BD-rate savings of -22.82% over unconstrained joint rate-distortion-task optimization and -29.81% over a simple rate-distortion baseline, maintaining same task performance while meeting target visual quality with reasonable error and no complexity overhead.

Conclusion: The method successfully reduces bitrate while maintaining machine task performance and meeting target visual quality constraints, demonstrating the effectiveness of quality-gated joint optimization for image coding for machines.

Abstract: Visual data is increasingly consumed by machine-vision systems rather than by human observers. Image Coding for Machines (ICM) compresses images assuming the main observer is a computer vision application and that the human observer needs to inspect or validate the decisions. Inspired by just-noticeable distortion, which sets the quality to the just-acceptable level for human observers, we aim to cap the human-observed quality at a desired level, with the goal of using the remaining coding capacity to improve the machine performance. We recast joint compression-segmentation training as a constrained optimization problem in which the codec must meet a predefined acceptable target visual quality while a task term consumes the remaining coding capacity. We solve this by designing a penalty function to guide the quality to the desired target. We propose two penalty functions, an absolute function and a bilinear function, the latter applying a steeper slope once the target visual quality is exceeded. Experimental results show that, under the quality constraint, the proposed method achieves a BD-rate of $-22.82\%$ over an unconstrained joint rate--distortion--task optimization and $-29.81\%$ over a simple rate--distortion baseline, showcasing bitrate reduction with the same task performance. This is achieved while the codec also meets the target visual quality with a reasonable error and without adding any complexity overhead.

</details>


### [6] [Geometric and Semantic Coupling for Interaction Understanding in 3D Scenes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25247)
*Hanyang Kong,Xingyi Yang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Segment-Snap is a method for understanding interactions in 3D scenes by connecting movable parts, their motion, and operational regions through physical relationships between parts and handles. It uses learned predictors to identify part surfaces and handles, combined with a geometric decoder that applies planar and upright priors to constrain motion without training a motion regressor.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding interactions in 3D scenes requires a joint description of movable parts, their motion, and the regions through which they can be operated. Current methods lack integrated approaches that connect these components through physical relationships.

Method: The method combines learned predictors for identifying broad part surfaces and small handles with a geometric decoder using planar and upright priors to constrain motion. Hinge lines are selected using predicted handle locations without training a motion regressor. A joint part-and-handle predictor supplies additional handle candidates, with motion classes refined using containing parts. Each information transfer is applied once without iterative feedback.

Result: On Articulate3D validation: handle guidance raises motion-gated AP from 13.74% to 40.98% at fixed masks and axes; additional handle candidates raise handle AP from 24.63% to 29.65%; part-based class correction adds 0.98 points; full context reaches 30.99%.

Conclusion: The method demonstrates the benefits of combining geometric and semantic evidence for interaction understanding. Handle guidance significantly improves motion prediction, while additional handle candidates and part-based class correction provide incremental improvements. The approach establishes the value of connecting physical relationships between parts and handles for interaction understanding in 3D scenes.

Abstract: Interaction understanding in 3D scenes requires a joint description of movable parts, their motion, and the regions through which they can be operated. We present Segment-Snap, which connects these outputs through the physical relationship between parts and handles. Learned predictors identify broad part surfaces and small handles. A geometric decoder uses planar and upright priors to constrain motion, then selects hinge lines using predicted handle locations, without training a motion regressor. Conversely, a joint part-and-handle predictor supplies additional handle candidates, whose motion classes are refined using containing parts. Each information transfer is applied once, without iterative feedback. On Articulate3D validation, handle guidance raises motion-gated AP from 13.74% to 40.98% at fixed masks and axes. Additional handle candidates raise handle AP from 24.63% to 29.65%; part-based class correction adds 0.98 points, and full context reaches 30.99%. Repeated training, learned-decoder controls and paired visualizations establish the benefits and limitations of combining geometric and semantic evidence for interaction understanding.

</details>


### [7] [ImIR: Image-Instruction Tuning for All-in-One Image Restoration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25267)
*Süleyman Aslan,Görkay Aydemir,Mısra Yavuz,Yunus Bilge Kurt,Nasrin Rahimi,Ahmet Rasim Emirdağı,Burak Can Biner,M. Akın Yılmaz*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes an image restoration method that adapts a large pretrained image-editing model (Qwen-Image-Edit) using a low-rank adapter. Instead of using text prompts, the method generates instructions directly from the degraded image through a lightweight token mapper that maps vision-language embeddings from degraded to clean images. A single adapter handles six restoration tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Practical image restoration requires handling many degradation types with one model. Existing methods using text prompts for conditioning have limitations: they require degradation labels and cannot handle tasks with non-unique targets. The authors seek a task-agnostic approach that generates instructions directly from the degraded image.

Method: The method uses two paths: (1) Structure path through the model's VAE, and (2) Semantic instruction path via a lightweight token mapper that shifts degraded image's vision-language embedding toward what a clean image would produce. The instruction is a continuous vector, allowing scaling for tasks with non-unique targets (e.g., low-light enhancement). One Qwen-Image-Edit model is adapted to six tasks using a single low-rank adapter trained for ~3 hours on one GPU.

Result: Image instruction outperforms text conditioning in matched comparisons. The method supports task-agnostic restoration without requiring degradation labels, which text-based variants cannot achieve.

Conclusion: The proposed image instruction approach successfully enables task-agnostic restoration using a single adapter. It outperforms text conditioning and eliminates the need for degradation labels, demonstrating the effectiveness of deriving semantic instructions directly from degraded images rather than relying on text prompts.

Abstract: Degradations vary widely across images, so a practical restoration system has to handle many degradation types with one model. A recent and effective recipe adapts a large pretrained image-editing model to restoration using a small low-rank adapter with a text prompt. We replace that prompt with an instruction derived from the degraded image itself. The image reaches the editor through two paths: its structure comes from the model's VAE, and its semantic instruction comes from a lightweight token mapper that shifts the degraded image's vision-language embedding toward the embedding a clean image would produce. Because the instruction is a continuous vector, scaling it yields a family of valid restorations for tasks whose target is not unique, such as low-light enhancement. We adapt one Qwen-Image-Edit model to six tasks with a single adapter trained in about three hours on one GPU. The image instruction outperforms text conditioning under a matched comparison, and it supports task agnostic restoration without a degradation label, which the text variant does not.

</details>


### [8] [RULER: Instance-aware Rubric Rewards for SVG Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25270)
*Hangyu Ran,Yuhao Zheng,Yingying Zhang,Kevin Qinghong Lin,Han Peng*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: RULER addresses the problem of evaluating and optimizing SVG generation from natural language by introducing rubric-based scoring instead of scalar metrics. The method uses a vision-language judge with a multi-axis rubric that correlates better with human judgments, and applies this to reinforcement learning via Group Relative Policy Optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The problem is that generating SVG from natural language has no absolute visual ground truth, making evaluation and policy optimization difficult. Existing scalar metrics (CLIP, Aesthetic) transfer poorly to stylized vector content and cause reward hacking when used as RL rewards.

Method: RULER converts each instruction into an instance-aware rubric of six items spanning semantic, visual, and stylistic axes. A judge VLM scores rendered rollouts item-by-item, and weighted satisfactions form a fine-grained reward optimized via Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). The rubric is derived from text alone, requiring no paired SVG ground truth or human preference labels.

Result: RULER lifts rubric score from 0.432/0.395 to 0.693/0.683 on MMSVG-Illustration and MMSVG-Icon datasets, surpassing dedicated SVG specialists and matching the larger DeepSeek-V3.

Conclusion: Rubric design is identified as the active lever for RL on open-ended SVG generation. The method demonstrates that rubric-based scoring is more effective than scalar metrics for evaluating and optimizing SVG generation from natural language instructions.

Abstract: Generating Scalable Vector Graphics (SVG) code from natural-language instructions is an open-ended task without absolute visual ground truth, leaving both evaluation and policy optimization without a faithful signal. Scalar metrics (CLIP, Aesthetic) calibrated on natural images transfer poorly to stylized vector content, and reusing them as RL rewards triggers reward hacking. We address both limitations with rubric-based scoring. We first establish empirically that prompting a vision-language judge with a multi-axis rubric correlates with human judgments far better than scalar metrics, both across samples and within instructions. Building on this finding, we introduce RULER (Instance-aware Rubric Rewards for Reinforcement Learning), which converts each instruction into an instance-aware rubric of six items spanning semantic, visual, and stylistic axes; a judge VLM scores rendered rollouts item-by-item, and the weighted satisfactions form a fine-grained reward optimized via Group Relative Policy Optimization. Because the rubric is derived from text alone, RULER requires neither paired SVG ground truth nor human preference labels. On MMSVG-Illustration and MMSVG-Icon, RULER lifts the rubric score from 0.432/0.395 to 0.693/0.683, surpassing dedicated SVG specialists and matching the substantially larger DeepSeek-V3, with ablations identifying rubric design as the active lever for RL on open-ended SVG generation. The project page is available at https://hangyuran.github.io/RULER/.

</details>


### [9] [Uncertainty-Aware 3D Residual Wavelet Diffusion for Ultra Low-Field MRI Super-Resolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25319)
*Rui W. Yeow,Millie Beament,Fred Dick,Raha Razin,Martina Bocchetta,David L. Thomas,Henry F. J. Tregidgo,Daniel C. Alexander,James H. Cole*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A 3D residual wavelet diffusion model for ultra low-field MRI super-resolution that generates per-voxel uncertainty maps while maintaining volumetric accuracy, enabling whole-brain posterior sampling on a single GPU.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ultra low-field MRI expands global access but produces low-quality scans. Current regression-based super-resolution returns deterministic estimates without quantifying anatomical ambiguity - regions where low-field input leaves anatomy underdetermined. Generative diffusion models could sample the posterior distribution but face memory bottlenecks, slow sampling, and domain shifts when applied to 3D whole-brain MRI.

Method: Three key innovations: (1) Lossless wavelet reparameterisation shrinks spatial grid to fit whole brain on single GPU; (2) Residual shifting accelerates sampling by starting from low-field input; (3) Domain randomisation promotes scanner generalization without paired training data. Evaluated on healthy cohort (n=19) and cognitive impairment pilot (n=11) imaged at 0.064T and 3T.

Result: On healthy cohort (n=19), method matches leading regression approach in volumetric accuracy while additionally generating per-voxel uncertainty maps highlighting underdetermined regions. On pilot dataset (n=11) with cognitive impairment, disease-relevant atrophy is preserved rather than normalised towards healthy prior.

Conclusion: The framework brings whole-brain posterior sampling to low-field super-resolution without sacrificing volumetric accuracy, providing uncertainty quantification that identifies regions where anatomy remains underdetermined.

Abstract: Ultra low-field MRI expands global access to neuroimaging but produces scans with low signal-to-noise ratio, reduced contrast, and thick slices. While regression-based super-resolution can recover anatomical detail for segmentation, it returns a single deterministic estimate that gives no indication of regions where the low-field input leaves anatomy underdetermined. Generative diffusion models offer an alternative by sampling the posterior distribution of plausible high-field images, quantifying this anatomical ambiguity. However, applying them to 3D whole-brain MRI is restricted by memory bottlenecks, slow sampling, and scanner domain shifts. We propose a 3D residual wavelet diffusion model that combines three ideas to overcome these hurdles. A lossless wavelet reparameterisation shrinks the spatial grid to fit a whole brain on a single GPU, residual shifting accelerates sampling by starting from the low-field input, and domain randomisation promotes scanner generalisation without paired training data. As the high-field reference is not a voxel-aligned ground truth, we evaluate downstream volumetric agreement. On a healthy cohort (n=19) imaged at 0.064T and 3T, our method matches a leading general-purpose regression approach in volumetric accuracy while additionally generating per-voxel uncertainty maps highlighting underdetermined regions. Furthermore, on a pilot dataset (n=11) of participants with cognitive impairment, disease-relevant atrophy is preserved rather than normalised towards a healthy prior. Our framework brings whole-brain posterior sampling to low-field super-resolution without sacrificing volumetric accuracy.

</details>


### [10] [MirrorDistill: Illumination-Aware Latent Distillation for Efficient Low-Light Restoration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25331)
*Farida Mohsen,Tala Zaim,Nurul Izni Rusli,Ali Al-Zawqari,Ali Safa,Samir Brahim Belhaouari*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MirrorDistill is an illumination-aware latent distillation framework for low-light image enhancement that uses feature mirroring to link low-light and clean domains. It employs a teacher-student architecture where a teacher decoder processes clean images to generate latent targets that supervise a student network at both encoder and decoder levels, with illumination-aware weighting to emphasize underexposed regions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most existing LLIE methods rely only on output-level reconstruction losses that supervise only the final restored image, leaving the intermediate feature recovery process weakly constrained. This paper aims to address this limitation by providing stronger supervision through feature-level alignment.

Method: The proposed MirrorDistill framework uses: (1) A shared encoder and exponential-moving-average (EMA) teacher decoder that process clean reference images to generate clean-domain latent targets; (2) These targets supervise the low-light student at two levels: raw encoder features and standardized multi-scale decoder projections; (3) Layer-by-layer alignment with illumination-aware weighting that gives greater emphasis to underexposed regions; (4) The teacher and reference branches are used only during training, so inference requires only the lightweight student encoder-decoder.

Result: MirrorDistill outperforms state-of-the-art methods on the real-captured LOL-v2-Real set while having the lowest compute complexity (GMACs). It remains competitive on LOL-v1 and LOL-v2-Synthetic datasets. Ablation studies demonstrate the contributions of encoder mirror, decoder mirror, and illumination-aware weighting.

Conclusion: The proposed illumination-aware latent distillation framework effectively addresses the weak intermediate feature supervision problem in LLIE. By using feature mirroring between teacher and student networks with illumination-aware weighting, the method achieves superior performance on real-world low-light images while maintaining low computational complexity, making it practical for deployment.

Abstract: Low-light image enhancement (LLIE) is an im- portant component of visual sensing systems operating under degraded illumination, including nighttime surveillance, au- tonomous navigation, remote sensing, and inspection in poorly lit industrial environments. Most LLIE methods rely on output- level reconstruction losses that supervise only the final restored image, leaving the intermediate feature recovery process weakly constrained. This paper proposes MirrorDistill, an illumination- aware latent distillation framework that links the low-light and clean domains through feature mirroring. During training, a shared encoder and an exponential-moving-average teacher decoder process the clean reference image to generate clean- domain latent targets. These targets supervise the low-light student at two levels: raw encoder features and standardized multi-scale decoder projections. The alignment is applied layer by layer, while a proposed illumination-aware weighting scheme gives greater emphasis to underexposed regions. The teacher and reference branches are used only during training, so inference requires only the lightweight student encoder-decoder and in- troduces no teacher-side computational cost. Under evaluation on the standard LOL benchmarks, MirrorDistill outperforms the state-of-the-art methods on the real-captured LOL-v2-Real set, while having the lowest compute complexity (GMACs) and while remaining competitive on the LOL-v1 and LOL-v2-Synthetic datasets. Ablation studies further show the contributions of the encoder mirror, decoder mirror, and illumination-aware weighting. Finally, we release our code as open-source for the benefit of future research.

</details>


### [11] [Sex Estimation from Footwear Outsole Impressions Using CNN Transfer Learning and Interpretable Image Statistics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25386)
*Jinyi Niu,Ziyi Song,Weining Shen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper investigates using convolutional neural networks (CNNs) to estimate sex from footwear outsole impressions, comparing transfer learning approaches with traditional feature-based classification methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Footwear outsole impressions are common forensic evidence, but quantitative methods for estimating wearer attributes (like sex) from these impressions remain underdeveloped. There is a need for more sophisticated analytical approaches in forensic footwear analysis.

Method: The study compared CNN transfer learning with traditional feature-based classification using a publicly available outsole-impression dataset. They used a shoe-level training/test partition to prevent data leakage. Methods evaluated included: (1) end-to-end fine-tuning of pretrained CNNs, (2) frozen feature extraction with SVM classification, and (3) hybrid feature fusion combining handcrafted, geometric, and metadata-derived descriptors.

Result: Fine-tuned CNNs achieved the strongest predictive performance and substantially outperformed traditional classifiers. Frozen-feature approaches offered a less computationally demanding alternative. Exploratory analysis revealed CNN representations associated with frequency threshold ratio, image contrast, and wavelet-based summaries, connecting learned representations to measurable properties of outsole impressions.

Conclusion: CNN transfer learning captures discriminative information beyond traditional handcrafted descriptors and offers a promising approach to footwear-based forensic screening. However, further validation on independently collected and casework-like impressions is required before operational use.

Abstract: Footwear outsole impressions are a common form of forensic pattern evidence, yet quantitative methods for estimating wearer attributes from these images remain relatively underdeveloped. We investigate binary sex estimation from footwear outsole impressions by comparing convolutional neural network (CNN) transfer learning with traditional feature-based classification. Using a publicly available outsole-impression dataset, we adopt a shoe-level training and test partition that keeps replicate scans of the same physical shoe together to reduce data leakage. We evaluate pretrained CNNs through end-to-end fine-tuning, frozen feature extraction followed by support vector machine classification, and hybrid feature fusion incorporating handcrafted, geometric, and metadata-derived descriptors. Fine-tuned CNNs achieve the strongest overall predictive performance and substantially outperform traditional classifiers trained on the manually specified descriptors alone, while frozen-feature approaches offer a less computationally demanding alternative. Exploratory analysis of low-dimensional CNN representations reveals associations with frequency threshold ratio, image contrast, and wavelet-based summaries, providing a connection between learned representations and measurable properties of outsole impressions. These findings suggest that CNN transfer learning captures discriminative information beyond the descriptors considered and offers a promising approach to footwear-based forensic screening. Further validation on independently collected and casework-like impressions is needed before operational use.

</details>


### [12] [Directional Total Variation-Regularized Implicit Neural Representations (DTV-INR) for Continuous Super-Resolution in Degraded Imaging Domains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25429)
*Mahmoud Saeedi Kelishami*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: DTV-INR is a novel variational framework for resolution-agnostic image super-resolution that combines implicit neural networks (SIREN) with an anisotropic total variation regularizer guided by structure tensors. The method uses a dynamic Riemannian metric tensor field to preferentially direct diffusion along structural contours, avoiding staircasing artifacts. Rigorous well-posedness analysis in H^1(Ω) is provided, with an alternating optimization algorithm for implementation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Implicit neural representations (INRs) for image super-resolution suffer from two key limitations: (1) lack of physical constraints leading to overfitting, and (2) classical staircasing artifacts from scalar total variation regularization. The paper addresses these by introducing anisotropic, structure-tensor-informed regularization that respects image structure while enforcing smoothness.

Method: The method casts continuous-to-discrete acquisition as an ill-posed inverse problem. A SIREN-based coordinate network is equipped with a dynamic Riemannian metric tensor field D(x). The anisotropic TV regularizer uses spectral decomposition of D(x) to direct diffusion parallel to dominant structural contours while penalizing cross-edge dissipation. An alternating projected optimization algorithm decouples network parameter tuning from adaptive tensor field updates.

Result: Experiments on clinical brain MRI and biomedical TEM images show substantial improvements: PSNR gains of +5.05 dB over unregularized INRs and +1.71-2.85 dB over isotropic TV-INR across continuous (non-integer) upsampling factors. The method demonstrates noise robustness up to σ_η = 0.10 and monotonic preconditioned convergence.

Conclusion: The proposed DTV-INR successfully integrates anisotropic regularization with implicit neural representations, achieving state-of-the-art super-resolution results while maintaining theoretical guarantees. The structure-tensor-informed approach effectively suppresses staircasing artifacts while preserving structural boundaries.

Abstract: In this paper, we introduce the Directional Total Variation-Regularized Implicit Neural Representation (DTV-INR), an advanced variational paradigm that synergistically integrates coordinate-driven implicit neural networks with an anisotropic, structure-tensor-informed total variation regularizer tailored for resolution-agnostic image super-resolution. Casting the continuous-to-discrete acquisition process into an ill-posed inverse problem framework, our formulation equips a SIREN-architected coordinate network with a dynamic Riemannian metric tensor field D(x). By leveraging its spectral decomposition, the proposed regularizer preferentially directs diffusion parallel to dominant structural contours while penalizing cross-edge dissipation, successfully circumventing the classical staircasing artifacts inherent to scalar total variation schemes. We rigorously prove the well-posedness of this formulation in H^1(Omega) by establishing the existence, uniqueness, and metric stability of the variational minimizer, and realize this via an alternating projected optimization algorithm that decouples network parameter tuning from adaptive tensor field updates. Comprehensive experiments conducted on clinical brain magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) and biomedical transmission electron microscopy confirm substantial quantitative and qualitative improvements, yielding PSNR enhancements reaching +5.05 dB over baseline unregularized INRs and +1.71-2.85 dB over isotropic TV-INR across continuous (non-integer) upsampling factors, alongside remarkable noise robustness up to sigma_eta = 0.10 and monotonic preconditioned convergence behavior.

</details>


### [13] [Combinatorial Network-Based Manifold Topological Deep Learning for Image Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25453)
*Alice Wachira,Xiang Liu,Zhe Su,Yiying Tong,Ge Wang,Guo-Wei Wei*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CNMTDL is a manifold topological deep learning framework that integrates Hodge decomposition with combinatorial attention mechanisms for medical image analysis, representing images as discrete manifolds and enabling enhanced higher-order message passing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional CNNs are limited in preserving geometric relationships and higher-order structural information in medical images because they model images as regular Euclidean grids. Existing MTDL methods have not fully exploited discrete manifold structures within combinatorial complex neural networks.

Method: The approach represents medical images as discrete manifolds, decomposes them into three Hodge components using Hodge decomposition, concatenates features from these components, and embeds them into a combinatorial complex architecture with attention-based blocks enabling enhanced higher-order message passing between 0-cells and 2-cells.

Result: CNMTDL was evaluated on six 2D and 3D datasets from the MedMNIST v2 benchmark, demonstrating its effectiveness for medical image analysis.

Conclusion: The integration of Hodge decomposition with combinatorial attention mechanisms in CNMTDL provides a promising paradigm for medical image analysis that better captures geometric and topological structures compared to conventional approaches.

Abstract: Medical image analysis remains fundamentally challenging because of the intricate geometric and topological structures present in medical data. Conventional convolutional neural networks model images as regular Euclidean grids, limiting their ability to preserve geometric relationships and higher-order structural information. Recently, manifold topological deep learning (MTDL) has emerged as a promising paradigm that integrates deep learning with geometric and topological representations. Nevertheless, existing methods have not yet fully exploited discrete manifold structures within combinatorial complex neural networks. To bridge this gap, we introduce CNMTDL, a MTDL framework that integrates Hodge decomposition with a combinatorial attention mechanism. In our approach, medical images are represented as discrete manifolds and decomposed into three Hodge components. Features extracted from these components are concatenated and embedded into a combinatorial complex architecture, enabling enhanced higher-order message passing between $0$-cells and $2$-cells through attention-based blocks. We evaluate CNMTDL on six two-dimensional and three-dimensional datasets from the MedMNIST v2 benchmark, demonstrating its effectiveness for medical image analysis.

</details>


### [14] [MIND the Gap: A Geographic Implicit Neural Representation with Adjustable Spatial Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25454)
*Isaac Corley,Arjun Rao,Esther Rolf,Konstantin Klemmer,Evan Shelhamer,Nils Lehmann,Marc Rußwurm,Gengchen Mai,Nathan Jacobs,Hannah Kerner*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper introduces MIND (Matryoshka Implicit Neural Distillation), a method to improve geographic implicit neural representations (INRs) by distilling embeddings from specialist pretrained geospatial models into a generalist coordinate embedding. The method uses nested supervision at multiple embedding dimensions (chunks), where early chunks capture coarse geographic variation and later chunks add fine-grained details. A new benchmark called CoordBench (52 datasets, 78 targets) is introduced to evaluate INR performance. MIND achieves state-of-the-art results in both local interpolation and regional holdout scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Geographic measurements are sparse, leaving large areas unlabeled. While geographic INRs can learn smooth embeddings queryable at any coordinate, they struggle with generalization to distant/remote regions - a critical need for remote sensing applications. Existing approaches lack effective methods for transferring knowledge from specialist models to generalist representations.

Method: MIND uses a distillation approach to combine embeddings from multiple specialist pretrained geospatial models into a single generalist coordinate embedding. The key innovation is nested supervision at several embedding dimensions, defining a series of contiguous chunks. This allows: (1) early chunks to capture coarse geographic variation, (2) later chunks to add fine-grained details. A downstream predictor can either retain only leading chunks or use the proposed Chunked Penalty to downweight later chunks without retraining the INR.

Result: MIND and its Chunked Penalty variant achieve the highest aggregate regression and classification scores among tested INRs. The method also achieves the highest scores under regional holdout evaluation, setting a new state-of-the-art for geographic INRs. CoordBench enables comprehensive evaluation across 52 datasets and 78 targets at various spatial scales.

Conclusion: MIND successfully addresses the generalization challenge in geographic INRs through matryoshka-style embedding distillation. The Chunked Penalty mechanism provides flexibility in balancing coarse vs. fine-grained geographic representation without retraining. CoordBench provides a rigorous benchmark for future INR research, particularly for remote sensing applications requiring generalization to unseen regions.

Abstract: Geographic measurements are often sparse, leaving large areas without labels for the quantities we want to map. Geographic implicit neural representations (INRs) address this by learning smooth, general-purpose embeddings that can be queried at any coordinate. Downstream models combine these embeddings with sparse labels to predict target values at unsampled locations without satellite imagery at inference. However, generalization to distant regions remains largely unexplored, despite its importance for remote sensing applications. We introduce Matryoshka Implicit Neural Distillation (MIND), which distills embeddings from specialist pretrained geospatial models into a single generalist coordinate embedding with adjustable spatial granularity. MIND uses nested supervision at several embedding dimensions, which define a series of contiguous chunks. In our experiments, early chunks capture coarser geographic variation, while later chunks add more fine-grained details. A downstream predictor can retain only leading chunks or be fitted with our Chunked Penalty to downweight later chunks while keeping the full embedding, without retraining the INR. To measure MIND and compare to existing approaches around the world, we introduce CoordBench, a large-scale INR evaluation suite of $52$ datasets and $78$ targets that aims to test both local interpolation and prediction in held-out regions at various spatial scales. MIND and its Chunked Penalty variant achieve the highest aggregate regression and classification scores among tested INRs, and the highest scores overall under regional holdout, setting a new state-of-the-art for geographic INRs.

</details>


### [15] [SAM-V: Geometry-Aware Segment Anything for Multi-View Instance Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25490)
*Jiangshan Gong,Yuqun Wu,Qiqian Fu,Yao Xiao,Chuhang Zou,Shenlong Wang,Derek Hoiem*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SAM-V proposes a novel approach for consistent multi-view instance segmentation by integrating 3D geometry features from VGGT directly into the 2D segmentation foundation model SAM, enabling end-to-end cross-view instance prediction without offline mask matching or explicit 3D reconstruction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for multi-view object segmentation face critical limitations: 3D instance segmentation suffers from scarce 3D annotations, while offline 2D mask-matching pipelines struggle with object identity ambiguity across frames under severe viewpoint and occlusion changes.

Method: SAM-V introduces a prompt-fusion mechanism that enriches sparse SAM prompt tokens with view-specific camera tokens and local VGGT features, making prompts both view-aware and spatially grounded. The mask decoder attends to dense 2D and 3D features, conditioning segmentation directly on multi-view geometry for consistent instance prediction in a single forward pass.

Result: On the IGGT 3D tracking benchmark, SAM-V improves overall IoU by 5 points and frame-level recall by 12 points on the ScanNet++ split over state-of-the-art, and leads on all metrics in the zero-shot ScanNet split.

Conclusion: By jointly leveraging strong 2D and 3D priors through end-to-end integration rather than post-hoc matching, SAM-V achieves superior consistent multi-view instance segmentation, demonstrating the effectiveness of conditioning mask decoding on multi-view geometry.

Abstract: Consistent multi-view object segmentation is critical for 3D perception and robotics, yet remains challenging under severe viewpoint and occlusion changes. Existing methods typically perform 3D instance segmentation on point clouds or rely on offline 2D mask-matching pipelines. However, 3D instance segmentation is limited by scarce 3D annotations, while offline 2D matching suffers from object identity ambiguity across frames. To leverage strong 2D and 3D priors jointly, we propose SAM-V (Geometry-Aware Segment Anything for Multi-View Instance Segmentation). Instead of combining the two priors through post-hoc matching, SAM-V directly integrates features from a feed-forward geometry model (VGGT) into a 2D segmentation foundation model (SAM), trained end-to-end for cross-view instance prediction. SAM-V introduces a prompt-fusion mechanism that enriches sparse SAM prompt tokens with view-specific camera tokens and local VGGT features, making the prompt representation both view-aware and spatially grounded, together with a mask decoder that attends to dense 2D and 3D features. By conditioning the mask decoding directly on multi-view geometry, SAM-V produces consistent multi-view segmentation of a prompted object in a single forward pass without offline mask matching or explicit 3D reconstruction. On the IGGT 3D tracking benchmark, where consistent instance identity across frames directly determines performance, SAM-V improves overall IoU by 5 points and frame-level recall by 12 points on the ScanNet++ split over the state-of-the-art multi-view instance segmentation baseline and leads on all metrics in the zero-shot ScanNet split. Our code and pretrained models are available at https://github.com/gong208/SAM-V.git.

</details>


### [16] [RGSQ: Riemannian Geometry-Sensitive Quantization for Large Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25492)
*Zhiping Wu,Dongdong Ren,Yangchengyu Zhou,Zhengjie Zhang,Wenbin Li,Hongbing Pan,Yang Gao*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: RGSQ is a novel post-training quantization method for vision-language models that uses Riemannian geometry and Fisher information to identify modality-specific sensitive directions, enabling better performance under extremely low-bit settings (W2A8 and W3A8) by steering quantization errors toward loss-insensitive axes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing PTQ methods for unimodal LLMs treat quantization errors as isotropic perturbations under Euclidean assumptions, which fails to capture the modality-specific sensitivity in VLMs. This leads to uneven bit-width distribution and inconsistent performance in low-bit settings when directly adapted to VLMs.

Method: The proposed RGSQ method: (1) formulates quantization as a reconstruction problem under a unified Fisher-Riemannian metric; (2) identifies modality-specific sensitive directions via Riemannian manifold mappings built from modality-partitioned empirical Fisher factors; (3) fuses these into a modality-aware Kronecker-structured metric; (4) applies geometry-aligned rotations to reorient the local tangent frame toward loss-insensitive axes; (5) applies a whitening transformation that maps the Riemannian objective to an equivalent Euclidean form, enabling standard unimodal PTQ methods to evaluate multimodal quantization error.

Result: RGSQ achieves the highest accuracy and stability across diverse mainstream VLM benchmarks under extremely low-bit settings (W2A8 and W3A8). It outperforms VLM-aware baselines (MBQ and MQuant) by up to 5.9% and surpasses single-modality improvements by up to 8.6%.

Conclusion: By incorporating Riemannian geometry and modality-specific Fisher information, RGSQ successfully addresses the limitations of traditional PTQ methods for VLMs, providing a unified framework that captures both vision and language modality sensitivities and enables effective low-bit quantization deployment.

Abstract: Large vision-language models (VLMs) can be efficiently deployed under stringent memory and latency constraints through post training quantization (PTQ). However, most PTQ methods are designed for unimodal large language models (LLMs). These methods treat quantization errors as isotropic perturbations under the Euclidean assumption, which provides weak guidance on directions most sensitive to quantization in VLMs. Consequently, directly adapting unimodal PTQ approaches or solely employing modality-specific scaling often leads to uneven bit-width distribution and inconsistent performance in low-bit settings. To address these challenges, we propose Riemannian Geometry-Sensitive Quantization (RGSQ), which formulates quantization as a reconstruction problem under a unified Fisher-Riemannian metric. RGSQ identifies modality-specific sensitive directions via Riemannian manifold mappings built from modality-partitioned empirical Fisher factors and fused into a modality-aware Kronecker-structured metric. We then apply geometry-aligned rotations to reorient the local tangent frame, steering low-bit perturbations toward loss-insensitive axes. Finally, we apply a whitening transformation that maps the Riemannian objective to an equivalent Euclidean form, enabling standard unimodal PTQ methods to evaluate multimodal quantization error under their original assumptions. Across an extensive and diverse set of mainstream VLM benchmarks, RGSQ achieves the highest accuracy and stability under extremely low-bit settings (W2A8 and W3A8). It outperforms VLM-aware baselines, such as MBQ and MQuant, by up to 5.9% and surpasses single-modality improvements by up to 8.6%.

</details>


### [17] [mbariml: a curation pipeline for turning deep-sea imagery and video into object-detection training data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25500)
*Lonny Lundsten,Kevin Barnard,Dave Caress*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: mbariml is a Python-based pipeline for analyzing deep sea video and images, designed to iteratively improve YOLO object detection models through an integrated human review and data labeling management system.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep sea object detection faces challenges due to sparse, faint, and hard-to-identify organisms. Traditional iterative approaches to improving detector performance require cumbersome data labeling and management. The authors needed a streamlined pipeline that integrates detection, human review, and training data export.

Method: The pipeline uses Ultralytics YOLO for detection on images/video. Detections are stored as reviewable ROIs, grouped by visual similarity for bulk human review. The system tracks video detections as provisional observations and uses an "operational middle-third heuristic" to select representative frames from tracks rather than retaining every detection.

Result: The paper describes the pipeline architecture stage by stage, including the middle-third heuristic for track observation selection. The system allows annotators to validate, relabel, resize, delete, or draw new localizations, with all edits written back to the database.

Conclusion: The human review stage is central to the design, enabling iterative improvement of object detection models through integrated detection, review, and training data export.

Abstract: Training data quantity and quality greatly affect object detection model performance, regardless of model architecture. When using object detection models on video and images from the deep sea, in which the objects of interest, primarily organisms, are sparse, faint, and hard to identify, incremental improvements to object detector performance may require an iterative approach to data labeling and management. This paper presents mbariml, a python-based video and image analysis pipeline built around the data labeling management process. mbariml uses an Ultralytics YOLO detection model, runs it over still images or video, stores every detection as a reviewable region of interest, groups those regions by visual similarity so that a human can accept or reject them in bulk, and exports the result as training data, statistics, image sidecars, and additional metadata. The human review stage is the centre of the design: an annotator can validate, relabel, resize, delete, and draw entirely new localizations, and every one of those edits is written back to the same database the detector wrote to. Video receives particular attention: the software treats each tracker-produced track as a provisional observation and selects one representative frame instead of retaining every detection in the track. We describe the pipeline stage by stage, including the operational middle-third heuristic used for track observation selection.

</details>


### [18] [SBMVTrack: Spike-Budgeted Multi-View Learning for Energy-Efficient UAV Tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25503)
*Pengzhi Zhong,Jiwei Mo,Haolun Li,Ge Zheng,Jingqi Wang,Xinyi Bo,Shuiwang Li*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SBMVTrack is a fully spiking framework for energy-efficient UAV visual tracking that introduces Energy-Weighted Spike Budgeting (EWSB) to constrain energy-weighted firing rate and saturation activity, and Masked Multi-View Target Modeling (MVTM) to enhance target representations through cross-view feature completion and identity-consistency learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing SNN-based trackers typically use spike firing rates only for energy evaluation and lack explicit optimization of actual spike activity. There is a need to reduce redundant spike computations while maintaining tracking performance.

Method: The paper proposes two key components: 1) Energy-Weighted Spike Budgeting (EWSB) - weights actual spike activity according to the computational cost of each spiking layer, constraining energy-weighted firing rate and saturation activity; 2) Masked Multi-View Target Modeling (MVTM) - treats initial template, online template, and search region as correlated temporal views, enhancing target representations through cross-view feature completion and identity-consistency learning.

Result: SBMVTrack effectively reduces the average spike firing rate and theoretical energy consumption while maintaining competitive tracking performance, achieving a better accuracy-energy trade-off on multiple benchmarks.

Conclusion: The proposed SBMVTrack demonstrates that spiking neural networks can achieve energy-efficient UAV tracking by explicitly optimizing spike activity through EWSB while maintaining accuracy through MVTM's multi-view target modeling approach.

Abstract: With sparse and event-driven computation, spiking neural networks show great potential for achieving accurate and energy-efficient UAV visual tracking. However, existing SNN-based trackers typically use spike firing rates only for energy evaluation and lack explicit optimization of actual spike activity. To address this, we propose SBMVTrack, a fully spiking framework for energy-efficient UAV tracking. SBMVTrack introduces Energy-Weighted Spike Budgeting (EWSB). EWSB weights actual spike activity according to the computational cost of each spiking layer. It constrains the energy-weighted firing rate and saturation activity, thereby reducing redundant spike computations. To improve tracking performance under the spike budget constraint, we propose Masked Multi-View Target Modeling (MVTM). This method treats the initial template, online template, and search region from the same sequence as correlated temporal views. It enhances the robustness of target representations through cross-view feature completion and identity-consistency learning. Extensive experiments on multiple benchmarks demonstrate that SBMVTrack effectively reduces the average spike firing rate and theoretical energy consumption. Meanwhile, it maintains competitive tracking performance, achieving a better accuracy-energy trade-off. The source code will be released upon acceptance.

</details>


### [19] [Real-World Perception for Autonomous Driving in Adverse Weather: Enhancing Standard Detectors via Foundation-Guided Auto-Annotation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25515)
*Sepideh Gohari,Goodarz Mehr,Azim Eskandarian*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes a foundation-guided auto-annotation pipeline that uses a large vision foundation model (SAM3) to generate pseudo-labels for training a standard object detector (YOLOv8), enabling improved performance in adverse weather and lighting conditions without architectural modifications or extensive manual annotation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard object detectors for autonomous vehicles degrade in adverse weather and lighting conditions. While large vision foundation models offer robust generalization, they are computationally expensive for real-time deployment. The authors seek a solution that enhances standard detectors without architectural changes.

Method: The authors benchmarked three models (YOLOv8, Co-DETR, SAM3) on a custom dataset with 25 unique operational scenarios across various routes, weather, and lighting conditions. SAM3 showed superior accuracy and resilience, so it was used as an offline auto-annotator to generate pseudo-labels on the unannotated subset of the dataset. YOLOv8 was then fine-tuned on these pseudo-labels.

Result: Fine-tuning YOLOv8 on SAM3-generated pseudo-labels yielded a 16.04% higher overall mean Average Precision (mAP), with improvements of 32.73% in Residential Direct Sunlight and 28.65% in Highway Fog scenarios, demonstrating improved cross-environmental stability.

Conclusion: Standard object detectors can achieve environmental resilience without extensive manual annotation or architectural modifications by leveraging foundation models for auto-annotation.

Abstract: Standard deployment-ready object detectors for autonomous vehicles degrade in adverse weather and lighting conditions without being trained on extensive domain-specific data. While large-scale vision foundation models offer robust zero-shot generalization, their high computational cost makes them impractical for real-time deployment. To bridge this gap, we propose a foundation-guided auto-annotation pipeline that enhances standard detectors without architectural changes. We first benchmark three distinct models, YOLOv8, Co-DETR, and SAM3, on our custom real-world driving dataset spanning 25 unique operational scenarios across various route, weather, and lighting conditions. Based on our analysis, SAM3 demonstrates superior accuracy and resilience across all scenarios. Thus, we deploy it as an offline auto-annotator to generate pseudo-labels on the unannotated subset of our dataset. Fine-tuning the baseline YOLOv8 on these annotations yields a 16.04% higher overall mean Average Precision (mAP) and improves cross-environmental stability compared to the baseline model, highlighted by a 32.73% and 28.65% mAP increase in Residential Direct Sunlight and Highway Fog, respectively. These results demonstrate that standard detectors can achieve environmental resilience without the need for extensive manual annotation or architectural modifications.

</details>


### [20] [Point Diffusion Mamba: Unified Diffusion-State-Space Modeling for Single-View 3D Reconstruction under Data Scarcity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25538)
*Wei Zhou,Xinzhe Shi,Xingxing Hao,Xing Hao,Kang Li,Jinye Peng,Ying He*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Point Diffusion Mamba (PDM) combines diffusion models with state-space models (Mamba) for single-view 3D reconstruction under data-scarce conditions, using hierarchical feature integration and dynamic weighted sampling to outperform state-of-the-art methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Single-view 3D reconstruction from ambiguous 2D observations is fundamentally ill-posed, especially in data-scarce regimes which remain critically underexplored. Existing methods struggle with limited training data.

Method: PDM integrates a lightweight reconstruction module for unordered point clouds with Local Geometric Aggregation and Mamba blocks for joint global-local modeling. It introduces a Hierarchical Feature Integration Network to fuse high-level semantic and local geometric features for each point, plus a Dynamic Weighted Sampling strategy that uses generative priors to enhance reconstruction quality.

Result: Experimental results on ShapeNet and Pix3D benchmarks demonstrate that PDM outperforms state-of-the-art methods in 3D reconstruction under data-scarce settings.

Conclusion: PDM provides an effective solution for single-view 3D reconstruction in data-scarce environments by combining diffusion generative power with Mamba efficiency, achieving superior performance over existing approaches.

Abstract: While single-view 3D reconstruction has seen significant progress, extrapolating complex 3D structures from inherently ambiguous 2D observations remains fundamentally ill-posed, particularly in the critically underexplored data-scarce regime. To address this challenge, we propose Point Diffusion Mamba (PDM), a method that integrates the generative power of diffusion models with the efficiency of state-space model for single-view 3D reconstruction under data-scarce conditions. Specifically, PDM employs a lightweight reconstruction module tailored to handle unordered point-cloud inputs effectively. By combining a Local Geometric Aggregation module with Mamba blocks, our approach jointly models global geometric structures and local details. In 3D reconstruction, each point in the initial noisy input requires a precise prediction, yet the high-level features extracted by the Mamba module capture only abstract semantic information from sparse points. To bridge this gap, we introduce the Hierarchical Feature Integration Network, which fuses high-level semantic and local geometric features for each point, overcoming the limitations of token-based point-cloud reconstruction. Furthermore, we propose a Dynamic Weighted Sampling strategy that adaptively unifies 3D generation with single-view reconstruction by leveraging generative priors to enhance reconstruction quality. Experimental results on the ShapeNet and Pix3D benchmarks demonstrate that PDM outperforms state-of-the-art methods, providing an effective solution for 3D reconstruction under data-scarce settings. Code is available at: https://github.com/NWUzhouwei/PDM.

</details>


### [21] [RootQuantV2: Adapting a Vision Foundation Model for Root-Trait Regression from Minirhizotron Imagery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25567)
*Kinjalk Parth,Sebastian Varela,Andrew D. B. Leakey*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: RootQuantV2 is a computer vision method that predicts root length and surface area directly from minirhizotron images using a self-supervised ViT (DINOv3), requiring only 11.9M trainable parameters (3.78% of the model) and achieving R² of 0.950/0.930 for length/area respectively.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-throughput phenotyping of root traits in field-grown crops is constrained by lack of automated solutions. While minirhizotrons are the standard non-destructive method, existing computer vision approaches require manual trace masks, and training data is scarce. However, large numeric archives of root traits already exist but remain underutilized.

Method: RootQuantV2 adapts a frozen DINOv3 ViT-L/16 using a hybrid parameter-efficient scheme. It replaces the CNN backbone from the original RootQuant with a self-supervised ViT. Only 11.9M parameters (3.78% of the model) are trained, enabling direct prediction of root traits from whole images without requiring manually traced masks.

Result: Length R² = 0.950, Area R² = 0.930. RMSE reduction: 24.3% for length, 20.7% for area compared to RootQuant.

Conclusion: RootQuantV2 successfully repurposes legacy numeric archives for high-throughput, automated root trait estimation. It demonstrates that self-supervised ViTs can effectively predict root traits directly from images, significantly improving upon RootQuant's CNN-based approach while requiring minimal training parameters.

Abstract: A lack of high-throughput phenotyping solutions for root traits in field-grown crops has severely constrained understanding and improvement of below-ground traits and processes. Minirhizotrons are the standard non-destructive root-phenotyping method in field environments. Computer vision solutions are needed to allow automated trait estimation at scale, but training data is scarce and human annotations are often inaccessible because they reside in proprietary software that only exports per-image scalar totals of root length and surface area. Nevertheless, large numeric archives of these root traits already exist. RootQuant showed that the traits can be predicted directly from the whole image by regression, thus removing manually traced masks from the pipeline; RootQuantV2 takes that idea further by replacing RootQuant's CNN backbone with a self-supervised ViT. We adapt a frozen DINOv3 ViT-L/16 with a hybrid parameter-efficient scheme. Training only 11.9M parameters (3.78% of the model), RootQuantV2 achieves length and area $R^2$ of 0.950 and 0.930, respectively, while lowering length/area RMSE by 24.3%/20.7% over RootQuant. RootQuantV2 thus repurposes legacy numeric archives for high-throughput, automated root trait estimation.

</details>


### [22] [Agentic Building-Aware Satellite Gaussian Splatting for Auditable Urban DSM Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25578)
*Wentao Sun,Zhengsen Xu,Yiping Chen,John S. Zelek,Jonathan Li*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper presents an agentic building-aware satellite Gaussian Splatting workflow for urban-scale 3D reconstruction that uses Segment Anything-derived building masks as semantic priors and an Agentic Reconstruction Controller to optimize reconstruction toward analyst-critical building regions rather than average visual fidelity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current neural rendering methods for urban-scale 3D reconstruction from satellite imagery optimize average visual fidelity, but analysts prioritize buildings first. This mismatch creates a need for building-aware reconstruction approaches.

Method: The proposed workflow incorporates: (1) Segment Anything-derived building masks as semantic priors to guide reconstruction, and (2) an Agentic Reconstruction Controller that selects, verifies, and records DSM (Digital Surface Model) reconstruction policies. The method uses building-aware weighting and a staged schedule to balance building-specific and full-scene accuracy.

Result: On the DFC2019 JAX_004 scene, building-aware weighting reduced building-region DSM MAE from 0.844 m to 0.806 m. The staged schedule improved full-scene MAE from 1.362 m to 1.349 m while retaining building gain. Across four JAX scenes, the Agent selected validated policies for both general DSM and building-focused DSM objectives.

Conclusion: Semantic priors can effectively shift reconstruction capacity toward analyst-critical building regions. The system produces building-inventory metadata and per-scene decision records, combining semantic priors, policy selection, region-specific DSM metrics, and DSM-derived GIS surface products for auditable urban 3D analysis.

Abstract: Urban-scale 3D reconstruction from satellite imagery supports disaster response, city monitoring, and geospatial digital twins, yet neural rendering methods typically optimize average visual fidelity rather than the structures that analysts inspect first: buildings. We present an agentic building-aware satellite Gaussian Splatting workflow that uses Segment Anything-derived building masks as semantic priors and an Agentic Reconstruction Controller to select, verify, and record DSM reconstruction policies. On the DFC2019 JAX\_004 scene, building-aware weighting reduces building-region DSM MAE from 0.844 m to 0.806 m, showing that semantic priors can shift reconstruction capacity toward analyst-critical regions. A staged schedule provides a balanced operating point, improving full-scene MAE from 1.362 m to 1.349 m while retaining a building gain. Across four JAX scenes, the Agent selects validated policies for both general DSM and building-focused DSM objectives, and produces building-inventory metadata and per-scene decision records. The system combines semantic priors, policy selection, region-specific DSM metrics, and DSM-derived GIS surface products for auditable urban 3D analysis.

</details>


### [23] [Hi-OPD: Hierarchy-Aware Open-Prompt Detection for Remote Sensing Images](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25584)
*Jinlong Hu,Yi Zhang,Zhiqi Xia,Yikang Zhou,Shunping Ji*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Hi-OPD addresses a critical failure mode in open-prompt object detection for remote sensing where fine-grained category detection (e.g., car, van) fails to generalize to broader ancestor categories (e.g., vehicle) due to inconsistent annotation granularity and missing labels. The paper proposes a hierarchy-aware detector with novel training mechanisms and achieves state-of-the-art results on multiple benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Flat open-prompt training fails to ensure descendant retrieval persists under ancestor queries when multi-source remote sensing annotations exhibit inconsistent granularity and missing labels. A detector may detect "car" and "van" under atomic prompts but miss the same instances under "vehicle" - this cross-level inconsistency is not exposed by flat AP metrics.

Method: Hi-OPD uses three key mechanisms: (1) Hierarchy-safe negative sampling for learning ancestor retrieval, (2) Path multi-positive supervision, (3) One-way upward consistency. Per-source risk exclusion handles potentially missing labels. ConvVPE converts K-shot support boxes into text-compatible embeddings using detector-native features and a shared contrastive head. The dataset RS153-HierOPD contains 175,644 training images with 3.48M boxes mapped to 153 atomic categories with sparse hierarchy and alias relations.

Result: Hi-OPD achieves 79.7 AP50 on DIOR and 72.3 AP50 on DOTA-v2.0, exceeding OpenRSD (76.7/71.8). Under controlled training, the hierarchy recipe raises DOTA-v2.0 parent AP50 from 7.2 to 71.5 and FAIR1M grandparent AP50 from 31.6 to 71.4, while atomic AP50 changes from 71.4 to 72.3. Text path achieves 99.7% CAR50 on three common sources and 99.9%/0.1% on FAIR1M grandparent relations. On held-out VEDAI, text AP50 is 75.9 (6.2 points above OpenRSD).

Conclusion: Explicit hierarchy training effectively repairs the cross-level inconsistency failure mode while retaining atomic detection and prompt transfer capabilities. The approach successfully bridges fine-grained and category-level detection in remote sensing object detection.

Abstract: Hi-OPD addresses a failure mode left uncontrolled by flat open-prompt training: descendant retrieval need not persist under ancestor queries when multi-source remote sensing annotations exhibit inconsistent granularity and missing labels. A detector may localize \textit{car} and \textit{van} under atomic prompts yet miss the same instances under \textit{vehicle}; flat AP does not expose this cross-level inconsistency.
  We propose Hi-OPD, a hierarchy-aware open-prompt detector, and construct RS153-HierOPD from 175,644 retained training image/tile records and 3.48M boxes mapped to 153 atomic categories with sparse hierarchy and alias relations. Hi-OPD learns ancestor retrieval through hierarchy-safe negative sampling, path multi-positive supervision, and one-way upward consistency, while per-source risk exclusion handles potentially missing labels. ConvVPE converts K-shot support boxes into text-compatible embeddings using detector-native features and the shared contrastive head.
  On Track A, Hi-OPD obtains 79.7/72.3 AP50 on DIOR/DOTA-v2.0, above the literature-reported OpenRSD results of 76.7/71.8. Under controlled training on the original converted annotations, the full hierarchy recipe raises DOTA-v2.0 parent AP50 from 7.2 to 71.5 and FAIR1M grandparent AP50 from 31.6 to 71.4, while DOTA-v2.0 atomic AP50 changes from 71.4 to 72.3. The text path reaches 99.7% CAR50 (0.3% violation) across the three common sources and 99.9%/0.1% on FAIR1M grandparent relations. On held-out VEDAI, text AP50 is 75.9, 6.2 points above OpenRSD. Joint AP and CAR show that explicit hierarchy training repairs this failure mode while retaining atomic detection and prompt transfer.

</details>


### [24] [Observer Choice and Threshold Selection in Retinal Vessel Segmentation: A Subject-Separated Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25597)
*Wenhao Xu,Yixian Kong,Ting Pan,Changwei Wang,Feilong Wang,Rongtao Xu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper investigates how the choice of annotation (observer) used for threshold selection affects the evaluation of retinal vessel segmentation models. Using CHASE DB1 dataset with 28 images from 14 subjects, the authors test Random Forests and Extra Trees with 5 threshold policies across 42 model fits.
