Results show maximin tuning provides no significant accuracy benefit (-0.073 percentage points, 95% CI [-0.384, 0.238]), demonstrating that threshold selection method has minimal practical impact on segmentation performance metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The annotation used to select a segmentation threshold is often treated as part of the evaluation protocol, but its effect can be conflated with model quality. The authors aim to quantify whether different threshold selection policies (fixed, observer-specific, mean-observer, or maximin) significantly impact segmentation performance metrics, particularly in retinal vessel segmentation.

Method: The study uses all 28 images from CHASE DB1 (both eyes of 14 subjects) with a fixed 7-fold cross-validation protocol keeping both eyes of each subject together. Random Forests and Extra Trees were trained against observer 1 with three random seeds, yielding 42 fits per model. Five threshold policies were applied to identical score maps: fixed 0.50, observer-1 tuning, observer-2 tuning, mean-observer tuning, and maximin tuning of per-image lower observer Dice. A conditional subject-bootstrap method was used for statistical inference.

Result: For Random Forests, maximin tuning changed the threshold in 19 of 21 fits, but worst-observer Dice decreased minimally from 70.53% to 70.45%. The paired difference was -0.073 percentage points with a 95% bootstrap CI of [-0.384, 0.238], including zero. Extra Trees showed the same directional effect. Identical observer-1-tuned masks scored 73.66% against observer 1 but only 71.06% against observer 2, showing the evaluation reference significantly affects reported performance.

Conclusion: The results demonstrate that maximin tuning of thresholds provides no statistically significant accuracy benefit in this cohort. The study strongly supports explicit reporting of both the threshold-selection reference and evaluation reference in segmentation papers, as different observers can yield substantially different performance scores (2.6 percentage points difference in this study). All data, code, and raw predictions are shared openly.

Abstract: The annotation used to select a segmentation threshold is part of the evaluation protocol, yet its effect is easily conflated with model quality. We examine this choice for retinal vessel segmentation using all 28 CHASE DB1 images and both human annotations. A fixed seven-fold protocol keeps both eyes of each of the 14 subjects together. Random forests and Extra Trees are fitted against observer 1 with three random seeds, yielding 42 fits. Five threshold policies share identical score maps: fixed 0.50, observer-1 tuning, observer-2 tuning, mean-observer tuning, and maximin tuning of the per-image lower observer Dice. For random forests, maximin changes the threshold in 19 of 21 fits, but worst-observer Dice decreases from 70.53 percent to 70.45 percent. The paired difference is -0.073 percentage points, with a conditional subject-bootstrap 95 percent interval of [-0.384, 0.238]. Extra Trees shows the same direction. Identical observer-1-tuned random-forest masks score 73.66 percent against observer 1 and 71.06 percent against observer 2. The results support explicit reporting of both the threshold-selection reference and evaluation reference; they do not support an accuracy benefit from maximin tuning in this cohort. All splits, raw predictions, metrics and code are supplied. AI assistance is disclosed.

</details>


### [25] [Evidence-gated multimodal parsing and vectorization of architectural floor plans](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25615)
*Hongxuan Chen,Wenda Wang,Jiachen Lu,Qirui Shen,Zilong Huang,Lei He,Xinyue Dong,Weixin Huang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SALI-FP is an evidence-gated multimodal pipeline that converts heterogeneous architectural floor plans into structured semantic outputs (maps, objects, vectors, and relation records) while using image evidence to constrain local revisions. Successfully processed 11,534 plans producing 752,510 valid polygon-bearing objects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Architectural floor plans present a high-friction barrier to archive digitization and early design-model preparation because heterogeneous graphics encode spatial semantics and editable geometry together, making automated extraction and conversion challenging.

Method: Evidence-gated multimodal pipeline that converts floor plans into reviewable semantic maps, objects, vectors, and relation records while constraining local revisions by image evidence.

Result: Produced structured outputs for all 11,534 heterogeneous plans, including 752,510 valid polygon-bearing objects. The same output form has supported initial drawing digitization and design-model preparation in practical design work. Includes a 30-case matched visual evidence set for benchmarking room-scale coverage, openings, oblique boundaries, and circulation continuity.

Conclusion: SALI-FP offers an engineering-oriented interpretation-to-geometry workflow for reviewed CAD/BIM preparation and existing-building information recovery, bridging the gap between legacy floor plans and modern digital design workflows.

Abstract: Architectural floor plans remain a high-friction barrier to archive digitization and early design-model preparation because heterogeneous graphics encode spatial semantics and editable geometry together. We introduce SALI-FP, an evidence-gated multimodal pipeline that converts a plan into reviewable semantic maps, objects, vectors, and relation records while constraining local revisions by image evidence. In a full production audit of 11,534 heterogeneous plans, SALI-FP produced structured outputs for every plan, including 752,510 valid polygon-bearing objects. The same output form has supported initial drawing digitization and design-model preparation in practical design work. Public-benchmark calibration is paired with a 30-case matched visual evidence set in Appendix F, where room-scale coverage, openings, oblique boundaries, and circulation continuity can be inspected directly. SALI-FP offers an engineering-oriented interpretation-to-geometry workflow for reviewed CAD/BIM preparation and existing-building information recovery.

</details>


### [26] [Towards Systematic Qualification of Vision-Language Models for Automotive Perception Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25945)
*Malsha Ashani Mahawatta Dona,Konstantinos Rokanas,Alexander Säfström,Krishna Ronanki,Christian Berger*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: The field of Artificial Intelligence has been adopted for many application domains. Vision Language Models are one of the recently advanced AI techniques that have been explored to support automotive features such as vehicle perception, and safety assurance. However, such language models are prone to hallucinations, posing a potential threat to the safety of automotive systems that may incorporate them. Within the automotive domain, VLMs could not only hallucinate traffic objects, but could also fail to identify traffic objects that are actually present, which may potentially lead to dangerous situations. Though we have observed a growing body of literature that proposes verification and validation techniques for safe and trustworthy AI, these methods are often studied in isolation, focusing either on run-time or design-time phases. Such isolated techniques could be insufficient in safety-critical, realistic contexts such as automotive perception systems. In this paper, we analyze design-time and run-time verification and validation techniques based on a taxonomy presented by Huang et al. We present an automotive study in which a design-time qualification workflow is proposed to complement run-time monitoring. This workflow combines a fixed safety-relevant ontology-based structured annotation system together with a synonym-based evaluation process to statistically evaluate three state-of-the-art VLMs against data from the nuScenes dataset. We observed that the proposed technique enables deterministic and repeatable quantification of the hallucinations VLMs generate in automotive perception-related tasks. The proposed workflow supports model comparison and deployment-oriented engineering decisions within the design-time verification and validation process and will contribute to a holistic verification strategy that strives towards trustworthy automotive perception systems

</details>


### [27] [Shallow to Deep: Aligning Token Pruning with Stage-wise Roles in LVLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25635)
*Shuo Zhang,Jintao Tong,Yixiong Zou,Yuhua Li,Ruixuan Li*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper addresses the problem of high computational costs in Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs) caused by redundant visual tokens. The authors identify that naive attention-based pruning in shallow layers degrades performance and propose STD, a hierarchical token pruning framework that adapts pruning strategies to network depth: High-Frequency Spectral Analysis for shallow layers, Gaussian-Smoothed Attention for intermediate layers, and Stability-Adaptive Trigger for deep layers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LVLMs suffer from high computational costs due to redundant visual tokens. While training-free attention-based multi-layer pruning in the vision encoder has been explored, pruning in shallow layers consistently degrades performance. The authors aim to understand why this happens and propose a solution.

Method: The authors first analyze attention patterns across network depth, discovering that shallow layers act as edge detectors with chaotic attention maps, intermediate layers handle local subject recognition with unstable semantic aggregation, and deep layers perform semantic aggregation. Based on these findings, they propose STD, a hierarchical token pruning framework with three specialized mechanisms: (1) High-Frequency Spectral Analysis for shallow layers to preserve structural edges, (2) Gaussian-Smoothed Attention for intermediate layers to maintain spatial coherence, and (3) Stability-Adaptive Trigger for deep layers to prune only during semantically stable phases.

Result: STD achieves state-of-the-art results: 1.1% improvement on LLaVA-1.5-7B with 88.9% token reduction, and 2.1% improvement on LLaVA-NeXT-7B with 94.4% reduction. It delivers a 3.9x speed-up in the prefilling stage and is plug-and-play, compatible with other methods.

Conclusion: The proposed STD framework successfully addresses the misalignment between pruning strategies and network stages by adapting token selection mechanisms to the functional role of each network layer. This work demonstrates that understanding network depth-specific functions is crucial for effective token pruning in LVLMs.

Abstract: Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs) incur high computational costs from redundant visual tokens. Although training-free attention-based multi-layer pruning in the vision encoder stage has been explored as an effective strategy, we find that pruning in shallow layers consistently degrades performance. In this paper, we aim to understand this problem and seek a solution. By analyzing attention patterns across network depth, we find that shallow layers primarily function as edge detectors with chaotic attention maps, while deeper layers transition through local subject recognition and unstable semantic aggregation. To address the misalignment between pruning strategies and network stages, we propose STD, a hierarchical token pruning framework that adapts token selection mechanisms to the functional role of each network stage. STD employs High-Frequency Spectral Analysis in shallow layers to deterministically preserve structural edges, uses Gaussian-Smoothed Attention in intermediate layers to maintain spatial coherence, and introduces a Stability-Adaptive Trigger in deep layers to execute pruning only during semantically stable phases. Extensive experiments show that STD outperforms state-of-the-art pruning methods by 1.1% on LLaVA-1.5-7B with 88.9% token reduction, while also being plug-and-play and highly effective when combined with other methods, and by 2.1% on LLaVA-NeXT-7B with 94.4% reduction, delivering a 3.9x speed-up in the prefilling stage. Our code will be released at https://github.com/Twilight03/STD.

</details>


### [28] [What Drives Hierarchy-Aware Image Retrieval? Taxonomy Alignment, Objective Choice, and Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25638)
*Ling Shi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether hierarchical retrieval improvements in vision models come from taxonomy-aware supervision or from geometry choice (Euclidean vs hyperbolic). Using a controlled 2×2 factorial design on DINOv2 features, they compare regression vs supervised contrastive losses across both geometries on CUB and NABirds datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Foundation vision models like DINOv2 provide strong generic representations, but high class-level retrieval accuracy doesn't guarantee that embeddings respect a target semantic taxonomy. The authors seek to understand what drives hierarchical retrieval improvements: the supervision type or the embedding geometry.

Method: A compute-matched 2×2 factorial design (Geometry × Loss) using frozen DINOv2 features. They compare Euclidean vs hyperbolic projections trained with either taxonomy-distance regression or taxonomy-aware supervised contrastive (SupCon) loss. All configurations use identical 768-256-32 projector capacity, optimization schedule, batch order, and 100-epoch budget. They evaluate with strict cross-class criteria excluding finer-grained matches on CUB and NABirds datasets.

Result: On CUB, objective-family contrasts (SupCon vs Regression) yield +0.0487 (Euclidean) and +0.0414 (hyperbolic) in hierarchy mAP, while geometry contrasts yield only +0.0102 and +0.0030. On NABirds, objective-family contrasts are +0.0467 and +0.0440, versus geometry contrasts of +0.0017 and -0.0009. A semantic-alignment control shows true taxonomy outperforms shuffled hierarchy, while curvature controls don't support negative curvature as the explanation.

Conclusion: The Taxonomy-SupCon objective family produces larger retrieval improvements than geometry choice across both taxonomies. Semantic alignment matters separately, while geometry effects remain hierarchy-dependent. The choice of supervision objective is more impactful than Euclidean vs hyperbolic geometry for taxonomy-aware image retrieval.

Abstract: Foundation vision models provide strong generic representations, yet high class-level retrieval accuracy does not necessarily imply that an embedding respects a target semantic taxonomy. We study strict explicit-taxonomy image retrieval on frozen DINOv2 features and ask: when hierarchical retrieval improves, how much of the change is associated with the organization of taxonomy-aware supervision, and how much with the Euclidean-hyperbolic geometry choice?
  We evaluate higher levels with strict cross-class criteria that exclude finer-grained matches, and compare Euclidean and hyperbolic projections trained with taxonomy-distance regression or a taxonomy-aware supervised contrastive objective. A compute-matched 2 x 2 Geometry x Loss factorial uses the same 768-256-32 projector capacity, optimization schedule, batch order, and fixed 100-epoch budget; the Loss axis denotes the Regression-to-Taxonomy-SupCon objective-family contrast. On CUB, the objective-family contrasts in mean hierarchy mAP (strict middle/high average, excluding Class/Leaf) are +0.0487 in Euclidean space and +0.0414 in hyperbolic space, compared with geometry contrasts of +0.0102 and +0.0030. On NABirds Parent-disjoint retrieval, the corresponding objective-family contrasts are +0.0467 and +0.0440, whereas geometry contrasts are +0.0017 and -0.0009.
  A semantic-alignment control shows that the true taxonomy substantially outperforms a structure-preserving shuffled hierarchy, while a NABirds curvature/radius control does not support stronger negative curvature as the explanation for the observed hierarchy gains. Across the two taxonomies, the Regression-to-Taxonomy-SupCon contrasts are larger in aggregate than the evaluated geometry contrasts; semantic alignment also matters separately, while geometry remains hierarchy-dependent.

</details>


### [29] [Decoupling Disease, Covariates, and Individual Variability: A Unified Disentanglement Framework for Medical Image Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25650)
*Shengjie Zhang,Jinglin Zhang,Zhuangzhuang Jiang,Ziqi Yu,Yipin Zhang,Qi Zhang,Xiang Chen,Haibo Yang,Fei Gao,Longbiao Cui,Yuan Zhou,Xiao-Yong Zhang,Alzheimer's Disease Neuroimaging Initiative*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MedIDL is a generalized medical imaging disentanglement learning framework that separates disease-related features from confounding covariates (age, gender, site) and individual variations by mapping image features into three mutually orthogonal latent spaces using specialized disentanglement heads.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional regression-based approaches for medical image classification may ignore non-linear relationships between image features and true covariates. Accurately isolating disease-related features from confounding covariates and individual variations remains a fundamental challenge in medical image analysis.

Method: MedIDL uses three specialized disentanglement heads: (1) a disease classification head guided by supervised loss, (2) a covariate-alignment head constrained by cross-subject similarity matching, and (3) a Gaussian head absorbing individual variations. These heads map image features into three mutually orthogonal latent spaces.

Result: MedIDL outperforms state-of-the-art supervised and self-supervised classification methods in accuracy across all 7 datasets encompassing diverse imaging modalities. Association analyses demonstrate successful isolation of target-specific latent representations. Gradient-based interpretability mappings localize pathognomonic patterns aligning with established clinical literature.

Conclusion: The MedIDL framework effectively disentangles disease-related features from covariates and individual variations, achieving superior classification performance while providing interpretable results that correspond to clinically established pathological patterns.

Abstract: Accurately isolating disease-related features from confounding covariates (e.g., age, gender, site) and individual variations remains a fundamental challenge in medical image classification. Traditional regression-based approaches may ignore non-linear relations between image features and true covariates. To overcome this issue, we present a generalized Medical Imaging Disentanglement Learning (MedIDL) framework. MedIDL maps image features into three mutually orthogonal latent spaces through specialized disentanglement heads: a disease classification head guided by a supervised loss, a covariate-alignment head constrained by cross-subject similarity matching, and a Gaussian head absorbing individual variations. We evaluated our framework across 7 datasets encompassing diverse imaging modalities. MedIDL outperforms state-of-the-art supervised and self-supervised classification methods in accuracy across all datasets. Association analyses demonstrate that MedIDL successfully isolates target-specific latent representations. Gradient-based interpretability mappings localize pathognomonic patterns aligning with established clinical literature.

</details>


### [30] [GameDirector: Decoupling Gameplay Logic from Rendering for Player-Configurable Game World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25652)
*Zijun Lin,Zhiyang Deng,Yuzhe Wu,Bihan Wen,Yeying Jin*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: GameDirector is the first agentic framework that decouples rule-based gameplay logic from visual rendering in game world models. It interprets visual observations, updates game states, controls NPCs, enforces rules, and translates decisions into text prompts for video generation, bridging the gap between hard-coded game engines and generative models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current game world models learn environment dynamics from pixel-level supervision but fail to deliver complete gameplay experiences because they cannot enforce explicit game mechanics (health deduction, skills, combat rules, termination conditions). Game engines can guarantee these mechanics but lack flexibility for player-driven creation. The authors seek to bridge these paradigms.

Method: GameDirector acts as an intelligent director that: 1) interprets visual observations, 2) updates game states, 3) tactically controls NPCs, 4) enforces gameplay rules, and 5) translates these decisions into text prompts that guide a video world model to render gameplay. Experiments used three games with data collected by an automated gameplay agent.

Result: GameDirector achieves accurate state tracking, reliable rule following, and improves boss action quality by more than 39.9% over various end-to-end game world model settings.

Conclusion: By externalizing player-controllable game logic, GameDirector establishes a middle ground between hard-coded simulation and generative modeling, enabling more flexible and closed-loop gameplay experiences.

Abstract: Recent game world models support realistic visual simulation and interactive gameplay based on player inputs. However, they typically learn environment dynamics from pixel-level supervision, jointly modeling perception, memory, state transitions, and rendering within a single end-to-end framework. While this design enables open-ended, action-controllable generation, it still falls short of delivering a complete gameplay experience. Games are governed by explicit mechanics, such as health deduction, skill activation, combat rules, and termination conditions. These mechanics depend on precise and consistent state transitions that generative models alone cannot reliably enforce. In contrast, game engines can guarantee such mechanics through hard-coded rules, but provide limited flexibility for player-driven creation. To bridge these paradigms, we introduce GameDirector, the first agentic framework that decouples rule-based gameplay logic from visual rendering. Given player-defined configurations, the framework acts as an intelligent director that interprets visual observations, updates game states, tactically controls NPCs, and enforces gameplay rules. It then translates these decisions into text prompts that guide the video world model to render the resulting gameplay. This separation allows players to configure characters, states, and rules much like a game developer while preserving coherent game mechanics. Experiments on three games, using data collected by our automated gameplay agent, show that GameDirector achieves accurate state tracking, reliable rule following, and improves boss action quality by more than 39.9% over various end-to-end game world model settings. Overall, by externalizing player-controllable game logic, GameDirector establishes a middle ground between hard-coded simulation and generative modeling, enabling more flexible and closed-loop gameplay experiences.

</details>


### [31] [Real-Time Atomic-Resolution Electron Phase Imaging without Probe Calibration via Ptychography-Supervised Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25684)
*H. Yue,C. -C. Chen,C. -N. Hsiao,J. Cheng,Y. Liu,X. Z. Liao,Steve F. Shu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper presents a ptychography-supervised local inference framework that enables real-time atomic-scale phase imaging by converting 4D-STEM data into fast phase predictions using a compact ML model, achieving ~1,000-fold speed-up over traditional iterative ptychography while preserving atomic resolution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional electron ptychography is computationally expensive and sensitive to experimental calibration, preventing live use during data acquisition. The goal is to enable real-time atomic-scale phase imaging for materials microscopy.

Method: The approach uses physics-constrained reference phase maps from a single AuPd dataset as teacher labels to train a compact model that predicts local phase patches directly from diffraction measurements, without explicit probe input or online iterative optimization. Full-field images are assembled by deterministic overlap stitching.

Result: The workflow achieves an online latency of ~0.27 ms per probe position and throughput of ~20,000 positions per second, representing ~1,000-fold speed-up over GPU-accelerated ePIE while preserving atomic-scale lattice contrast and reciprocal-space fidelity. The model transfers across materials (WS2), defocus conditions (high-entropy alloy nanoparticles), and instruments (hBN at 300 kV) without fine-tuning.

Conclusion: The approach amortizes ptychographic redundancy into a fast, generalizable workflow that enables real-time atomic-scale phase imaging for materials microscopy, with potential applications in studying defects, interfaces, and weakly scattering atoms.

Abstract: Atomic-scale phase imaging is central to resolving defects, interfaces, and weakly scattering atoms that govern the behavior of nanoscale materials. Electron ptychography delivers sub-ångström phase sensitivity but remains an offline technique, because its iterative reconstruction is computationally expensive and sensitive to experimental calibration, preventing live use during data acquisition. Here, a ptychography-supervised local inference framework is presented that converts four-dimensional scanning transmission electron microscopy (4D-STEM) into an acquisition-compatible phase-imaging workflow. Physics-constrained reference phase maps reconstructed from a single experimental AuPd dataset serve as teacher labels for a compact model that predicts local phase patches directly from diffraction measurements, without explicit probe input or online iterative optimization. Full-field images are assembled by deterministic overlap stitching. The workflow reaches an online latency of about 0.27 ms per probe position and a throughput of about 20,000 positions per second, an approximately 1,000-fold speed-up over GPU-accelerated ePIE, while preserving atomic-scale lattice contrast and reciprocal-space fidelity. Without fine-tuning, the same model transfers across materials (WS2), defocus conditions (high-entropy alloy nanoparticles), and instruments (hBN at 300 kV). The approach amortizes ptychographic redundancy into a fast, generalizable workflow that enables real-time atomic-scale phase imaging for materials microscopy.

</details>


### [32] [Initialization and Stopping Tolerance in CPU Dermoscopic Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25685)
*Wenhao Xu,Yixian Kong,Ting Pan,Changwei Wang,Feilong Wang,Rongtao Xu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This study examines how contour initialization and numerical stopping criteria interact in active-contour segmentation, using scikit-image Chan-Vese implementation on ISIC 2017 skin lesion images. The authors find that Otsu initialization improves Dice score compared to checkerboard (0.6660 vs 0.6011), but Otsu thresholding alone achieves even higher performance (0.6897). The stopping tolerance significantly affects results, with tighter tolerances reducing the initialization advantage. The authors argue that evaluations should include unrefined masks as comparators and report initialization and stopping parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate how contour initialization methods and numerical stopping criteria interact to affect active-contour segmentation evaluation, addressing the lack of standardized reporting in this area.

Method: Used scikit-image Chan-Vese implementation on ISIC 2017 dataset (600 images). Compared Otsu thresholding with checkerboard-, disk-, and Otsu-initialized contours under default and tighter level-set tolerances. A development set of 100 images selected input channel; all 600 images were evaluated.

Result: At default tolerance, Otsu initialization increased mean Dice from 0.6011 (checkerboard) to 0.6660 (difference: 0.0649). Otsu thresholding alone achieved 0.6897. Disk initializer stopped after one iteration on 471/600 images. Tightening tolerance reduced Otsu-seed advantage to 0.0197, with most runs reaching 500-iteration limit. The advantage reversed between small- and large-lesion strata.

Conclusion: An improvement over a generic initializer can coexist with deterioration relative to the threshold baseline. Evaluations should retain the unrefined mask as a comparator and report initial-field definition, stopping tolerance, and observed iteration counts together for reproducibility and proper benchmarking.

Abstract: Contour initialization and numerical stopping can jointly affect the evaluation of active-contour segmentation. We examine their interaction using the open-source scikit-image Chan-Vese implementation on a resized ISIC 2017 mirror. A fixed development set of 100 images selects a common input channel; all 600 images in the repository's held-out partition are then evaluated. Otsu thresholding is compared with checkerboard-, disk-, and Otsu-initialized contours under default and tighter level-set tolerances. At the default tolerance, Otsu initialization increases mean image Dice from 0.6011 to 0.6660 relative to checkerboard initialization, a paired difference of 0.0649 (95% image-bootstrap interval [0.0452, 0.0860]). Otsu thresholding alone achieves 0.6897. The default disk initializer stops after one iteration on 471 images. Tightening the tolerance reduces the Otsu-seed advantage over checkerboard initialization to 0.0197, with most runs reaching the 500-iteration limit. The default-tolerance advantage also reverses between small- and large-lesion strata. These findings show that an improvement over a generic initializer can coexist with deterioration relative to the threshold baseline. Evaluations should retain the unrefined mask as a comparator and report the initial-field definition, stopping tolerance, and observed iteration counts together.

</details>


### [33] [C2FXNet: Coarse-to-Fine Scene Expert for Unified Object Detection across Adverse Weather](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25693)
*Tianle Fang,Zhenbing Liu,Chong Yin,Bolun Li,Haoxiang Lu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: C2FXNet is a unified object detector for adverse weather conditions that achieves robust detection across foggy, dark, and clear conditions without scene-specific training. It combines coarse scene reasoning via GRU-based Multi-step Reasoning Router with fine-grained semantic refinement through Fine Scene Refinement module, and dynamically fuses scene-specific experts using Scene-aware Mixture-of-Experts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Object detection in adverse weather is challenging because severe degradations weaken visual quality and disrupt semantic feature representations. Existing methods rely on condition-specific designs, limiting their generalization ability within a unified detector.

Method: C2FXNet introduces a dual-level guidance mechanism: (1) Multi-step Reasoning Router (MRR) performs GRU-based recurrent scene reasoning over compressed multi-scale visual cues and frozen coarse scene prototypes; (2) Fine Scene Refinement (FSR) module uses image-specific semantic cues to modulate high-level features for local variation handling; (3) Scene-aware Mixture-of-Experts (SMoE) dynamically combines scene-specific experts under joint guidance of MRR and FSR.

Result: C2FXNet achieves 63.70% mAP on RTTS, 71.14% mAP on ExDark, and 54.19% mAP on the newly constructed Adverse Weather Dataset (AWD), consistently outperforming state-of-the-art methods across foggy, dark, and clear conditions.

Conclusion: By coupling coarse scene reasoning with fine-grained semantic refinement, C2FXNet enables robust multi-scene detection without scene-specific training, demonstrating strong generalization across diverse adverse weather conditions.

Abstract: Object detection in adverse weather remains challenging because severe degradations weaken visual quality and disrupt semantic feature representations across diverse scenes. Existing methods usually rely on condition-specific designs, which limits their ability to generalize within a unified detector. In this paper, we propose a Coarse-to-Fine Scene Expert Network (C2FXNet) that achieves unified detection through hierarchical scene guidance. Specifically, C2FXNet introduces a dual-level guidance mechanism consisting of a Multi-step Reasoning Router (MRR), which performs GRU-based recurrent scene reasoning over compressed multi-scale visual cues and frozen coarse scene prototypes, and a Fine Scene Refinement (FSR) module, which uses image-specific semantic cues to modulate high-level features for local variation handling. Furthermore, a Scene-aware Mixture-of-Experts (SMoE) dynamically combines scene-specific experts under the joint guidance of MRR and FSR. By coupling coarse scene reasoning with fine-grained semantic refinement, C2FXNet enables robust multi-scene detection without scene-specific training. Extensive experiments on RTTS, ExDark, and our newly constructed Adverse Weather Dataset (AWD) demonstrate that C2FXNet consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods across foggy, dark, and clear conditions, reaching 63.70%, 71.14%, and 54.19% mAP on RTTS, ExDark, and AWD, respectively. The source code will be released at https://github.com/PolarisFTL/C2FXNet.

</details>


### [34] [Interpretable AI plus Handheld, Portable Retinal Photographs: A Low-Cost Glaucoma Screening Solution for West Africa](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25697)
*Charis Y. N. Chiang,Tarela Sarimiye,Adeyinka Ashaye,Martin Buist,Michael A. Hauser,Olusola Olawoye,Michaël J. A. Girard*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This study developed an interpretable AI framework for glaucoma screening from low-cost portable handheld retinal fundus photographs in a Nigerian population (681 participants, 1,362 eyes). The AI system achieved comparable performance between handheld (Volk Viva) and tabletop (Canon CR-2-AF) cameras, with classification AUCs of 0.85 and 0.93 respectively, demonstrating potential for community-level glaucoma screening in resource-limited settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Glaucoma is a leading cause of blindness worldwide, with high prevalence in West Africa. However, screening is limited in resource-poor settings due to lack of specialist access and expensive clinical equipment. This study aimed to address these barriers by developing an AI system that works with low-cost, portable handheld retinal cameras for community-based glaucoma screening.

Method: The study used data from a community-based study in Nigeria with 681 participants (1,362 eyes): 414 glaucoma, 478 glaucoma suspect, and 470 non-glaucoma eyes. Fundus photographs were acquired using two devices: the low-cost handheld Volk Viva camera and the Canon CR-2-AF tabletop camera. The researchers fine-tuned component models separately for each device to perform: (1) vessel segmentation, (2) cup and disc boundary segmentation, and (3) optic nerve head feature extraction. A final classification model combined these components to classify images as glaucoma, glaucoma suspect, or non-glaucoma. Interpretation was provided through feature-weight analysis and Gradient-weighted Class Activation Mapping (Grad-CAM).

Result: The models performed well on both devices: Vessel segmentation achieved 0.98 Dice Coefficient (DC) for Volk and 0.94 DC for Canon; Cup and disc segmentation achieved 0.95 DC for Volk and 0.96 DC for Canon; Optic nerve head feature detection showed AUCs of 0.83±0.03 (Volk) and 0.87±0.04 (Canon); Final classification achieved AUCs of 0.85±0.01 (Volk) and 0.93±0.01 (Canon). The system provided decision confidence scores and decision-rationale visualizations for each image.

Conclusion: The Volk Viva handheld device showed reasonably comparable performance to the Canon CR-2-AF tabletop camera in component models, with only modest reduction in classification performance. This demonstrates that interpretable AI combined with low-cost, portable imaging has significant potential for enhancing community-level glaucoma screening, particularly in settings with limited specialist access and resources.

Abstract: Purpose: To develop and evaluate an interpretable artificial intelligence (AI) framework for glaucoma screening from low-cost portable, handheld retinal fundus photographs in a West African population and to compare its performance with clinical tabletop fundus imaging. Methods: We used data from a community-based study of 681 participants (1,362 eyes) in Nigeria, comprising 414 glaucoma, 478 glaucoma suspect, and 470 non-glaucoma eyes. Fundus photographs were acquired using the low-cost handheld, portable Volk Viva retinal camera and the Canon CR-2-AF tabletop camera. We fine-tuned component models separately to each device to perform vessel segmentation, cup and disc boundary segmentation, and feature extraction to detect optic nerve head features. A final classification model combined these components to classify scans as glaucoma, glaucoma suspect or non-glaucoma. Feature-weight analysis and Gradient-weighted Class Activation Mapping were used for interpretation. Results: The models performed well on both Volk Viva and Canon CR-2-AF images: Vessel segmentation: 0.98 Dice Coefficient (DC) (Volk) and 0.94 DC (Canon); Cup and disc segmentation: 0.95 DC (Volk) and 0.96 DC (Canon); Optic nerve head feature detection: area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUCs) of 0.83$\pm$0.03 (Volk) and 0.87$\pm$0.04 (Canon); Classification model: AUCs of 0.85$\pm$0.01 (Volk) and 0.93$\pm$0.01 (Canon). Reports for each image, present model decision confidence scores and decision-rationale visualizations to support clinical interpretation. Conclusions: Volk Viva results were reasonably comparable to Canon CR-2-AF in the component models and not far behind in classification. This shows that interpretable AI combined with low-cost, portable imaging may enhance community-level glaucoma screening, especially in settings with limited specialist access and resources.

</details>


### [35] [FoMo: Forking Moment in Generative Trajectory as a Perceptual Distance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25716)
*Jaihyun Lew,Mingi Jung,Minjun Park,Wooseok Song,Sungroh Yoon*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes a fully automated data generation pipeline for reference-based image quality assessment (IQA) that uses diffusion model trajectories as perceptual distance labels. The key insight is that images diverging early in the diffusion process share only coarse structure (perceptually far apart), while those diverging late differ only in fine details (perceptually close). This "Forking Moment" (FoMo) serves as a reference-grounded distance label to supervise IQA metric training, eliminating the need for expensive human annotations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional reference-based IQA metrics rely on human-annotated data, which has two key limitations: (1) Mean Opinion Score (MOS) labels are expensive to collect at scale and suffer from noise due to inconsistent human judgments; (2) Two-alternative forced choice (2AFC) pairwise labels, while more reliable, capture only relative comparisons rather than pointwise distance. The authors seek a fully automated approach that generates pointwise perceptual distance labels without any human annotation.

Method: The proposed method exploits the generative dynamics of diffusion models as a perceptual distance proxy. The approach leverages the observation that diffusion models generate coarse structure in early timesteps and fine details in later timesteps. By identifying when two images "fork" in the generation trajectory (FoMo), the method derives a reference-grounded distance label: early forking indicates large perceptual distance, late forking indicates small perceptual distance. This FoMo label is used to supervise the training of a reference-based IQA metric. The method supports universal comparison between arbitrary image pairs.

Result: Extensive experiments across diverse backbone architectures confirm the effectiveness of the proposed generation pipeline. The method outperforms human-annotated datasets in multiple benchmarks for reference-based IQA.

Conclusion: The diffusion trajectory aligns well with the human visual system (HVS), and the FoMo (Forking Moment) can serve as an effective perceptual distance proxy. This fully automated pipeline eliminates the need for expensive human annotation while achieving superior performance on benchmark datasets, demonstrating the potential of leveraging generative model dynamics for perceptual distance measurement.

Abstract: Reference-based image quality assessment (IQA) metrics aim to reflect how humans perceive the perceptual distance between a pair of images. To learn how the human visual system (HVS) operates, recent reference-based IQA metrics heavily rely on human-annotated data. Mean opinion score (MOS)-based pointwise scoring, which assigns a scalar quality value per image, is preferable for annotation but is prohibitively expensive to collect at scale and is known to be noisy due to inconsistent human judgments. As an alternative, two-alternative forced choice (2AFC) pairwise labels have gained popularity due to their reliability and efficiency, but they capture only relative comparisons between pairs. In this paper, we propose a fully automated data generation pipeline that generates pointwise perceptual distance labels between image pairs without any human annotation. Our approach exploits the generative dynamics of diffusion models as a perceptual distance proxy, where the coarse structure of an image is generated in the early timesteps and the fine details are generated in the later timesteps. Images that fork early in the generation process share only coarse structure and are perceptually far apart; images that fork late differ only in fine detail. We demonstrate that the diffusion trajectory aligns well with the human visual system, and use this forking moment, FoMo, as a reference-grounded distance label to supervise the training of a reference-based IQA metric. The pointwise labels, which support universal comparison between arbitrary image pairs, enable an information-rich training objective. Extensive experiments across diverse backbone architectures confirm the effectiveness of our generation pipeline, outperforming human-annotated datasets in multiple benchmarks.

</details>


### [36] [Annual Earth-observation embeddings encode wildfire disturbance and support simplified burned area mapping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25731)
*Jovana Knezevic,Clement Atzberger,Zhengpeng Feng,Adam F. A. Pellegrini,Srinivasan Keshav,David Coomes*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates that annual Earth-observation embeddings (Tessera and AlphaEarth) can effectively map burned areas without requiring fire-specific imagery or dense time-series analysis. Tessera embeddings strongly encode wildfire disturbance signals, enabling linear models to achieve high burn-scar delineation accuracy (F1 = 0.90) and cross-continental transfer (F1 = 0.88), while also recovering fire ignition timing with 13 days mean absolute error.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Medium-resolution burned area mapping is vital for wildfire monitoring but remains difficult to scale due to requirements for curated fire-specific imagery or dense time-series analysis. This study investigates whether annual Earth-observation embeddings can retain sufficient wildfire disturbance signals to map burned areas without these requirements.

Method: The study tested Tessera and AlphaEarth embeddings across five scenarios: (1) individual burn-scar delineation, (2) mapping all same-year fires within an area, (3) regional wall-to-wall mapping across California, (4) cross-continental transfer from US to European fires, and (5) intra-annual fire timing detection. Models were trained on single embeddings and compared against pre/post-fire HLS imagery combinations.

Result: Tessera strongly encoded wildfire disturbance signals, enabling linear models to separate burned from unburned pixels. Models trained on single Tessera embeddings matched or exceeded equivalent models using paired pre/post-fire imagery, and outperformed post-fire imagery alone. For benchmark scenes mapping all same-year fires, F1 = 0.90 was achieved. California wall-to-wall mapping recovered 97% of reference burned area with better detection of small/medium fires than GABAM or MCD64A1. Cross-continental transfer (2018-2021 US fires to 2024-2025 European fires) achieved F1 = 0.88. Fire ignition timing was recovered with 13 days mean absolute error for well-detected fires.

Conclusion: Annual embeddings achieve high burned area segmentation accuracy while moving the burden of dense time-series processing upstream, providing a promising path towards simpler regional burned area mapping. Limitations include performance decline for fires ignited near year-end and systematic false positives in some unseen landscapes.

Abstract: Medium-resolution (10-30 m) burned area mapping is vital for monitoring wildfires and their impacts, but remains difficult to scale. Existing methods require either curated fire-specific imagery or dense time-series analysis. Here, we tested whether annual Earth-observation embeddings retain wildfire disturbance signals sufficiently to map burned areas without either requirement. Using Tessera and AlphaEarth embeddings, we tested individual burn-scar delineation, mapping of all same-year fires within an area, regional wall-to-wall mapping, cross-continental transfer, and intra-annual fire timing. Tessera strongly encoded wildfire disturbance, allowing even linear models to separate burned from unburned pixels; the signal was weaker in AlphaEarth. Models trained on a single Tessera embedding matched or exceeded equivalent models using paired pre- and post-fire HLS imagery, and outperformed post-fire imagery alone. The same approach mapped all same-year fires within benchmark scenes (F1 = 0.90). Applied across California, with no California fire data used for downstream training, it recovered 97% of reference burned area and detected substantially more small and medium-sized fires than GABAM or MCD64A1. Separately, a model trained on 2018-2021 US fires transferred without retraining to 88 European fires from 2024-2025 (F1 = 0.88). For well-detected fires, ignition timing was recovered with a mean absolute error of 13 days. Performance declined for fires ignited near the end of the calendar year, and wall-to-wall deployment produced systematic false positives in some unseen landscapes. Annual embeddings nevertheless achieve high segmentation accuracy while moving the burden of dense time series processing upstream, providing a promising path towards simpler regional burned area mapping.

</details>


### [37] [Fysiverse-3D-Vision Technical Report: Generating Executable 3D Worlds from Images through Unified Spatial Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25741)
*Dingkang Yang,Yizhou Liu,Wendong Cheng,Zizhi Chen,Shunli Wang,Yang Liu,Hongsheng Li,Lihua Zhang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Fysiverse-3D-Vision is a unified vision-language-geometry framework that reconstructs 3D scenes and generates executable assets from a single image, decoupling spatial layout reasoning from asset synthesis to enable interactive editing and physical simulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing 3D generative approaches couple spatial layout estimation with specific asset generators, struggling to jointly model object semantics, metric geometry, and scene-level spatial relationships needed for interactive editing, physical simulation, and embodied applications.

Method: The framework integrates textual supervision, semantic visual cues, and geometric representations within a unified Transformer. It uses an object-conditioned layout module with cross-attention between target object representations and global geometric features to predict object translation, rotation, and scale. Training progresses through geometry-language alignment, layout reasoning with preservation of reconstruction capability, and physical consistency refinement via collision-aware optimization.

Result: Superior geometric consistency, layout estimation, rendering quality, and physical property understanding compared with existing approaches.

Conclusion: Fysiverse-3D-Vision successfully decouples spatial layout reasoning from asset synthesis, providing an adaptable interface for interactive scene editing, object-level manipulations, and executable 3D content generation.

Abstract: Generative models have advanced image-conditioned 3D content creation, yet generating controllable and executable 3D scenes from a single image remains challenging. Existing 3D generative approaches can synthesize visually plausible objects and scenes, but their spatial layout estimation is coupled with specific asset generators. They struggle to jointly model object semantics, metric geometry, and scene-level spatial relationships, which are essential for interactive editing, physical simulation, and embodied applications. We propose Fysiverse-3D-Vision, a unified vision-language-geometry framework for generative 3D scene reconstruction and executable asset construction from a single image. We establish a shared representation where spatial reasoning and geometric reconstruction mutually enhance each other, allowing object layouts to be inferred beyond the constraints of individual asset generators. Our model integrates textual supervision, semantic visual cues, and geometric representations within a unified Transformer to capture scene context, metric geometry, and object-level interactions. An object-conditioned layout module performs cross-attention between target object representations and global geometric features to predict object translation, rotation, and scale. Training progressively learns geometry-language alignment, introduces layout reasoning while preserving reconstruction capability, and refines physical consistency through collision-aware optimization. By separating spatial layout reasoning from asset synthesis, Fysiverse-3D-Vision provides an adaptable interface for interactive scene editing, object-level manipulations, and executable 3D content generation. Experiments demonstrate that our framework achieves superior geometric consistency, layout estimation, rendering quality, and physical property understanding compared with existing approaches.

</details>


### [38] [SAMI3D-DW: Interactive Segmentation of Any 3D Medical Images](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25743)
*Ping Gong,Shiyuan Su,Fandong Zhang,Xinchen Han,Haowei Sun,Yiming Li,Yizhou Yu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SAMI3D-DW is an interactive 3D medical image segmentation model trained on large-scale proprietary datasets, achieving state-of-the-art performance on a CT/MR benchmark with 4,326 cases across 107 anatomical and pathological categories. It outperforms nnInteractive and VISTA3D, reaching 0.8002 category-macro Dice after five corrective clicks and enabling efficient annotation of complex structures like intracranial vessels and NF1 tumors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite progress in interactive segmentation (nnInteractive, VISTA3D), reliable segmentation across diverse clinical targets remains challenging, especially for complex anatomical structures and heterogeneous pathologies in the long-tailed spectrum.

Method: SAMI3D-DW is trained on Deepwise's large-scale proprietary medical image datasets. Evaluation was performed on a CT/MR benchmark with 4,326 cases from 219 source datasets, spanning 107 anatomical and pathological categories, using a medical taxonomy and category-balanced DSC score.

Result: SAMI3D-DW achieves the highest category-macro Dice in both interaction modes. With one point: 0.5764 vs 0.5315 (nnInteractive); with five points: 0.7771 vs 0.7494; with bounding-box: 0.7130 vs 0.6530; after five corrective clicks: 0.8002 vs 0.7868. It is the only box-compatible model to exceed 0.80. For NF1 tumor annotation, it reduces annotation time to approximately one-fifteenth of manual annotation.

Conclusion: SAMI3D-DW enables efficient segmentation of complex anatomical structures (e.g., intracranial vessel trees) with minimal user interaction, significantly reducing annotation time and supporting volumetric treatment-response assessment for conditions like NF1.

Abstract: Interactive segmentation of 3D medical images supports quantitative analysis of anatomical structures and disease while allowing users to specify and refine their targets.
  Despite substantial progress by nnInteractive and VISTA3D, reliable segmentation across diverse clinical targets remains challenging, particularly for complex anatomical
  structures and the heterogeneous, long-tailed spectrum of pathology. We present SAMI3D-DW V1 (hereafter SAMI3D-DW), an interactive 3D segmentation model trained on
  Deepwise's large-scale proprietary medical image datasets. We evaluate the model under simulated user interactions on a CT/MR benchmark comprising 4,326 cases from 219
  source datasets, spanning 107 anatomical and pathological categories, organized by a medical taxonomy and evaluated with a category-balanced DSC score. SAMI3D-DW achieves
  the highest category-macro Dice among evaluated methods in both interaction modes. With one point, it scores 0.5764 versus 0.5315 for nnInteractive, the strongest
  baseline, rising to 0.7771 versus 0.7494 with five points. With bounding-box initialization, the scores are 0.7130 versus 0.6530. After five corrective clicks, SAMI3D-DW
  reaches 0.8002 versus 0.7868, making it the only evaluated box-compatible model to exceed 0.80. For radiologists and clinicians, SAMI3D-DW enables segmentation of complex
  anatomical structures, including intracranial vessel trees on CT and MR angiography, with a few clicks. In a preliminary in-house comparison involving neurofibromatosis
  type 1 (NF1), SAMI3D-DW-assisted tumor annotation took minutes per case and approximately one-fifteenth of the time required for manual annotation, highlighting its
  potential to support volumetric treatment-response assessment.

</details>


### [39] [Reading Right, Answering Wrong: How Visual Configuration Changes Affect Evidence Use in VLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25770)
*Dingyang Lin,Yingfeng Luo,Chenglong Wang,Chenwei Zhu,Anxiang Ma,Jingbo Zhu,Tong Xiao*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Vision-language models (VLMs) exhibit unstable behavior when images are resized - small resizes can flip correct answers to errors. The study investigates whether visual configuration changes (tiling, token arrangement) contribute to this instability, finding that configuration switches cause more errors. Over half the time, models can still read correct answers when explicitly told what to read, suggesting configuration changes impair information utilization. Field cues and model transcriptions as guidance correct 97.2% of such errors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLMs achieve strong performance on VQA tasks but show surprising instability - small image resizes can transform correct answers into errors. This vulnerability raises concerns about VLM reliability and deployment safety. The research seeks to understand whether visual configuration changes (image tiling, token arrangement) contribute to this instability.

Method: Empirical study across seven checkpoints and four benchmarks, examining how configuration changes (image tiling, token arrangement) affect model correctness. Attention intervention analysis on LLaVA-NeXT to understand mechanisms. Guided answering using field cues and model transcriptions as corrective interventions.

Result: Configuration changes during resizing cause more correctness flips than resizing alone. In over 50% of cases, models answer incorrectly but can still read the correct answer when explicitly told what to read. Attention analysis shows configuration changes weaken utilization of readable information. Field cue guidance and model transcriptions together correct 97.2% of errors with readable information.

Conclusion: Visual configuration changes can significantly impact how VLMs use information they can still read. This reveals a previously overlooked failure mode where models possess readable information but fail to utilize it due to configuration shifts. The proposed guidance methods (field cues and transcriptions) effectively correct most such errors, suggesting practical remediation strategies for VLM instability.

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) have achieved strong performance on tasks such as visual question answering, yet small image resizes can turn correct answers into errors. We investigate whether changes in visual configuration, such as image tiling and token arrangement, contribute to this instability. Across seven checkpoints and four benchmarks, equally small resizes cause more correctness flips when they switch configurations. Surprisingly, in over half of these cases, models answer the question incorrectly but can still read the correct answer when told what to read. Furthermore, attention interventions in LLaVA-NeXT suggest that configuration changes can weaken the use of readable information during answering. We therefore guide models using field cues and their own transcriptions. With annotation assistance, these forms of guidance together correct 97.2% of errors with readable information. These findings show that configuration changes can affect how models use information they can still read.

</details>


### [40] [Video-HopChain: Multi-Hop Questions and Confidence-Gated Exploration for Video Reasoning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25773)
*Trung Nguyen Quang,Yuhao Dong,Shuo Sun,Shuai Liu,Shulin Tian,Kim-Hui Yap,Ziwei Liu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper introduces Video-HopChain, a dataset of 22,550 multi-hop video questions over 13,378 videos, and Confidence-Gated Exploration (CGE) to address GRPO's limitations in reinforcement learning. The approach improves video understanding benchmarks from 55.4 to 59.3 mean score.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-hop data synthesis improves vision-language reasoning on still images, but this approach remains unexplored in video. Existing RLVR datasets rarely require chain-of-thought reasoning, leaving weaknesses unexposed. Additionally, GRPO's learning signal depends on reward variance within groups, causing hard questions (all incorrect rollouts) and easy questions (all correct rollouts) to provide no gradient signal.

Method: 1) Build Video-HopChain dataset: 22,550 multi-hop video questions (3-6 chained yes/no questions per video), each yielding one of two integers depending on answer; final answer is the sum of integers for exact match reward. 2) Two-stage training: first train Qwen3-VL-8B with GRPO on standard video dataset, then on Video-HopChain. 3) Introduce Confidence-Gated Exploration (CGE): with 8 rollouts per question, sample first 4 normally; if all correct or all incorrect, sample last 4 with policy's most confident token masked inside reasoning span; remove masked positions from loss while all 8 rollouts enter the advantage.

Result: Two-stage GRPO training raises mean performance from 55.4 to 57.9 across eight video understanding and reasoning benchmarks, improving every benchmark. With CGE added, the mean further rises to 59.3.

Conclusion: Multi-hop video reasoning training effectively improves video understanding and reasoning capabilities. CGE successfully addresses GRPO's limitation in handling hard and easy questions by recovering gradient signals through confident token masking, enabling further improvements within the same compute budget.

Abstract: HopChain has shown on still images that multi-hop data synthesis improves vision-language reasoning, because long chain-of-thought reasoning exposes errors that compound across steps, while most data used for reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) rarely demands a chain of visual evidence, so these weaknesses are likely to stay unexposed. We observe the same problem in video, where this framework has not yet been explored. We therefore build Video-HopChain, a dataset of 22,550 multi-hop video questions over 13,378 videos, together with a held-out benchmark of 1,000 questions. Each question chains three to six yes/no questions about moments in one video, and each yields one of two integers depending on its answer. The final answer is the sum of these integers, so an exact match on that sum gives the verifiable reward that RLVR needs. We first train Qwen3-VL-8B with GRPO on a standard video dataset, and a second stage on Video-HopChain then raises the mean over eight video understanding and reasoning benchmarks from 55.4 to 57.9 and improves every one of them. Training on such a dataset, however, exposes a known limitation of GRPO: its learning signal comes from the reward variance within a group, so hard questions whose rollouts are all incorrect and easy questions whose rollouts are all correct both leave the group with no gradient. To recover these groups at the same compute budget, we introduce Confidence-Gated Exploration (CGE). With 8 rollouts per question, CGE samples the first 4 as usual. If these 4 are either all correct or all incorrect, it samples the last 4 with the policy's most confident token masked inside the reasoning span, and removes the masked positions from the loss while all 8 rollouts enter the advantage. With CGE, the mean rises further to 59.3. We release the dataset, the checkpoint, and the data generation and training code.

</details>


### [41] [TRACE: Trajectory Representation and Consistency Estimation for AI-Generated Video Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25775)
*Huangsen Cao,Hongkang chu,Siyao Yu,Xin Ding,Jianfeng Dong,Yongwei Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: TRACE uses a pretrained video DiT as a velocity-field probe to extract transferable forensic signals for AI-generated video detection, achieving superior generalization across diverse generators.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AI-generated video detectors rely on generator-specific artifacts (appearance, semantic inconsistencies, temporal patterns), limiting their ability to generalize to unseen synthesis models. There is a need for transferable forensic cues that work across different generators.

Method: TRACE leverages a pretrained video DiT as a velocity-field probe to extract representations at multiple flow time points. It models cross-frame consistency through velocity differences between adjacent frames and introduces a Real-Centered Trajectory Optimization objective for generator-invariant representation learning.

Result: TRACE substantially outperforms prior state-of-the-art methods on both open- and closed-source video generation models, demonstrating effective generalization across diverse generators on AIGVDBench.

Conclusion: Velocity responses under a pretrained Flow Matching model provide transferable forensic signals beyond visual artifacts, enabling robust detection of AI-generated videos across unseen generators.

Abstract: Recent advances in generative video models have enabled the synthesis of visually realistic content, posing significant challenges to synthetic video detection. Existing detectors often rely on appearance artifacts, semantic inconsistencies, and temporal patterns that may be generator-specific, limitating generalization to unseen synthesis models. We investigate whether responses to a pretrained generative model provide more transferable forensic cues. Our key observation is that real and AI-generated videos exhibit distinct \emph{velocity responses} under a pretrained Flow Matching video model. This distinction persists when different pretrained video-generation backbones are used as probes, suggesting that velocity responses offer transferable forensic signals beyond visual artificts. Motivated by this observation, we propose \textbf{TRACE} (\emph{\underline{T}rajectory \underline{R}epresentation \underline{a}nd \underline{C}onsistency \underline{E}stimation}), a generation-process-aware framework for AI-generated video detection. TRACE leverages a pretrained video DiT as a velocity-field probe to extract representations at multiple flow time points, and models cross-frame consistency through velocity differences between adjacent frames. We further introduce a \emph{Real-Centered Trajectory Optimization} objective that encourages generator-invariant representation learning. Extensive experiments on AIGVDBench demonstrate that TRACE generalizes effectively across diverse generators, substantially outperforming prior state-of-the-art methods on unseen open- and closed-source video generation models.

</details>


### [42] [When Point Clouds Outperform Pixels: Rethinking Zero-Shot Multimodal Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25793)
*Chenglin Ye,Lupeng Liu,Dongbo Yu,Jun Xiao,Yunbiao Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Zero-shot multimodal anomaly detection commonly assumes that RGB and point cloud modalities are equally reliable and can contribute uniformly to anomaly localization. We challenge this assumption. Using a set of recently proposed stringent metrics that penalize false anomaly responses in normal regions, we find that point clouds are substantially more reliable than RGB under zero-shot category shift. Motivated by this observation, we propose WOOPS (\textbf{W}hen P\textbf{o}int Cl\textbf{o}uds Out\textbf{p}erform Pixel\textbf{s}), a reliability-aware zero-shot multimodal anomaly detection framework. To strengthen the more reliable geometric modality, we design a Multi-view Information Decoupling module to suppress heterogeneous information from multi-view point cloud projections and enhance point cloud feature quality. To avoid unconditional fusion, we further introduce a Modality Reliability Calibration module to adaptively calibrate modality contributions according to their reliability. Extensive experiments show that our method achieves the best or competitive performance under the new metrics in both unimodal and multimodal settings. Further analysis demonstrates that point cloud information also improves RGB-only inference, while ablations verify the effectiveness of both modules. Code will be released upon acceptance.

</details>


### [43] [LiFR v2: Completion-Augmented Event Propagation for High-Rate Dense Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25803)
*Tao Wan,Xiaoshan Wu,Yifei Yu,Bo Wang,Xiaoyang Lyu,Muxin Liu,Aoxuan Pan,Zhongrui Wang,Xiaojuan Qi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: LiFR v2 is a unified framework for high-rate dense prediction combining RGB keyframes and event camera data. It addresses the limitations of low RGB frame rates in dynamic environments by introducing an Event-Guided Completion Module (EGCM) to recover representations in unsupported regions and a History Retrieval Module (HRM) to reuse completed representations across queries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-rate dense perception in dynamic environments is limited by the low update rate of RGB cameras. Event cameras provide temporally dense but spatially sparse measurements, but direct fusion cannot fully exploit this complementarity. Event-guided propagation fails on newly appearing or disoccluded regions without valid RGB support.

Method: LiFR v2 introduces two key modules: (1) Event-Guided Completion Module (EGCM) to recover task-relevant representations where propagation is unsupported, and (2) History Retrieval Module (HRM) to reuse completed representations across successive queries. The framework supports semantic segmentation, monocular depth estimation, and multi-task dense prediction.

Result: LiFR v2 achieves 74.37% mIoU on DSEC and 56.13% on SHF-Emerge, improving LiFR-Seg by 1.85 percentage points on SHF-Emerge, while reducing SHF-Emerge depth RMSE from 1.564 m to 1.118 m over the propagation baseline. It also exceeds 100 FPS for both segmentation and depth.

Conclusion: LiFR v2 demonstrates accurate and efficient high-rate perception beyond RGB frame rates, successfully handling rapid object emergence and disocclusion through the proposed EGCM and HRM modules.

Abstract: High-rate dense perception in dynamic environments is limited by the low update rate of RGB cameras, as rapid scene changes can occur between frames. Event cameras offer temporally dense but spatially sparse measurements, complementary to spatially dense RGB observations. Direct fusion cannot fully exploit this complementarity, while event-guided propagation fails on newly appearing or disoccluded regions without valid RGB support. We present LiFR v2, a unified propagation-completion-memory framework for causal anytime and streaming dense prediction from an RGB keyframe and events. LiFR v2 introduces an Event-Guided Completion Module (EGCM) to recover task-relevant representations where propagation is unsupported, and a History Retrieval Module (HRM) to reuse completed representations across successive queries. The framework supports semantic segmentation, monocular depth estimation, and multi-task dense prediction, and we further introduce SHF-Emerge to evaluate rapid object emergence and disocclusion. LiFR v2 achieves 74.37% mIoU on DSEC and 56.13% on SHF-Emerge, improving LiFR-Seg by 1.85 percentage points on the latter, while reducing SHF-Emerge depth RMSE from 1.564 m to 1.118 m over the propagation baseline. It also exceeds 100 FPS for both segmentation and depth, demonstrating accurate and efficient high-rate perception beyond RGB frame rates.

</details>


### [44] [MorphoSHAP: Rethinking the Unit of Attribution in Explanation for Deep Visual Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25815)
*Anirudh Prabhakaran,Alexandre Rocchi,Gianni Franchi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MorphoSHAP is a model-agnostic post-hoc explanation method for image classifiers that uses morphological shapes as the fundamental units in a Shapley attribution game, replacing traditional pixel/patch-based approaches. It provides multi-level explanations (spatial, textual, and class-level) by leveraging the Tree of Shapes representation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current visual attribution methods use pixels, superpixels, or regular patches, which can localize important regions but provide limited information about their structure. There is a need for explanations that go beyond localization to describe what type of structure carries the evidence and how strongly it affects predictions.

Method: MorphoSHAP uses morphological shapes as players in a Shapley attribution game. It leverages the Tree of Shapes to describe each shape by its scale, geometry, and signed contribution. This approach enables spatial explanations (where evidence lies), textual explanations (what structure carries it), and global class-level explanations.

Result: MorphoSHAP achieves strong insertion/deletion performance across five diverse datasets and three architectures. It outperforms competing attribution methods on several benchmarks. User studies demonstrate that MorphoSHAP provides explanations that are easy to use and preferred over standard attribution baselines.

Conclusion: MorphoSHAP is the first SHAP-based image attribution framework to combine spatial, textual, and global class-level explanations. It provides more interpretable and useful explanations for image classification models while maintaining strong performance metrics.

Abstract: Visual attribution methods typically explain predictions using pixels, superpixels, or regular patches. These representations can localize important regions, but provide limited information about their structure. We introduce MorphoSHAP, a model-agnostic post-hoc method that instead uses morphological shapes as the players of a Shapley attribution game. Using the Tree of Shapes, each shape is described by its scale, geometry, and signed contribution, providing explanations of where the evidence lies, what type of structure carries it, and how strongly it affects the prediction. This shared morphological vocabulary enables spatial, textual, and global class-level explanations beyond image-specific heatmaps. To the best of our knowledge, MorphoSHAP is the first SHAP-based image attribution framework to combine these different forms of explanation. Across five diverse datasets and three architectures, MorphoSHAP achieves strong insertion/deletion performance and outperforms competing attribution methods on several benchmarks. Finally, a user study shows that MorphoSHAP provides explanations that are easy to use and are preferred over standard attribution baselines.

</details>


### [45] [Identity-Centric Video Summarization via Hierarchical Fusion of Biometric, Appearance, and 3D Body Features](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25837)
*Milad Mirjalili,Enrique Alegre Gutiérrez,Eduardo Fidalgo Fernández,Víctor González Castro,Rocío Alaiz Rodríguez,Manuel Castejón Limas*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A video summarization algorithm that combines multi-object tracking with person re-identification using facial embeddings, 3D body-shape features, and visual appearance. It uses hierarchical identity assignment and bidirectional anchoring to maintain stable trajectories under challenging conditions, then generates identity-specific summaries using multi-factor weighting and Adaptive Non-Maximum Suppression.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address challenges in video summarization when dealing with severe occlusion or low visual quality, where traditional tracking methods fail to maintain stable identity trajectories. The goal is to generate compact, diverse, and informative video summaries that preserve key biometric, social, and motion information.

Method: 1) Multi-modal feature integration: facial embeddings + 3D body-shape features + visual appearance
2) Hierarchical identity assignment through bidirectional anchoring
3) Trajectory recovery under severe occlusion/low quality
4) Multi-factor weighting scheme for keyframe selection (biometric clarity, social interaction, motion dynamics)
5) Adaptive Non-Maximum Suppression for temporal diversity

Result: Tracking stability: IDF1 = 97.89%, MOTA = 95.79%. Compared to Top-K selection: visual diversity +146%, temporal coverage +89%, information retrievability +3.5%

Conclusion: The proposed algorithm achieves excellent tracking performance and significantly outperforms baseline methods in visual diversity, temporal coverage, and information retrievability. The combination of multi-modal features and bidirectional anchoring effectively handles challenging video conditions.

Abstract: This work presents a video summarization algorithm based on multi-object tracking and person reidentification. We integrate facial embeddings, 3D body-shape features, and visual appearance into a unified tracking framework. These representations enable hierarchical identity assignment and tracking through bidirectional anchoring, which robustly recovers trajectories under severe occlusion or low visual quality. From these stable trajectories, we generate a compact set of summaries for each identity. We select keyframes using a multi-factor weighting scheme that optimizes biometric clarity, social interaction, and motion dynamics, while Adaptive Non-Maximum Suppression ensures temporal diversity. Evaluation on a custom dataset demonstrates tracking stability, achieving an IDF1 of 97.89% and a MOTA of 95.79%. Compared to Top-K selection, our algorithm also increases visual diversity by 146%, temporal coverage by 89%, and information retrievability by 3.5%.

</details>


### [46] [Metric-Bench: Exploring In-context Spatial Metric Reasoning in VLMs for Indoor Scenes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25841)
*Yuling Xi,Haokai Zhang,Muzhi Zhu,Hao Zhong,Zongze Du,Hengyu Zhao,Chenchen Jing,Yufei Yin,Bin Qin,Yongjie Yang,Zhenbo Luo,Hao Chen,Chunhua Shen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Metric-Bench introduces a benchmark for metric-spatial reasoning in Vision Language Models using in-image reference objects to enable 2D-to-3D mapping without camera intrinsics. Combined with MetricReasoner (reinforcement fine-tuning with structured prompts and numerical rewards), the approach significantly improves metric reasoning while preserving general VLM capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current spatial reasoning in VLMs is bottlenecked by rigid pixel-level supervision, which compromises general multimodal intelligence and causes performance degradation or catastrophic forgetting. There is a need for metric-spatial reasoning that can leverage contextual information without relying on camera intrinsics.

Method: 1) Metric-Bench: A benchmark incorporating in-image reference objects with known physical dimensions to guide models in learning 2D-to-3D mapping. 2) MetricReasoner: A task-adapted reinforcement fine-tuning recipe using structured prompts and verifiable numerical rewards for reference-grounded metric reasoning.

Result: Outperforms existing and larger proprietary models by 43.1%. Improves downstream embodied performance by 30.4% on RoboSpatial and 9.3% on ERQA. Delivers gains on general benchmarks (15.9% on V★Bench, 88.9% on BLINK) without compromising general VLM capabilities.

Conclusion: The proposed approach successfully enhances spatial metric understanding while maintaining or improving general multimodal reasoning capabilities, addressing the trade-off between specialized task performance and broad VLM capabilities.

Abstract: Metric reasoning is a critical and challenging task for Vision Language Models (VLMs), playing a pivotal role in embodied AI tasks such as robotic manipulation and autonomous navigation. However, current spatial reasoning remains bottlenecked by rigid pixel-level supervision; such localized optimization often compromises general multimodal intelligence, triggering performance degradation or catastrophic forgetting of broad reasoning capabilities. To address these limitations, we introduce Metric-Bench, a focused benchmark designed to guide metric-spatial reasoning using contextual information. By incorporating in-image reference objects with known physical dimensions, Metric-Bench guides models to implicitly learn the 2D-to-3D mapping without camera intrinsics. We further present MetricReasoner, a task-adapted reinforcement fine-tuning recipe for reference-grounded metric reasoning, using structured prompts and verifiable numerical rewards.
