Motivation: Flaky tests produce non-deterministic outcomes without code changes, eroding CI confidence and delaying deliveries. While vocabulary-based ML prediction has proven effective for Java and JavaScript, no study has evaluated it for Swift—a language dominated by UI and asynchronous testing. This research aims to extend vocabulary-based flakiness detection to the Swift ecosystem.

Method: The study collected 91 flaky and 22,349 stable tests from 15 open-source Swift projects through re-execution and commit-history mining. Five classifiers (Random Forest, Decision Tree, Naive Bayes, SVM, KNN) were trained on TF-IDF unigram+bigram features under stratified 5-fold cross-validation. Information-gain analysis was performed to identify discriminative tokens.

Result: Random Forest achieved the best performance (Precision=0.92, F1=0.86, AUC=0.95), substantially outperforming trivial baselines (MCC=0.75 vs. 0.08). Flakiness markers include concurrency primitives (async, await), expectation-based synchronization (expectation, fulfill), error propagation (throws), and timing dependence (timeout, wait, now). Stability markers include synchronous assertion vocabulary (xctassertequal). The model fails when flakiness is hidden in shared infrastructure or when async constructs are used deterministically.

Conclusion: Vocabulary-based flakiness detection is effective for Swift, with Random Forest achieving high precision and AUC. The approach identifies complementary signal types: flakiness markers (async/timing constructs) and stability markers (synchronous assertions). However, lexical prediction has intrinsic limits when flakiness originates outside test bodies or when async code is deterministic. This extends vocabulary-based flakiness detection to the Swift ecosystem while characterizing both its effectiveness and boundaries.

Abstract: Flaky tests produce non-deterministic outcomes without code change, eroding CI confidence and delaying deliveries. While vocabulary-based machine learning prediction has proven effective for Java and JavaScript, no study has evaluated it for Swift, a language whose testing style is dominated by UI and asynchronous code. We collect 91 flaky and 22,349 stable tests from 15 open-source Swift projects via re-execution and commit-history mining, then train five classifiers (Random Forest, Decision Tree, Naive Bayes, SVM, KNN) on TF-IDF unigram+bigram features under stratified 5-fold cross-validation. Random Forest achieves the best performance (Precision = 0.92, F1 = 0.86, AUC = 0.95) and substantially outperforms trivial baselines, among them a vocabulary-threshold rule applied to the most informative tokens, confirming a genuine discriminative signal (MCC = 0.75 vs. 0.08 for the best baseline). Information-gain analysis reveals two complementary signal types. Flakiness markers appear predominantly in unstable tests and comprise concurrency primitives (async, await), expectation-based synchronisation (expectation, fulfill), error propagation (throws), and explicit timing dependence (timeout, wait, now). Stability markers, chiefly the assertion vocabulary of plainly synchronous tests (xctassertequal), count as evidence against flakiness. Error analysis shows that the model fails when flakiness is hidden in shared infrastructure outside the test body or when async constructs are used in a deterministic context, exposing the intrinsic limit of lexical prediction. These results extend vocabulary-based flakiness detection to the Swift ecosystem and characterise both its effectiveness and its boundaries.

</details>


### [72] [Dynamic Conformance Testing of WebGPU Through Specification-Driven Mutation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25520)
*Mahya Samdaliri,Zhihao Yao,Kasthuri Jayarajah*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: LANTERN is a specification-guided dynamic conformance testing framework for WebGPU that mutates CTS tests using constraints extracted from the WebIDL definitions and natural-language specification text. It generates valid and invalid test variants to discover bugs in Chromium implementations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The official WebGPU Conformance Test Suite (CTS) only tests well-formed usage and doesn't stress implementations with semantic edge cases or adversarial inputs. General-purpose fuzzers fail with WebGPU due to its complex graphics stack and multi-process architecture. There was a need for a testing approach that could uncover browser bugs during WebGPU testing.

Method: LANTERN extracts explicit syntactic API rules from WebIDL definitions and recovers semantic constraints (command ordering, object lifetimes) from natural-language specification text. These rules guide AST-located textual transformations that generate both valid and intentionally invalid CTS variants. The tests are executed at scale on AddressSanitizer-instrumented Chromium to discover bugs.

Result: Discovered three reproducible bugs in Chromium versions current at the time of study, including a heap corruption vulnerability.

Conclusion: Syntactically seeded, semantics-aware mutation of conformance tests provides an effective way to uncover browser bugs during WebGPU testing. This approach successfully found critical bugs that the standard CTS missed.

Abstract: WebGPU is a low-level graphics and compute API that exposes modern GPU functionality to web applications. While the official WebGPU Conformance Test Suite (CTS) focuses on well-formed usage under the WebGPU specification, it is not designed to stress implementations with semantic edge cases or adversarial inputs. General-purpose fuzzers, in contrast, struggle with WebGPU because of its complex graphics stack and multi-process architecture. We introduce LANTERN, a specification-guided dynamic conformance testing framework that mutates CTS tests using constraints extracted from the WebGPU specification. LANTERN extracts explicit syntactic API rules from WebIDL definitions and recovers semantic constraints, such as command ordering and object lifetimes, from natural-language specification text. Selected rules guide AST-located textual transformations that generate both valid and intentionally invalid CTS variants. We execute the resulting tests at scale on AddressSanitizer-instrumented Chromium to discover bugs. Our evaluation discovers three reproducible bugs, including a heap corruption, in Chromium versions current at the time of study. These results demonstrate that syntactically seeded, semantics-aware mutation of conformance tests provides a way to uncover browser bugs during WebGPU testing.

</details>


### [73] [Evaluating Shaker for Flaky Test Detection in Python Projects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25528)
*Gabriela Leal,Denini Silva,Leopoldo Teixeira*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents the first empirical evaluation of Shaker (a flaky test detection tool originally designed for Java/Android) applied to Python. Contrary to the original results showing 95% detection rate, the study found Shaker provides no statistically significant advantage over plain re-execution (37.2% vs 35.8%). Key findings reveal that less than half of known flaky tests reproduce as flaky, and those that do are primarily caused by network interactions and randomness rather than the concurrency issues Shaker targets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation was to validate whether Shaker, which was effective for Java and Android (detecting 95% of flaky tests), would work equally well for Python. The authors sought to empirically test this tool in a different programming environment.

Method: The study used a paired design comparing Shaker against budget-matched ReRun baseline. They used 137 non-order-dependent flaky tests from Gruber et al.'s ground-truth dataset. Each test was run 100 times under each technique, giving both methods the same number of executions (13,700 total runs per technique).

Result: Shaker detected 37.2% of flaky tests while ReRun detected 35.8%—a difference that is not statistically significant (McNemar exact p = 0.84). Fewer than 50% of the ground-truth flaky tests reproduced as flaky at all. The tests that did reproduce were dominated by network interactions and randomness rather than concurrency issues (which Shaker is designed to detect).

Conclusion: The study concludes that Shaker provides no detection advantage for Python due to: (1) poor reproducibility of flaky tests across environments, and (2) the flakiness being caused by network/random factors rather than concurrency. The authors also highlight a broader issue: reusing flaky-test ground truths across different execution environments can silently convert genuine flaky tests into apparent false negatives, making any tool's measured recall artificially low.

Abstract: Flaky tests pass or fail non-deterministically on unchanged code, eroding trust in test suites and inflating the cost of every failure. Shaker detects them by injecting resource contention (CPU, memory, and I/O stress) to amplify non-determinism caused by concurrent execution, and was reported to detect 95% of the flaky tests in a Java and Android benchmark against 37.5% for plain re-execution (ReRun). We present the first empirical evaluation of Shaker for Python. Drawing non-order-dependent flaky tests from the ground-truth dataset of Gruber et al., we compare Shaker against a budget-matched ReRun baseline in a paired design, giving both techniques the same number of test executions: Each of 137 tests is run 100 times under each. As configured for Java and Android, Shaker provides no statistically significant detection advantage over plain re-execution (37.2% vs. 35.8%; McNemar exact p = 0.84). Two findings explain why. First, fewer than half of the ground-truth flaky tests reproduce as flaky at all on independent hardware under either technique, and most of the tests that fail to reproduce never diverge once across 100 runs. Second, the tests that do reproduce are dominated by flakiness from network interactions and randomness rather than the concurrency Shaker targets. Beyond the tool, this exposes a broader hazard for the field: reusing a flaky-test ground truth across execution environments silently converts genuine flaky tests into apparent true negatives, deflating any tool's measured recall.

</details>


### [74] [An Empirical Analysis of Cross-OS Portability Issues in Python Projects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25531)
*Denini Silva,MohamadAli Farahat,Marcelo d'Amorim*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: First large-scale empirical study of cross-OS portability issues in Python, analyzing 2,042 repositories. Found 11.2% of 500 tested projects exhibit OS-dependent test failures, and 102 genuine portability problems from 240 GitHub issues. Developed a taxonomy with 7 categories, 24 sub-categories, and repair patterns. Static analysis tools provide minimal detection support, while LLMs achieve 40-79% accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While Python is designed as cross-platform, real-world applications encounter portability failures across different operating systems. No comprehensive baseline existed for understanding and addressing these issues.

Method: Two complementary approaches: (1) systematic cross-OS test reexecution on 500 projects, (2) manual analysis of 240 GitHub issues. Evaluated existing static analysis tools and large language models for portability detection and fix generation.

Result: 11.2% of projects exhibit OS-dependent test failures. 102 genuine portability problems identified across 95 projects. Taxonomy developed with 7 primary failure categories (file/directory operations, process management, library dependencies most prevalent), 24 sub-categories, 15 diagnostic signatures, and 4 repair patterns. Static analysis tools provide minimal support. LLMs achieve 40-79% accuracy in identifying issues and 50-77% success in generating fixes. 33 contributed pull requests with 17 merged, zero rejected.

Conclusion: This work establishes the first comprehensive baseline for understanding and addressing cross-OS portability issues in Python, providing actionable insights for developers, tool designers, and researchers.

Abstract: While Python is designed as a cross-platform language, real-world applications encounter portability failures when deployed across different operating systems. We present the first large-scale empirical study of cross-OS portability issues in Python, analyzing 2,042 open-source repositories using two complementary approaches: systematic cross-OS test reexecution and manual analysis of GitHub issues. Our cross-platform testing of 500 projects reveals that 11.2% exhibit OS-dependent test failures. Through systematic analysis of 240 GitHub issues, we confirm 102 genuine portability problems spanning 95 additional projects. We develop a comprehensive taxonomy identifying 7 primary failure categories - with file/directory operations, process management, and library dependencies being most prevalent - along with 24 distinct sub-categories, 15 diagnostic signatures, and 4 systematic repair patterns. Our evaluation reveals that existing static analysis tools provide minimal support for portability detection, while large language models achieve 40-79% accuracy in identifying issues and 50-77% success in generating fixes when provided with structured guidance. Through 33 contributed pull requests, we demonstrate practical applicability and developer acceptance (17 merged, zero rejected) of our findings. This work establishes the first comprehensive baseline for understanding and addressing cross-OS portability issues in Python, providing actionable insights for developers, tool designers, and the broader research community.

</details>


### [75] [Understanding Maintenance and Support in a Community-Driven Scientific Workflow Ecosystem: A Cross-Space Study of Galaxy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25587)
*Khairul Alam,Kowsik Roy,Md Shamimur Rahman,Banani Roy*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper conducts a large-scale empirical study of Galaxy, a scientific workflow system, analyzing 11,762 GitHub issues, 52,203 pull requests, and 6,235 Community Forum discussions to characterize maintenance concerns, resolution factors, and cross-space traceability. The study identifies 9 issue topics, 14 PR topics, and 14 forum topics using BERTopic modeling, revealing limited explicit traceability (97.77% within GitHub, only 294 connecting GitHub with Forum).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Galaxy's sustainability depends on continuous maintenance across software, tools, workflows, infrastructure, documentation, and user-support. However, maintenance knowledge is distributed across development and community-support spaces, making it difficult to understand what is maintained, how maintenance artifacts are resolved, and how user-facing concerns connect to repository-level development.

Method: Large-scale empirical study using 11,762 GitHub issues, 52,203 pull requests, and 6,235 Community Forum discussions. Used BERTopic modeling for topic identification, analyzed resolution outcomes and resolution time, and investigated explicit and candidate connections among maintenance artifacts across spaces.

Result: Identified nine issue topics, 14 pull-request topics, and 14 forum topics, revealing maintenance landscape spanning workflow execution, data management, tools and dependencies, infrastructure, testing, scientific resources, documentation, and user support. Resolution analyses show coordination, diagnostic, contributor, automation, and engagement characteristics exhibit different associations with resolution outcomes and time. Limited explicit traceability: 97.77% of 16,426 resolved explicit relationships occur within GitHub, while only 294 connect GitHub artifacts with Community Forum discussions.

Conclusion: Galaxy maintenance is characterized as a distributed ecosystem-level process. Opportunities exist to improve diagnostic reporting, lifecycle-aware triage, cross-space traceability, and reuse of community-support knowledge.

Abstract: Galaxy is a widely used, community-driven scientific workflow system whose sustainability depends on continuous maintenance across its software, tools, workflows, infrastructure, documentation, and user-support ecosystem. However, maintenance knowledge in Galaxy is distributed across development and community-support spaces, making it difficult to understand what is maintained, how maintenance artifacts are resolved, and how user-facing concerns connect to repository-level development. We conduct a large-scale empirical study of Galaxy using 11,762 GitHub issues, 52,203 pull requests, and 6,235 Community Forum discussions. We characterize maintenance and support concerns, examine factors associated with resolution outcomes and resolution time, and investigate explicit and candidate connections among maintenance artifacts across these spaces.
  Using BERTopic modeling, we identify nine issue topics, 14 pull-request topics, and 14 forum topics, revealing a maintenance landscape spanning workflow execution, data management, tools and dependencies, infrastructure, testing, scientific resources, documentation, and user support. Resolution analyses show that coordination, diagnostic, contributor, automation, and engagement characteristics exhibit different associations with whether artifacts are resolved and how quickly resolution occurs. We further find limited explicit traceability between development and support spaces: 97.77\% of 16,426 resolved explicit relationships occur within GitHub, while only 294 connect GitHub artifacts with Community Forum discussions, despite additional semantic and technical relatedness across these spaces. Together, these findings characterize Galaxy maintenance as a distributed ecosystem-level process and identify opportunities to improve diagnostic reporting, lifecycle-aware triage, cross-space traceability, and the reuse of community-support knowledge.

</details>


### [76] [What Was Once Learned May Need to Be Unlearned: Machine Unlearning for Deprecated API Knowledge in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25786)
*Jin Liu,Yanzhong He,Guancheng Lin,Xiao Liu,Jacky Wai Keung,Xiao Yu,Xiaoxue Ma*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents MUDAPIBench, a benchmark for evaluating machine unlearning methods that remove deprecated API knowledge from code language models. The study evaluates 8 unlearning methods across 3 LLMs using 7,000+ instances derived from 145 deprecated-to-up-to-date API mappings in Python libraries, finding Gradient Difference (GD) offers the best trade-off between forgetting deprecated APIs and preserving other capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models for code completion often generate deprecated APIs because their pre-training data contains code from historical library versions. Existing solutions (inference-time intervention, model editing, machine unlearning) have limitations: predefined replacements are restrictive when multiple plausible completions exist, and prior studies rarely verify whether models actually exhibit the targeted deprecated behavior or assess unintended side effects on other APIs.

Method: The authors constructed MUDAPIBench, a behavior-grounded benchmark with over 7,000 model-specific instances from 145 deprecated-to-up-to-date API mappings across 8 Python libraries. Instances were retained only when the original model generated the target deprecated API. They evaluated 8 representative unlearning methods across 3 code LLMs, assessing deprecated API forgetting, up-to-date API generation, preservation of other/unrelated API behaviors, general code-generation capability, and efficiency.

Result: Gradient Difference (GD) provides the best overall trade-off, effectively suppressing deprecated APIs while preserving other capabilities with moderate computational costs. Further findings reveal substantial variation across libraries and that APIs deprecated after the model's training-data cutoff are harder to forget. Layer-wise analyses show GD achieves effective forgetting with comparatively controlled internal changes.

Conclusion: Machine unlearning can successfully remove deprecated API knowledge from code LLMs, with GD offering the best balance between effectiveness and preservation. The MUDAPIBench benchmark provides a rigorous evaluation framework for future research. Key challenges remain for APIs deprecated after the model's training cutoff, and careful layer-wise analysis is needed to understand the mechanisms of successful unlearning.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) for code completion may generate deprecated APIs because their pre-training corpora contain code from historical library versions. Existing approaches use inference-time intervention, model editing, or machine unlearning, but multiple plausible completions make predefined replacements restrictive. Moreover, existing studies rarely verify whether models exhibit the targeted deprecated behavior or evaluate unintended changes to other APIs.
  We conduct a systematic empirical study of machine unlearning for deprecated API knowledge and construct MUDAPIBench, a behavior-grounded benchmark with over 7,000 model-specific instances derived from 145 deprecated-to-up-to-date API mappings across eight Python libraries. Instances are retained only when the original model generates the target deprecated API. We evaluate eight representative unlearning methods across three code LLMs on deprecated API forgetting, up-to-date API generation, preservation of other and unrelated API behaviors, general code-generation capability, and efficiency.
  Results show that Gradient Difference (GD) provides the best overall trade-off, suppressing deprecated APIs while preserving other capabilities with moderate computational costs. Further analyses reveal substantial variation across libraries and show that APIs deprecated after the model's training-data cutoff are harder to forget. Layer-wise analyses indicate that GD achieves effective forgetting with comparatively controlled internal changes.

</details>


### [77] [Confidence-Guided Cross-Modal Knowledge Transfer for Multimodal Anomaly Detection in Microservice Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25856)
*Peipeng Wang,Xiuguo Zhang,Lihua Zhang,Zhiying Cao,Jingchun Zhou,Zheng Li*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: CMT-AD is a confidence-guided cross-modal knowledge transfer method for anomaly detection in microservice systems. It addresses challenges of dynamically changing modality reliability and cross-modal heterogeneity by using soft clustering-based confidence scores to guide knowledge transfer from high-confidence to low-confidence modalities, along with a gated intermediate modality and consistency constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate anomaly detection is essential for reliable microservice systems. While multimodal approaches have gained attention, effectively leveraging cross-modal information remains challenging due to: (1) dynamically changing modality reliability caused by factors like load fluctuations, and (2) heterogeneity in structure and semantics across modalities.

Method: CMT-AD proposes: (1) Joint modeling of metrics and logs in a unified deep clustering framework; (2) Estimating modality reliability through soft clustering distributions to generate confidence scores; (3) Using confidence scores to guide cross-modal interactions and supplement low-confidence modalities with knowledge from high-confidence ones; (4) Introducing a gated intermediate modality with structural and semantic consistency constraints to align original modalities and preserve similarity structures and semantic distributions; (5) Incorporating intra-modal and cross-modal regularization terms to enhance cluster compactness and mitigate negative transfer.

Result: Evaluated on three large-scale datasets, CMT-AD outperforms state-of-the-art approaches and achieves an F1-score higher than 0.9.

Conclusion: The confidence-guided cross-modal knowledge transfer approach effectively addresses the challenges of modality reliability variation and cross-modal heterogeneity in microservice anomaly detection, achieving superior detection performance with F1 > 0.9.

Abstract: Accurate anomaly detection is essential for reliable and secure operations of microservice systems. While an increasing number of studies have shifted from unimodal modeling to multimodal interaction and fusion, effectively leveraging reliable cross-modal information remains challenging. The challenge primarily stems from two aspects. Firstly, different modalities are influenced by factors like load fluctuations, leading to dynamically changing reliability. Secondly, multimodal data exhibit heterogeneity in both structure and semantics. Therefore, we propose a confidence-guided Cross-Modal knowledge Transfer method for multimodal Anomaly Detection (CMT-AD). It jointly models metrics and logs within a unified deep clustering framework and estimates modality reliability through the soft clustering distributions, where clustering uncertainty is quantified into confidence scores. Guided by these confidence scores, the model actively analyzes the contributions of each modality in cross-modal interactions and supplements low-confidence modalities with knowledge from high-confidence ones. To further mitigate cross-modal heterogeneity, we introduce a gated intermediate modality and design structural and semantic consistency constraints that align the original modalities with the intermediate modality to preserve similarity structures and semantic distributions across modalities. Furthermore, intra-modal and cross-modal regularization terms are incorporated to enhance cluster compactness and mitigate negative transfer. We evaluated CMT-AD on three large-scale datasets, and the results demonstrate that CMT-AD outperforms state-of-the-art approaches and achieves an F1-score higher than 0.9.

</details>


### [78] [When Should Dependency Updates Invoke Repair Agents? A Lightweight Routing Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25911)
*Liheng Fan,Jialun Yin,Yuzhi Chen*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: DepFixRouter is a lightweight routing system that predicts which dependency-update pull requests require substantive compatibility repair, enabling selective escalation to expensive repository-level coding agents. Using creation-time signals (PR titles, bot/dependency flags) with a LinearSVC classifier, it achieves 0.488 repair F1 and captures 51.4% of repairs in the top 20% of routed PRs, reducing LLM calls by 66.7% in a diagnosis-agent pilot.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dependency-update PRs are frequent but mostly routine. Invoking coding agents on every update wastes model calls, CI time, and review attention. The goal is to develop a pre-agent routing mechanism that identifies which updates likely need substantive repair, avoiding unnecessary agent invocations.

Method: The study collected 497 labeled GitHub dependency-update PRs (72 requiring substantive repair). A LinearSVC classifier was trained using creation-time signals: PR titles and bot/dependency flags. The system was evaluated using repair F1 score and recall within top-k% routed PRs. A 60-case diagnosis-agent pilot tested the router's effectiveness in reducing LLM calls and tokens.

Result: A creation-time-safe LinearSVC achieved 0.488 repair F1, capturing 51.4% of repairs within the top 20% routed PRs. This improved calls per captured repair from 6.90 (route-all/random) to 2.68. Retrospective full-history signals improved top-20% recall to 65.3%, revealing significant hindsight leakage. In the 60-case pilot, router-gated diagnosis reduced actual LLM calls by 66.7% and tokens by 66.1%.

Conclusion: DepFixRouter effectively serves as a lightweight escalation layer between routine dependency-update automation and expensive repository-level agents, enabling budget-aware maintenance without relying on retrospective repair evidence for deployment-time routing.

Abstract: Dependency-update pull requests are frequent and mostly routine, but a small subset requires non-trivial compatibility repair. Recent repository-level coding agents make such repair increasingly plausible, yet invoking them on every dependency update wastes model calls, CI time, repository context, and review attention. We frame this as a pre-agent routing problem: deciding which dependency-update pull requests should be escalated before downstream diagnosis or repair attempts. We introduce DepFixRouter, a lightweight router that ranks dependency updates by historical compatibility-repair likelihood using creation-time textual and metadata signals. On 497 labeled GitHub dependency-update candidates, only 72 require substantive repair. A creation-time-safe LinearSVC using only PR titles and bot/dependency flags reaches 0.488 repair F1 and captures 51.4% of repairs within the top 20% routed pull requests, improving calls per captured repair from 6.90 under route-all or random policies to 2.68. Retrospective full-history signals improve top-20% recall to 65.3%, revealing substantial hindsight leakage in pull-request histories rather than deployment-time routing utility. In a 60-case diagnosis-agent pilot, router-gated diagnosis reduces actual LLM calls by 66.7% and tokens by 66.1%, suggesting budgetaware escalation while measuring diagnosis rather than patch generation. DepFixRouter can serve as a lightweight escalation layer between routine dependency-update automation and expensive repository-level agents, enabling budget-aware maintenance without relying on retrospective repair evidence for deployment-time routing.

</details>


### [79] [Compiling Sufficient Governance Context from Declared Losses and Reachable States: Exact Observation-Contract Synthesis with Cardinality and Cost Objectives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26016)
*Gaston Besanson*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper develops a framework for computing minimal sufficient governance contexts - selecting minimal sets of observable attributes that determine a declared verdict in finite state models. The approach uses SAT/MaxSAT encoding to find minimal observation contracts, with applications in code/cloud governance domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to formally determine which observable attributes are sufficient to determine a declared verdict (e.g., governance decisions) in a system, distinguishing indispensable attributes from jointly sufficient contracts, and selecting optimal contracts under cardinality or cost objectives.

Method: The method involves: (1) computing sufficient observation sets from finite reachable-state models; (2) distinguishing individually indispensable attributes from jointly sufficient contracts; (3) synthesizing inclusion-minimal sufficient contracts via exhaustive enumeration when affordable; (4) using SAT/MaxSAT encoding for minimum-cardinality or minimum-cost contracts; (5) evaluating on constructed code/cloud domains and comparing four baselines in AuthorityBench.

Result: On the code/cloud domain: the indispensable core is not sufficient as an observation contract, two distinct reducts exist, and a cost model separates them. On the larger domain: the same pattern recurs but cost model doesn't separate alternatives (cost tie). SAT/MaxSAT solves scaling problems where exhaustive enumeration fails; MaxSAT showed no cardinality advantage over plain SAT. The declared-only baseline is not exactly sufficient on any domain.

Conclusion: The framework successfully identifies minimal sufficient governance contexts. SAT/MaxSAT approaches are more scalable than exhaustive enumeration. Cost models may or may not separate alternatives. The work does not provide portable certificates but rather check summaries; future work includes an end-to-end case study.

Abstract: We call the object this paper derives and certifies a minimal sufficient governance context: given a finite reachable-state model, a deterministic declared verdict, and candidate observable attributes, we compute sufficient observation sets, distinguish attributes that are individually indispensable from contracts that are jointly sufficient, and select among sufficient contracts under a cardinality or declared-cost objective. An observation contract is a set of candidate attributes whose values determine the declared verdict on every reachable state; an authority contract is one selected under an objective and bound to a gate schema. We synthesize every inclusion-minimal sufficient contract where exhaustive enumeration is affordable, and a minimum-cardinality or minimum-cost contract by SAT/MaxSAT encoding otherwise, checking sufficiency directly. On a constructed code/cloud domain, the individually-indispensable core is not sufficient as an observation contract and two distinct reducts exist; a preregistered cost model separates them exactly. On a second, larger, constructed domain, the same pattern recurs, but that domain's cost model does not separate the alternatives: a fully explained cost tie, reported as found. We measure discernibility-family scaling where exhaustive enumeration is confirmed infeasible within a registered timeout, while SAT/MaxSAT synthesis solves in well under a second; MaxSAT showed no measured cardinality advantage over plain SAT. AuthorityBench compares four baselines across three domains; the declared-only baseline is not exactly sufficient on any. Every selected contract is checked for sufficiency, with a counterexample on failure and a check summary, not a portable certificate, on success -- the compiler-focused scope of a two-scope table; an independently specified end-to-end case study is registered follow-up work, not claimed here.

</details>


### [80] [On Behavioral Alignment of Model-Code and Human-Code Understandability via Behavioral Proxies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26101)
*Xiaokai Rong,Aashish Yadavally,Anh H. N. Nguyen,Hridya Dhulipala,Tien N. Nguyen*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper proposes a relational view of code understandability that encompasses both human and AI readers (LLMs). It introduces behavioral proxies (BPMU) to measure model-code understandability and investigates alignment between LLM and human perspectives across different developer groups.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional code understandability research treats it as a human-centric or code-centric property, but the authors argue it should be viewed as a relational property between reader and code. With LLMs increasingly used in software engineering, the notion of "reader" needs to be generalized to include both humans and AI models.

Method: The authors used a dataset from a prior study containing human-rated understandability judgments. They evaluated multiple open and closed-source LLMs using four behavioral proxies: P0 based on program comprehension question answering, and P1-P3 based on program intent summarization (semantic self-consistency). They compared LLM alignment with human understandability against shallow ML baselines and performed stratified analysis across different participant groups (professional developers vs students).

Result: LLMs demonstrate stronger behavioral alignment with general human code understandability compared to shallow ML baselines. The alignment is strongest with professional developers but significantly weaker with student groups. Role-conditioned prompting fails to improve alignment for students. Semantic self-consistency proves to be a reliable and extensible measure for quantifying model code understandability.

Conclusion: Current LLMs cannot accurately mimic the perspectives of human readers with varying expertise levels. The proposed semantic self-consistency method serves as a reliable behavioral proxy for measuring model code understandability, with broad implications for software engineering research and practice.

Abstract: Code understandability is a critical aspect of software quality. Prior research has largely focused on this attribute from a human-centric or code-centric perspective, while it should be viewed as a relational property arising from the interaction between a reader and the code. With the increasing adoption of large language models in software engineering, we posit that the notion of "reader" should be generalized to encompass both humans and models. Building on this relational perspective, we extend the concept of code understandability to distinguish between human and model code understandability, aiming to investigate the behavioral alignment between the two. To this end, we use a dataset from a prior study containing human-rated judgments of understandability across diverse participant groups, and evaluate multiple open and closed-source LLMs. To operationalize such behavioral alignment, we introduce four behavioral proxies of model-code understandability (BPMU): P0, based on program comprehension-focused question answering; and P1--P3, based on program intent summarization (derived from our proposed semantic self-consistency). Our findings show that LLMs exhibit stronger behavioral alignment with general human code understandability than previously used shallow machine learning baselines. Stratified analyses further reveal that model code understandability aligns most closely with professional developers, but significantly less with other student groups. Role-conditioned prompting does not improve the alignment for the latter, suggesting that current LLMs cannot accurately mimic the perspectives of human readers with varying expertise. Finally, we demonstrate that semantic self-consistency is a reliable and extensible measure to be used as a behavioral proxy for quantifying model code understandability, with broad implications in both software engineering research and practice.

</details>


### [81] [Post-Hoc Attention Steering of Large Language Models for Robust Code Understanding under Obfuscation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26102)
*Xiaokai Rong,Aashish Yadavally,Tien N. Nguyen*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: CodeSteer is a novel approach that improves LLMs' robustness to code obfuscation by steering attention toward semantically relevant program elements (backward slices and control-flow paths) using lightweight program analysis at inference time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Code obfuscation is widely used to conceal program logic, posing challenges for analysis. LLMs show significant performance degradation on obfuscated code, suggesting they rely on superficial lexical cues rather than deep semantic reasoning. This limits their utility in malware analysis, vulnerability detection, and reverse engineering.

Method: CodeSteer integrates lightweight program analysis with inference-time attention steering to reallocate model attention toward semantically relevant program elements: backward slices for output prediction and control-flow paths for execution reasoning. This guides LLMs toward core input-to-output dependencies of a program.

Result: Experiments across multiple models and datasets demonstrate that CodeSteer significantly improves performance on obfuscated code, often recovering comparable accuracy to the level of unobfuscated programs. A case study on buffer overflow detection shows practical utility for malware/vulnerability analysis.

Conclusion: CodeSteer effectively addresses LLM limitations on obfuscated code by redirecting attention to semantic elements, achieving near-parity with unobfuscated code performance. This approach shows promise for practical applications in malware analysis, vulnerability detection, and reverse engineering of obfuscated code.

Abstract: Code obfuscation is widely used in software systems and malware to conceal program logic and hinder analysis, posing significant challenges for both human developers and automated tools. While large language models (LLMs) have shown strong capabilities in code understanding, their robustness to obfuscation remains poorly understood. Our preliminary study shows that LLM performance significantly degrades on obfuscated code, suggesting a reliance on superficial lexical cues rather than deep semantic reasoning. To address this limitation, we propose CodeSteer, a novel attention steering approach that reallocates model attention toward semantically relevant program elements, including backward slices for output prediction and control-flow paths for execution reasoning. Our method integrates lightweight program analysis with inference-time attention steering to guide LLMs toward the core input-to-output dependencies of a program. Experiments across multiple models and datasets demonstrate that CodeSteer significantly improves performance on obfuscated code, often recovering comparable accuracy to the level of unobfuscated programs. We also show CodeSteer's practical utility through a case study on buffer overflow detection, highlighting its potential for malware/vulnerability analysis and reverse engineering of obfuscated code.

</details>


### [82] [Why Do LLMs Fail at OCL Generation? A Graph Reasoning Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26122)
*Hamza Attarwala,Moataz Chouchen,Mohammad Hamdaqa,Omar Alam*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This study investigates why Large Language Models fail when generating Object Constraint Language (OCL) constraints from UML class diagrams. By treating the task as graph reasoning and evaluating six LLMs on the PathOCL dataset, the researchers find that structural complexity and navigation depth are the primary bottlenecks, not linguistic factors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing research on LLM-based OCL generation focuses on improving accuracy but lacks understanding of why models fail. This study addresses this gap by investigating the underlying causes of LLM failures in OCL generation.

Method: Empirical evaluation using the PathOCL dataset across six state-of-the-art LLMs. The researchers analyzed the impact of UML structural properties (navigation depth, model complexity), lexical similarity, prompt ordering strategies, and graph-aware prompting on OCL correctness.

Result: OCL generation performance significantly degrades with increasing navigation depth and structural complexity. Lexical similarity has limited influence, while textual ordering of UML elements affects performance. Graph-based prompting yields partial improvements but does not eliminate structural reasoning errors.

Conclusion: OCL generation is primarily constrained by graph reasoning limitations rather than purely linguistic factors. Structural reasoning is identified as a key bottleneck for current LLMs in model-driven engineering tasks.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used to generate Object Constraint Language (OCL) constraints from natural language specifications and UML class diagrams. However, existing work mainly focuses on improving accuracy, with limited understanding of why these models fail. Aims. This study investigates the underlying causes of LLM failures in OCL generation, framing the task as a graph reasoning problem over UML class diagrams. Method. We conduct an empirical evaluation using the PathOCL dataset across six state-of-the-art LLMs. We analyze the impact of UML structural properties (e.g., navigation depth and model complexity), lexical similarity, prompt ordering strategies, and graph-aware prompting on OCL correctness. Results. We find that OCL generation performance significantly degrades with increasing navigation depth and structural complexity. Lexical similarity has limited influence, while textual ordering of UML elements affects performance. Graph-based prompting yields partial improvements but does not eliminate structural reasoning errors. Conclusions. OCL generation is primarily constrained by graph reasoning limitations rather than purely linguistic factors. These results highlight structural reasoning as a key bottleneck for current LLMs in model-driven engineering tasks.

</details>


### [83] [A Large-Scale Longitudinal Study of Multi-CI Service Adoption](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26181)
*Taher A. Ghaleb,Rasha Bin-Thalab*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents a large-scale longitudinal study of multi-CI service adoption across 135,227 GitHub repositories, finding that ~20% of repos use multiple CI services, typically during migration rather than sustained parallel use. GitHub Actions became dominant in 2020, while Travis CI's pricing change triggered significant abandonment. Only 11% of commits mention strategic reasons for CI changes, suggesting operational maintenance patterns are more reliably captured than strategic intent.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work examined CI adoption through individual services, specific languages, or developer interviews, but the lifecycle of multi-service usage remains underexplored. With CI evolving from a single practice to a landscape of competing services, understanding how projects adopt, evolve, and abandon CI services over time is crucial for tooling, migration support, and software delivery infrastructure research.

Method: Large-scale longitudinal study analyzing 135,227 GitHub repositories across seven programming languages and eight CI services. Combined quantitative analysis (tracking adoption patterns over time) with qualitative analysis (examining CI configuration rationales in commit messages).

Result: Key findings: (1) ~20% of repositories use multiple CI services, often transitional during migration; (2) CI service choice is the dominant factor across adoption, complexity, maintenance, and engagement; (3) GitHub Actions adoption crossed 50% of first adoptions in 2020 across all languages (synchronized platform-level transition); (4) Travis CI's pricing change became the strongest abandonment signal; (5) Only ~11% of commits state strategic reasons for CI changes, indicating operational maintenance patterns are more reliably captured than strategic intent; (6) Multi-CI adoption is associated with project maturity, while abandonment is more strongly tied to service identity than repository characteristics.

Conclusion: Multi-CI adoption is primarily driven by project maturity, while abandonment is more influenced by service-level factors (e.g., pricing changes) than repository characteristics. The shift to GitHub Actions was a coordinated platform transition, and CI configuration changes are largely reactive rather than strategic. These findings provide empirical evidence for CI tooling improvements, migration support tools, and future research on managing evolving software delivery infrastructure.

Abstract: Continuous Integration (CI) adoption has evolved from a single practice into a broad landscape of competing services, and many projects no longer rely on a single CI service. However, how this multi-service behavior evolves over a project's lifetime remains unknown. Prior work has examined CI adoption and migration through individual services, specific languages, or developer interviews, leaving the lifecycle of multi-service usage underexplored. In this paper, we conduct a large-scale longitudinal study of multi-CI service adoption covering 135227 GitHub repositories from seven programming languages and involving eight CI services. We characterize how CI services are adopted, evolved, and abandoned over time through quantitative and qualitative analyses. About one in five repositories use multiple CI services, often transitional during migration rather than for sustained parallel use. CI service choice is the dominant factor across adoption, complexity, maintenance, and engagement, with language effects limited to specific cases. Our results reveal that the shift to GitHub Actions was a synchronized platform-level transition, crossing 50\% of first adoptions in 2020 across all studied languages, while the decline of Travis CI following its pricing change became the strongest abandonment signal. Our analysis of CI configuration rationales shows that activity is mostly reactive: only about 11\% of commits state a strategic reason, indicating that commit messages capture operational maintenance patterns more reliably than strategic intent. Finally, we find that multi-CI adoption is primarily associated with project maturity, whereas abandonment is more strongly associated with service identity than repository characteristics. These findings provide empirical evidence for CI tooling, migration support, and future research on managing evolving software delivery infrastructure.

</details>


### [84] [WatchPoint: Executable User Feedback for Real-World Agentic Web Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26204)
*Guanqun Yang,Wei Yang,Xueqing Liu*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: WatchPoint is a simulated-user system that mimics real developer debugging behavior by generating and executing diagnostic scripts against live web applications, achieving 57.6% task recovery on Web-Bench compared to 54.5% by human testers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing feedback mechanisms for coding agents rely on passive approaches (screenshots, LLM-as-a-judge, natural-language corrections) rather than actively interacting with the live application like human developers do when debugging.

Method: WatchPoint generates and executes diagnostic scripts against the running application to produce structured observations that guide the coding model's retry. Evaluation uses Web-Bench, a benchmark of 50 multi-file web projects with 1,000 sequentially dependent tasks verified by deterministic end-to-end tests. A controlled user study was conducted to verify simulation realism.

Result: WatchPoint recovers 57.6% of the tasks it diagnoses. Human testers achieve a comparable recovery rate of 54.5%, demonstrating that automated diagnostic scripts can substitute for interactive human testing. The study also identifies capability gaps governing when simulated-user feedback is helpful versus when it should be withheld.

Conclusion: Automated diagnostic scripts can effectively substitute for interactive human testing on sequential web development tasks, with WatchPoint slightly outperforming human testers. The identified capability gaps pattern can help determine optimal feedback strategies for coding agents.

Abstract: When a professional web developer's code fails a test, they do not simply re-read the stack trace. They open the application in a browser, click buttons, inspect computed styles, and run diagnostic commands to understand what went wrong. Existing feedback mechanisms for coding agents rely on screenshots, LLM-as-a-judge scoring, or natural-language corrections, but few interact with the live application the way a developer would. We introduce WatchPoint, a simulated-user system that mimics real developer behavior by generating and executing diagnostic scripts against the running application, producing structured observations that guide the coding model's retry. Unlike prior approaches that target single-file edits or evaluate using non-executable metrics, we operate on Web-Bench, a benchmark of 50 multi-file web projects comprising 1,000 sequentially dependent tasks, verified by deterministic end-to-end tests. WatchPoint recovers 57.6% of the tasks it diagnoses, and a controlled user study confirms the simulation's realism: human testers achieve a comparable recovery rate (54.5%), providing evidence that automated diagnostic scripts can substitute for interactive human testing on sequential web development tasks. We further identify a pattern of capability gaps that governs when simulated-user feedback is helpful and when it should be withheld.

</details>


### [85] [Reducing Hallucinations in Large Language Models Through Integrated Self-Verification and Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26229)
*Ashly Joseph*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: CoVe-RAG+ is a unified framework that combines Chain-of-Verification (CoVe) with Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) to mitigate hallucinations in LLM outputs for engineering tasks. It verifies claims against external authority sources (engineering standards, CAD information, simulation reports) and applies iterative self-verification, achieving 28% improvement in factual accuracy over baseline methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly used for advanced engineering tasks like CAD documentation, standards compliance, and knowledge retrieval, but they produce hallucinations—convincing but inaccurate outputs. This limits their trustworthiness in high-precision engineering applications where factual accuracy and compliance are critical.

Method: CoVe-RAG+ integrates Chain-of-Verification (CoVe) with Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) in a unified framework. It supports LLM verification against external authority sources (engineering standards, CAD information, simulation reports) and applies an iterative self-verification process to validate important claims. The framework provides verification reports and source traceability.

Result: Experimental results show a 28% improvement in factual accuracy relative to baseline CoVe and RAG methodologies. The framework also enhances user confidence through elucidative verification reports and source traceability.

Conclusion: CoVe-RAG+ provides a scalable and reliable option for implementing LLMs in engineering design processes where factual accuracy is critical. It effectively addresses the hallucination problem while maintaining practical applicability for CAD model documentation, standards compliance verification, and reutilization of historical design data.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are progressively used for advanced engineering tasks, includes Computer-Aided Design (CAD) documentation, standards compliance verification, and knowledge retrieval. Still, they are prone to produce hallucinations, outputs that seem convincing but aren't based on context that limit their trustworthiness in high-end engineering applications where precision and compliance are crucial. The paper introduces CoVe-RAG+, a unified framework that integrates Chain-of-Verification (CoVe) with Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) to mitigate hallucinations in the results generated by large language models (LLMs). CoVe-RAG+ supports LLM verification in external sources of authority, such as engineering standards, CAD information, and simulation reports, while applying an iterative self-verification process to validate important claims. CoVe-RAG+ is assessed on engineering activities such as CAD model documentation, standards compliance verification, and the reutilization of historical design data. Experimental findings indicate a 28% improvement in factual accuracy relative to baseline CoVe and RAG methodologies. Moreover, CoVe-RAG+ strengthens user confidence by providing elucidative verification reports and source traceability. The findings indicate that CoVe-RAG+ provides a scalable and reliable option for implementing LLMs in engineering design processes where factual accuracy is critical.

</details>


### [86] [CANcept: Model-based CAN Traffic Generation and Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26263)
*Lino Wertz,Junes Sheikhi,Florian Fehrle,Adrian Rupp,Tianhai Liu,Philipp Kern,Carsten Sinz,Bernhard Beckert*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper presents an open-source model-based tool for testing timing-related safety properties of Controller Area Network (CAN)-based software. The tool combines a Traffic Schedule Model (TSM) for message timing/content transformations and a DBC-based Communication Model (DCM) for encoding/decoding between message-level traffic and CAN frames.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing open-source tools for testing CAN-based software require encoding scenarios in low-level scripts, which are difficult to maintain and evolve. There is a need for precise control over transmitted data and communication timing when testing timing-related safety properties.

Method: The tool combines two models (TSM and DCM) in one execution mechanism to generate and manipulate CAN traffic, including replay of transformed traces. The approach uses a model-based specification approach rather than low-level scripting.

Result: Preliminary evaluation indicates the tool realizes specified scenarios with low timing deviation and stable execution under elevated traffic rates.

Conclusion: The tool provides a viable solution for model-based CAN traffic testing, offering maintainable specifications and reliable timing control for safety-critical automotive software testing.

Abstract: Testing timing-related safety properties of Controller Area Network (CAN)-based software requires precise control over transmitted data and communication timing. Existing open-source tools typically encode such scenarios in low-level scripts, making them difficult to maintain and evolve. We present \toolName{}, an open-source model-based tool for specifying, generating, replaying, and manipulating CAN traffic. A traffic schedule model (TSM) defines message timing and content transformations, and a DBC-based communication model (DCM) defines encoding and decoding between message-level traffic and CAN frames. \toolName{} combines both models in one execution mechanism for generated and manipulated traffic, including replay of transformed traces. A preliminary evaluation indicates that \toolName{} realizes the specified scenarios, with low timing deviation and stable execution under elevated traffic rates.

</details>


### [87] [On the Lexical Superstition of Large Language Models for Code Comprehension: Re-evaluation on Code of Low Lexical Quality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26388)
*Xin Shen,San-Zhuo Xi,Yali Du,Ming Li*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper investigates a vulnerability in LLMs called "lexical overemphasis" - where models assign disproportionate weight to identifier names (variable/function names) in code, even when these names are misleading but program structure is preserved. The authors introduce Face/Off, a semantics-preserving identifier-renaming framework to test this.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Identifier names in code are statistically informative but unreliable. The authors investigate whether current LLMs incorrectly prioritize lexical cues (identifier names) over actual program semantics when the two conflict.

Method: The authors introduce Face/Off, a semantics-preserving identifier-renaming framework, and evaluate progressive naming conditions across multiple models and code-comprehension tasks. They test prompt-based and fine-tuning-based interventions, and include a type-inference control experiment.

Result: Lexical overemphasis is pervasive across evaluated models and primary tasks: performance decreases as identifier information is removed or made misleading, and outputs are directed toward meanings suggested by misleading names. This pattern persists under prompt-engineering and fine-tuning interventions. The effect is smaller when answers are locally recoverable without the target name.

Conclusion: Lexical overemphasis is an entrenched problem in current LLMs, representing a systematic vulnerability in how models balance lexical cues against program structure. This does not mean identifiers are unhelpful, but suggests the need for evaluations and modeling methods that preserve natural code regularities while keeping conclusions grounded in accurate, formalized code semantics.

Abstract: Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have made them widely used for code-related tasks. Identifier names are statistically informative in naturally occurring code, but their information is not always reliable. We investigate whether current LLMs assign disproportionate weight to lexical cues when renaming preserves program structure. We introduce Face/Off, a semantics-preserving identifier-renaming framework, and evaluate progressive naming conditions across multiple models and code-comprehension tasks. Within this framework, lexical overemphasis is pervasive across the evaluated models and primary tasks: performance generally decreases as identifier information is removed or made misleading, and outputs are often directed toward the meanings suggested by misleading names. The pattern persists under representative prompt- and fine-tuning-based interventions, suggesting that lexical overemphasis is an entrenched problem. A type-inference control confirms a boundary: naming effects are smaller when the answer is locally recoverable without the target name. These results do not imply that identifiers are unhelpful; rather, they reveal a systematic vulnerability in how current LLMs balance lexical cues against program structure. Our findings motivate evaluations and modeling methods that preserve the benefits of natural code regularities while keeping conclusions grounded in accurate, formalized code semantics.

</details>


### [88] [FeatLens: Feature-Guided Dynamic Code Graph Construction and Retrieval for Repository-Level Code Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26480)
*Xutian Li,Bo Xiong,Yifeng Zhu,Kunze Li,Xianlin Zhao,Runbang Yan,Yanzhen Zou,Lu Zhang,Bing Xie*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: FeatLens is a feature-guided dynamic code graph construction and retrieval approach for repository-level code generation. It uses a feature index linking natural language descriptions to code entities, dynamically constructs task-specific seed graphs, and applies semantic-structural graph reasoning with personalized PageRank for dependency retrieval, eliminating the need for persistent whole-repository graphs or LLM exploration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing code generation methods for repository-level tasks rely on code similarity search, persistent whole-repository graphs, or LLM-driven graph exploration, which incur high costs for graph construction, reasoning, and token usage. Feature-oriented methods exist but focus on requirement decomposition and planning rather than code dependency retrieval.

Method: FeatLens builds a feature index linking natural-language feature descriptions to function-level code entities. For each generation task, it dynamically constructs a task-specific seed graph from the feature index and applies semantic-structural graph reasoning with personalized PageRank to select a compact reasoning graph. This replaces persistent graph maintenance and LLM exploration with deterministic, lightweight dependency retrieval.

Result: FeatLens achieves the best DR@15 (Dependency Retrieval @ 15) of 0.501 on DevEval and 0.460 on EvoCodeBench. On DevEval generation, it achieves DIR@1 (Direct Implementation Retrieval @ 1) of 52.91% with DeepSeek-V3.2 and 53.58% with GPT-5-mini, while maintaining competitive Pass@1 and producing shorter code. Compared to the strongest graph-based baseline, it reduces graph nodes by 61.0%, edges by 86.2%, and total token overhead by 45.9%, with no LLM tokens used during retrieval.

Conclusion: FeatLens demonstrates that feature-guided dynamic graph construction can effectively replace persistent whole-repository graphs and LLM-driven exploration for repository-level code generation, achieving superior dependency retrieval accuracy while significantly reducing computational overhead and token costs.

Abstract: Recent code generation research has moved from isolated function completion toward repository-level generation in existing codebases. To implement a target function correctly, an LLM must identify reusable repository dependencies such as existing functions, APIs, and cross-file definitions. Existing retrieval methods provide such context through code similarity search, persistent whole-repository graphs, or LLM-driven graph exploration, but often incur high graph construction, reasoning, and token costs. Feature-oriented methods offer a natural view of software functionality, yet they mainly support requirement decomposition, planning, or feature editing rather than code dependency retrieval. This paper presents \textbf{FeatLens}, a feature-guided dynamic code graph construction and retrieval approach for repository-level code generation. FeatLens builds a feature index that links natural-language feature descriptions to function-level code entities. Given a generation task, it dynamically constructs a task-specific seed graph from the feature index and applies semantic-structural graph reasoning with personalized PageRank to select a compact reasoning graph. This design replaces persistent whole-repository graph maintenance and LLM exploration with deterministic and lightweight dependency retrieval. Experiments on DevEval and EvoCodeBench show that FeatLens achieves the best DR@15 among sparse, dense, and graph-based baselines (0.501 and 0.460). On DevEval generation, it obtains the highest DIR@1, reaching 52.91\% with DeepSeek-V3.2 and 53.58\% with GPT-5-mini, while maintaining competitive Pass@1 and producing shorter code. Compared with the strongest graph-based baseline, FeatLens reduces graph nodes by 61.0\%, edges by 86.2\%, and total token overhead by 45.9\%, with no LLM tokens used during retrieval.

</details>


### [89] [From Approval to Execution: Reconstruction-Aware Repair Analysis for LLM-Agent Software](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26529)
*Junchi Zhu,Zhenguang Liu,Shaojing Fan,Jianhai Chen,Qinming He*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper addresses a security flaw in LLM-agent software approval mechanisms: approvals are often based on one version of an action, but the actual object consumed during execution may be a different version reconstructed through workflow reload, transcript projection, argument rebinding, or durable-state lookup. Existing analyses can verify that fields were inspected but cannot confirm the inspected version reaches the sink. The paper proposes reconstruction-stable authorization (ReSA) with three contributions: (1) a formal framework over representation transitions, sink dependencies, consumed versions, and grant scope; (2) an obligation system to judge repairs and expose residual sink suffixes; (3) APAS-Finder implementation that freezes predictions before sink observation. The approach achieved 100% agreement with 28 controlled outcomes, identified 4 matched pairs requiring opposite judgments despite identical field-flow/check coverage, and prevented 60/60 out-of-scope effects in released consumers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Approval mechanisms in LLM-agent software are meant to safeguard consequential actions, but a critical gap exists: the action approved may differ from the action ultimately executed due to reconstruction processes. Existing fieldflow and check-coverage analyses can verify that expected fields were inspected but cannot confirm the inspected version reaches the execution sink. This leads to "locally complete repairs" that still leave residual authorization bypasses after reconstruction.

Method: The paper makes three key contributions: (1) Formulates reconstruction-stable authorization (ReSA) over representation transitions, sink dependencies, consumed versions, and grant scope; (2) Derives obligations to judge candidate repairs and expose residual sink suffixes; (3) Implements APAS-Finder that freezes predictions before an independent sink oracle observes execution. Evaluation involved 28 controlled outcomes, comparison with CodeQL composite analysis, and testing on released consumers.

Result: Predictions agreed with all 28 controlled outcomes. Four matched pairs required opposite judgments despite identical field-flow and check coverage - CodeQL composite recovered all 8 when supplied object flow, dominance, interference, and grant contract. Two analysts agreed on all 4 model-validation dispositions and 19/20 sink dependencies. On released consumers, five repairs prevented 60/60 tested out-of-scope effects, while three mechanism contrasts exposed predicted residual effects.

Conclusion: Repair sufficiency in LLM-agent authorization depends on the reconstructed action consumed at execution time, not merely on an approval record or earlier checked representation. This challenges the assumption that approval mechanisms provide adequate security safeguards and demonstrates the need for reconstruction-aware authorization analysis.

Abstract: Approval mechanisms have become a primary safeguard for consequential actions in LLM-agent software. Yet the action shown for approval is often not the object ultimately consumed: workflow reload, transcript projection, argument rebinding, and durable-state lookup may reconstruct it before execution. Existing fieldflow and check-coverage analyses can establish that expected fields were inspected, but not that the inspected object version reaches the sink or that no replacement intervenes between check and use. Consequently, a locally complete repair may still leave a residual authorization bypass after reconstruction. We address this problem through three designs. (1) We formulate reconstruction-stable authorization (ReSA) over representation transitions, sink dependencies, consumed versions, and grant scope. (2) We derive obligations that judge candidate repairs and expose residual sink suffixes. (3) We implement the analysis in APAS-Finder and freeze predictions before an independent sink oracle observes execution. Predictions agree with all 28 controlled outcomes. Four matched pairs require opposite judgments despite identical field-flow and check coverage; a CodeQL composite recovers all eight when supplied object flow, dominance, interference, and the same grant contract. Two analysts agree on all four model-validation dispositions and 19/20 sink dependencies. On released consumers, five repairs prevent 60/60 tested out-of-scope effects, while three mechanism contrasts expose the predicted residual effects. Repair sufficiency therefore depends on the reconstructed action consumed, not merely on an approval record or earlier checked representation.

</details>


### [90] [TraceVIC: Causal Reasoning over Code Evolution for Identifying Vulnerability-Inducing Commits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26711)
*Fnu Tanish,Samiha Shimmi,Samikshya Chapagain,Hamed Okhravi,Mona Rahimi,Lei Zhang*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: TraceVIC is a temporal graph-based approach that identifies vulnerability-inducing commits (VICs) by reasoning over code evolution history rather than using simple positional heuristics. It localizes root-cause lines, traces their histories, and constructs graph representations to rank candidate commits based on their contribution to vulnerable conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing VIC identification techniques rely on git blame with positional heuristics (earliest/most recent modification), but the true VIC may occur anywhere in revision history, and vulnerable behavior may depend on code evolving across multiple revisions. This necessitates reasoning about how vulnerability-relevant code evolves over time.

Method: TraceVIC localizes likely root-cause lines and traces their histories across revisions. It constructs graph representations capturing: (1) program structure within each revision, and (2) evolution of vulnerability-relevant code across history. Temporal edges preserve correspondences between program elements across consecutive revisions, enabling direct ranking of candidate commits by their contribution to the vulnerable condition.

Result: Ablation results show modeling full revision history improves F2 from 0.637 to 0.814. TraceVIC improves F2 by up to 28.7% over state-of-the-art methods and identifies a valid VIC for 78 of 79 vulnerabilities across four unseen C/C++ projects.

Conclusion: TraceVIC demonstrates that reasoning about code evolution history is crucial for accurate VIC identification. The temporal graph-based approach significantly outperforms positional heuristic methods, achieving high accuracy across unseen projects.

Abstract: Software vulnerabilities are often discovered long after they are introduced, making it difficult to identify the vulnerability-inducing commit (VIC) responsible for introducing the underlying vulnerable condition. Existing VIC identification techniques largely rely on git blame to trace vulnerable code through revision history and use positional heuristics, such as selecting its earliest or most recent modification. However, the true VIC may occur anywhere within this history, and vulnerable behavior may depend on code that evolves across multiple revisions. We therefore argue that VIC identification requires reasoning about how vulnerability-relevant code evolves, rather than simply where a candidate commit appears in the revision history.
  We present TraceVIC, a temporal graph-based approach for identifying and ranking VICs by reasoning over code evolution. TraceVIC first localizes likely root-cause lines and traces their histories across revisions, constructing graph representations that capture program structure within each revision and the evolution of vulnerability-relevant code across the history. It reasons over the resulting revision history, using temporal edges to preserve correspondences between program elements across consecutive revisions, and directly ranks candidate commits according to their contribution to the vulnerable condition.
  Ablation results show that modeling the full revision history improves F2 from 0.637 to 0.814. TraceVIC improves F2 by up to 28.7% over state-of-the-art methods and identifies a valid VIC for 78 of 79 vulnerabilities across four unseen C/C++ projects.

</details>


### [91] [Metrics Failure in LLM-Based Code Vulnerability Repair: An Empirical Study and a Change-Aware Screen](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26749)
*Om Nepal,Sushant Aryal,Oluseyi Olukola,Nick Rahimi*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper argues that compile rate is an unreliable metric for evaluating LLM-based C/C++ vulnerability repair, supported by five controlled experiments across 203 vulnerable functions, three LLMs, and three prompting strategies. The authors find compile rate is dominated by artifacts, shifts significantly with compiler flags, ranks models inversely to reference-similarity metrics, and rewards non-repairs. They also evaluate CodeBLEU and diff_F1 as alternatives, with diff_F1 showing some promise as a preliminary screen.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The automated repair of C/C++ security vulnerabilities using LLMs is an emerging field, but progress is typically measured by "compile rate" - whether the generated patch compiles. The authors argue this metric is scientifically unreliable and need to demonstrate this through controlled experiments.

Method: Five controlled experiments over 203 vulnerable functions from Big-Vul dataset, testing three open-source code LLMs (350M to 6.7B parameters) with three prompting strategies. The experiments examine: (1) response to intervention, (2) attribution of compile failures, (3) sensitivity to compiler flags, (4) correlation with reference-similarity metrics, and (5) behavior under optimization. Also evaluated CodeBLEU and diff_F1 as alternatives.

Result: Compile rate (i) barely responds to quality-improving interventions; (ii) 64% of compile failures are not attributable to the model but to evaluation-harness/dataset artifacts; (iii) shifts 1.8-2.7x with identical patches under different compiler-standard flags; (iv) ranks models in opposite order to reference-similarity metrics; (v) rewards non-repairs when used as optimization target. CodeBLEU fails: unchanged vulnerable input outscores all models. diff_F1 gives zero credit to no-op patches and near-zero to deletion-based gaming, while crediting genuine partial edits.

Conclusion: Compile rate is scientifically unreliable as a metric for single-function vulnerability repair. The authors argue for change-aware, execution-grounded evaluation of LLM-based vulnerability repair. diff_F1 may serve as a cheap preliminary screen before deeper analysis, though it is not a repair-quality metric itself.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly applied to the automated repair of C/C++ security vulnerabilities, and compile rate is a commonly reported proxy for progress: whether the generated patch compiles. We argue that compile rate is a scientifically unreliable metric for single-function vulnerability repair, and we support this with five controlled experiments over 203 vulnerable functions from Big-Vul, three open-source code LLMs (350M to 6.7B parameters), and three prompting strategies. Compile rate (i) barely responds to an intervention that substantially improves the generated code; (ii) is dominated by evaluation-harness and dataset artifacts rather than model quality, with about 64% of compile failures not attributable to the model, a share that is nearly invariant across models; (iii) shifts by 1.8 to 2.7 times on identical patches under a single compiler-standard flag, with zero regressions; (iv) ranks the three models in the opposite order to reference-similarity metrics; and (v) rewards non-repairs when used as an optimization target, since a compiler-feedback loop raises compile rate while similarity to the human fix falls, with manual inspection finding deletion- and placeholder-style non-repairs among the newly compiling outputs. The natural fallback, whole-function CodeBLEU, also fails: an unchanged copy of the vulnerable input outscores every model. We also examine diff_F1, a change-aware screen that scores only the edited region. It gives exactly zero credit to a no-op and near-zero credit to some, though not all, of the deletion-based gaming patches we observed, while still crediting genuine partial edits, so it may serve as a cheap screen before deeper, execution-based analysis. It is not a repair-quality metric, and we report where it falls short. Our findings argue for change-aware, execution-grounded evaluation of LLM-based vulnerability repair.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [92] ["As a Language Model...": Chat Template Switches LLM Self-Referential Voice and Activation Steering Reproduces It](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25021)
*Jędrzej Maczan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Chat templates control whether LLMs add disclaimers like "I'm just an AI" to self-reports. When present, templates increase disclaimer voice and decrease experiential voice; when absent, the opposite occurs. A specific direction in activation space can steer this behavior, meaning a model's self-description is partially set by deployment choices, not just weights.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The researchers aimed to understand what drives LLMs to add self-disclaimers when asked about themselves. They questioned whether these responses reflect the model's true self-knowledge or rather how the model is deployed (e.g., chat template usage).

Method: Tested 8 open-source instruct models up to 9B parameters with and without chat templates. Analyzed internal activations of 3 models to identify a direction that controls disclaimer voice. Performed steering experiments by adding/removing this direction and testing a random direction as control.

Result: Chat templates act as a switch: when present, they increase disclaimer voice ("I'm just an AI") and decrease experiential voice ("I feel"); when absent, the reverse occurs.
