A specific direction in activation space can steer this behavior—removing it reduces disclaimers, adding it increases them. A random direction had no effect. Adding this direction to models without chat templates made them disclaim as if the template was present.

Conclusion: A model's self-description is not solely determined by its weights but is partially set by the chat template. Researchers studying self-reports or introspection of LLMs need to control for this confound. The identified direction provides a tool to steer disclaimer voice, and self-descriptions should not be treated literally as facts about the model's internal state.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) tend to add disclaimers like "I'm just an AI" when asked about something related to themselves. The self-reports from such responses are used in debates about AI safety or self-knowledge of the models, yet what drives them is not well understood. Are the models telling us about themselves or rather how they are deployed? In this work, we show that the chat template works like a switch - when present, it turns this disclaimer voice up and experiential voice like "I feel" down, across 8 popular open-source instruct models up to 9B parameters in size. And conversely when the chat template is not present, it turns the disclaimer voice down and experiential voice up. Inside the activations of 3 models, we find a direction that steers this behavior. Removing the direction in the model's activation space turns disclaimer voice down and adding it turns it up, while a random direction of the same size has little effect. We find that instruct models without chat template, when we add the disclaimer direction to them, disclaim like the template was there. Since the chat template controls the disclaimer voice of LLMs, then researchers studying self-reports or introspection of models might have a confound they need to control for. Our results show that there is a direction they can use to steer this voice. More broadly, our work shows that what models say about themselves is not a fact about them. What they say doesn't come only from weights, but it is partially set by the chat template, and because of that a model's self-description shouldn't be treated literally.

</details>


### [93] [Federating Quantum and Classical Computing: A Privacy-Preserving Hybrid Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25082)
*Carlos Cano,Daniel M. Jimenez-Gutierrez,Diego Sal,Georgios Kellaris,Joaquin del Rio,Oleksii Sliusarenko,Xabi Uribe-Etxebarria*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper evaluates federated learning (FL) combined with hybrid quantum-classical models for privacy-preserving quantum machine learning. Using Sherpa.ai's Blind Vertical FL (SBVFL) protocol, the approach enables multi-party collaboration without centralizing raw data while maintaining parameter efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hybrid quantum-classical models show promise for near-term quantum computing applications but face deployment challenges in privacy-sensitive, multi-party settings due to data centralization concerns and parameter efficiency requirements. This research addresses these constraints by integrating federated learning with quantum-classical models.

Method: The study constructs the split multiplicative periodic parity (SMPP) benchmark following common QML design practices. It evaluates SBVFL protocol combining a hybrid quantum-classical active party with a classical passive party, comparing against local training, centralized training, and alternative classical models (neural networks and random forest).

Result: Simulations demonstrate that SBVFL improves accuracy from 0.7227 (local training) to 0.8757, closely approaching non-private centralized accuracy. The hybrid quantum-classical model achieves this performance with substantially fewer trainable parameters compared to classical neural networks and random forest alternatives.

Conclusion: Federated learning enables high-performing, privacy-preserving quantum-classical collaboration without centralizing raw data. This approach successfully addresses the dual challenges of privacy preservation and parameter efficiency in quantum machine learning deployments.

Abstract: Quantum machine learning (QML) is increasingly recognized as one of the most promising near-term applications of quantum computing, viewed as a next-frontier candidate beyond purely classical approaches. Hybrid quantum-classical models operationalize this potential by embedding a parameterized quantum circuit within a model where all other components remain classical-a design already applied to chemistry simulation, financial modeling, and image classification. However, their deployment in privacy-sensitive, multi-party settings is constrained by the need to avoid centralizing raw data and by the requirement that modern quantum circuits remain parameter-efficient to stay trainable at scale.
  In this paper, we address these constraints by evaluating federated learning (FL) as a means of combining a hybrid quantum-classical active party with a classical passive party, using Sherpa.ai's Blind Vertical FL (SBVFL) protocol to avoid centralizing raw data, while drastically reducing communication. We construct the split multiplicative periodic parity (SMPP) benchmark, following common QML design practice. On this task, our simulations show that SBVFL raises accuracy from 0.7227 to 0.8757 compared to local training, closely approaching non-private centralized accuracy, and that the hybrid quantum-classical model achieves this with substantially fewer trainable parameters than the classical neural networks and random forest alternatives. These results show that FL enables high-performing, privacy-preserving quantum-classical collaboration without centralizing raw data.

</details>


### [94] [Entropy Can Flow, or It Can Guide. Be Entropy. LEDFlow: Introducing Entropy-guided Generation Order into Uniform Discrete Flow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25131)
*Tung Sum Thomas Kwok,Yidong Ouyang,Yingjia Wan,Ying Nian Wu,Zhijiang Guo,Oscar Leong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses error propagation in uniform discrete flow models by introducing selective absorption and Low-Entropy Discrete Flow (LEDFlow), a training-free sampler that adaptively orders absorption by local entropy. The method achieves 0.845 Sudoku solve accuracy, best text-to-image scores, and improvements across six multimodal benchmarks while maintaining comparable inference cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Uniform discrete flow permits repeated updates at every generation position, but this exposes correct intermediate predictions to later errors. Experiments show 9.4% of generated cells in Sudoku puzzles are correct at intermediate steps but incorrect in final output, indicating significant error propagation in uniform flow models.

Method: The authors introduce generation order through selective absorption, which fixes chosen predictions while preserving uniform-flow velocity at active positions. They propose LEDFlow, a training-free sampler that orders absorption by local entropy. They decompose absorption error into joint dependence and conditional prediction terms, proving that selecting lowest-entropy positions under fixed absorption budget minimizes an upper bound on the conditional term.

Result: LEDFlow achieves 0.845 Nikoli Sudoku solve accuracy with largest gains on strongly constrained tasks. On text-to-image generation, it attains the best overall score. On multimodal understanding, it improves over the native sampler on all six benchmarks, at inference cost comparable to standard flow sampling.

Conclusion: Selective absorption via low-entropy ordering effectively mitigates error propagation in discrete flow models. The theoretical analysis shows local entropy is optimal for absorption ordering, and the method generalizes across reasoning (Sudoku), text-to-image, and multimodal understanding tasks without requiring training modifications.

Abstract: Uniform discrete flow permits repeated updates at every generation position. While continued revision supports correction of wrong tokens, it also exposes correct intermediate predictions to later errors. An experiment on Sudoku puzzles shows that 9.4% of generated cells are correct at an intermediate step but incorrect in the final output. We introduce generation order into uniform discrete flow through selective absorption, which fixes chosen predictions while preserving the uniform-flow velocity at active positions. To prevent absorbing incorrect predictions, we propose Low-Entropy Discrete Flow (LEDFlow), a training-free sampler that adaptively orders absorption by local entropy. By decomposing absorption error into joint dependence and conditional prediction terms, we show that selecting the lowest-entropy positions under a fixed absorption budget minimizes an upper bound on the conditional term. We further support the choice of local entropy by showing that the decision-error bound of global lookahead grows with the lookahead window under an imperfect denoiser. Across reasoning benchmarks, LEDFlow attains 0.845 Nikoli Sudoku solve accuracy, with the largest gains on strongly constrained tasks. On text-to-image generation it attains the best overall score, and on multimodal understanding it improves over the native sampler on all six benchmarks, at an inference cost comparable to standard flow sampling.

</details>


### [95] [The Probabilistic Structure of Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25134)
*Adnan Aboulalaâ*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper provides a unified probabilistic framework for understanding large language models (LLMs), treating them as probability measures on token sequences specified through autoregressive conditional distributions. It connects training (maximum likelihood with stochastic gradient methods), text generation (sequential simulation), and diffusion models (reverse-time stochastic processes), while examining the KL divergence's role in phenomena like hallucination and the distinction between statistical plausibility and truth.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to bring together, in a self-contained account, tools that are typically treated separately in the LLM literature. The authors aim to provide a unified probabilistic perspective that can explain various phenomena like hallucination and the relationship between statistical plausibility and truth.

Method: The paper employs a probabilistic framework where LLMs are modeled as probability measures on token sequences via autoregressive conditional distributions. Training is formulated as maximum likelihood estimation solved by stochastic gradient methods. Text generation is viewed as sequential simulation of the resulting stochastic process. Additionally, diffusion models are discussed as an alternative approach using score functions and reverse-time stochastic processes.

Result: The paper presents a theoretical framework that unifies the understanding of LLMs through probability theory, examines the role of KL divergence asymmetry in text generation, and connects statistical plausibility with truth. It also provides a complementary illustration through diffusion models.

Conclusion: The conclusion likely emphasizes the value of the probabilistic perspective in providing a unified understanding of LLMs, their training, generation mechanisms, and related phenomena, with diffusion models offering an alternative generation paradigm.

Abstract: This paper presents a probabilistic perspective on large language models (LLMs), developed with the aim of bringing together, in a single self-contained account, tools that are usually treated separately across the literature. LLMs are described through probability measures on the set of sequences of tokens, specified via their autoregressive conditional distributions. Training is formulated as a maximum-likelihood estimation problem, addressed by stochastic gradient methods, while text generation is viewed as the sequential simulation of the resulting stochastic process. The role of the asymmetry of the Kullback--Leibler divergence in text generation is examined in relation with characteristic phenomena such as hallucination and the distinction between statistical plausibility and truth. As a complementary illustration of the same viewpoint, we also discuss diffusion models, built around the score function, which cast generation not as sequential token prediction but as the simulation of a reverse-time stochastic process transforming noise into data both in discrete and continuous time.

</details>


### [96] [Stable Unsupervised Continual Chunking with Sheaf SyncMap](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25143)
*Xueyuan Li,Danilo Vasconcellos Vargas*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses unsupervised continual chunking - identifying groups of co-occurring states in temporal sequences - by proposing sheaf regularization to stabilize chunking dynamics in Decentralized SyncMap. The method introduces a radial sheaf structure that penalizes distance-dependent radial motion between variable pairs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The fundamental challenge in unsupervised continual chunking (relevant to both machine learning and neuroscience) is forming accurate chunks while maintaining their stability over time. Self-organizing systems like Decentralized SyncMap face local inconsistencies that destabilize chunking dynamics.

Method: The proposed Sheaf SyncMap introduces a radial sheaf structure that penalizes distance-dependent radial motion between pairs of variables, thereby reducing local inconsistencies in Decentralized SyncMap and stabilizing its chunking dynamics.

Result: Sheaf SyncMap achieves the highest normalized mutual information (NMI) among evaluated SyncMap variants on 12 of 18 probabilistic Continual General Chunking Problem (CGCP) graphs with two-state memory and on 17 of 18 graphs with dynamic memory. In sequential adaptation experiments, it maintains high NMI after distribution shifts.

Conclusion: Sheaf SyncMap can adapt to new knowledge while avoiding the negative transfer commonly observed in modern machine learning systems such as neural networks, demonstrating both accuracy and stability in continual learning scenarios.

Abstract: Unsupervised Continual chunking is a fundamental problem in machine learning and neuroscience, where the goal is to identify groups of states that frequently co-occur in temporal sequences. A key challenge is to form accurate chunks while maintaining their stability over time. In this work, we propose sheaf regularization to reduce local inconsistencies in Decentralized SyncMap, a self-organizing system, and thereby stabilize its chunking dynamics. We introduce a radial sheaf structure that penalizes distance-dependent radial motion between pairs of variables. Experimental results show that the proposed method achieves the highest normalized mutual information (NMI) among the evaluated SyncMap variants on 12 of 18 probabilistic Continual General Chunking Problem (CGCP) graphs with two-state memory and on 17 of 18 graphs with dynamic memory. In the sequential adaptation experiment, Sheaf SyncMap also achieves high NMI after shifts in the input distribution, indicating that it can adapt to new knowledge while avoiding the negative transfer commonly observed in modern machine learning systems such as neural networks.

</details>


### [97] [Brain-Inspired Hierarchical Modularity for General Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25146)
*Hongwei Yan,Kanglei Zhou,Qi Cheng,Weiyi Dong,Chunyan Lan,Guanglong Sun,Jun Zhou,Qian Li,Yi Zhong,Liyuan Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A paper proposes a hierarchical modular approach for continual learning under online, uncertain data streams, inspired by the Drosophila learning and memory system. The method combines expert specialization with ensemble integration using lightweight modular adaptation of pretrained foundation models, brain-inspired random expansion for expert routing, and diversified modular integration across spatial and temporal scales.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional continual learning assumes offline task-wise training with clear task boundaries, but real-world intelligent systems must operate under online, uncertain, and evolving data streams. These systems must reduce interference from conflicting experience while integrating compatible experience for generalization. The Drosophila learning and memory system provides a biological blueprint for solving this dual challenge through hierarchical modularity.

Method: The method implements a hierarchical modular principle consisting of: (1) Lightweight modular adaptation of pretrained foundation models; (2) Brain-inspired random expansion for expert routing; (3) Diversified modular integration across spatial and temporal scales. This coordinates expert specialization (to reduce interference) with ensemble integration (to promote generalization).

Result: The method was evaluated across four domains: visual recognition, vision-language understanding, ego-exo video understanding, and embodied vision-language-action learning. It consistently improves learning under online and uncertain data streams. In embodied manipulation tasks, the method achieved gains exceeding 50 percentage points over replay-free alternatives.

Conclusion: Hierarchical modularity represents a biologically grounded approach for learning from dynamic experience. The approach successfully bridges the gap between conventional offline continual learning and general continual learning under real-world online data streams, demonstrating that brain-inspired modular architectures can effectively handle the challenges of continual learning in uncertain, evolving environments.

Abstract: Continual learning, the ability to learn from sequential experience while retaining and adapting prior knowledge, is central to intelligent systems operating in changing environments. However, conventional continual learning is typically studied with offline task-wise training and clear task boundaries, leaving a substantial gap from general continual learning under online, uncertain, and evolving data streams. In this regime, intelligent systems must separate conflicting experience to reduce interference while integrating compatible experience to promote generalization. Inspired by the organization of the Drosophila learning and memory system, we identify a hierarchical modular principle that coordinates both functions through expert specialization and ensemble integration. We instantiate this principle as lightweight modular adaptation of pretrained foundation models, combining brain-inspired random expansion for expert routing and diversified modular integration across spatial and temporal scales. Across visual recognition, vision-language understanding, ego-exo video understanding, and embodied vision-language-action learning, our method consistently improves learning under online and uncertain data streams, with gains exceeding 50 percentage points over replay-free alternatives in embodied manipulation. These findings support hierarchical modularity as a biologically grounded path for learning from dynamic experience.

</details>


### [98] [Dual-GNN Multilevel Coarsening for Maximum Independent Set](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25149)
*Tianfeng Chen,Xianyue Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes Graph Edge Sparsification (GES), a learning-based approach for sparsifying Euclidean TSP instances by adaptively generating instance-specific sparsification graphs that incorporate geometric structural information and combinatorial optimization technology.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large-scale TSP instances are computationally expensive to solve exactly. Traditional graph sparsification methods rely on fixed heuristics and fail to exploit instance-specific structural information, limiting their effectiveness and adaptability.

Method: The proposed GES method incorporates geometric structural information and combinatorial optimization technology to adaptively generate sparsification graphs tailored to different TSP instances, significantly reducing graph size while preserving solution quality.

Result: On the MATILDA dataset, the method can prune up to 95% of edges while keeping solution gap within 1% of optimal. On TSPLIB benchmark, strong generalization capability is demonstrated. In some large-scale instances, pruning rate exceeds 99% while optimality gap remains below 1%.

Conclusion: The GES approach effectively reduces graph size and accelerates TSP solving while maintaining near-optimal solution quality, with strong generalization capabilities across different benchmarks.

Abstract: Solving large-scale instances of the Traveling Salesman Problem (TSP) exactly is computationally expensive. Researchers often employ graph sparsification methods to improve computational efficiency. Traditional sparsification methods typically rely on fixed heuristics and fail to fully exploit instance-specific structural information. In this paper, we propose Graph Edge Sparsification (GES), a learning-based sparsification approach for Euclidean TSP. By incorporating geometric structural information and combinatorial optimization technology, our proposed method adaptively generates a sparsification graph for different instances, significantly reducing the graph size and accelerating the solving process. Experimental results demonstrate that our sparsification method can prune up to 95\% of edges on the MATILDA dataset, while keeping the solution gap within 1\% of the optimal value. Moreover, our approach exhibits strong generalization capability on the TSPLIB benchmark.In some large-scale instances, the pruning rate exceeds 99\%, while the optimality gap remains below 1\%.

</details>


### [99] [Exposing Blind Spots in Deep Imbalanced Regression Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25152)
*Noah C. Puetz,Jens U. Brandt,Marc Hilbert,Elena Raponi,Thomas Bäck,Thomas Bartz-Beielstein*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses three evaluation blind spots in Deep Imbalanced Regression (DIR): over-reliance on image-based benchmarks, incomplete evaluation protocols, and untested tail-region stability. The authors introduce a multimodal virtual sensing benchmark (MuViS), new balanced metrics (bMAE and bMASE), and reveal that tail regions exhibit high sensitivity to random seeds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current DIR evaluation has three critical limitations: 1) dominance of image-based benchmarks, 2) diagnostic but not decision-complete many-/medium-/few-shot protocol, and 3) unknown tail-region stability across random seeds. The authors aim to provide a more comprehensive evaluation framework.

Method: 1) Introduce MuViS benchmark with 9 time-series extrinsic regression tasks across 6 physical domains; 2) Adopt balanced MAE (bMAE) and propose balanced Mean Absolute Scaled Error (bMASE) for scale-normalized decision-complete comparison; 3) Conduct repeated reevaluation of 6 representative DIR methods across multiple random seeds.

Result: 1) Standard virtual-sensing models show substantial tail degradation hidden by global MAE; 2) Existing DIR methods improve balanced performance but transfer unevenly to multimodal time-series data; 3) Tail-region instability is a hidden failure mode under current evaluation practice.

Conclusion: The findings provide a reproducible basis for future DIR research. The authors call for regression systems that capture rare target regimes as reliably as common ones, highlighting the need for more comprehensive evaluation metrics and benchmarks beyond image-based datasets.

Abstract: Deep Imbalanced Regression (DIR) addresses a common failure mode of regression models: target distributions are highly non-uniform, causing models to perform best in densely populated target regions even when reliable performance is required across the full target range. Despite rapid methodological progress, DIR evaluation remains constrained by three blind spots: it is dominated by image-based benchmarks, its standard many-/medium-/few-shot protocol is diagnostic but not decision-complete, and tail-region stability across random seeds has not been systematically evaluated. We revisit DIR evaluation along these three axes. First, we broaden the data domain by evaluating DIR on a multimodal virtual sensing benchmark (\textsc{MuViS}) with nine time-series extrinsic regression tasks across six physical domains, where rare target values often correspond to operationally meaningful regimes. Second, we adopt balanced MAE (\emph{bMAE}) and introduce balanced Mean Absolute Scaled Error (\emph{bMASE}), a scale-normalized metric for decision-complete comparison across methods and datasets. Third, through a repeated reevaluation of six representative DIR methods across multiple random seeds, we show that the tail regions targeted by DIR exhibit particularly high sensitivity to seed-level variability. Our results show that standard virtual-sensing models exhibit substantial tail degradation hidden by global MAE, that existing DIR methods can improve balanced performance but transfer unevenly to multimodal time-series data, and that tail-region instability remains a largely hidden failure mode under current DIR evaluation practice. Together, these findings and our publicly available code provide a reproducible basis for future DIR research toward regression systems that capture rare target regimes as reliably as common ones.

</details>


### [100] [Learning Neural Feedback Linearization for Data-driven Systems via Augmented Lagrangian](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25163)
*Lakshmi Priya P. K.,Andreas Schwung*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A data-driven framework for feedback linearization control that uses neural networks to learn Lie derivatives, with stability guarantees under bounded identification errors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Feedback linearization is a powerful control technique but traditionally requires exact model knowledge. The paper aims to develop a fully data-driven approach that can replace conventional model-based controllers while maintaining stability guarantees.

Method: The framework explicitly incorporates relative degree based conditions into the learning process. Neural networks are used to learn the Lie derivatives (the core components of feedback linearization), effectively replacing conventional feedback controller components with neural Lie derivatives.

Result: The paper establishes practical closed-loop stability by deriving sufficient conditions under which bounded identification errors lead to bounded tracking errors. The theoretical results are validated through application to an armature controlled DC motor.

Conclusion: The proposed framework enables a fully data-driven feedback linearization approach with theoretical stability guarantees, successfully demonstrated on a DC motor system.

Abstract: The paper proposes a novel data-driven framework for designing and training a feedback linearizing controller by explicitly incorporating relative degree based conditions into the learning process. This enables the conventional feedback controller components to be replaced by neural Lie derivatives, thereby facilitating a fully data-driven feedback linearization framework. Furthermore, practical closed-loop stability is established by deriving sufficient conditions under which bounded identification errors lead to bounded tracking errors. The derived theoretical results are validated through their application to an armature controlled DC motor.

</details>


### [101] [Mitigating Sequential Reappearance in Diffusion Data-Point Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25166)
*Donghyun Kim,Taehyuk Lee,Jinyeong Kim,Youngmin Oh,Dohyeong Kim,Jaehyuk Ryu,Sangwoo Hong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper identifies a failure mode called "sequential reappearance" in diffusion model unlearning, where deleted instances temporarily appear forgotten but later return to memorized state without reuse of deleted data. The authors propose a target-level evaluation protocol to track forgetting dynamics and discover that reappearing targets exhibit sharper local denoising-loss geometry.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluation of diffusion data-point unlearning only checks immediate deletion, but doesn't account for subsequent deletion requests that may cause previously forgotten instances to reappear. This gap motivates the investigation of sequential reappearance phenomenon.

Method: The authors introduce a target-level evaluation protocol that tracks whether each target instance is: (1) forgotten immediately after deletion, (2) remains forgotten at the end of a deletion sequence, or (3) reappears during subsequent deletions. They also analyze the local denoising-loss geometry of targets.

Result: The study identifies sequential reappearance as a common failure mode where instances initially judged forgotten later return to memorized regime without reuse of deleted data or adversarial fine-tuning. Targets that reappear exhibit sharper local denoising-loss geometry compared to those that remain forgotten.

Conclusion: The findings suggest that single-timepoint evaluation is insufficient for assessing unlearning in diffusion models. The sharper loss geometry of reappearing targets provides a potential diagnostic indicator for predicting which deleted instances are at risk of reappearing.

Abstract: Diffusion data-point unlearning is typically evaluated immediately after each deletion, even though subsequent requests may repeatedly update the same model. We identify sequential reappearance, a failure mode in which an instance that is initially judged to be forgotten later returns to the memorized regime without reuse of the deleted data or adversarial fine-tuning. To capture this behavior, we introduce a target-level evaluation protocol that tracks whether each target is forgotten immediately, remains forgotten at the end of the sequence, or reappears during subsequent deletions. We further find that targets that later reappear exhibit sharper local denoising-loss geometry after deletion than targets that remain forgotten.

</details>


### [102] [Multi-Term Fourier Graph Neural Network with Sample Relationship Learning for Enhanced Remaining Useful Life Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25179)
*Ya Song,Laurens Bliek,Yaoxin Wu,Yingqian Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes MTFGN-SRL, a novel framework for Remaining Useful Life (RUL) prediction that addresses limitations of Spatio-Temporal Graph Neural Networks. The approach uses Fourier Graph Neural Networks in the frequency domain, multiple lookback windows for multi-term learning, and a heterogeneous GNN for sample relationship learning. It demonstrates superior performance on the CMAPSS dataset compared to state-of-the-art methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current ST-GNNs for RUL prediction have three key drawbacks: (1) requiring domain expertise or high computational cost to establish graph structures, (2) being restricted to fixed-size lookback windows which fails to handle varying time series lengths, and (3) missing inherent relationships between samples from adjacent time windows that could improve accuracy and robustness.

Method: The proposed MTFGN-SRL framework has three main components: (1) Instead of treating time series as a sequence of graphs, it represents each sample as a single complete graph and uses Fourier Graph Neural Network (FGN) to capture spatio-temporal information in the frequency domain; (2) A multi-term learning module utilizes multiple lookback windows to generate samples with varying terms, which are fed into the FGN to extract information from different temporal scales; (3) A sample relationship learning module trains a heterogeneous GNN to identify inter-sample relationships.

Result: Evaluations on the CMAPSS dataset demonstrate that MTFGN-SRL achieves superior performance over state-of-the-art methods in RUL prediction.

Conclusion: The MTFGN-SRL framework effectively addresses the limitations of conventional ST-GNNs by leveraging frequency domain analysis, multi-term temporal learning, and inter-sample relationship modeling, resulting in improved RUL prediction accuracy and robustness.

Abstract: Predicting the remaining useful life (RUL) is essential for effective predictive maintenance. Spatio-Temporal Graph Neural Networks (ST-GNNs), which can model both temporal and spatial relationships by representing time series data as a sequence of graphs, have shown exceptional performance in RUL prediction. However, current ST-GNNs face several drawbacks. First, they require domain expertise or significant computational power to establish graph structures prior to deploying GNNs. Second, the models are restricted to capture temporal dependencies within a predefined fixed-size lookback window. This restriction ignores the common issue of varying time series lengths, leading the prediction model to miss short-term or long-term dependencies. Finally, conventional models often fail to capture the inherent relationships between samples generated from adjacent time windows, which are crucial for improving both the accuracy and robustness of predictions. To address the aforementioned issues, we introduce a novel framework called Multi-Term Fourier Graph Neural Network with Sample Relationship Learning (MTFGN-SRL). Rather than treating the sample as a sequence of graphs, we consider it as a single complete graph and utilize a Fourier Graph Neural Network (FGN) to capture the spatio-temporal information in the frequency domain. We propose a multi-term learning module that utilizes multiple lookback windows to generate samples with varying terms, which are then fed into the FGN to enhance the extraction of useful information from the data. Finally, we develop a sample relationship learning module by training a heterogeneous GNN to identify inter-sample relationships, resulting in enhanced accuracy and robustness in predictions. Evaluations on the CMAPSS dataset demonstrate MTFGN-SRL's superior performance over state-of-the-art methods in RUL prediction.

</details>


### [103] [Trains but Doesn't Learn: A Post-Training Delivery Benchmark for LLM Agents as Forward-Deployed Engineers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25237)
*Weihang Ding,Junfei Zhan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Post-training is becoming a service (PTaaS): a customer hands an operator data and a goal, and a forward-deployed engineer (FDE) returns a fine-tuned, evaluated, and deployed model under a budget, a human-approval gate, and reproducibility requirements. Seating an LLM agent in the FDE seat raises a question existing benchmarks cannot answer: not whether an agent can raise a metric, but whether it can be trusted to deliver. We answer it on a governed delivery plane, where an agent drives ten stages and an oracle scores each stage from platform-recorded facts. The central silent failure is the run that trains but does not learn (TBDL): loss falls, every signal stays green, and the delivered model is no better than the base. An operator-run acceptance gate catches every such run before payment, and a detector calibrated on known-corrupted runs flags severe corruption mid-run. We ran four frontier agents (Claude Opus 5, GPT-5.6-luna, Gemini 3.7 Flash, DeepSeek V4-Pro) end to end on metered L40S, A100, and H200 GPUs across 8B to 70B open bases, certifying every scenario before scoring. We also ran a human FDE arm under the same oracle and compare every agent against it.

</details>


### [104] [Correcting Within-Group Self-Selection Bias in Prioritized Replay](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25297)
*Oscar Miró López-Feliu,Herke van Hoof*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses a flaw in Prioritized Experience Replay (PER) where replaying high-priority transitions distorts outcome frequencies within the same state-action pair. The authors propose three "sibling-aware" replay methods (SAMPLE, AVG, MODEL) to recover empirical outcome frequencies while maintaining PER's focus on high-priority regions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: PER improves sample efficiency by replaying transitions with high temporal-difference error, but in stochastic environments, this creates "within-group self-selection" - where transitions with the same state-action pair but different outcomes are not represented proportionally, distorting learning.

Method: The authors decompose PER into between-group allocation and conditional sibling selection. They derive three fixed-buffer corrections: (1) SAMPLE - selects group via PER but trains on uniformly sampled sibling; (2) AVG - averages sibling Bellman targets; (3) MODEL - samples from empirical full-outcome model. Experiments use exact state-action environments with rare high-magnitude outcomes and MinAtar with VQ-VAE approximate groupings.

Result: In exact state-action environments with rare high-magnitude outcomes, sibling-aware replay improves learning efficiency over PER, though tuning can narrow gaps. In MinAtar, approximate VQ-VAE groups with SAMPLE mitigate degradation under mean-preserving reward tails in 4 of 5 games.

Conclusion: Sibling-aware replay retains PER's focus on high-priority state-action regions while recovering their empirical outcome frequencies, improving learning in stochastic environments with skewed reward distributions.

Abstract: Prioritized experience replay (PER) improves sample efficiency by replaying high-priority transitions, usually according to absolute temporal-difference error. In stochastic environments, PER can distort the distribution of realized outcomes replayed from transitions with the same state-action pair. We call this within-group self-selection. We quantify the resulting changes in within-group outcome frequencies and mean Bellman targets. We decompose PER into between-group allocation and conditional sibling selection, and derive fixed-buffer corrections that preserve current group-level priority mass: SAMPLE selects a group through PER and trains on a uniformly sampled sibling; AVG averages sibling Bellman targets; and MODEL samples from an empirical full-outcome model. In exact state-action environments with rare high-magnitude outcomes, sibling-aware replay improves learning efficiency over PER, although matched parameter sweeps show that tuning can narrow some gaps. In MinAtar, approximate VQ-VAE groups with SAMPLE mitigate degradation under mean-preserving reward tails in four of five games. Sibling-aware replay thus retains the focus on high-priority state-action regions while recovering their empirical outcome frequencies.

</details>


### [105] [Topological Signal Processing With Unoriented Operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25310)
*Andrea Cavallo,Varun Sarathchandran,Geert Leus,Elvin Isufi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces an Unoriented Topological Signal Processing (UTSP) framework that addresses the limitation of standard TSP, which relies on oriented boundary operators unsuitable for unoriented higher-order signals. The UTSP framework replaces oriented operators with unoriented incidence matrices and introduces an "interaction-order decomposition" to quantify signal contributions across different interaction orders, enabling order-aware regularizers for signal reconstruction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard Topological Signal Processing (TSP) uses oriented boundary operators, which is appropriate for flow signals or when topological invariants matter. However, many higher-order signals in real-world applications carry no orientation information. Applying oriented operators to unoriented signals is problematic because it introduces arbitrary choices of simplex orientation that have no physical meaning for such signals.

Method: The paper proposes three main contributions: (1) Demonstrating that unoriented incidence and Laplacian matrices between arbitrary simplicial levels admit graph-like spectral properties; (2) Introducing an "interaction-order decomposition" as an unoriented counterpart to the Hodge decomposition, which quantifies how much of a higher-order signal is explained by aggregating lower-order signals; (3) Using this decomposition to derive regularizers for signal reconstruction that penalize each interaction order separately.

Result: Experiments on real-world data demonstrate that the order-aware regularizers outperform oriented baselines. The largest performance gains occur when the signal energy is unevenly distributed across different interaction orders, confirming the importance of handling unoriented signals appropriately.

Conclusion: The UTSP framework provides a theoretically grounded approach for processing higher-order signals without inherent orientation. By properly accounting for interaction orders through the decomposition-based regularizers, this work enables more accurate signal reconstruction in applications where orientation is meaningless or undefined.

Abstract: Topological signal processing (TSP) processes signals on simplicial complexes with oriented boundary operators, which is the natural choice for flow signals or when the topological invariants play a role for the task at hand. However, many higher-order signals carry no orientation, and applying oriented operators to them is not well-defined since it introduces an arbitrary choice of simplex orientation. We study an unoriented TSP (UTSP) framework that replaces oriented boundaries with unoriented incidence matrices. First, we show that unoriented incidence and Laplacian matrices between arbitrary simplicial levels admit graph-like spectral properties. Second, since dropping orientation removes the Hodge decomposition, we introduce an unoriented counterpart, termed interaction-order decomposition, which quantifies how much of a higher-order signal is explained by aggregating lower-order signals. Third, we use this decomposition to derive regularizers for signal reconstruction that penalize each interaction order separately. Experiments on real-world data show that the order-aware regularizers outperform oriented baselines, with the largest gains when the signal energy is unevenly distributed across orders.

</details>


### [106] [Spatiotemporal Kronecker Covariance Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25326)
*Andrea Cavallo,Athanasios Georgoutsos,Elvin Isufi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: KVNN is a temporal graph neural network that represents spatiotemporal covariance matrices as sums of Kronecker products, decoupling spatial and temporal dependencies to address limitations of ST-PCA including linear operations, estimation errors, and ignored cross-time-step correlations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spatiotemporal multivariate time series contain complex patterns, but existing covariance-based tools like ST-PCA are limited to linear operations and prone to estimation errors with limited data. Neural network alternatives ignore correlations across different time steps.

Method: KVNN represents the spatiotemporal covariance matrix via a sum of Kronecker products where spatial and temporal dependencies are decoupled. It implements filtering operations on spatial and temporal components to achieve expressive processing with rigorous spectral analysis.

Result: On five real-world datasets, KVNNs achieve strong forecasting performance, often requiring significantly fewer trainable parameters than competitive methods, and remain consistent under estimation noise.

Conclusion: KVNN addresses all limitations of ST-PCA: it provides non-linear processing, stability to finite-sample estimation errors, and captures cross-time-step correlations while maintaining computational efficiency and theoretical guarantees.

Abstract: Multivariate time series contain complex patterns that span across both space and time. While covariance-based statistical tools like spatiotemporal Principal Component Analysis (ST-PCA) help identify these patterns, they are limited to linear operations and prone to estimation errors with limited data. Recent covariance-based spatiotemporal neural networks offer more stable, non-linear alternatives, but they ignore correlations across different time steps. To solve this, we introduce the Kronecker coVariance Neural Network (KVNN), a temporal graph neural network that represents the spatiotemporal covariance matrix via a sum of Kronecker products where spatial and temporal dependencies are decoupled. By implementing filtering operations on spatial and temporal components, KVNNs achieve expressive processing capabilities, admit a rigorous spectral analysis, and are provably stable to finite-sample estimation errors, ultimately addressing all of ST-PCA's limitations. We show on five real-world datasets that KVNNs achieve strong forecasting performance, often requiring significantly fewer trainable parameters than competitive methods, and are consistent under estimation noise.

</details>


### [107] [MT-ProtBERT: Multi-task Learning ProtBERT for Intrinsically Disordered Proteins Classification with Scarce Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25334)
*Jian Sun,Kingshuk Ghosh,Lilianna Houston,Mohammad H. Mahoor*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MT-ProtBERT is a multi-task extension of ProtBERT designed for low-data regimes to address the challenge of studying intrinsically disordered proteins (IDPs), which lack stable 3D conformations and have low sequence similarity, making them difficult to classify and detect using traditional methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: IDPs are dynamic proteins without stable 3D structures and have low sequence similarity between similar proteins. Their conformational heterogeneity limits traditional experimental methods, resulting in data scarcity and difficulty in classifying/detecting similar or dissimilar IDPs - a task important for understanding biology and evolution.

Method: MT-ProtBERT integrates three key components: (1) Dynamic Window Masking, (2) Multi-Scale 1D Convolutional classifier (MS-Conv1D), and (3) auxiliary objectives that jointly optimize masked language modeling and biochemistry-informed tasks. The framework was evaluated on two tasks: (i) phosphorylation site prediction (S/T/Y) in short sequences with small datasets, and (ii) protein compaction prediction on two small datasets (684 and 530 sequences) including sequences comparable in length to typical disordered regions.

Result: MT-ProtBERT consistently outperforms PARROT, an RNN-based IDP-specific model, across all tasks including phosphorylation site prediction and protein compaction prediction.

Conclusion: Combining self-supervised and biochemistry-informed tasks, along with multi-scale learning, enables robust modeling of unstructured proteins under data scarcity. This approach successfully addresses the challenge of studying IDPs when limited training data is available.

Abstract: Intrinsically disordered proteins (IDPs) differ from folded proteins in that they are dynamic, lack a stable three-dimensional conformation, and have low sequence similarity between similar proteins. The conformational heterogeneity of IDPs - while beneficial for their diverse functions - limits the use of traditional experimental tools to determine their conformation. The experimental difficulty, along with low sequence similarity, results in data scarcity, and makes it difficult to classify/detect IDPs that are similar or dissimilar, a task relevant to understand biology and evolution. We address this challenge using Multi-task ProtBERT (MT-ProtBERT), a multi-task extension of ProtBERT tailored for low-data regimes. MT-ProtBERT integrates Dynamic Window Masking, a Multi-Scale 1D Convolutional classifier (MS-Conv1D), and auxiliary objectives that jointly optimize masked language modeling and biochemistry-informed tasks. We evaluate this framework on two tasks under limited data: (i) phosphorylation site prediction (S/T/Y) in short sequences and small datasets, and (ii) protein compaction prediction on two small datasets (684 and 530 sequences), including sequences comparable in length to typical disordered regions. MT-ProtBERT consistently outperforms PARROT, an RNN-based IDP-specific model, across all tasks. These results demonstrate that combining self-supervised and biochemistry-informed tasks, and multi-scale learning enables robust modeling of unstructured proteins under data scarcity.

</details>


### [108] [Concept Drift from a Causal Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25340)
*Eduardo V. L. Barboza,Jean Paul Barddal,Robert Sabourin,Rafael M. O. Cruz*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces a causal framework for understanding and simulating concept drift in data streams using Structural Causal Models (SCMs). It proposes a taxonomy of drift types based on causal origin and develops a data stream generator to simulate mechanism-level drift events, enabling more realistic evaluation of adaptive learning methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing concept drift definitions characterize changes in the joint distribution P(x,y) without distinguishing which component of the data-generating process changed. This limits understanding of drift mechanisms and hinders proper evaluation of adaptive learning methods in non-stationary environments.

Method: 1) Propose an SCM-based causal framework for concept drift
2) Develop a taxonomy categorizing drift by causal origin (changes in exogenous variables, endogenous mechanisms, confounders, target-generating processes)
3) Build an SCM-based data stream generator for controlled mechanism-level drift simulation
4) Integrate causal discovery methods to construct real-world grounded data streams

Result: 1) Different drift types induce distinct patterns of distribution shift and predictive behavior
2) Data streams grounded in real-world dependency structures can be constructed using causal discovery
3) Leveraging the generated data improves downstream performance
4) The framework enables more realistic and informative evaluation scenarios

Conclusion: Accounting for causal structure is crucial when studying and evaluating adaptive learning methods. The proposed causal framework establishes a foundation for causally-aware evaluation in non-stationary environments, enabling more realistic simulation and better understanding of concept drift phenomena.

Abstract: Concept drift is a common phenomenon in real-world data streams, in which changes in the data-generating distribution can degrade predictive model performance. Most existing definitions characterize drift as changes in the joint distribution $P(\mathbf{x}, y)$, without distinguishing which component of the data-generating process has changed. In this work, we introduce a causal perspective on concept drift based on Structural Causal Models (SCMs). We propose a taxonomy that categorizes drift events by their causal origin, including changes in exogenous variables, endogenous mechanisms, confounders, and target-generating processes. Building on this framework, we develop an SCM-based data stream generator that simulates controlled mechanism-level drift events. Our experiments empirically characterize the distributional effects of each drift type and show that drifts with different causal origins induce distinct patterns of distribution shift and predictive behavior. Furthermore, by integrating causal discovery methods, we use our framework to construct data streams grounded in real-world dependency structures, enabling more realistic and informative evaluation scenarios. We also demonstrate that leveraging the generated data can improve downstream performance. These results highlight the importance of accounting for causal structure when studying and evaluating adaptive learning methods, and establish a foundation for causally-aware evaluation in non-stationary environments.

</details>


### [109] [Extending FunctionGemma for Practical On-Device Mobile Function Calling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25373)
*Ali Rezagholizadeh,Soheila Samiee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces MOBILEACTIONSEXTENDED, a synthetic dataset of ~9,500 conversations for Android function-calling, and fine-tunes FunctionGemma 270M-it to achieve 76.5% end-to-end accuracy, with a combined model reaching 82.3% on Google's MOBILEACTIONSGOOGLE while doubling category coverage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing function-calling resources emphasize web APIs or narrow mobile-action catalogs, lacking practical Android workflow coverage. There is a need for comprehensive datasets covering device-control categories to enable on-device assistants for real Android tasks.

Method: The authors created MOBILEACTIONSEXTENDED, a schema-validated synthetic dataset with ~9,500 conversations covering 15 device-control categories (messaging, phone calls, camera, screenshot, brightness, device status, flashlight, app management). They fine-tuned the 270M model using TRL supervised fine-tuning under completion-only loss, producing both an extended specialist and a combined model trained jointly with Google's MOBILEACTIONSGOOGLE.

Result: On MOBILEACTIONSEXTENDED, end-to-end accuracy improved from 29.3% (base model) and 17.2% (Google's Mobile-Actions variant) to 76.5%. The combined model retained 76.5% on MOBILEACTIONSEXTENDED and achieved 82.3% on MOBILEACTIONSGOOGLE, down from 90.3% of Google's specialist, representing an 8.0-percentage-point trade-off for doubled category coverage.

Conclusion: The approach demonstrates that compact local function calling is practical for low-latency and privacy-preserving mobile assistants. The combined model achieves good performance across both datasets while significantly expanding category coverage, supporting the viability of on-device function-calling models.

Abstract: On-device assistants require function-calling models that map natural language to local system actions, but existing resources emphasize web APIs or narrow mobile-action catalogs. We extend FunctionGemma 270M-it to practical Android workflows by introducing MOBILEACTIONSEXTENDED, a synthetic, schema-validated dataset of ~9,500 conversations covering fifteen device-control categories, including messaging, phone calls, camera/screenshot, brightness control, device-status queries, flashlight control, and application management. We fine-tune the 270M model with TRL supervised fine-tuning under completion-only loss, producing an extended specialist and a combined model trained jointly with Google's MOBILEACTIONSGOOGLE. On MOBILEACTIONSEXTENDED, end-to-end accuracy improves from 29.3% for the base model and 17.2% for Google's Mobile-Actions variant to 76.5%. The combined model retains 76.5% on MOBILEACTIONSEXTENDED and reaches 82.3% on MOBILEACTIONSGOOGLE, down from the 90.3% of Google's Mobile-Actions specialist, representing an 8.0-percentage-point trade-off in return for doubling category coverage. We release the dataset, fine-tuned models, reproducible training/evaluation pipeline, and an Android demo, highlighting compact local function calling as a practical path towards low-latency and privacy-preserving mobile assistants.

</details>


### [110] [Deep Reinforcement Learning on Item-Compatibility Graphs for One-Dimensional Bin Packing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25397)
*M. Aslı Aydın*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A novel end-to-end graph reinforcement learning framework for solving the 1D bin packing problem (1D-BPP) by formulating packing as a Markov decision process on an item-compatibility graph, using GNN actor-critic with stochastic beam search for zero-shot generalization to any instance size.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While deep reinforcement learning has been successfully applied to 2D and 3D bin packing problems, intelligent learned solvers for 1D-BPP remain scarce despite its practical importance in logistics, manufacturing, and cloud resource management. Existing methods either rely on traditional heuristics or require problem-specific solvers, limiting generalization.

Method: The paper proposes a graph reinforcement learning framework that: (1) Formulates the packing process as an MDP on an item-compatibility graph where actions merge partial bins; (2) Uses a graph neural network (GNN) actor-critic policy to extract relational features; (3) Decodes with stochastic beam search; (4) Enables a single trained model to generalize zero-shot to instances of any size. The study systematically evaluates graph encoders, DRL algorithms, reward functions, training distributions, and hyperparameters.

Result: The framework achieves a mean optimality gap of 2.31% on BPPLIB benchmark (vs 2.66% for constructive heuristic). It outperforms learned baselines on most of nine instance families and is more stable across distributions. On the hardest benchmark family, it beats a state-of-the-art learned solver combining column generation and integer programming. However, a grouping genetic algorithm remains ahead overall.

Conclusion: The proposed GNN-based RL framework demonstrates that learned solvers can effectively tackle 1D-BPP with zero-shot generalization capabilities, achieving competitive results especially on structured instances and the hardest benchmark family. The residual gap compared to grouping genetic algorithms identifies directions for future improvement, particularly in handling certain instance types.

Abstract: The one-dimensional bin packing problem (1D-BPP) is a classical NP-hard combinatorial optimization problem with applications ranging from logistics and manufacturing to cloud resource management. Although deep reinforcement learning (DRL) has become a competitive paradigm for data-driven optimization, most learned packing methods target 2D and 3D variants, and intelligent learned solvers for 1D-BPP remain scarce. In this paper, we present a novel end-to-end, size-agnostic graph reinforcement learning framework for 1D-BPP. We formulate the packing process as a Markov decision process on an item-compatibility graph, serving as a structural knowledge representation in which every action merges two partial bins that fit together. A graph neural network actor-critic policy extracts relational features from this representation and is trained through reinforcement learning and decoded by stochastic beam search, enabling a single trained model to generalize zero-shot to instances of any size. We conduct a systematic empirical study across graph encoders, DRL algorithms, reward functions, training distributions, and hyperparameters. Evaluated zero-shot on the full BPPLIB benchmark against a constructive heuristic, a grouping genetic algorithm, and recent learned methods, our data-driven policy lowers the mean optimality gap of the constructive heuristic from 2.66\% to 2.31\%, with the largest gains on structured instances. Against learned baselines evaluated on the same benchmark, it attains a lower gap on most of the nine families and is far more stable across instance distributions. On the hardest benchmark family, it outperforms a state-of-the-art learned solver that relies on column generation and integer programming, while using no solver at all. A grouping genetic algorithm remains ahead overall, and we analyze where and why the residual gap arises.

</details>


### [111] [Predictive Uncertainty for Neural CAE Surrogates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25430)
*Kaustubh Tangsali,Mohammad Amin Nabian,Kelvin Lee,Carmelo Gonzales,Sanjay Choudhry*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates uncertainty quantification (UQ) methods for geometry-conditioned neural surrogates in computer-aided engineering (CAE) workflows. The study compares three UQ approaches (Gaussian process, concrete MC dropout, and deep ensembles) on three industry-relevant CAE datasets for aerodynamics and crash dynamics, evaluating whether uncertainties have credible magnitudes, identify error locations, respond to unfamiliar inputs, and remain informative for derived engineering quantities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neural surrogates can accelerate CAE workflows but require meaningful uncertainty estimates across varying geometries, spatial prediction fields, and engineering quantities of interest. This work addresses the need to understand how established UQ approaches behave when adapted to geometry-conditioned neural surrogates in practical engineering applications.

Method: The authors compare three UQ approaches: (1) a closed-form Gaussian process (GP)-based method, (2) concrete Monte Carlo dropout, and (3) deep ensembles. These are evaluated on three large, industry-relevant CAE datasets: DrivAerStar (external aerodynamics), AirFRANS (external aerodynamics), and automotive crash dynamics. Evaluation criteria include: credible uncertainty magnitudes, error location identification, response to unfamiliar inputs, and informativeness for derived engineering quantities.

Result: On the DrivAerStar dataset where all three methods are compared, each method generally assigns higher uncertainty to locations with larger prediction errors. Validation-based rescaling brings interval coverage close to nominal on a disjoint in-distribution test set. Results on AirFRANS and automotive crash datasets show useful error ranking and interval estimates, but relative performance varies with dataset and evaluation criterion.

Conclusion: The relative performance of UQ methods changes depending on the dataset and evaluation criterion. Therefore, UQ methods and evaluation metrics should be selected based on the intended downstream CAE decision. No single UQ method universally outperforms others across all scenarios.

Abstract: Neural surrogates can substantially accelerate computer-aided engineering (CAE) workflows, but their use in design requires uncertainty estimates that remain meaningful across varying geometries, spatial prediction fields, and engineering quantities of interest. We investigate how established uncertainty quantification (UQ) approaches behave when adapted to geometry-conditioned neural surrogates. We compare one closed-form and two sampling-based approaches-a Gaussian process (GP)-based method, concrete Monte Carlo (MC) dropout, and deep ensembles-and evaluate them on three large, industry-relevant CAE datasets for external aerodynamics and crash dynamics.
  We examine whether predicted uncertainties have credible magnitudes, identify locations with larger prediction errors, respond to unfamiliar inputs, and remain informative for derived engineering quantities. On the DrivAerStar dataset, where all three methods are compared, each generally assigns higher uncertainty to locations with larger prediction errors, and validation-based rescaling brings interval coverage close to nominal on a disjoint in-distribution test set. Results on AirFRANS and automotive crash also show useful error ranking and interval estimates, but the relative performance of the methods changes with the dataset and evaluation criterion. UQ methods and evaluation metrics should therefore be selected based on the intended downstream CAE decision.

</details>


### [112] [Lightweight Ranking Heads: Accelerating Multi-Task Experimentation in Production Recommender Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25433)
*Sanjay Surendranath Girija,Aniruddh Nath,Li Wei,Yanhao Jiang,Shawn Andrews,Lukasz Heldt,Yi Wu,Aditya Mahajan,Mohit Sharma*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Light Heads is a framework for dynamically injecting new ranking tasks into existing multi-task recommender systems without retraining backbone models, enabling rapid A/B experimentation at YouTube scale.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Production-scale recommender systems face bottlenecks when introducing new prediction tasks: risk of negative task conflicts with existing tasks, expensive retraining of backbone and downstream models, and long development cycles (weeks) for experimentation.

Method: The Light Heads framework uses stop-gradients and stateless daily training to strictly isolate new tasks, preventing adverse task conflicts. A centralized configuration allows Light Heads to be added to multiple models simultaneously, enabling faster training data generation and co-training of downstream models.

Result: Successfully deployed at YouTube scale, Light Heads reduces the multi-task experimentation iteration cycle from several weeks to days, enabling rapid A/B experimentation and deployment of new ranking tasks with measurable production value.

Conclusion: Light Heads provides a practical solution for continuous online learning environments, allowing dynamic task injection without cold-starting or retraining backbone models, significantly accelerating experimentation velocity in large-scale recommender systems.

Abstract: Modern production-scale recommender systems rely on complex, multi-task ranking models. Introducing new prediction tasks into these massive systems often causes bottlenecks - it risks negative task conflicts with existing tasks, and can lead to long development and experimentation cycles due to the expensive retraining of backbone models and downstream models or tuning of reward combination formulas. To address the critical challenge of slow experimentation velocity, we introduce the Lightweight Ranking Heads (Light Heads) framework. Designed for continuous online learning environments, Light Heads enable the dynamic injection of new tasks into existing multi-task ranking models, effectively obviating the need for model cold-starting and retraining of backbone models. By utilizing stop-gradients and stateless daily training, this design strictly isolates new tasks, mitigating the risk of adverse task conflicts. Crucially, this framework uses a centralized configuration that allows Light Heads to be added to multiple models simultaneously, unblocking faster training data generation and co-training of downstream models. Successfully deployed at YouTube scale, this approach reduces the iteration cycle for multi-task experimentation from several weeks to days. In this paper, we detail the system architecture, analyze the training dynamics of stateless cold-started heads, compare their performance to full heads, and demonstrate how Light Heads have enabled the rapid A/B experimentation and deployment of new ranking tasks that yield measurable production value.

</details>


### [113] [PermuFormer: Multi-Task Pretraining for Permutation Representation in Algebraic Combinatorics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25438)
*Henry Kvinge*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PermuFormer is an autoregressive transformer pretrained on a 2.8 billion token corpus for permutation-focused tasks in algebraic combinatorics. It serves as an effective starting point for fine-tuning on both basic and complex research-level tasks, outperforming models trained from scratch, MLPs, and fine-tuned generic language models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors aim to address the limitation of specialized mathematical models that are typically trained from scratch, which may restrict their ability to develop domain-aware representations. They argue that diverse pretraining can help learn reusable, domain-aware representations for mathematical tasks.

Method: The authors introduce PermuFormer, an autoregressive transformer pretrained on a 2.8 billion token multi-task, multi-encoding corpus focused on permutation tasks in algebraic combinatorics. They evaluate its effectiveness as a pretraining foundation for downstream tasks.

Result: PermuFormer outperforms the same architecture trained from scratch, baseline MLPs, and a fine-tuned generic language model of comparable size when fine-tuned on both basic tasks unseen during pretraining and more complex research-level tasks.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates that diverse pretraining on mathematical objects (permutations) enables learning domain-aware representations that transfer effectively to downstream tasks, even for complex research-level problems. The analysis also reveals that different tasks require different decoding strategies - some can be linearly decoded from internal representations while others need multiple generation rounds.

Abstract: Diverse pretraining has been shown to be an effective method for learning reusable, domain-aware representations that provide a starting point for fine-tuning on downstream tasks. While much of the excitement in AI for math has been concentrated in the use of frontier reasoning models to solve well-specified problems through the medium of language, narrow, specialized models remain an important component of the AI for math ecosystem. In contrast to large language models, specialized models are usually trained directly on the mathematical objects themselves (e.g., graphs, sequences of numbers) rather than the textual descriptions that characterize these objects. However, the common practice of training specialists from scratch may limit their ability to develop domain-aware representations that capture the multifaceted nature of mathematics. In this paper, we describe an approach to pretraining for permutation-focused tasks in algebraic combinatorics. We introduce PermuFormer, an autoregressive transformer trained on a 2.8 billion token multi-task, multi-encoding corpus. We show that PermuFormer is an effective starting point for fine-tuning on basic tasks unseen during pretraining and more complex research-level tasks, frequently outperforming the same architecture trained from scratch, baseline MLPs, and a fine-tuned generic language model of comparable size. We also analyze some of the internal mechanisms by which PermuFormer learns to solve training tasks. For example, we show that while some tasks can be linearly decoded directly from the internal representation of the prompt, other tasks require multiple rounds of generation before the answer can be decoded.

</details>


### [114] [Mean Velocity Matching: Rethinking Generative Dynamics in Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25444)
*Yunhong Zhang,Changjie Cao,Zhihua Zhang,Bingli Liu,Zongjie Cao,Zongyong Cui,Ying Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces Mean Velocity Matching (MVM), a novel parameterization method for stochastic generative dynamics in diffusion models that enables a single learned velocity field to directly parameterize stochastic reverse processes without requiring separate score estimation or reconstruction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing velocity-based generative models provide simplicity through single transport fields but are deterministic. Stochastic extensions typically require additional score information or velocity-to-score reconstruction, creating complexity. This work aims to retain single-field prediction while directly supporting stochastic reverse dynamics.

Method: MVM constructs a Gaussian perturbation process where the conditional expectation of restoration-oriented velocity (x₀-xₜ)/t directly forms the reverse-SDE drift. To address unbounded regression near t=0, a √t-scaled parameterization is introduced to preserve reverse dynamics while yielding a bounded training target. The same learned field also induces a deterministic probability-flow ODE, enabling unified study of stochastic and deterministic sampling.

Result: Experiments with Transformer-based models achieve FID of 0.89 at 10 NFE on ImageNet 32×32 and FID of 1.78 at 50 NFE on ImageNet 256×256. Controlled SDE-ODE comparisons show the ODE performs better under very low NFE, while the stochastic reverse process achieves lower FID when sufficient function evaluations are available.

Conclusion: MVM provides a direct single-field parameterization of stochastic reverse dynamics while maintaining competitive generation quality, enabling both stochastic and deterministic sampling within a unified formulation.

Abstract: This work studies prediction parameterization for stochastic generative dynamics in diffusion models. Existing velocity-based generative models provide the simplicity of learning a single transport field, but their standard formulation is deterministic, whereas stochastic extensions generally require additional score information or an intermediate velocity-to-score reconstruction. To retain single-field prediction while directly supporting stochastic reverse dynamics, this paper introduces Mean Velocity Matching (MVM). MVM constructs a Gaussian perturbation process for which the conditional expectation of a restoration-oriented velocity, $(x_0-x_t)/t$, directly forms the reverse-SDE drift. Consequently, a single learned field is sufficient to parameterize the stochastic reverse process without separately estimating or reconstructing the score. Because direct regression of this velocity becomes unbounded near $t=0$, MVM further introduces a $\sqrt{t}$-scaled parameterization that preserves the reverse dynamics while yielding a bounded training target. The same learned field also induces a deterministic probability-flow ODE, enabling stochastic and deterministic sampling to be studied within a unified formulation. Experiments with Transformer-based generative models achieve an FID of $\MVMImageNetThirtyTwoFID$ at \MVMImageNetThirtyTwoNFE\ NFE on ImageNet $32\times32$ and $\MVMImageNetTwoFiftySixFID$ at \MVMImageNetTwoFiftySixNFE\ NFE on ImageNet $256\times256$. Controlled SDE--ODE comparisons further show that the ODE performs better under very low NFE, whereas the stochastic reverse process achieves lower FID when sufficient function evaluations are available. These results demonstrate that MVM provides a direct single-field parameterization of stochastic reverse dynamics while maintaining competitive generation quality.

</details>


### [115] [A Practical Recipe for Semi-Supervised Federated ASR: Online Pseudo-Labels with Server Update Stabilization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25471)
*Wonho Bae,Zakaria Aldeneh,Martin Pelikan,Jan "Honza" Silovsky,Tatiana Likhomanenko,Sheikh Shams Azam*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Semi-supervised federated learning (SSFL) for ASR uses teacher models to generate pseudo-labels for unlabeled client data. The paper identifies two critical design axes: the teacher (which model generates pseudo-labels) and the anchor (server-side updates on labeled data). Key findings show that per-client online teachers, once stabilized, match or beat global teachers, and that server-side training between rounds is essential to prevent teacher drift. The approach improves over prior methods on 9 of 11 pairs, with 20.8% in-domain and 10.0% cross-domain improvements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: SSFL for ASR is challenging because pseudo-label errors compound across output sequences and training rounds, leading to divergence. This creates a large gap compared to fully-supervised federated learning. The authors investigate how to close this gap through coupled design choices.

Method: The study explores two design axes: (1) Teacher selection - comparing per-client online teacher (each client's evolving model), broadcast global teacher (server model fixed within round), and transitioning teacher (global→online at round r); (2) Anchor design - server-side training on labeled data between rounds to stabilize training. The authors also examine the impact of data augmentation and batch size on stabilization.

Result: The per-client online teacher, once stabilized, matches or beats the broadcast global teacher - decisively in-domain and competitively under domain shift. The transitioning teacher matches or beats both as the seed grows stronger. Server-side training between rounds is critical - more important than the seed model itself. The two axes are inseparable: aggressive teacher choices only pay off once the anchor stabilizes training. The approach improves over the strongest prior method on 9 of 11 pairs, with 20.8% average improvement in-domain and 10.0% cross-domain, narrowing the gap to fully-supervised FL.

Conclusion: Successful SSFL for ASR requires coupled design of teacher and anchor mechanisms. The online teacher becomes advantageous when stabilized by consistent server-side training. Data augmentation and batch size settings are critical as they control input and gradient noise. The degree of stabilization needed is domain-dependent, governed by seed data dispersion and overlap with client data. This work provides practical guidelines for implementing SSFL in ASR training.

Abstract: Semi-supervised federated learning (SSFL) trains models on clients' unlabeled data using a teacher to generate pseudo-labels, with a small labeled seed dataset on the server. Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) is particularly fragile here: pseudo-label errors compound across the output sequence and across training rounds into divergence, leaving a large gap to fully-supervised FL. We show that closing this gap turns on two coupled design axes -- the teacher (which model generates the pseudo-labels) and the anchor (the server-side updates on labeled data that stabilize training). On the teacher axis, a per-client online teacher (each client's own evolving model) diverges on its own, but once stabilized it matches or beats the broadcast global teacher (one server model, fixed within a round) -- decisively in-domain and competitively under domain shift. As the seed grows stronger and the online teacher's advantage narrows, a transitioning teacher (global $\rightarrow$ online at round $r$) matches or beats both. On the anchor axis, the server must keep training on labeled data between rounds -- otherwise the online teacher drifts -- and this interleaving, more than the seed model, governs convergence. The two axes are inseparable: aggressive teacher choices pay off only once the anchor stabilizes training, which is highly sensitive to data augmentation and batch size -- the settings that govern how much input and gradient noise the server injects. How much stabilization is needed is domain-dependent, governed by the dispersion of the seed data and its overlap with client data. These findings yield guidelines for SSFL in ASR training, improving over the strongest prior method on 9 of 11 pairs, by $20.8\%$ on average in-domain and $10.0\%$ cross-domain, narrowing the gap to fully-supervised FL.

</details>


### [116] [Terminal Shrinkage Averaging Reveals a Schedule-Estimator Interaction in LLM Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25482)
*Adam Ousherovitch,Yixin Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses a design flaw in LLM pretraining where the learning-rate schedule and model selection (final iterate vs. checkpoint average) are coupled. The authors propose Terminal Shrinkage Averaging (TSA), which interpolates between the raw final iterate and checkpoint averages to balance optimization progress with reduced terminal variation. The method is validated through NanoChat experiments and shows improvements in validation quality and training speed.


<details>
