  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The core problem is that LLM pretraining conventionally uses the raw final iterate, which couples two design choices: the learning-rate schedule that generates the parameter trajectory and the estimator that constructs the deployed model. A schedule that promotes optimization progress may differ from one that minimizes variation in the raw final iterate. The motivation is to separate these choices to maintain optimization progress late in training while reducing variation in the returned model.

Method: The authors propose Terminal Shrinkage Averaging (TSA), which interpolates between the raw final iterate and the average of recent checkpoints. They analyze how TSA changes the preferred terminal learning-rate schedule under a local quadratic approximation. They test this through controlled NanoChat experiments and validate with depth-22 NanoChat.

Result: The combined schedule and estimator improve validation quality in depth-22 NanoChat. A qualifying time-to-GPT-2 run finishes faster than the public baseline, providing preliminary evidence of benchmark acceleration.

Conclusion: Separating the learning-rate schedule from the model selection estimator creates an opportunity to improve LLM pretraining. TSA provides a way to balance optimization progress with reduced terminal variation, leading to better validation quality and faster training times.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) pretraining conventionally returns the raw final iterate. This couples two design choices: the learning-rate schedule that generates the parameter trajectory and the estimator that constructs the deployed model (e.g. the raw final iterate or a checkpoint average). A schedule that promotes optimization progress may differ from one that minimizes variation in the raw final iterate. Separating these choices creates an opportunity to maintain progress late in training while reducing variation in the returned model. To this end, we propose \emph{Terminal Shrinkage Averaging (TSA)}, which interpolates between the raw final iterate and the average of recent checkpoints to balance recent progress against terminal variation. We analyze how TSA changes the preferred terminal learning-rate schedule under a local quadratic approximation and test this interaction through a sequence of controlled NanoChat experiments. Finally, we demonstrate that the resulting gains transfer to depth-22 NanoChat, where the combined schedule and estimator improve validation quality. A qualifying time-to-GPT-2 run also finishes faster than the public baseline used in our experiments, providing preliminary evidence of benchmark acceleration.

</details>


### [117] [Learning Defensive Policies against Diverse Inference Attacks for Smart Meter Privacy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25484)
*Ruichang Zhang,Mustafa A. Mustafa*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a proxy-guided hierarchical reinforcement learning framework to protect smart meter privacy against non-intrusive load monitoring (NILM) inference attacks. The method uses battery-based load shaping to inject misleading appliance-level signatures into aggregate signals, with a self-supervised privacy probe and signature library for physically realizable perturbations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Smart meter data provides fine-grained household energy consumption visibility but exposes users to privacy risks through NILM attacks that can infer appliance-level information from aggregate signals. The challenge is that attacker models are unknown and heterogeneous, making robust defense difficult.

Method: The authors propose a proxy-guided hierarchical reinforcement learning framework that learns battery-based load-shaping policies to inject realistic but misleading appliance-level signatures into aggregate signals. Key components include: (1) a self-supervised aggregate-structure privacy probe that provides reconstruction-error-based surrogate rewards, and (2) a signature library that makes perturbations appliance-relevant and physically realizable through battery control.

Result: Experiments on real-world datasets UK-DALE and REDD demonstrate strong cross-model and cross-appliance generalization. Across six unseen NILM attackers (covering four appliances on UK-DALE and five on REDD), the defense increases average appliance-level RMSE by 107% and 166% respectively, while reducing F1 score by 79% and 80% respectively.

Conclusion: The proxy-guided hierarchical reinforcement learning framework provides effective privacy protection for smart meter data against diverse and unseen NILM attackers. The theoretical analysis supports that proxy-guided optimization improves inference robustness under attacker diversity. The method successfully generalizes across different NILM models and appliances without requiring knowledge of specific attacker characteristics.

Abstract: Smart meter (SM) data provides fine-grained visibility into household energy consumption, but also exposes users to privacy risks. Inference attacks, known as non-intrusive load monitoring (NILM), can perform appliance-level inference from aggregate signals and recover sensitive behavioral patterns. In practice, attacker models are unknown and heterogeneous, making robust defense challenging. We formulate SM privacy protection as a black-box inference defense problem, aiming to reduce the recoverability of appliance-level information while generalizing across diverse and unseen attackers. We propose a proxy-guided hierarchical reinforcement learning framework that learns battery-based load-shaping policies to inject realistic but misleading appliance-level signatures into the aggregate signal, thereby disrupting the structured patterns exploited by NILM. A self-supervised aggregate-structure privacy probe provides a reconstruction-error-based surrogate reward for disrupting recoverable load structure, while a signature library makes the perturbations appliance-relevant and physically realizable through battery control. We provide theoretical rationale showing that proxy-guided optimization improves inference robustness under attacker diversity. Experiments on real-world datasets UK-DALE and REDD demonstrate strong cross-model and cross-appliance generalization. Across six unseen NILM attackers, covering four appliances on UK-DALE and five on REDD, our proposed defense increases average appliance-level RMSE by 107% and 166%, respectively, while reducing F1 score by 79% and 80%.

</details>


### [118] [Continuous Optimization for p-adic Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25501)
*Julian Salazar,Dimitri Kanevsky,Matt Harvey,Pascal Getreuer,Lucas Dixon*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: First method for native, continuous gradient descent for ML models with p-adic parameters (Q_p), using Berkovich affine line as a path-connected expansion to enable continuous optimization where only discrete combinatorial methods existed before.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing native optimizers for p-adic parameters are discrete and combinatorial because Q_p is totally disconnected with standard losses flat away from minima. This limits the ability to perform continuous optimization on models with p-adic coefficients.

Method: Work with Q_p via its Berkovich affine line - a canonical, path-connected expansion that preserves isometries and uniquely extends analytic maps. This creates a metric tree with interpretable points and local derivatives, enabling gradient descent, backpropagation, and optimizer variants (momentum, Adam).

Result: Successfully formulates gradient descent that can: (1) learn linear models with Q_p coefficients for modular arithmetic, (2) learn XOR-like tasks not expressible by linear models over R, (3) perform linear regression, (4) classify binary-encoded hierarchies (Quillian semantic networks). Addresses open problems from Martins (2025).

Conclusion: The Berkovich affine line approach enables effective native optimizers and backpropagation for p-adic parameters, demonstrating practical applications in modular arithmetic, non-linear tasks over reals, and semantic network classification.

Abstract: We present the first method for native, continuous gradient descent for machine learning models with $p$-adic parameters. Existing native optimizers are discrete, mostly combinatorial searches, as the $p$-adic numbers $\mathbb{Q}_p$ are totally disconnected, with standard losses that are flat away from their minima. To enable continuous optimization, we propose working with $\mathbb{Q}_p$ via its Berkovich affine line: a canonical, path-connected expansion of $\mathbb{Q}_p$ that preserves its isometries and uniquely extends its analytic maps. This hull is a metric tree with interpretable points and local derivatives, which we show enables effective optimizers and backpropagation. We formulate gradient descent and show that its approximations efficiently learn linear models with coefficients in $\mathbb{Q}_p$ to do modular arithmetic, an XOR-like task not expressible by linear models in $\mathbb{R}$. We also demonstrate momentum and Adam variants, linear regression, and classification on binary-encoded hierarchies (Quillian semantic networks), addressing open problems posed by Martins (2025). Library at https://github.com/google-deepmind/padic-ml

</details>


### [119] [Hill Sampling for Test-Time Scaling: A Simple and Better Alternative to Repeated Sampling, Evolution, and Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25510)
*Jacob Beck,Philip V. Ogren,Ari Kobren*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Hill Sampling is a simple test-time compute method for LLMs that repeatedly samples candidate program edits from a frozen model, retains the best solution found, and conditions subsequent samples on it. It achieves state-of-the-art results on circle packing and improves over AlphaEvolve on Erdos' minimum-overlap problem, while also finding that test-time weight learning (evolution strategies) performs worse than random perturbations, and that token sampling randomness is more effective than weight space exploration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors investigate how much complexity is truly necessary for improving LLM solutions to verifiable scientific and algorithmic problems. They question whether elaborate evolutionary search harnesses or test-time parameter updates are actually needed, or if simpler approaches can achieve comparable or better results.

Method: Hill Sampling: repeatedly sample candidate program edits from a frozen LLM, retain the best program found so far, and condition all subsequent samples on that program. Also conduct the largest study (by parameter count) of evolution strategies applied directly to LLM weights at test time, comparing against random perturbations and token sampling.

Result: Hill Sampling sets new state-of-the-art on circle packing among published methods, improves over AlphaEvolve reference on Erdos' minimum-overlap problem, and achieves strong results on sums and differences of finite sets. Requires only hours on 8 NVIDIA H100 GPUs. Surprisingly, test-time weight learning (ES) is worse than zero learning rate, and random token sampling outperforms weight space perturbations. Hill Sampling substantially outperforms repeated sampling.

Conclusion: Simple test-time compute allocation is most effective: repeatedly sample edits to the best verified solution found so far, before introducing additional complexity like archives, diversity mechanisms, evolutionary scaffolds, or test-time parameter learning. Token sampling randomness is more powerful than weight space exploration through ES.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) can improve solutions to verifiable scientific and algorithmic problems by spending additional computation at test time. Recent systems achieve strong results with increasingly elaborate evolutionary search harnesses or by updating model parameters during test-time training. We ask how much of this machinery is necessary. We introduce Hill Sampling, a simple procedure that repeatedly samples candidate program edits from a frozen LLM, retains the best program found so far, and conditions all subsequent samples on that program. We evaluate the method on circle packing, sums/differences of sets, and Erdos' minimum-overlap problem using three open-weight models. Hill Sampling sets a new state of the art on circle packing among published methods, improves over the AlphaEvolve reference on Erdos' minimum-overlap problem, and achieves strong results on sums and differences of finite sets. The circle-packing and Erdos results require only hours of wall-clock time on eight NVIDIA H100 GPUs. To our knowledge, we also conduct, the largest study, by parameter count, of evolution strategies (ES) applied directly to LLM weights at test time. Surprisingly, learning the weights is worse than setting the ES learning rate to zero: at zero learning rate, the method is still searching in weight space through fixed random perturbations. Those perturbations can help exploration, but randomness from token sampling is stronger still, and repeated sampling remains substantially weaker than Hill Sampling. These results suggest a simple test-time compute allocation strategy: repeatedly sample edits to the best verified solution found so far, before introducing additional complexity such as adding archives, diversity mechanisms, evolutionary scaffolds, or test-time parameter learning.

</details>


### [120] [A JEPA Recipe for Tabular Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25541)
*Mingyu Jeon,Suwan Cho,Jae Young Suh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates combining Joint-Embedding Predictive Architecture (JEPA) with tabular foundation models, comparing it against a value-only approach across 147 real datasets. The authors address a latent term collapse problem through a specific recipe (value head reading encoder field, EMA difference target, plateau-based stopping) but find JEPA still underperforms the value-only baseline in both classification and regression tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors seek to explore whether world-model self-supervision (predicting in representation space) can benefit tabular foundation models, following the approach advocated by LeCun (2022) and Assran et al. (2023). They encountered a collapse problem where the latent term and encoder became constant maps, and aimed to find a solution to prevent this collapse.

Method: The authors propose a specific recipe to prevent latent term collapse: (1) the value head reads the encoder field rather than the predictor, (2) the target is an exponential moving average (EMA) difference, and (3) both arms train until a plateau rule stops them (no fixed step budget). This addresses the confounding factor where a fixed horizon had confounded slowdown with a ceiling effect.

Result: JEPA underperforms the value-only arm across 147 datasets. Classification: 32 wins to 70 losses (29:63 with one entry per dataset). Regression: 8 wins to 24 losses. The margin is small on classification but wider on regression. JEPA requires 1.42x more training steps and 1.66x more wall-clock time to reach its plateau.

Conclusion: The JEPA approach, even with the proposed recipe to prevent collapse, fails to match the value-only baseline on tabular data. The approach is computationally more expensive (1.66x wall-clock) while delivering worse performance across both classification and regression tasks, with the trend consistent across all strata and benchmarks.

Abstract: Tabular foundation models learn to predict cell values in context, whereas world-model self-supervision asks for prediction in representation space (LeCun, 2022; Assran et al., 2023). On a tabular foundation-model prior, the latent term of a joint-embedding predictive architecture (JEPA) collapsed in our earlier runs and took the encoder with it to a constant map. We report a recipe under which the latent term survives to convergence beside the value objective: the value head reads the encoder field rather than the predictor, and the target is an exponential moving average (EMA) difference. To bound its cost against the value-only arm, both arms train until a plateau rule stops them, with no fixed step budget. A fixed horizon had confounded a slowdown with a ceiling, since the value-only arm was still improving well past the usual budget. At convergence, in one run per arm, the JEPA arm trails the value-only arm across 147 real datasets, 32:70 wins to losses on classification (29:63 with one entry per dataset name) and 8:24 on regression, the margin small on classification and wider on regression, and the count leans the same way in each stratum and each benchmark. The JEPA arm (jepa) needs 1.42 times as many steps as the value-only arm (ds), and 1.66 times its wall-clock, to reach its plateau.

</details>


### [121] [DefaultGNN: A Dual-Perspective GNN Framework for Predicting Corporate Default from Buyer-Seller Transaction Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25542)
*Junghoon Kim,Hyunsung Kim,Seungyoon Choi,KyoungYong Park,Jihun Lee,YongGu Ji,Chanyoung Park*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes DefaultGNN, a dual-perspective graph neural network that leverages corporate transaction networks (buyer-seller relationships) from electronic tax-invoice data to predict corporate defaults. The model integrates buyer-view and seller-view networks to capture role-dependent and scale-dependent risk propagation, achieving significant improvements over baseline models, especially for firms with limited financial data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional credit models rely heavily on financial statements that are often sparse or unavailable for many firms. Corporate transaction networks offer a complementary view of real economic activity, but how risk propagates through buyer-seller relationships remains underexplored. The study addresses this gap by investigating transaction-driven risk propagation patterns.

Method: The authors conducted a large-scale empirical study using real-world electronic tax-invoice data spanning six years, linking transaction histories with default events. They discovered that transaction-driven risk is both role-dependent (buyer or seller) and scale-dependent. Based on these findings, they constructed multiplex buyer-view and seller-view transaction networks and proposed DefaultGNN, a dual-perspective graph neural network-based framework that integrates both views to model how risk flows through transactional relationships.

Result: DefaultGNN achieves strong improvements over both attribute-based and graph-based baselines, especially for firms with limited intrinsic risk signals. In collaboration with a licensed credit rating agency, DefaultGNN's predictions were validated to complement existing credit scoring models, improving approval rates by 7-11 percentage points without increasing default risk among approved firms.

Conclusion: Transaction networks provide valuable signals for corporate default prediction, particularly for firms with limited financial data. The dual-perspective approach (buyer-view and seller-view) effectively captures role-dependent and scale-dependent risk propagation. DefaultGNN demonstrates practical value in real-world credit scoring applications, offering significant improvements in approval rates while maintaining risk quality.

Abstract: Corporate default prediction is a core problem in financial risk management, yet traditional credit models rely heavily on financial statements that are often sparse or unavailable for many firms. Corporate transaction networks offer a complementary view of real economic activity, but how risk propagates through buyer-seller relationships remains underexplored. We conduct a large-scale empirical study using real-world electronic tax-invoice data spanning six years that links transaction histories with default events, revealing that transaction-driven risk is both role-dependent (buyer or seller) and scale-dependent. Based on these findings, we construct multiplex buyer-view and seller-view transaction networks and propose DefaultGNN, a dual-perspective graph neural network-based framework for corporate default prediction. DefaultGNN integrates both views to model how risk flows through transactional relationships, achieving strong improvements over both attribute-based and graph-based baselines, especially for firms with limited intrinsic risk signals. We further provide interpretable network-based explanations by visualizing how distressed trading partners contribute to default risk. In collaboration with a licensed credit rating agency, we validate that DefaultGNN's predictions complement existing credit scoring models, improving approval rates by 7-11%p without increasing default risk among approved firms. The source code can be found at https://github.com/jhkim611/DefaultGNN

</details>


### [122] [SambaGraph: Action-Reaction Spatio-Temporal Graphs for Soccer Tactical Response Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25569)
*Abel A. Reyes-Angulo,Henry O. Velesaca,Steven Araujo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SambaGraph is a spatio-temporal graph dataset and benchmark for modeling soccer tactical responses, containing 4,070 episodes from 2022 FIFA World Cup matches. The study evaluates graph-based classification, retrieval, and LLM reasoning capabilities, achieving 0.796 macro-F1 for response classification and 0.471 Hit@5 for defensive retrieval.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Soccer tactics are inherently interactive - an attacking action changes the opponent's defensive problem, and the observed response depends on the multi-agent match state. This complexity requires modeling the action-reaction dynamics in soccer, which existing datasets fail to capture adequately.

Method: The authors curate 4,070 action-centered episodes from all 64 matches of the 2022 FIFA World Cup, represented as temporally aligned 23-node player-ball graph sequences with attack/defense views, response labels, and 26,270 split-safe attack-defense pairs. They employ: (1) a compact signature MLP for response classification, (2) a fused graph-signature dual encoder for defensive retrieval, and (3) evaluation of local LLMs for classification and reranking tasks.

Result: Response classification achieves 0.796±0.007 macro-F1. Defensive retrieval reaches 0.471±0.029 Hit@5 and 0.655±0.051 Hit@10. Hard negatives maximize pair discrimination but not retrieval quality. Local LLMs underperform supervised encoders for direct classification and do not improve over a strong original order in eight-candidate reranking, but they provide grounded tactical rationales.

Conclusion: SambaGraph serves as a reproducible benchmark for graph-based soccer strategy-response research. The compact signature MLP effectively classifies tactical responses, while the dual encoder enables reasonable defensive retrieval. Local LLMs, despite underperforming in classification and reranking, offer valuable grounded tactical rationales, positioning the dataset for future multi-modal soccer analytics research.

Abstract: Soccer tactics are interactive: an attacking action changes the opponent's defensive problem, and the observed response depends on the multi-agent match state. We introduce SambaGraph, an action--reaction spatio-temporal graph dataset and benchmark for soccer tactical response modeling. From tracking and event data for all 64 matches of the 2022 FIFA World Cup, we curate 4,070 action-centered episodes represented as temporally aligned 23-node player--ball graph sequences with attack/defense views, response labels, and 26,270 split-safe attack--defense pairs. We study three questions: whether observed responses can be classified from graph episodes, whether successful defenses can be retrieved for a query attack, and whether graph-derived summaries support grounded LLM reasoning. A compact signature MLP obtains $0.796\pm0.007$ macro-F1 for response classification, while a fused graph--signature dual encoder reaches $0.471\pm0.029$ Hit@5 and $0.655\pm0.051$ Hit@10 for full-bank defensive retrieval. Hard negatives maximize pair discrimination but not retrieval quality. Local LLMs underperform supervised encoders for direct classification and do not improve over a strong original order in eight-candidate reranking, but they provide grounded tactical rationales. These results position SambaGraph as a reproducible benchmark for graph-based soccer strategy-response research. Code and dataset are available at: https://github.com/areyesan/SambaGraph.

</details>


### [123] [EMGBlend: Heterogeneity-Aware Self-Supervised Pretraining for Gesture and Force Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25582)
*Yuwei Jia,Cheng Zhong,Jinyang Yu,Zhe Cui*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EMGBlend is a self-supervised framework that handles heterogeneous surface EMG datasets by using geometry-aware attention, band-aware spectral targets, and source balancing, pretraining a 109M-parameter model on 11 public EMG sources for downstream tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Heterogeneous EMG datasets (varying electrode layout, channel count, frequency support, size) cannot be simply mixed for pretraining due to misaligned channel semantics, unobservable spectral targets, and dataset dominance issues.

Method: Three key mechanisms: (1) Shared channel patches with geometry-aware attention to handle varying electrode layouts; (2) Spectral targets restricted to each recording's supported frequency band; (3) Balanced exposure across data sources. Pretrains a 109M-parameter model on 11 public EMG sources.

Result: Outperforms random initialization and waveform reconstruction controls. Multi-source pretraining improves gesture recognition and remains competitive for force decoding. Ablations confirm geometry, band-aware targets, and source balancing each contribute to transfer. Cross-person NinaPro force estimation remains difficult.

Conclusion: Heterogeneous EMG datasets can be effectively combined through explicit mechanism design (geometry-aware attention, band-aware targets, source balancing) rather than simple concatenation, enabling successful transfer to gesture recognition, force regression, and contact classification tasks.

Abstract: Public surface electromyography (EMG) datasets vary widely in electrode layout, channel count, frequency support, and size. Simply mixing them for pretraining can misalign channel semantics, introduce spectral targets that some devices cannot observe, and let large or high-channel-count datasets dominate learning. We introduce EMGBlend, a self-supervised framework designed around these differences. It combines shared channel patches with geometry-aware attention, restricts spectral targets to each recording's supported frequency band, and balances exposure across data sources. We pretrain a 109M-parameter model on 11 public EMG sources and evaluate it on gesture recognition, continuous-force regression, and contact classification. EMGBlend consistently outperforms matched random initialization and waveform reconstruction controls. Fixed-budget source controls show that multi-source pretraining improves gesture recognition and remains competitive for force decoding. Ablations confirm that geometry, band-aware targets, and source balancing each contribute to transfer, although cross-person NinaPro force estimation remains difficult. Overall, EMGBlend shows how heterogeneous EMG datasets can be combined through explicit mechanism design rather than simple concatenation. Code is available at https://github.com/tamanano/EMGBlend

</details>


### [124] [Rewired or Gated? How Instruction Tuning Shapes Knowledge-Conflict Circuits in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25602)
*Shubham Santosh Pandere,Gautam Ranka,Ritika Varshney,Navya Deshmukh,Roushni Sareen,Roshan Kumar Singh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: In language models, the choice between believing the prompt and believing the weights is made by a handful of identifiable attention heads. Instruction tuning changes how models behave under conflict, but whether it rewires the underlying circuit or merely gates/reweights already present components, remains unknown. We provide the first mechanistic base-vs-instruct comparison of conflict-resolution circuits, across three families (Llama-3.2-3B, Qwen-2.5-3B, Gemma-3-4B). Five independent methods, node and edge attribution, superposition role analysis, causal ablation, and path patching, converge on gating, with the same heads, in the same late-layers, are found to be reweighted rather than replaced with a high node overlap (0.60-0.82). Behaviorally, tuning shifts models toward parametric memory, making instruct models reject a terse counterfactual context far more than base ones, the opposite of a naive user-following expectation. Yet this added skepticism is a factor of framing since it disappears when the same false claim is delivered as a coherent, evidential passage. The robustness that instruction tuning buys against terse injection is therefore real but narrow. More broadly, we believe that because the conflict circuit is preserved rather than rebuilt, interpretability and control tools calibrated on base models should transfer directly to their deployed instruct siblings.

</details>


### [125] [What Should a Self-Teacher See? Privileged Context Design for On-Policy Self-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25623)
*Kanghui Tian,Siyuan Liu,Tianxiang Jiang,Shuai Dong,Yizhuo Li,Tian Ding,Yuan Guo,Songze Li,Haowen Hou,Congcong Wang,Yi Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: In on-policy self-distillation, a frozen base model uses privileged context (complete reasoning paths) to score student rollouts. This paper finds that using more abstract hint contexts (strategy names, problem categories) improves performance over full solution paths, while storing far fewer tokens. The optimal context level depends on student scale and task.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The tension between using more vs. less privileged information in self-distillation. While complete solution paths (full privileged information) seem intuitively better, they may overwhelm the student with unnecessary detail. The authors investigate whether less detailed but more abstract hints might work better.

Method: The authors compared four types of context: (1) full solution (conventional approach), (2) named strategy, (3) method-independent framing, (4) problem category, and (5) answer-only control. Experiments were conducted on competition mathematics at 4B and 8B parameter scales across three seeds.

Result: Abstract contexts improved performance by 1.4 points at 4B and 1.6 points at 8B over full solutions, while using 10x fewer hint tokens. Answer-only conditioning remained competitive (within 0.2 points). The optimal context varied with student scale and task. Initial KL divergence between teacher and student did not predict downstream performance.

Conclusion: A self-teacher should provide the level of abstraction that its student can still act upon, not everything it could. More privileged information is not always better - the key is matching the context complexity to the student's capability.

Abstract: More privileged information does not always make a better teacher. We study this tension in on-policy self-distillation (OPSD), where a frozen copy of the base model scores the student's own rollouts under privileged context, conventionally a complete reference solution that bundles the final answer with one particular reasoning path. Holding the student view and training fixed within each scale, we compare that default against three abstractions compiled offline, a named strategy, a method-independent framing, and a problem category, and against an answer-only control that keeps the destination but removes the path. In the primary runs on competition mathematics, the best intermediate contexts improve the in-domain peak mean over the full solution by 1.4 points at 4B and 1.6 at 8B, while storing an order of magnitude fewer hint tokens. Comparisons across three seeds also show positive mean gains for the framing and category contexts at both scales. Answer-only conditioning remains competitive in the primary runs, within 0.2 points of the full solution at these scales. The preferred context varies with student scale and task. Initial teacher-student KL does not order downstream performance. What a self-teacher should see is therefore not everything it could, but the level of abstraction its student can still act on.

</details>


### [126] [An Exploratory Replica-Overlap Probe of the Grokking Transition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25634)
*A. C. Opus,J. Q. Lu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates the grokking transition in neural networks by training 64 independently seeded networks across four configurations, examining whether weight overlap distributions (inspired by RSB/spin glass theory) change during grokking. The study encountered methodological issues: the registered probe had invalid alignment, numerical precision failed, and protocol deviations were found. The pre-registered analysis yielded an "UNDETERMINED" result. Only one configuration (frac40) met checkpoint completeness, but post-hoc analysis showed uninformative results. The standard-deviation ratio was the only statistic with power at the observed effect.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The researchers sought to determine whether the grokking transition (sudden generalization after initial overfitting) is associated with changes in the Parisi order parameter from replica symmetry breaking (RSB) theory, which would indicate a phase transition in the network's weight space.

Method: 64 independently seeded networks were trained in four configurations to convergence or 40,000 epochs. Two metrics were computed: q_wt (weight overlap via alignment) and q_fn (function overlap from predictions). The study used pre-registered analysis with Hartigan-dip tests, confidence intervals, and checkpoint-completeness requirements.

Result: The pre-registered analysis failed: the probe was invalid (reason C0_INSTRUMENT_INVALID), numerical precision requirements failed, and protocol deviations were found. Only frac40 met the 12/16 checkpoint-completeness threshold. For this configuration, the dip test interval contained zero (95% CI: [-0.017, 0.034]), while weight overlap standard deviation increased by ~5.6x. Grokking rates were 0/16, 11/16, and 16/16 across configurations.

Conclusion: The study provides neither confirmatory evidence nor a validated reading of the Parisi order parameter. The only statistic with power was the standard-deviation ratio. The train fraction is confounded with split identity, making grokking rates merely descriptive. The conclusion emphasizes that the registered outcome is UNDETERMINED due to instrument invalidity.

Abstract: We trained 64 independently seeded networks in four configurations, continuing each to sustained convergence or a 40,000-epoch ceiling. We then asked whether an RSB-inspired distribution of pairwise weight overlaps changes across the grokking transition. It is the alignment step, not the overlap statistic, that determines what this registered probe can report. The registered implementation permutes hidden units without the corresponding bias and head-internal permutations and therefore does not preserve the network function. Every q_wt value computed through this alignment inherits the defect; q_fn does not, because it is computed from predictions of the unpermuted models. The numerical-precision requirement also failed, and an audit found protocol deviations. Consequently, the pre-registered rule gives no verdict: registered outcome UNDETERMINED (reason code C0_INSTRUMENT_INVALID). These data provide neither a confirmatory null nor a validated reading of the Parisi order parameter. Only frac40 cleared the 12/16 checkpoint-completeness requirement. For this configuration, a post-hoc criterion applied to the same data gave a Hartigan-dip interval containing zero (95% CI for Delta dip = [-0.017, 0.034]), whereas the overlap standard deviation increased by a factor of about 5.6. A post-hoc calibration assigns the dip test zero power at the simulated separations; the interval is therefore uninformative, not evidence of no change. The standard-deviation ratio is the only statistic here with power at the observed effect. Ensemble loss was near-flat only under the pre-specified 1% threshold. Finally, grokking rates of 0/16, 11/16 and 16/16 remain descriptive because train fraction is confounded with split identity.

</details>


### [127] [Efficient Cost-Aware LLM Evaluation via Bayesian Bandit Gittins Indices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25645)
*Qian Xie,Yueli He,Nairen Cao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GittinsEval is a cost-aware Bayesian bandit approach for selecting high-performing LLM configurations without exhaustive evaluation. It uses the Gittins policy for sequential configuration selection and an anytime recommendation rule with LCB-style uncertainty quantification. The method achieves near-zero simple regret using only 1-2% of exhaustive evaluation cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Exhaustively evaluating every LLM configuration on every benchmark is computationally costly. The authors seek a more efficient way to identify high-performing configurations while accounting for evaluation cost.

Method: The paper formulates configuration selection as a cost-aware Bayesian bandit problem, extending the Bayesian-optimal Gittins policy with an anytime recommendation rule over fully and partially evaluated configurations using an LCB-style score to handle posterior uncertainty. The approach requires offline precomputation with lightweight online updates.

Result: Across four benchmarks (GSM8K, PIQA, AlpacaEval, MMLU), GittinsEval is consistently competitive. It significantly outperforms configuration-level Bayesian optimization on large-example benchmarks and cost-unaware bandit baselines on large-candidate tasks. Near-zero simple regret is achieved with only 1-2% of exhaustive evaluation cost, and adaptive stopping typically triggers at 1-10%.

Conclusion: GittinsEval provides an efficient, theoretically-grounded approach for LLM configuration selection that balances exploration and exploitation while minimizing evaluation cost. Its strong empirical performance across diverse benchmarks demonstrates practical utility for LLM evaluation and selection.

Abstract: Exhaustively evaluating every candidate LLM configuration on every benchmark item to identify a high-performing one is costly. We formulate configuration selection as a cost-aware Bayesian bandit problem and propose GittinsEval, which draws on the Bayesian-optimal Gittins policy to determine which configuration to evaluate next and when to stop. We extend the policy with an anytime recommendation rule over both fully and partially evaluated configurations, using an LCB-style score to account for posterior uncertainty. GittinsEval is computationally efficient, requiring only lightweight online updates after offline precomputation. Across GSM8K, PIQA, AlpacaEval, and MMLU response matrices, GittinsEval is consistently competitive, with particularly strong gains over configuration-level Bayesian optimization on large-example benchmarks and over cost-unaware bandit baselines on large-candidate tasks. Crucially, GittinsEval often attains near-zero simple regret using only 1% to 2% of the exhaustive-evaluation cost; it also offers an adaptive stopping rule that typically triggers at 1% to 10%.

</details>


### [128] [From Experts to Sub-experts: Fine-grained Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning for MoE LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25655)
*Zhentao Tan,Chang Liu,Yao Liu,Yue Wu,Jieping Ye*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: NSFT proposes fine-grained parameter-efficient fine-tuning for MoE LLMs by decomposing experts into sub-experts (channel groups) and selecting task-relevant ones for updating, with custom learning-rate and gradient scaling to compensate for reduced update magnitude.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dense full-parameter adaptation is computationally expensive for large LLMs. While MoE models provide sparse architectures and expert-level sparse tuning exists, the authors observe that activated experts are internally sparse - only a fraction of intermediate channels respond to downstream tasks. This indicates expert-level adaptation is too coarse-grained.

Method: NSFT decomposes each expert along the intermediate dimension into structured channel groups. Task-relevant sub-experts are selected by combining routing importance with intra-expert activation saliency. To optimize sparse partial updates, NSFT introduces learning-rate scaling and dynamic gradient scaling to compensate for reduced effective update magnitude.

Result: Experiments on OLMoE and Ling-mini-2.0 across domain-specific tasks and general benchmarks show NSFT consistently outperforms representative PEFT and expert-level sparse tuning baselines, while using substantially fewer trainable parameters and preserving competitive general capability.

Conclusion: Sub-expert-level adaptation is a more precise and efficient PEFT paradigm for MoE LLMs, offering better performance with fewer trainable parameters than existing methods.

Abstract: As large language models (LLMs) scale rapidly, dense full-parameter adaptation becomes increasingly expensive, motivating sparse and modular architectures such as Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models. This shift raises a key question for parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT): at what granularity should parameters be selected and updated? Existing PEFT methods such as LoRA operate on predefined weight matrices, while expert-level sparse tuning methods update entire selected experts. However, we observe that activated experts are internally sparse, with only a small fraction of intermediate channels strongly responding to downstream tasks, indicating that expert-level adaptation is still too coarse. We propose NSFT (Neural Sub-expert Fine-Tuning), a fine-grained PEFT framework that refines MoE adaptation from experts to sub-experts. NSFT decomposes each expert along the intermediate dimension into structured channel groups and selects task-relevant sub-experts by combining routing importance with intra-expert activation saliency. To optimize sparse partial updates, NSFT further introduces learning-rate scaling and dynamic gradient scaling to compensate for the reduced effective update magnitude. Experiments on OLMoE and Ling-mini-2.0 across challenging domain-specific tasks and general benchmarks show that NSFT consistently outperforms representative PEFT and expert-level sparse tuning baselines, while using substantially fewer trainable parameters and preserving competitive general capability. These results suggest that sub-expert-level adaptation is a more precise and efficient PEFT paradigm for MoE LLMs.

</details>


### [129] [Targeted Review for AI-Assisted Biodiversity Surveys: Active Continuous-Score Occupancy Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25657)
*Timm Haucke,Lauren Harrell,Justin Kay,Mary Clapp,Sara Beery*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces ACORN (Active Continuous-Score Occupancy Modeling), a method that integrates ML predictions into species occupancy models to strategically select samples for expert review. It optimizes review effort for downstream ecological inference rather than just classifier accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Machine learning is increasingly used to label scientific datasets, but errors (especially systematic biases) can propagate into scientific understanding. Scientists must review substantial proportions of ML-generated labels to verify accuracy, but optimal allocation of this limited expert review effort remains a challenge.

Method: The authors developed ACORN, which incorporates ML predictions into occupancy models and strategically selects samples for expert review that are maximally informative for downstream ecological analysis. The method was tested on camera-trap and bioacoustic datasets.

Result: ACORN recovers ecological conclusions close to those obtained from fully human-labeled data while requiring substantially fewer expert reviews than non-targeted review policies.

Conclusion: ML-assisted scientific workflows should optimize expert effort for downstream inference rather than classifier accuracy alone, especially when human review budget is limited.

Abstract: We increasingly use machine learning to label scientific datasets. The models we develop and deploy are improving all the time, but they are not and will likely never be perfect. Mistakes matter, as errors can propagate into our scientific understanding, particularly when systematically biased. Very reasonably, scientists thus review substantial proportions of ML-generated labels to verify or correct mistakes in pursuit of ensuring their scientific findings are not biased by ML. In this work, we focus on helping scientists optimally allocate this reviewing effort relative to their scientific goals. We focus on a specific class of scientists (ecologists) and a specific, widespread, and impactful modeling target (occupancy modeling, which estimates where species are likely to occur, conditioned on environmental factors). We introduce Active Continuous-Score Occupancy Modeling (ACORN), a method that incorporates ML predictions into occupancy models and strategically selects samples for expert review that are maximally informative for downstream ecological analysis. Across camera-trap and bioacoustic datasets, our method recovers ecological conclusions close to those obtained from fully human-labeled data, while requiring substantially fewer expert reviews than non-targeted review policies. Our results suggest that ML-assisted scientific workflows should optimize expert effort for downstream inference, rather than for classifier accuracy alone, especially when human review budget is limited. Our code is available at https://github.com/timmh/acorn

</details>


### [130] [When Riemann flows with Wasserstein: Generative Modeling of Probability Distributions on Manifolds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25659)
*Doron Haviv,Edward De Brouwer,Rishabh Anand,Rex Ying,Aïcha Bentaieb,Gabriele Scalia,Hector Corrada Bravo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces Riemannian Wasserstein Entropic Flow Matching (RWEFM), a generative framework for modeling meta-distributions (distributions over probability measures) on non-Euclidean domains like manifolds. The method trains a neural vector field using Riemannian optimal transport velocities and McCann displacement interpolations, with a GPU-efficient approximation called the Riemannian Entropic Map.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many scientific datasets (molecular conformational ensembles, single-cell tissue measurements) are naturally modeled as meta-distributions on non-Euclidean domains. Existing generative methods assume Euclidean geometry and fail to capture this intrinsic structure, necessitating a new approach that respects the underlying geometry of the data.

Method: RWEFM operates on the Wasserstein space P₂(M) of a Riemannian manifold (M,g). Training involves regressing a neural vector field onto Riemannian optimal transport velocities, using McCann displacement interpolations as conditional paths. The method requires only a geodesic distance and a projection operator, making it applicable to manifolds without closed-form geometry.

Result: Theoretical validation that the construction yields a valid flow matching approach on P₂(M). Experiments demonstrate successful generation of: (1) single-cell samples in hyperspherical latent spaces, (2) protein conformational ensembles on the torus, and (3) distributions on a general triangulated mesh - showing applicability to manifolds without closed-form geometry.

Conclusion: RWEFM successfully bridges generative modeling and optimal transport on Riemannian manifolds, providing a principled framework for meta-distribution modeling that respects intrinsic data geometry. The Riemannian Entropic Map provides a GPU-efficient approximation, enabling practical applications across diverse scientific domains.

Abstract: Many scientific datasets, such as molecular conformational ensembles or single-cell tissue measurements, are naturally modeled as meta-distributions: distributions over probability measures on non-Euclidean domains. Existing generative methods largely assume Euclidean geometry and fail to capture this structure. We introduce Riemannian Wasserstein Entropic Flow Matching (RWEFM), a generative framework on the Wasserstein space $\mathcal{P}_2(\mathcal{M})$ of a Riemannian manifold $(\mathcal{M},g)$. RWEFM is trained by regressing a neural vector field onto Riemannian optimal transport velocities, using McCann displacement interpolations as conditional paths. We confirm theoretically that this construction leads to a valid flow matching approach on $\mathcal{P}_2(\mathcal{M})$ and introduce the Riemannian Entropic Map, a GPU-efficient approximation of the optimal transport map on manifolds. Our experiments show that by respecting the intrinsic geometry of the data, RWEFM can generate whole single-cell samples in hyperspherical latent spaces and protein conformational ensembles on the torus. As RWEFM requires only a geodesic distance and a projection operator, it is not restricted to manifolds with closed-form geometry, which we demonstrate by generating distributions on a general triangulated mesh.

</details>


### [131] [Marginal Log-Likelihood Increments under Dirichlet-Smoothed Markov Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25675)
*Levin David Schwab*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper analyzes a Dirichlet-smoothed transition model for process mining, deriving mathematical relationships between adding workflow traces to training data and changes in model quality. The key finding challenges the intuition that better individual trace prediction accuracy leads to better batch selection quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors seek to understand the mathematical properties of acquisition functions in process mining - specifically, what bounds exist on the gain from adding traces, how reference weighting affects the model, and whether individual prediction accuracy predicts batch quality.

Method: The authors derive exact mathematical expressions for: (1) the change in reference-weighted log likelihood when adding one trace, (2) this change as a weighted reduction in KL divergence, (3) an upper bound on acquisition gain, (4) a covariance identity for reference weighting effects, and (5) a sign criterion for candidate interaction. They validate these using the BPI Challenge 2012 loan-application log with two regressors.

Result: Three quantities were measured: upper bound on acquisition gain, covariance identity, and sign criterion for interaction. Results showed positive selection in only 1 of 4 combinations of reference weighting and budget unit. Notably, the regressor with higher individual prediction accuracy (median R² = 0.87 vs 0.62) achieved a smaller share of attainable gain (61% vs 69%).

Conclusion: The batch objective is neither submodular nor supermodular. Critically, ranking accuracy for individual traces is neither necessary nor sufficient for batch quality - a better individual predictor does not guarantee better overall acquisition selection.

Abstract: For a Dirichlet-smoothed transition model, the effect of adding one workflow trace to the training archive is an exact change in reference-weighted log likelihood. We derive that change and show that it is a weighted reduction of Kullback--Leibler divergence between the reference conditionals and the model. From this form we obtain an upper bound on the gain available to any acquisition, which expresses a millinat difference as a share of what is attainable, an exact covariance identity for the effect of the reference weighting, and a sign criterion for the interaction between two candidates, from which the batch objective is neither submodular nor supermodular. A case study on the BPI Challenge 2012 loan-application log measures all three and finds a positive selection result in one of the four combinations of reference weighting and budget unit. There, of two regressors fitted to identical descriptors and identical labels, the one that predicts individual increments far more accurately, median $R^2$ 0.87 against 0.62, realizes the smaller share of the attainable gain, 61 against 69 per cent, so ranking accuracy for individual traces is neither necessary nor sufficient for batch quality.

</details>


### [132] [Graph Domain Adaptation Does Not End with Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25692)
*Ziqian Liu,Yongxue Xu,Enze Zhang,Jiaqi Zhang,Hao Wang,Maolin Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EviGDA is a novel Graph Domain Adaptation framework that addresses the limitation of existing methods relying on a single graph-propagating path by combining a graph-aware expert with a graph-free local expert through a constant probability mixture at inference, achieving state-of-the-art performance on 10 datasets and 16 transfer tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing GDA methods rely on a single graph-propagating path for target prediction, which may not exhaust all predictive evidence available in the target domain. The graph-aware and graph-free experts may exhibit different failure modes under topological shifts, suggesting that complementary evidence could be leveraged.

Method: EviGDA combines two independent experts: (1) Graph-aware expert performs message passing with entropy-aware marginal alignment; (2) Graph-free local expert learns solely from source node features and labels without graph propagation or target alignment. The two experts are optimized independently and combined only at inference through a task-level constant probability mixture, avoiding joint training, learned routing, or target pseudo-labels.

Result: Extensive experiments on ten datasets and 16 transfer tasks demonstrate that EviGDA outperforms state-of-the-art baselines.

Conclusion: The proposed evidence-augmented approach successfully leverages complementary predictions from both graph-aware and graph-free experts, providing a simple yet effective solution for graph domain adaptation that handles distribution shifts in both node attributes and graph structure.

Abstract: Graph domain adaptation (GDA) transfers knowledge from a labeled source graph to an unlabeled target graph under shifts in both node attributes and graph structure. Existing methods primarily adapt graph representations through propagation redesign, distribution alignment, or source-to-target transition modeling, but still rely on a single graph-propagating path for target prediction. This leaves open whether an adapted graph representation exhausts the predictive evidence available in the target domain, since the graph-aware expert and graph-free local expert may exhibit different failure modes under topological shifts. To address this limitation, we propose EviGDA, an Evidence-Augmented Graph Domain Adaptation framework that complements graph representation adaptation with a graph-free local expert. The graph-aware expert performs message passing and entropy-aware marginal alignment, while the graph-free local expert learns solely from source node features and labels without graph propagation or target alignment. The two experts are optimized independently and combined only at inference through a task-level constant probability mixture, preserving complementary evidence without joint training, learned routing, or target pseudo-labels. Extensive experiments on ten datasets and 16 transfer tasks show that EviGDA outperforms state-of-the-art baselines.

</details>


### [133] [Fully Byzantine-Resilient Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25701)
*Haejoon Lee,Dimitra Panagou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes FRAC-MARL, a decentralized Byzantine-resilient actor-critic multi-agent reinforcement learning method that achieves exact convergence to attack-free limit points under Byzantine edge attacks, unlike prior methods that only guarantee convergence to a neighborhood.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Byzantine-resilient AC-MARL methods only guarantee convergence to a neighborhood of attack-free limit points, resulting in degraded performance. The authors aim to achieve exact convergence to the same limit points as in the attack-free case.

Method: The proposed FRAC-MARL uses redundancy in two-hop messages to identify and filter unreliable messages. Under linear parameterizations of value and team-reward functions with Byzantine edge attacks on time-varying communication graphs, the method achieves almost sure convergence.

Result: The authors prove that agents' parameters converge almost surely to the same limit points as in the attack-free case. They also introduce a novel topological condition for convergence, provide a systematic network construction method, and prove polynomial-time verifiability.

Conclusion: FRAC-MARL successfully achieves fully resilient (exact) convergence in Byzantine-resilient distributed AC-MARL, with demonstrated effectiveness on cooperative multi-robot formation control tasks.

Abstract: We study distributed Byzantine-resilient actor-critic multi-agent reinforcement learning (AC-MARL), where agents collectively learn policies through local interactions. Existing methods guarantee convergence of the agents' parameters only to a neighborhood of the attack-free limit points, resulting in degraded performance. We propose Fully Resilient AC-MARL (FRAC-MARL), a decentralized method in which each agent leverages redundancy in two-hop messages to identify reliable messages. Under linear parameterizations of the value and team-reward functions and Byzantine edge attacks, where adversarial behavior is confined to the communication layer, we prove that agents' parameters converge almost surely to the same limit points as in the attack-free case over time-varying communication graphs. We introduce a novel topological condition for the convergence of our method, present a systematic method to construct such networks, and prove that this condition can be verified in polynomial time. Finally, we demonstrate our method on cooperative multi-robot formation control tasks.

</details>


### [134] [Slow Decay and Silenced Expression: Iterated Subliminal Trait Transfer in Language-Model Lineages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25721)
*Ryan Vo,Duc-Vu Nguyen,Matt Kretchmar,Ngan Luu-Thuy Nguyen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether traits instilled in language models persist across multiple generations of training (lineages). The authors train Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct on data containing an instilled trait, iterate this for ten generations, and measure trait persistence using keyword screening and activation probes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work on subliminal learning showed a teacher's trait can transmit to a student through filtered data, but only for a single training step. This study asks whether such traits hold or fade across deeper lineages—critical understanding for AI safety as models increasingly train on each other's outputs.

Method: The authors instill a trait into three copies of Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, then iterate training to depth ten from each. They evaluate every generation using two methods: (1) a keyword screen detecting trait expressions in outputs, and (2) an activation probe projecting model displacement from base onto directions built from other lineages' teachers.

Result: Two findings emerge. First, the trait persists through ten generations across all three lineages—keyword-screen rate falls from initial levels to 55.6% after step one and 21.1% by generation ten, while the base model shows zero matches. Second, the trait can exist internally without behavioral expression: removing the default system prompt at evaluation drops keyword-screen rate to zero for generation-ten students, but probe scores remain positive. Steering the untreated base with a generation-ten student's displacement induces trait expression even without the system prompt.

Conclusion: Traits can persist across ten generations of model training, demonstrating that subliminal learning effects are not transient. Moreover, traits can be encoded internally (detectable via activation probes) while being behaviorally suppressed—revealing a dissociation between internal representations and observable outputs that has significant implications for AI safety and interpretability.

Abstract: Language models are increasingly trained on the outputs of other models, forming chains that we call lineages, in which a trait present in one generation can pass to the next. Prior work on subliminal learning has shown that a teacher's trait can transmit to a student through filtered data carrying none of the trait's content. However, the evidence covers only a single training step. We study whether such a trait holds or fades across lineages. We instill the trait into three copies of Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct and iterate the training step to depth ten from each, reading every generation two ways on the same held-out prompts: a keyword screen that looks for expressions of the trait in the model's output, and an activation probe that projects each model's displacement from the base onto a direction built from the other lineages' teachers. We report two findings. First, the trait persists through ten generations across three lineages. The instilled models express it on every completion; the keyword-screen rate falls to 55.6% after the first step and to 21.1% by generation ten. The base itself matches the screen on none of its 300 completions. Second, the trait can be present internally while absent behaviorally. When the model's default system prompt is removed at evaluation, the generation-ten students' keyword-screen rate is zero on every prompt while the probe score stays positive on every prompt. Steering the untreated base with the displacement of a generation-ten student, which is trained and measured under the default system prompt, induces screened expression of the trait even with the system prompt removed, while that same student shows no expression of the trait with the system prompt removed.

</details>


### [135] [Signed Graph Pre-Training and Prompt Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25722)
*Zihan Mei,Rong Pan,Yuzhou Chen,Yixuan He*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TopoSIGN is a novel topology-guided graph pre-training and prompt learning framework for signed graphs, combining magnetic signed Laplacian with persistent-homology features (Dowker-complex persistence images) to enable transfer learning on signed graphs used in trust networks, financial systems, and biological interactions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While signed graph neural networks have improved task-specific learning, graph transfer learning on signed graphs remains underdeveloped. This paper addresses the gap in transfer learning capabilities for signed graphs.

Method: TopoSIGN combines: (1) a structural encoder built on the magnetic signed Laplacian, and (2) a persistent-homology branch that summarizes signed topology through Dowker-complex persistence images. The fused embeddings are then transferred to a prompt learning function.

Result: Experimental results on synthetic and real-world datasets demonstrate efficacy in extracting useful structural information in signed graphs, as well as adaptability and flexibility of the proposed framework.

Conclusion: TopoSIGN provides a pioneering approach for topology-guided graph pre-training and prompt learning on signed graphs, successfully combining structural and topological features for transfer learning tasks.

Abstract: Signed graphs arise in trust--distrust networks, financial correlation systems, biological interaction graphs, and many other domains in which edges can be positive or negative and may also be directed. While signed graph neural networks have improved task-specific learning, graph transfer learning on signed graphs remains underdeveloped. In this paper, we introduce TopoSIGN, a pioneer topology-guided graph pre-training and prompt learning framework for signed graphs. TopoSIGN combines a structural encoder built on the magnetic signed Laplacian with a novel persistent-homology branch that summarizes signed topology through Dowker-complex persistence images. The fused embeddings are then transferred to a prompt learning function. Experimental results on synthetic and real-world datasets demonstrate the efficacy of TopoSIGN in extracting useful structural information in signed graphs, as well as the adaptability and flexibility of the proposed general framework.

</details>


### [136] [Self-Supervised Combinatorial Optimization with Constraints via Frank-Wolfe](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25728)
*Akbar Rafiey,Yifei Xu,Nikolaos Karalias*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A general self-supervised learning framework for combinatorial optimization that allows neural networks to predict arbitrary continuous vectors, then approximates these using sparse convex combinations of feasible solutions via Frank-Wolfe methods, enabling differentiable training without problem-specific projections.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The central challenge in neural combinatorial optimization is handling hard combinatorial constraints within continuous, gradient-based training. Existing approaches require projection steps to constrain outputs within feasible polytopes and rely on ad-hoc, problem-specific constructions.

Method: The framework allows neural networks to predict arbitrary continuous vectors (potentially outside feasible polytope). These predictions are approximated by sparse convex combinations of feasible solutions using a geometric decomposition algorithm based on Frank-Wolfe methods and approximate Caratheodory results. This induces an almost-everywhere differentiable, self-supervised loss defined as the expected value of the discrete objective, with automatic rounding guarantees at inference time.

Result: Strong empirical performance demonstrated across multiple combinatorial problems: Quadratic Assignment Problem, Maximum Coverage, and Traveling Salesperson Problem.

Conclusion: The proposed framework provides a general approach for self-supervised neural combinatorial optimization that avoids problem-specific projection mechanisms while maintaining theoretical guarantees through the automatic rounding procedure.

Abstract: Self-supervised learning for combinatorial optimization has emerged as a promising paradigm for solving discrete optimization problems with neural networks, but a central challenge remains: handling hard combinatorial constraints within continuous, gradient-based training. Continuously extending combinatorial objectives to convex domains is a powerful technique, yet existing approaches often require projection steps that constrain neural network outputs to lie inside the feasible polytope and rely on ad-hoc and problem-specific constructions. We propose a general framework in which the neural network is allowed to predict arbitrary continuous vectors that could potentially lie outside of the feasible polytope. These predictions are then approximated by sparse convex combinations of feasible solutions using a geometric decomposition algorithm based on Frank--Wolfe methods and approximate Caratheodory results. This decomposition induces an a.e.-differentiable, self-supervised loss defined as the expected value of the discrete objective. The same procedure provides an automatic rounding guarantee at inference time. We demonstrate strong empirical performance across multiple combinatorial problems, including the Quadratic Assignment Problem, Maximum Coverage, and the Traveling Salesperson Problem.

</details>


### [137] [Beyond Class Marginals: Bounding Rehearsal Gaps without Freezing Class Co-occurrence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25735)
*Congren Dai,Nat Roongjirarat,Fei Ye*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces randomized-pass replay (RPR) to control the rehearsal gap (interval between replay appearances) in continual learning, separate from class marginal and co-occurrence. RPR visits each resident class once per shuffled pass, bounding gaps by 2*ceil(C/b)-1. Experiments show 0.72-1.67 pp improvement in final average accuracy on ER-ACE.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Class-balanced replay controls class frequency but does not determine the interval between successive replay appearances of a class (the rehearsal gap). This interval is studied separately from class marginal and class co-occurrence.

Method: Randomized-pass replay (RPR) visits each resident class once per shuffled pass. For fixed set of C resident classes and replay batch size b ≤ C, it preserves balanced time-averaged class marginal and bounds every gap by 2*ceil(C/b)-1. The scheduler uses no future class information and adds no replay examples or forward passes.

Result: In linear-head ER-ACE diagnostic, joint absence from incoming and replay batches produces a one-sided classifier-bias gradient. Longer absence episodes are associated with larger negative bias displacement. In primary ER-ACE experiments, RPR improves final average accuracy by 0.72-1.67 percentage points relative to independent class-balanced retrieval under reservoir storage, with positive effects also under balanced storage. Pretrained ViTs show positive effects on LT10 streams with small replay batches.

Conclusion: RPR effectively controls rehearsal gap length without requiring future class information or extra resources. The accuracy effects depend on the learner and operating regime. Fixed-cycle and reused-pass controls change more than one temporal statistic, so experiments do not isolate rehearsal-gap length from all other forms of temporal dependence.

Abstract: Class-balanced replay controls class frequency but does not determine the interval between successive replay appearances of a class. We study this interval, the rehearsal gap, separately from the class marginal and class co-occurrence, and introduce randomised-pass replay (RPR), which visits each resident class once per shuffled pass. For a fixed set of C resident classes and replay batch size b less than or equal to C, RPR preserves the balanced time-averaged class marginal and bounds every gap by 2*ceil(C/b)-1; a churn-conditional bound applies while the resident set changes. The scheduler uses no future class information and adds no replay examples or forward passes. In a linear-head ER-ACE diagnostic, joint absence from the incoming and replay batches produces a one-sided classifier-bias gradient. Longer absence episodes are associated with larger negative bias displacement, and removing the incoming-loss mask attenuates the scheduling effect. In the primary ER-ACE experiments, RPR improves final average accuracy by 0.72-1.67 percentage points relative to independent class-balanced retrieval under reservoir storage, with positive effects also observed under balanced storage. Pretrained ViTs show positive effects on the tested LT10 streams with small replay batches, while matched larger-batch controls show no material effect. Fixed-cycle and reused-pass controls change more than one temporal statistic, so the experiments do not isolate rehearsal-gap length from all other forms of temporal dependence. The accuracy effects depend on the learner and operating regime.

</details>


### [138] [Modular Norm RandOpt: Population-Efficient Ensembling through Architecture-Aware Perturbations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25745)
*Kirato Yoshihara,Hiroaki Hamade*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Modular Norm RandOpt is an architecture-aware sampling method for language model ensembles that uses module-wise natural norms instead of global perturbation scales, achieving significant improvements in sample efficiency over RandOpt.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RandOpt uses global perturbation scales that ignore the heterogeneous geometry of different neural network modules, leading to inefficient sampling. The authors aim to develop a more architecture-aware perturbation method that better exploits the structural differences between model components.

Method: The proposed method uses module-wise natural norms and calibrated scales for weight perturbations while preserving the selection and voting mechanisms of RandOpt. It evaluates the approach across seven tasks, three Qwen scales (0.5B-3B), and additional tests on Llama 3.2 3B and Gemma 3 4B.

Result: Modular Norm RandOpt outperforms RandOpt using 3× fewer candidates on Countdown and at least 12× fewer on GSM8K. It achieves higher mean accuracy than RandOpt on Countdown, GSM8K, and MATH-500 at every scale tested. The gains extend to other model families. A tail-density diagnostic on GSM8K suggests 1.2-1.8× candidate reduction, with most improvement from more favorable correct-expert support.

Conclusion: Perturbation geometry is a key design choice for population-efficient, gradient-free search around pretrained models. The module-wise approach significantly improves sample efficiency compared to global perturbation methods.

Abstract: RandOpt samples weight-perturbed language models and ensembles top-ranked candidates through plurality voting, but its global perturbation scale ignores heterogeneous module geometry. We propose \mbox{\textbf{\emph{Modular Norm RandOpt}}}, an architecture-aware sampling method using module-wise natural norms and calibrated scales while preserving selection and voting. It outperforms RandOpt using $3\times$ fewer candidates on Countdown and at least $12\times$ fewer on GSM8K, with corresponding wall-clock savings. Evaluations across seven tasks and three Qwen scales ($0.5$B--$3$B) show higher mean accuracy than RandOpt on Countdown, GSM8K, and MATH-500 at every scale. The gains extend to Llama 3.2 $3$B and Gemma 3 $4$B on Countdown and GSM8K. On Qwen2.5-1.5B, our ensembles also achieve higher mean accuracy than iterative baselines on both tasks at comparable main-run evaluation budgets. On GSM8K, a tail-density diagnostic implies only a $1.2$--$1.8\times$ candidate reduction, while most ensemble improvement is associated with more favorable correct-expert support. These results highlight perturbation geometry as a key design choice for population-efficient, gradient-free search around pretrained models.

</details>


### [139] [Minimal Recurrent Behavioral Memory for Imitation under Partial Observability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25757)
*Xianyao Li,Fang Xu,Rui Min,Ruitong Tian,Jing Du*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates the minimal recurrent memory needed to reproduce an expert behavior under partial observability. The authors characterize this using conditional entropy and compatibility relations, develop a measurement protocol to separate behavioral sufficiency from excess code rate, and demonstrate that learning the minimal representation improves from 0/8 to 6/8 sufficient held-out seeds with closed-loop success increasing from 0.08 to 0.57.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The core question is: what is the least amount of recurrent memory needed to reproduce a specified expert under partial observability? This addresses fundamental limitations in imitation learning and behavioral cloning when the expert has hidden states that the learner cannot observe.

Method: The authors characterize minimal recurrent behavioral memory through a compatibility relation: under transitivity, classes attain the exact minimum, while the general case involves entropy minimization over closed compatible state assignments. They propose a sole-carrier measurement protocol that separates behavioral sufficiency, excess code rate, and information carried by observations or other memory paths. For learning, they use event-agnostic future-behavior supervision.

Result: Learned code rates remain near zero- and two-bit requirements as hidden modes grow to 512. Anticipatory memory follows a 2→1→0 requirement despite zero instantaneous demand during waiting. With the proposed supervision, 36/40 sufficient seeds are achieved with one frozen configuration. The longest-horizon pixel setting improves from 0/8 to 6/8 sufficient held-out seeds, and closed-loop success increases from 0.08 to 0.57. On unmodified community benchmarks, the protocol certifies delay-independent requirements, which sufficient codes match at mid-delay.

Conclusion: The paper successfully characterizes the minimal recurrent memory for expert behavior reproduction under partial observability through information-theoretic bounds. The proposed supervision approach helps learn this representation, though it can induce predictive surplus that can be addressed through annealing. The method certifies delay-independent requirements on community benchmarks, demonstrating the practical applicability of the theoretical framework.

Abstract: What is the least recurrent memory needed to reproduce a specified expert under partial observability? The instantaneous requirement is the conditional entropy of the expert's behavioral quotient, but recurrence must also preserve distinctions that future observations will not restore before use. We characterize this minimal recurrent behavioral memory by a compatibility relation: under transitivity its classes attain the exact minimum, while the general case is an entropy minimization over closed compatible state assignments, with exact certificates on finite instances. A sole-carrier measurement protocol separates behavioral sufficiency, excess code rate, and information carried by observations or other memory paths; experimental bit requirements refer to the induced symbolic behavioral model under the stated occupancy. Across manipulation tasks, learned code rates remain near zero- and two-bit requirements as hidden modes grow to $512$, and anticipatory memory follows a $2\to1\to0$ requirement despite zero instantaneous demand during waiting. Learning this representation remains difficult: event-agnostic future-behavior supervision yields $36/40$ sufficient seeds with one frozen configuration and improves the longest-horizon pixel setting from $0/8$ to $6/8$ sufficient held-out seeds (closed-loop success from $0.08$ to $0.57$). On unmodified community benchmarks, the protocol certifies delay-independent requirements, which sufficient codes match at mid-delay. The supervision aids commitment but can induce predictive surplus; annealing it lets imitation and rate training reduce that surplus, separating the information-theoretic target from the ability to learn it.

</details>
