### [140] [Disentangling Heterogeneous Traffic Dynamics for Multi-Step Traffic Forecasting via Adaptive Spectral Decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25777)
*Zijun Huang,Chenrui Fu,Wenhao Wang,Xiaochuan Gou,Chih-Chieh Hung,Guanyao Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ADNet is a component-specific traffic forecasting framework that adaptively disentangles traffic dynamics into dominant and residual components using a learnable complementary spectral decomposition mechanism, achieving superior performance in multi-step traffic forecasting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate multi-step traffic forecasting is challenging because observed traffic signals contain heterogeneous temporal dynamics with different characteristics and predictability levels. Existing approaches model these dynamics within a unified representation or rely on predefined decomposition rules, which limits their ability to flexibly separate persistent patterns from rapidly varying fluctuations.

Method: ADNet introduces a learnable complementary spectral decomposition mechanism that determines the contribution of each frequency bin to two components (dominant and residual). Unlike hard frequency partitioning, every frequency bin can contribute to both components with different learned proportions, allowing decomposition to be optimized jointly with the forecasting objective. The reconstructed components are modeled by two dedicated spatiotemporal forecasting branches, and their predictions are integrated for final multi-step forecast.

Result: Experiments on Alameda and Orange regions of the TraffiDent dataset show ADNet achieves the best performance in 20 of 24 region-horizon-metric comparisons, with particularly clear gains at longer forecasting horizons. Capacity-controlled ablation experiments demonstrate that the learnable decomposition substantially outperforms fixed decomposition and provides additional improvements beyond the dual-branch architecture alone.

Conclusion: The results demonstrate the effectiveness of adaptive decomposition and component-specific modeling for multi-step traffic forecasting. ADNet's ability to flexibly separate persistent patterns from rapidly varying fluctuations through learnable spectral decomposition leads to significant performance improvements.

Abstract: Accurate multi-step traffic forecasting remains challenging because observed traffic signals contain heterogeneous temporal dynamics with different characteristics and levels of predictability. Existing approaches typically model these dynamics within a unified representation or rely on predefined decomposition rules, which may limit their ability to flexibly separate persistent patterns from rapidly varying fluctuations. To address this issue, we propose the Adaptive Decomposition Network (ADNet), a component-specific forecasting framework that adaptively disentangles traffic dynamics into dominant and residual components. ADNet introduces a learnable complementary spectral decomposition mechanism that determines the contribution of each frequency bin to the two components. Unlike hard frequency partitioning, every frequency bin can contribute to both components with different learned proportions, allowing the decomposition to be optimized jointly with the forecasting objective. The reconstructed components are then modeled by two dedicated spatiotemporal forecasting branches, and their predictions are integrated to generate the final multi-step forecast. Experiments on the Alameda and Orange regions of the TraffiDent dataset show that ADNet achieves the best performance in 20 of the 24 reported region-horizon-metric comparisons, with particularly clear gains at longer forecasting horizons. Capacity-controlled ablation experiments further show that the learnable decomposition substantially outperforms a fixed decomposition and provides additional improvements beyond the dual-branch architecture alone. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of adaptive decomposition and component-specific modeling for multi-step traffic forecasting.

</details>


### [141] [A Lightweight Plastic-Memory Framework for Graph Few-Shot Class-Incremental Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25781)
*Zihan Mei,Zhili Qin,Tongze Zhang,Hongyuan Liu,Junming Shao,Qinli Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a lightweight plastic-memory framework for few-shot incremental learning on graphs. The framework uses an evolving micro-clustering structure to dynamically update class prototypes, sub-prototypes, and their interaction weights, combined with a memory-driven meta-learning approach to balance stability for old tasks and adaptability for new tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional incremental learning approaches on graphs suffer from catastrophic forgetting and rely on replay or distillation techniques that incur high computational costs. Additionally, real-world scenarios often have scarce labeled data for new classes, making few-shot incremental learning critical.

Method: The proposed framework consists of: 1) A plastic-memory module with an evolving micro-clustering structure that dynamically updates representations of class prototypes, sub-prototypes, and their interaction weights; 2) A memory-driven meta-learning framework with an inner loop for adaptability to new tasks and an outer loop for stability on earlier tasks.

Result: Extensive experiments on four benchmark datasets demonstrate the framework's superior performance in balancing stability for old knowledge and adaptability to new knowledge.

Conclusion: The proposed plastic-memory framework effectively addresses few-shot incremental learning on graphs by maintaining a lightweight yet effective memory module that evolves over time, outperforming existing approaches in balancing stability and adaptability.

Abstract: Graph Incremental Learning has garnered increasing attention as dynamic graph data continues to emerge across diverse fields. Conventional approaches primarily address catastrophic forgetting by preserving node-related knowledge through replay or distillation techniques; however, they often incur high computational costs and inefficiency. This issue is further exacerbated in real-world scenarios where labeled data for new classes is scarce. In this paper, we propose a novel lightweight plastic-memory framework specifically designed for few-shot incremental learning on graphs. The core idea of our framework is the construction of a plastic-memory module that evolves over time, continuously updating and expanding its memory to accommodate new classes while retaining previously learned knowledge. In contrast to existing techniques, our memory module is both lightweight and effective, featuring an innovative evolving micro-clustering structure that dynamically updates representations of class prototypes, sub-prototypes, and their interaction weights. Building on this memory module, we introduce a memory-driven meta-learning framework that enhances adaptability to new tasks in its inner loop while maintaining stability for earlier tasks in the outer loop. Extensive experiments on four benchmark datasets demonstrate the framework's superior performance in balancing stability for old knowledge and adaptability to new knowledge.

</details>


### [142] [Evaluating Accuracy and Probabilistic Reliability of Zero-Shot Time Series Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25788)
*Panagiotis Michael,Moysis Symeonides,Demetris Trihinas*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A benchmark study evaluating six Time Series Foundation Models (TSFMs) on energy, traffic, and financial datasets, revealing a fundamental trade-off between predictive accuracy and probabilistic calibration, with xLSTM offering robust calibration while patch-based transformers excel in accuracy but suffer from calibration issues at long horizons.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing TSFMs research overlooks the trade-offs between predictive accuracy and probabilistic calibration, which is critical for real-world deployment where both point predictions and uncertainty quantification matter.

Method: Benchmark study comparing six TSFMs against statistical baselines and a supervised DL model across energy, traffic, and financial datasets.

Result: TSFMs outperform statistical methods and supervised models, but exhibit a trade-off: xLSTM provides robust probabilistic calibration across horizons; patch-based transformers offer competitive accuracy but have calibration issues at long horizons; transformer-based models show context saturation points for optimal zero-shot reasoning.

Conclusion: Evidence-based guidance for practitioners to balance generalization and uncertainty quantification when deploying TSFMs in real-world applications.

Abstract: Time Series Foundation Models (TSFMs) promise a paradigm shift toward zero-shot forecasting by eliminating task-specific training. However, existing works often overlook trade-offs between predictive accuracy and probabilistic calibration. This paper presents a benchmark study of six TSFMs evaluated on energy, traffic, and financial datasets. We contrast their performance against statistical baselines and a supervised DL model. The study reveals that while TSFMs outperform statistical methods and supervised models, they are subject to a fundamental trade-off between point accuracy and probabilistic reliability. Specifically, xLSTM architectures provide robust probabilistic calibration across horizons. In contrast, patch-based transformers offer competitive accuracy but face calibration issues at long horizons, while transformer-based models exhibit context saturation points for optimal zero-shot reasoning. These findings offer evidence-based guidance for balancing generalization and uncertainty quantification in real-world deployments.

</details>


### [143] [Latest Exact Match Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25802)
*Moritz Brösamle*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LEMA (Latest Exact Match Attention) is a transformer attention variant where queries and keys are binarized, and each query attends only to the latest exactly matching key. It establishes equivalence with word-RAMs computationally, while demonstrating strong performance on associative recall and language modeling tasks with efficient constant-time inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses the gap between transformer attention mechanisms and word-RAM computational models. Prior hard attention variants were either too restrictive or lacked efficient bidirectional simulation. The goal is to create an attention mechanism that has theoretical equivalence with word-RAMs while maintaining practical trainability and good performance on sequence modeling tasks.

Method: The authors propose: (1) LEMA mechanism with binarized queries/keys and exact-match-only attention; (2) Theoretical proof showing LEMA transformers can simulate word-RAMs and vice versa; (3) Training method using straight-through estimator for binarization and soft attention surrogate annealed toward LEMA; (4) Dictionary-based inference implementation keeping dictionaries in main memory.

Result: LEMA models on associative recall tasks outperform gated DeltaNet (GDN) with growing state storage. LEMA language models with 834M parameters match softmax transformers of ~half their size in loss. They excel at recalling repeated rare phrases and needle-retrieval over longer distances than GDN, though behind softmax transformers. Inference achieves constant generation speed comparable to GDN.

Conclusion: LEMA establishes a close computational correspondence with word-RAMs in both compute and memory. The training method enables effective learning with non-differentiable operations. While not matching softmax transformers on all tasks, LEMA offers a compelling trade-off with growing state, constant-time inference, and theoretical equivalence to word-RAMs, making it a promising alternative for efficient sequence modeling.

Abstract: We introduce latest exact match attention (LEMA), an attention variant for transformers where queries and keys are binarized and each query attends only to the latest exactly matching key. We prove that LEMA transformers with chain of thought can simulate word-RAMs, as was recently shown for the less restrictive rightmost hard attention. In contrast to prior hard attention variants, the restriction to exact matches enables an efficient converse direction: word-RAMs can simulate LEMA transformers at a cost per token independent of the context length. Together, these results yield a close correspondence between the two computational models in terms of both compute and memory. Beyond the theory, we propose a training method for LEMA transformers that handles their non-differentiable operations with a straight-through estimator for the binarization and a soft attention surrogate annealed towards LEMA. On a synthetic associative recall task, LEMA models trained this way use their growing state to store and recall a large number of associations, outperforming gated DeltaNet (GDN) with its fixed state size. As a first scaling test, we train LEMA language models with up to 834 million parameters. They match softmax transformers of around half their size in loss and, on repeated rare phrases and a needle-retrieval task, remain behind softmax transformers but recall across longer distances than GDN models of comparable size. Finally, we implement dictionary-based inference for LEMA transformers and show constant generation speed comparable to GDN despite their growing state, with the dictionaries residing in main memory rather than VRAM. Code is available at https://github.com/moritzbroe/latest_exact_match_attention.

</details>


### [144] [Auditing Proxy-Based Validation Across Text Spans](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25808)
*Daein Weon,Dong Ho Kang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper identifies a critical validation issue in NLP evaluation: when evaluation scores and proxy labels are computed from the same text span, their agreement may stem from shared surface evidence rather than the semantic construct the proxy represents. The authors propose a "validation contract" framework requiring off-span re-evaluation and demonstrate the problem through HotpotQA and OR-Bench experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The research addresses a fundamental flaw in proxy-based validation for NLP systems. When scores and proxy labels are computed from overlapping text spans, surface-level similarities (e.g., recurring phrases, templates) can create false agreement between the score and the proxy, even when neither accurately reflects the target construct (e.g., actual correctness or refusal behavior).

Method: The authors introduce a "validation contract" framework requiring explicit declaration of: (1) the score, (2) its span, (3) the proxy, and (4) the target construct. They re-evaluate proxy rules strictly outside the scored span. Experiments include: (1) HotpotQA correctness experiments varying shared text boundaries (50-character vs 120-character prefixes), and (2) OR-Bench experiments with template suppression and matched-volume deletion.

Result: In HotpotQA experiments, at 50-character prefix, the score agrees with proxy +0.184 better than with correctness, but this gap collapses to +0.045 at 120 characters. The score predicts answer string appearance (AUC 0.634) while equivalence test shows chance agreement with correctness. On OR-Beck, suppressing recurring opening templates removes most score's association with refusal proxy, while construct agreement stays at chance. Only 3 of 11 external contracts support off-span control, and no sampled routing studies released necessary generations.

Conclusion: The authors call for standardized proxy-based validation reporting: (1) declare the span each label is read from, (2) report construct agreement alongside proxy agreement, and (3) release generations enabling proxy re-reading off the scored span. This addresses widespread validation issues in NLP evaluation where surface artifacts rather than semantic understanding drive apparent score-proxy agreement.

Abstract: Evaluation scores are often validated by their agreement with inexpensive proxy labels. When the score and the proxy are computed from the same text span, however, that agreement can arise from surface evidence the two share rather than from the semantic construct the proxy is meant to represent. We make the distinction explicit by declaring the score, its span, the proxy and the target construct as a validation contract, then re-evaluating that proxy rule strictly outside the scored span. In a controlled HotpotQA correctness experiment varying only the shared text boundary, the score agrees with its proxy far better than with correctness at a 50-character prefix: the gap is +0.184, collapsing to at most +0.045 from 120 characters onward. At that short prefix the score still predicts whether the answer string appears later (AUC 0.634) while an equivalence test places its agreement with correctness at chance, so the reported proxy agreement does not establish that the score ranks correctness. On OR-Bench, suppressing each model's recurring opening templates removes most of the score's association with the refusal proxy, while matched-volume deletion removes almost none and construct agreement stays at chance. Only three of eleven external contracts support the off-span control, and none of the routing studies we sampled released the generations it needs. We therefore ask that a proxy-based validation claim declare the span each label is read from, report the construct agreement beside the proxy agreement, and release the generations that let the proxy be re-read off the scored span.

</details>


### [145] [You Only Need 2/3 of the Chosen Experts: An Empirical Study of Dynamic Expert Pruning in Fine-Grained MoE LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25809)
*Yuanteng Chen,Qiwei Lai,Chen Tianqi,Peisong Wang,Yuantian Shao,Nanxin Zeng,Zhilei Liu,Chuangyi Li,Jing Liu,Jian Cheng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper presents a systematic empirical study of fine-grained mixture-of-experts (MoE) architectures in LLMs, addressing three key questions about expert selection redundancy, pruning effectiveness, and model sensitivity. Through analysis of 12 checkpoints across 9 architecture families using 11 benchmarks, the authors find that expert selection is highly redundant—retaining ~67% of selected experts preserves 98.8% performance—enabling 1.2-1.7x speedup. Dynamic allocation methods show value only under aggressive pruning, while model size and modality significantly affect pruning sensitivity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fine-grained MoE architectures have become mainstream in open-weight LLMs with hundreds of experts, but existing evidence comes from coarser architectures and multiple-choice benchmarks. Three critical questions remained unanswered: (1) how redundant per-token expert selection is, (2) how effectively existing pruning methods exploit that redundancy, and (3) what governs a model's sensitivity to pruning.

Method: The authors conducted a systematic empirical study across 12 fine-grained MoE checkpoints spanning 9 architecture families, evaluated on a core suite of 11 benchmarks covering knowledge QA, mathematics, code generation, and general reasoning. They tested uniform retention (simple baseline) versus various published pruning rules at matched expert budgets across two serving backends.

Result: Key findings: (1) Expert selection is highly redundant—uniformly retaining about two-thirds of selected experts preserves 98.8% of unpruned performance on average; (2) This simple baseline requires only a one-integer change and delivers 1.2-1.7x measured speedup; (3) At conservative budgets, even the best published pruning rules differ from uniform truncation by under 1%; (4) Under aggressive pruning, the best rules recover up to 3.0% over uniform truncation, with gains concentrated in generative tasks; (5) Larger and thinking models are more resilient to pruning, while multimodal models are more vulnerable.

Conclusion: Fine-grained MoEs can dispense with a significant portion of expert computation—approximately one-third of selected experts can be pruned with minimal performance loss. Dynamic allocation earns its complexity only under aggressive pruning budgets; at conservative budgets, simple uniform truncation suffices. Model characteristics (size, thinking capability, modality) significantly influence pruning sensitivity. These findings inform practical deployment decisions and future pruning method development for MoE-based LLMs.

Abstract: Fine-grained mixture-of-experts (MoE) architectures have become a mainstream design for open-weight LLMs, with hundreds of experts and increasingly many selected per token. This shift makes dynamic expert pruning an attractive route to cheaper inference. Yet existing evidence comes largely from coarser architectures and likelihood-scored multiple-choice benchmarks, leaving three central questions open in the fine-grained regime: how redundant per-token expert selection is, how effectively existing pruning methods exploit that redundancy, and what governs a model's sensitivity to pruning. We fill this gap with a systematic empirical study of twelve fine-grained MoE checkpoints spanning nine architecture families, with a core suite of eleven benchmarks covering knowledge QA, mathematics, code generation, and general reasoning. We find that expert selection is far more redundant than the field's operating points assume: uniformly retaining about two thirds of the selected experts preserves 98.8% of unpruned performance on average, requiring only a one-integer change and delivering 1.2-1.7x measured speedup across two serving backends. This simple baseline leaves little room for dynamic allocation at conservative budgets: even the best published rules differ from it by under 1% at matched expert budgets. Their value emerges under aggressive pruning, where the best rules recover up to 3.0% over uniform truncation, with gains concentrated in the generative tasks that suffer the sharpest degradation. Sensitivity to aggressive pruning also depends on the model: larger and thinking models are more resilient, whereas multimodal models are more vulnerable. Together, these findings reveal how much expert computation fine-grained MoEs can dispense with, and establish when dynamic allocation earns its complexity, informing both practical deployment and future pruning methods.

</details>


### [146] [Multi-View Fair Clustering Guided by Cross-View Sensitive Information Discrepancy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25811)
*Mudi Jiang,Jiahui Zhou,Xinying Liu,Zengyou He,Zhikui Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses fairness in multi-view clustering by proposing a framework that estimates sensitive dependence per view and uses bias-ranked asymmetric alignment to prevent views with high sensitive information from influencing those with lower sensitive dependence. Fairness regularization is applied to consensus soft assignments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing fair multi-view clustering methods treat different views uniformly without considering their varying levels of sensitive information. This can lead to less sensitive-dependent views being negatively influenced by highly sensitive-dependent views during cross-view learning, degrading both clustering performance and fairness.

Method: The paper proposes: (1) Estimating sensitive dependence of each view, (2) Developing a bias-ranked asymmetric alignment mechanism where views with higher sensitive dependence learn from those with lower sensitive dependence, (3) Using cross-view discrepancies to adaptively regulate alignment, and (4) Imposing fairness regularization on consensus soft assignments.

Result: The proposed method achieves favorable balance between clustering quality and group fairness on benchmark datasets.

Conclusion: The method successfully addresses the cross-view sensitive information discrepancy problem in fair multi-view clustering, providing a more nuanced approach to fairness than uniform treatment of views.

Abstract: Multi-view clustering (MVC) aims to uncover latent cluster structures by exploiting complementary information from multiple views. Despite substantial progress in clustering performance, fairness remains an important concern when MVC is applied to socially sensitive scenarios. Recent fair multi-view clustering methods have introduced fairness constraints into representation learning or clustering assignments. However, these methods generally treat different views under a largely uniform fairness mechanism, without explicitly distinguishing their varying levels of sensitive dependence during cross-view learning. In practice, different views may encode substantially different levels of sensitive information. Ignoring such cross-view discrepancy can allow highly sensitive-dependent views to influence less sensitive-dependent ones during cross-view learning, potentially degrading both clustering performance and fairness. To address this issue, we propose a novel multi-view fair clustering framework guided by cross-view sensitive information discrepancy. Specifically, we estimate the sensitive dependence of each view and develop a bias-ranked asymmetric alignment mechanism that encourages views with higher sensitive dependence to learn from those with lower sensitive dependence, while cross-view discrepancies are further exploited to adaptively regulate the alignment process. Moreover, fairness regularization is imposed on the consensus soft assignments to further promote group fairness. Extensive experiments on benchmark datasets demonstrate that the proposed method achieves a favorable balance between clustering quality and group fairness.

</details>


### [147] [CacheDyG: Decoupling Temporal Propagation for Efficient Dynamic Graph Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25814)
*PinHeng Zong,Ye Yuan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CacheDyG is a novel cache-refine framework that decouples temporal propagation from parameter updates in dynamic graph learning by using non-trainable buffers to store graph-aware node-time representations, significantly reducing trainable parameters and runtime while improving predictive performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dynamic graph neural networks typically intertwine temporal graph propagation with every optimization epoch and maintain large trainable representations for each node-time pair, causing substantial training and parameter overhead due to repeatedly recomputing largely unchanged historical structures.

Method: CacheDyG decouples temporal propagation from routine parameter updates by constructing a time-ordered temporal dependency cache storing graph-aware node-time representations in non-trainable buffers. During standard training epochs, it reads from the cache and updates only a lightweight cache refiner, an adaptive residual gate, and the link predictor. Selective cache refresh keeps cached representations aligned with the supervised objective while avoiding epoch-wise sparse propagation.

Result: On five dynamic graph benchmarks, CacheDyG achieves more competitive predictive performance than baselines while using substantially fewer trainable parameters and lower runtime.

Conclusion: Cache-based decoupling provides an effective principle for scalable dynamic graph learning, enabling efficient training without sacrificing predictive accuracy.

Abstract: Dynamic graphs are widely used to model time-evolving relational systems in real-world applications. Dynamic graph neural networks provide an effective framework for capturing both structural dependencies and temporal dynamics in such data. However, they typically intertwine temporal graph propagation with every optimization epoch and often maintain large trainable representations for each node-time pair. This design repeatedly recomputes largely unchanged historical structures, leading to substantial training and parameter overhead. To address this critical issue, we propose CacheDyG, a Cache-refine framework for efficient Dynamic Graph learning. Specifically, it decouples temporal propagation from routine parameter updates by constructing a time-ordered temporal dependency cache that stores graph-aware node-time representations in non-trainable buffers. During standard training epochs, CacheDyG reads from the cache and updates only a lightweight cache refiner, an adaptive residual gate, and the link predictor. Selective cache refresh further keeps cached representations aligned with the supervised objective while avoiding epoch-wise sparse propagation. Experiments on five dynamic graph benchmarks show that CacheDyG adopts substantially fewer trainable parameters and lower runtime to obtain more competitive predictive performance than baselines. These results demonstrate that cache-based decoupling provides an effective principle for scalable dynamic graph learning.

</details>


### [148] [Protocol before progress: leakage-aware evaluation of AIS trajectory prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25827)
*Zobeir Raisi,Vali Mohammad Nazarzehi Had*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates evaluation protocols for vessel-trajectory prediction models using AIS data, revealing that reported performance gains often come from data leakage rather than architectural improvements. The authors propose a leakage-aware protocol with vessel-, time-, and region-disjoint splits and test it on Danish and US Gulf coast traffic data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors observed that reported gains in vessel-trajectory prediction are credited to new architectures, but the evaluation protocol is rarely examined as a source of error reduction. They aim to audit whether performance improvements come from genuine architectural advances or from evaluation artifacts/data leakage.

Method: The authors built a leakage-aware protocol with vessel-, time-, and region-disjoint splits. They applied this protocol to two corpora: 31 days of Danish national AIS traffic and 30 days of US Gulf coast traffic off Houston and Galveston. They audited three models: TrAISformer, GATransformer, and controlled AISFormer-inspired reconstructions.

Result: Three protocol effects appeared in both corpora: (1) TrAISformer's best-of-16 oracle decoder lowers error by 2.1-3.2x relative to greedy decoding; (2) a split sharing vessels lowers greedy error by 23-25% at one hour vs 2% for a compact 0.43M-parameter encoder; (3) a region-disjoint split raises TrAISformer's one-hour error from 2.2 to 24.6 km on US corpus because 99.9% of test contexts fall in unseen longitude bins. GATransformer's graph attention provides no measurable benefit, while its waterway feature is worth 12-22%. Time-disjoint split effect is unstable across corpora (13% vs 2%).

Conclusion: Architectural mechanisms matter less than evaluation protocols. Most reported performance gains likely come from data leakage in evaluation rather than genuine architectural improvements. The authors release their splits and code for reproducible research.

Abstract: Reported gains in vessel-trajectory prediction from Automatic Identification System (AIS) data are credited to new architectures, but the evaluation protocol is rarely measured as a source of error reduction. We build a leakage-aware protocol with vessel-, time- and region-disjoint splits and apply it to two corpora with different traffic: 31 days of Danish national AIS traffic and 30 days of US Gulf coast traffic off Houston and Galveston. On both, we audit TrAISformer, GATransformer, and controlled AISFormer-inspired reconstructions. Three protocol effects appear in both corpora. First, TrAISformer's best-of-16 oracle decoder lowers error by a factor of 2.1-3.2 relative to greedy decoding. Second, a split that shares vessels lowers its greedy error by 23-25% at one hour, against 2% or less for a compact 0.43 M-parameter encoder. Third, a region-disjoint split raises TrAISformer's one-hour error from 2.2 to 24.6 km on the US corpus, because 99.9% of the test contexts fall in longitude bins never seen in training; the encoder built on local offsets is unaffected by this. Architectural mechanisms matter less: GATransformer's graph attention gives no measurable benefit on either corpus, while its waterway feature is worth 12-22%. The effect of a time-disjoint split is not stable across corpora (13% versus 2%). We release the splits and code.

</details>


### [149] [In-Context Guidance: Learning Inter-Task Synergies via Numerical Foundational Models for Few-Shot Multitask Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25836)
*Tingyang Wei,Haofeng Wu,Jiao Liu,Zhao Wei,Puay Siew Tan,Yew-Soon Ong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ICG-MTO is a novel framework that leverages numerical foundational models to improve inter-task coupling estimation in few-shot multi-task optimization scenarios. It uses in-context learning to infer guidance signals that characterize predictive relationships among tasks, providing regularization during data-scarce early stages of optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-task optimization (MTO) often suffers from inaccurate inter-task relationship estimation under limited evaluation budgets, leading to negative transfer. The core problem is that conventional methods rely solely on scarce observed data, which is insufficient for reliable coupling estimation in few-shot scenarios.

Method: The framework operates through three stages: (1) constructing an algorithm-specific in-context query from evaluated solutions, (2) using a frozen foundational model to infer a guidance signal characterizing predictive relationships among tasks, and (3) translating this signal into algorithm-specific guidance for maximum-a-posteriori coupling estimation. Instantiated in multitask Bayesian optimization (ICG-MTBO) using directional fitness-class queries and in MFEA-II using decision-space-overlap queries.

Result: Experiments across synthetic benchmarks and a real-world robot arm control problem demonstrate the effectiveness and generality of ICG-MTO for few-shot multitask optimization, with evaluations under different acquisition functions and evolutionary multitasking.

Conclusion: ICG-MTO effectively addresses the challenge of limited evaluation budgets in MTO by leveraging foundational models to provide regularization during early optimization stages. The approach gradually relinquishes control as task-specific observations accumulate, showing promise for few-shot multitask optimization problems.

Abstract: Multi-task optimization (MTO) addresses a set of optimization tasks simultaneously, often suffering from inaccurate inter-task relationship estimation under limited evaluation budgets, leading to negative transfer. This paper introduces In-Context Guidance Multitask Optimization (ICG-MTO), a novel framework that leverages numerical foundational models to improve inter-task coupling estimation in few-shot scenarios. Unlike conventional methods that rely solely on scarce observed data, ICG-MTO employs a frozen foundational model to infer auxiliary guidance through in-context learning. The framework operates through three stages: constructing an algorithm-specific in-context query from evaluated solutions, using the foundational model to infer a guidance signal characterizing predictive relationships among tasks, and translating this signal into algorithm-specific guidance for maximum-a-posteriori coupling estimation. This approach provides regularization during the early, data-scarce stages of optimization and gradually relinquishes control as task-specific observations accumulate. We instantiate the framework in multitask Bayesian optimization as ICG-MTBO, using directional fitness-class queries to guide inter-task coupling estimation, and further instantiate it in MFEA-II using decision-space-overlap queries to guide random mating probability estimation. Experiments across synthetic benchmarks and a real-world robot arm control problem, together with evaluations under different acquisition functions and evolutionary multitasking, demonstrate the effectiveness and generality of ICG-MTO for few-shot multitask optimization.

</details>


### [150] [Gaussian Flow-Matching Schedules: Implications for Sampling and Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25839)
*Arsène Claustre,Hugo Negrel,Claire Boyer,Kimia Nadjahi,Eric Vanden-Eijnden*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper analyzes flow-matching schedules in generative modeling, showing how variance paths and factorization choices independently control sampling dynamics and regression variance for centered commuting Gaussians. The work connects finite-step Euler accuracy with drift bounds and provides closed-form solutions for optimal variance minimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Flow matching is a popular generative modeling approach, but the relationship between scheduling choices and both sampling quality and training variance is not fully understood. This work aims to disentangle these design choices and provide theoretical guidance for optimal schedule selection.

Method: The authors analyze the mathematical structure of flow matching for centered commuting Gaussians, decomposing the schedule into two independent components: a variance path (controlling intermediate laws and probability flow) and a factorization (controlling irreducible regression variance). They derive drift bounds for exact N-step sampling and obtain closed-form factorizations.

Result: The paper shows that variance path fully determines the intermediate laws and probability flow, while factorization leaves this flow unchanged but controls irreducible regression variance. A necessary drift bound is derived connecting geodesic and logarithmic paths for exact N-step sampling. Closed-form factorizations are provided that either minimize time-averaged regression variance or make it constant along the path.

Conclusion: For centered commuting Gaussians, flow-matching schedules can be decomposed into independent design choices with clear trade-offs. The variance path controls sampling dynamics while factorization controls regression variance, enabling principled schedule selection based on specific optimization objectives.

Abstract: Flow-matching schedules affect both sampling dynamics and the variance of the regression target. For centered commuting Gaussians, we show that a direction-dependent schedule decomposes into two independent design choices: a variance path, which fully determines the intermediate laws and probability flow, and a factorization, which leaves this flow unchanged while controlling irreducible regression variance. On the sampling side, we analyze finite-step Euler accuracy and derive a necessary drift bound for exact N -step sampling, connecting the geodesic and the logarithmic path. On the training side, for any fixed path, we derive closed-form factorizations that either minimize time-averaged regression variance or make it constant along the path.

</details>


### [151] [Neural Approximation by Function Composition: Rigidity and Doubly Exponential Convergence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25874)
*Wentao Huang,Haizhang Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates how the composition of functions in deep neural networks creates approximation power, specifically through geometrically weighted sums of iterates of a scalar generator function. It establishes a rigidity theorem showing that piecewise linear generators can only represent at most quadratic C^3 functions, and constructs smooth generators achieving doubly exponential error decay for neural network approximation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding why deep neural networks are powerful approximators is a fundamental question. The authors focus on the mechanism of function composition itself, building on the tent-map construction (using x - x^2) and recursive representations from prior work by Yarotsky and W. E. This work aims to reveal limitations and develop better methods for analyzing approximation power of deep networks.

Method: The paper uses two main approaches: (1) Rigidity analysis via Taylor expansion and fixed-point arguments to prove the limitation theorem for piecewise linear generators; (2) Constructive analysis using exact remainder identities to design smooth generators with optimal depth allocation for various function classes.

Result: Key results include: (1) A rigidity theorem proving that continuous piecewise linear generators with finite segments can only represent at most quadratic C^3 functions; (2) For non-affine quadratics, the geometric factor must be at least 1/4; (3) A smooth generator construction achieving doubly exponential error decay O(e^{-cL^{1/d}}) for power series with absolutely summable coefficients on [-1,1]^d.

Conclusion: The findings demonstrate that generator dynamics and remainder estimates govern depth allocation and approximation rates in deep neural networks. The rigidity theorem reveals fundamental limitations of the tent-map approach, while the constructive results provide optimal error decay rates for smooth function approximation, offering new theoretical insights into neural network expressivity.

Abstract: Deep neural networks approximate functions by composing affine maps with nonlinear activations, but how composition itself creates approximation power is not yet fully understood. We investigate a fundamental mechanism: geometrically weighted sums of iterates of a single scalar generator function. This mechanism underpins the classical tent-map construction of the function \(x - x^2\) and related recursive representations used by Yarotsky, W. E, et al., to analyze the approximation powers of deep neural networks.
  First, we establish a rigidity theorem: for continuous piecewise linear generators with a finite number of segments, any \(C^3\) function that can be represented in this way is at most quadratic. For non-affine quadratic functions, the geometric factor is at least $1/4$. This result both reveals limitations of the tent-map approach and complements existing methods based on hierarchical bases and recursive polynomial constructions. Second, using an exact remainder identity as guidance, we construct a smooth generator whose iterates yield doubly exponential error decay in total depth for square approximation and, through multiplication modules, for each fixed polynomial. For power series with absolutely summable coefficients on \([-1,1]^d\), distributing depth according to monomial degree yields a uniform approximation error of order \(O(e^{-cL^{1/d}})\) on each interior cube. These findings demonstrate how generator dynamics and remainder estimates govern depth allocation and approximation rates of deep neural networks.

</details>


### [152] [Evaluating the Effectiveness of SechKAN on 1D Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25876)
*Hoang-Thang Ta*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SechKAN is a Kolmogorov-Arnold Network variant using hyperbolic secant (sech) functions as basis functions with 1D projection for parameter reduction. It achieves competitive performance on 1D classification tasks (UCI HAR, ElectricDevices, Crop) compared to EfficientKAN, MLP, CNN1D, ResNet1D, and DSCNN1D, with particularly strong results on Crop. Performance is influenced by grid size and normalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Kolmogorov-Arnold representation theorem (KART) has inspired Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks (KANs) for various applications. This paper investigates SechKAN, a KAN variant, to evaluate its effectiveness on 1D classification tasks compared to existing network architectures.

Method: SechKAN uses hyperbolic secant (sech) functions as basis functions with a 1D projection technique to reduce the number of parameters to a level comparable to MLPs. The model was evaluated on three 1D classification datasets (UCI HAR, ElectricDevices, Crop) and compared with EfficientKAN, MLP, CNN1D, ResNet1D, and DSCNN1D under approximately comparable parameter budgets. Ablation studies examined grid size and normalization effects.

Result: SechKAN achieves competitive performance across all three datasets, with particularly strong performance on Crop. Ablation studies show that grid size and normalization affect performance, indicating that SechKAN's effectiveness depends on the specific dataset and architectural choices.

Conclusion: SechKAN is a viable KAN variant for 1D classification tasks, achieving competitive results especially on Crop dataset. The performance is sensitive to hyperparameters such as grid size and normalization, suggesting that proper tuning is essential for optimal results.

Abstract: The connection between the Kolmogorov-Arnold representation theorem (KART) and neural network design has led to the development of Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks (KANs), with applications ranging from STEM problems to AI tasks. In this paper, we investigate the effectiveness of a KAN variant, SechKAN, which relies on hyperbolic secant (sech) functions as basis functions, with a 1D projection to reduce the number of parameters to a level comparable to MLPs. We evaluate SechKAN on three 1D classification datasets: UCI Human Activity Recognition (UCI HAR), ElectricDevices, and Crop, and compare it with several effective networks, including EfficientKAN, MLP, CNN1D, ResNet1D, and DSCNN1D, using approximately comparable parameter budgets. The results indicate that SechKAN achieves competitive performance across the three datasets, with particularly strong performance on Crop. Ablation studies further show that grid size and normalization affect performance, suggesting that SechKAN's effectiveness depends on the dataset and architectural choices. Our source code and experimental implementation are publicly available at: https://github.com/hoangthangta/SechKAN_1D.

</details>


### [153] [AURA: Angular Update Rate Adaptation for training complex-valued neural networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25914)
*Enrico Ballini,Allan Peter Engsig-Karup,Tito Andriollo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AURA is a per-parameter step-size adaptation method for complex-valued neural networks (CVNNs) that measures agreement between consecutive updates in length, alignment, and rotation sense. It can be added to any first-order optimizer without changing its update direction, requiring only inexpensive vector operations per step.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: First-order optimizers for CVNNs inherit step-size rules from real-valued cases, ignoring valuable angular information in the complex plane. Existing methods lack proper step-size adaptation mechanisms that account for the complex domain's geometric properties.

Method: AURA measures agreement between consecutive updates of each complex parameter across three dimensions: length, alignment, and sense of rotation. The step size is enlarged when updates are consistent and reduced when inconsistent. It requires no additional gradient evaluations and uses only cheap vector operations per step.

Result: AURA improves convergence of its base optimizer (Adam, Muon) in most test cases with small per-step overhead. The method was tested on four cases of increasing complexity: scalar complex function approximation, and three others involving physics-informed training. Conditions under which AURA fails to improve performance were identified.

Conclusion: AURA provides an effective step-size adaptation mechanism for complex-valued neural networks that can be layered onto existing first-order optimizers. It successfully leverages angular information in the complex plane to improve convergence, though its effectiveness varies depending on the problem characteristics.

Abstract: Complex-valued neural networks (CVNNs) are increasingly adopted for complex-valued data; however, they are often trained with first-order optimizers inherited from the real-valued case. The efficiency of these methods depends largely on the step size, and their step-size rules ignore the angular information available in the complex plane. We address step-size adaptation in the complex domain by introducing AURA (Angular Update Rate Adaptation), a per-parameter step-size adaptation that can be added on top of any first-order optimizer, and removed from it, without altering its update direction. AURA measures the agreement between consecutive updates of each complex parameter, in length, alignment, and sense of rotation, and enlarges the step when they are consistent and reduces it when they are not. It requires no additional gradient evaluations and only inexpensive vector operations per step. We combine AURA with Adam and Muon and compare the resulting methods with well-known first-order optimizers on four test cases of increasing complexity, ranging from the approximation of scalar complex functions to physics-informed training. Fully connected neural networks are used throughout this work. All hyperparameters other than the step size are held fixed across test cases; for one case, we also tune the hyperparameters of each optimizer under the same budget. Our empirical tests show that AURA improves the convergence of its base optimizer in most cases with a small per-step overhead, and we identify the conditions under which it fails to do so.

</details>


### [154] [Beyond Scalar Sensitivity: Activation-Aware Mixed-Precision LLM Quantization with Cross-Layer Refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25916)
*Akihiro Yoshida,Yuma Ichikawa*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses mixed-precision weight quantization for LLMs, which is typically formulated as a Multiple-Choice Knapsack Problem (MCKP). The authors prove that scalar sensitivity proxies incur multiplicative distortion up to √(κ(A)κ(B)) relative to full activation-aware quadratic, and propose CASA, a two-phase method using activation-aware metrics and cross-layer local search to achieve lower perplexity than baselines at ultra-low bit-widths.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing mixed-precision quantization solvers rely on scalar sensitivity proxies that collapse each weight matrix's Hessian into a single number and treat modules independently. This approach is theoretically flawed because the optimal scalar proxy can incur distortion up to √(κ(A)κ(B)), where κ(A) and κ(B) are condition numbers of input- and output-side Hessian factors. This bound varies from 10^1 to 10^13 for typical LLM modules, making inter-module sensitivity ranking unreliable, especially at ultra-low bit-widths.

Method: The authors propose Cross-layer Activation-aware Sensitivity Allocation (CASA), a two-phase method: (1) Stage 1 replaces the scalar proxy with an activation-aware metric derived from Kronecker-factored Hessian, reducing MCKP to a form with closed-form solution for continuous relaxation; (2) Stage 2 uses cross-layer-aware local search to evaluate bit-width updates using end-to-end model loss.

Result: Experiments on multiple LLMs across different bit budgets show that CASA achieves lower perplexity than latest scalar-proxy baselines, especially at ultra-low bit-widths (<3 bits per weight). The performance gain in zero-shot accuracy tracks the per-model average condition-number over modules, confirming the distortion bound as a practical indicator of scalar-proxy failure.

Conclusion: The theoretical bound on scalar-proxy distortion (√(κ(A)κ(B))) is a practical indicator of when scalar-proxy methods fail. CASA effectively addresses these limitations by using activation-aware metrics and cross-layer optimization, achieving superior quantization performance at ultra-low bit-widths while maintaining theoretical tractability.

Abstract: Mixed-precision weight quantization is commonly formulated as a Multiple-Choice Knapsack Problem (MCKP), yet existing solvers rely on scalar sensitivity proxies that collapse each weight matrix's Hessian into a single number and treat every module independently. We prove that even the optimal scalar proxy incurs multiplicative distortion up to $\sqrt{κ(\mathbf{A})κ(\mathbf{B})}$ relative to the full activation-aware quadratic, where $κ(\mathbf{A})$ and $κ(\mathbf{B})$ denote the condition numbers of the input- and output-side Hessian factors. This bound varies from $10^1$ to $10^{13}$ for typical LLM modules, making inter-module sensitivity ranking unreliable. To address these limitations, we propose Cross-layer Activation-aware Sensitivity Allocation (CASA), a two-phase method. In Stage 1, the scalar proxy is replaced by an activation-aware metric derived from the Kronecker-factored Hessian, reducing the MCKP to a form whose continuous relaxation admits a closed-form solution. In Stage 2, a cross-layer-aware local search evaluates bit-width updates using the end-to-end model loss. Experiments on multiple LLMs across different bit budgets show that CASA achieves lower perplexity than the latest scalar-proxy baselines, especially at ultra-low bit-widths ($<3$ bits per weight). Moreover, the performance gain in zero-shot accuracy tracks the per-model average condition-number over modules, confirming the distortion bound as a practical indicator of scalar-proxy failure.

</details>


### [155] [Certified Against Which Oracle? Execution Labels Set the Reported Risk of Conformal Abstention for Text-to-SQL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25938)
*Jiamiao Liu,Dewen Qiao,Yu Zhang,Xuetao Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates the reliability of conformal abstention certificates for text-to-SQL models, revealing that these certificates are only as trustworthy as the correctness labels used for calibration. The authors show that using a different oracle (the benchmark's test suite) instead of the default shipped database significantly increases the reported risk, and that expert-annotated labels reveal even higher risk than either oracle reports.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The uncertainty pipelines for text-to-SQL rely on execution consistency, which uses correctness labels from a single database that comes with the benchmark. The authors suspected this oracle is too lenient and wanted to investigate whether the certificates are truly reliable when evaluated under stricter or different standards.

Method: The authors conducted a preregistered intervention on Spider-Realistic benchmark, replacing the default database with the benchmark's distilled multi-instance test suite. They tested across four SQL-specialist checkpoints and two split schemes. They also obtained blinded labels from two SQL experts and used AI to analyze the nature of errors made by the stricter oracle.

Result: Swapping the oracle raises the certificate's held-out risk by 2.73 to 10.23 points above what its own labels report. Under expert labels, a certificate calibrated at nominal 0.10 carries 20.0 and 17.2 points of risk. The stricter oracle makes errors in both directions: it rejects some correct answers and accepts some incorrect ones. An AI census found semantic errors in 25-33% of rejected answers, with the rest attributed to underspecified questions, synthetic instances, or suspected reference-query defects. Every execution-consistency score looks better under the labels of the oracle that built its clusters (16/16 combinations). Using expert labels to build clusters instead of shipped-database clusters raises AUROC by 6.96 points on one checkpoint and 1.53 on the other.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that conformal abstention certificates for text-to-SQL should be reported with both oracles, and oracle-relative differences should only be interpreted as semantic risk after the benchmark is audited. Consistency scores should be evaluated under an oracle that did not build them. The authors provide specific recommendations for improving evaluation methodology in this field.

Abstract: A conformal abstention certificate for text-to-SQL is only as truthful as the correctness labels it is calibrated on. The uncertainty pipelines that read confidence off execution consistency take those labels from the single database a benchmark ships, an oracle known to be lenient. We run a preregistered intervention on Spider-Realistic, swapping that database for the benchmark's distilled multi-instance test suite. Across four SQL-specialist checkpoints and two split schemes, the swap raises the certificate's held-out risk 2.73 to 10.23 points above the risk its own labels report. Neither oracle reports the risk experts assign. Under blinded labels from two SQL experts, a certificate calibrated at a nominal 0.10 carries 20.0 and 17.2 points of risk on two checkpoints. The stricter oracle errs in both directions: most of the answers it rejects are not judged wrong, and some of those it accepts are. An AI-assigned census of what it rejects finds a semantic error in a quarter to a third of them, depending on the population. It attributes most of the rest to underspecified questions, synthetic instances or suspected reference-query defects, a flag supported by a preregistered blinded expert audit. The oracle also decides how a confidence score is judged. Every execution-consistency score looks better under the labels of the oracle that built its clusters, in 16 of 16 combinations. Under expert labels, building such a score on suite clusters instead of shipped-database clusters raises its area under the ROC curve (AUROC) by 6.96 points on one checkpoint and 1.53 on the other. On the second, the expert interval excludes the 8.3 points the suite labels report. A certificate should be reported with both oracles, and an oracle-relative difference read as semantic risk only after the benchmark is audited. A consistency score should be evaluated under an oracle that did not build it.

</details>


### [156] [Exploring Solver-Level Warmstarting for Neural Network Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25962)
*Annelot Bosman,Minghao Liu,Marta Kwiatkowska,Holger Hoos,Jan van Rijn*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper explores solver-level warmstarting for neural network verification to reduce computational time by exploiting information from previous solutions. The approach is tested across various modifications including perturbation radii, input data, and network architectures, showing significant verification speedups and ability to solve previously intractable instances.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neural network verification is computationally intractable (NP-complete) even for common adversarial robustness specifications. The authors aim to address this computational challenge by leveraging information from previous verification runs to speed up subsequent verifications.

Method: The authors implemented a generalizable pipeline that applies solver-level warmstarting to state-of-the-art neural network verifiers. They systematically tested the approach by modifying multiple properties: perturbation radii, input data samples, and network architectures themselves. This allows the solver to reuse computation from previous solutions rather than starting from scratch.

Result: Warmstarting significantly reduces verification time in most cases. Additionally, warmstarting enables successful verification of instances that could not be solved within the original time limit when starting from scratch.

Conclusion: Solver-level warmstarting is a practical and effective approach for accelerating neural network verification. The method is generalizable and adaptable to existing verification tools, providing substantial performance improvements while maintaining the ability to verify previously unsolvable instances.

Abstract: Neural network verification has become a key tool for providing formal guarantees on the behaviour of neural networks. However, many verification problems remain computationally intractable in the worst case: even for common adversarial robustness specifications, verification is NP-complete. Here, we explore the application of solver-level warmstarting for neural network verification to exploit information from previous solutions. We study the effect on running time as several properties are modified, including perturbation radii, input data and the networks themselves, using a pipeline that is generalisable and potentially adaptable to state-of-the-art verifiers. Our results show that warmstarting can significantly reduce verification time in most cases. Moreover, warmstarting enables the successful verification of instances that could not be solved from scratch within the given time limit.

</details>


### [157] [GeoPair: Geometry-Preserving Cross-Layer Factorization for Training-Free Transformer Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25963)
*Baher Mohammad,Ammar Ali,Stamatios Lefkimmiatis*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces a training-free framework for compressing transformer architectures by identifying structurally compatible projections and learning shared representations that preserve layer-specific calibration geometry, outperforming heuristic-based approaches across various architectures and modalities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current post-training compression methods for transformers either optimize layers in isolation or rely on heuristic grouping strategies that disregard layer-specific activation geometries, leading to suboptimal compression with potential loss of functional fidelity.

Method: The proposed framework sequentially optimizes cross-layer weight pairings and shared-dictionary factorizations. It identifies structurally compatible projections and learns a shared representation that preserves each layer's distinct calibration geometry. The method is coupled with structured sparsity to achieve efficient weight decompositions.

Result: The method achieves state-of-the-art results across diverse architectures, scales, and modalities, consistently outperforming independent structured weight decompositions and alternative pairwise weight factorizations that use heuristic grouping strategies.

Conclusion: By replacing heuristic engineering strategies with a convergent, optimization-driven pipeline, the work establishes a theoretically grounded foundation for scalable transformer compression across different modalities.

Abstract: Transformer architectures exhibit cross-layer redundancies, yet post-training compression pipelines typically optimize layers in isolation or rely on heuristic grouping strategies that disregard layer-specific activation geometries. We introduce a principled, training-free framework that sequentially optimizes cross-layer weight pairings and shared-dictionary factorizations. Rather than forcing weights of adjacent layers to share a basis or heuristically merging activation statistics, our approach identifies structurally compatible projections and learns a shared representation that better preserves each layer's distinct calibration geometry. Coupled with structured sparsity, this yields highly efficient weight decompositions without sacrificing functional fidelity. Across diverse architectures, scales, and modalities, our method achieves state-of-the-art results, consistently outperforming independent structured weight decompositions and alternative pairwise weight factorizations, which operate under heuristic grouping strategies. By replacing heuristic engineering strategies with a convergent, optimization-driven pipeline, we establish a theoretically grounded foundation for scalable, transformer compression across different modalities.

</details>


### [158] [Interweaving Marginals into Multivariate Sample Paths: Training-Free Dependence Construction for Probabilistic Time Series Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25980)
*Jinmyeong Choi,Jinkwan Jang,Seul Lee,Taesup Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper addresses a fundamental limitation in probabilistic time series foundation models: they provide accurate marginal predictions for each channel/horizon but fail to capture joint dependencies across multivariate trajectories. The authors propose treating dependence reconstruction as a separate post-processing task.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Probabilistic TSFMs produce coordinate-wise predictive distributions, but these marginals don't determine joint distributions over multivariate future trajectories. This limits their ability to capture correlations between different time series channels.

Method: The authors develop a training-free coupling method that combines frozen TSFM marginals into multivariate forecast sample paths. They fix the empirical marginal sample multiset at every channel-horizon coordinate to isolate the effect of coupling from marginal estimation quality.

Result: Historical temporal and channel relations substantially improve corresponding dependence diagnostics. This pattern persists when removing the fixed-marginal constraint, when sampling paths directly, and even under native multivariate backbone inference.

Conclusion: Dependence reconstruction should be treated as a distinct post-processing problem for probabilistic TSFMs, separate from the marginal prediction task. This approach allows better capture of multivariate correlations without requiring retraining of the base model.

Abstract: Probabilistic time series foundation models (TSFMs) provide coordinate-wise predictive distributions, but these marginals do not determine a joint distribution over multivariate future trajectories. We study training-free coupling of frozen TSFM marginals into multivariate forecast sample paths. Our primary evaluation fixes the empirical marginal sample multiset at every channel--horizon coordinate across methods, isolating the effect of coupling alone. Historical temporal and channel relations substantially improve their corresponding dependence diagnostics. The same pattern persists when the fixed-marginal constraint is removed and paths are sampled directly, and remains present under native multivariate backbone inference. These results support treating dependence reconstruction as a distinct post-processing problem for probabilistic TSFMs.

</details>


### [159] [Theory for groupoid equivariant neural networks: an approach for steerable CNNs on bounded domains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25987)
*Alberto Ibort,Maria Jimenez-Vazquez,Juan M. Perez-Pardo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper develops a theory of groupoid-equivariant convolutional neural networks for bounded or stratified domains where global group actions are inappropriate. The key contribution is a bisection-equivariant kernel theorem that characterizes equivariant kernels via transport constraints, with application to bounded planar domains showing order-of-magnitude accuracy improvements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard equivariant CNNs assume global group action on signal spaces, which fails for bounded domains where rigid motions may only be admissible locally, and boundaries introduce geometric types invisible to transitive group actions. This limits the applicability of equivariant architectures to many real-world problems with complex domain geometries.

Method: The authors develop a theoretical framework using: (1) groupoids as symmetry datums, (2) selected pseudogroups of local bisections, (3) measures on the domain, and (4) input/output representation bundles. For integral channels, they prove a bisection-equivariant kernel theorem that relates equivariance to transport constraints on two-point kernels, classified by joint-stabilizer intertwiners on orbit pairs. Implementation uses offline nullspace bases and sparse gather-transform-scatter operations.

Result: The bisection-equivariant kernel theorem shows that equivariance is equivalent to a transport constraint on the two-point kernel, with solutions classified by joint-stabilizer intertwiner on each orbit of pairs. The Poisson-Dirichlet kernel study demonstrates that exact inverse preserves global symmetries of rectangles but not general proper local bisections. Numerical experiments show significant advantages when symmetries cannot be globally implemented, with at least one order of magnitude accuracy improvement over tested baselines.

Conclusion: The groupoid framework successfully extends equivariant neural networks to bounded/stratified domains where traditional group-based approaches fail. The proposed architecture provides substantial accuracy improvements, validating the theoretical framework's practical utility for boundary-aware geometric deep learning.

Abstract: Equivariant convolutional neural networks are usually built from a group acting globally on the space of signals. This hypothesis is inappropriate for many bounded or stratified domains: an ambient rigid motion may be admissible only on part of the domain, and the boundary introduces geometric types that are invisible to a transitive group action. We develop a theory of groupoid-equivariant neural networks in which the symmetry datum consists of a groupoid, a selected pseudogroup of local bisections, a measure, and input and output representation bundles. For integral channels on the object space, we prove a bisection-equivariant kernel theorem: equivariance is equivalent to a transport constraint on the two-point kernel, and its solutions are classified by one joint-stabilizer intertwiner on each orbit of pairs. As a case study we apply the theory to bounded planar domains.
  The resulting architecture is implemented through offline nullspace bases and sparse gather--transform--scatter operations. A Poisson--Dirichlet kernel study is used separately to assess boundary-aware inductive bias; the exact inverse is shown to preserve the global symmetries of the rectangle but not general proper local bisections. The numerical results show that the proposed architectures provide significant advantages when symmetries cannot be globally implemented by group actions and provide an accuracy improvement of at least one order of magnitude with respect to the models tested.

</details>


<div id='cs.SD'></div>

# cs.SD [[Back]](#toc)

### [160] [Narrowband Voice Communication Using Streaming Neural Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25379)
*Dahong Luo,Anannya Trehan,Aritrik Ghosh,Nirupam Roy*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: TinyCall is a lightweight neural audio codec for real-time speech communication on resource-constrained edge devices (ESP32, Raspberry Pi), achieving intelligible speech at 2.3 kbps through minimal architecture, RFSQ quantization, and MFCC-based perceptual loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Low-bitrate speech communication on resource-constrained edge devices remains challenging due to stringent computational, memory, and bandwidth constraints, particularly for emergency communication and bandwidth-limited scenarios.

Method: The paper proposes: (1) a minimal neural audio codec architecture; (2) pseudo-lookahead decoding and decoder-input caching to convert causally trained codec into streamable codec; (3) Residual Finite Scalar Quantization (RFSQ) to replace conventional RVQ for reduced inference complexity; (4) progressive three-stage training strategy for stable optimization under latent quantization; (5) MFCC-based perceptual loss to preserve speaker characteristics including harmonic structure and vocal timbre.

Result: Real-time operation achieved on Raspberry Pi 3 while maintaining intelligible speech reconstruction at bitrates as low as 2.3 kbps. The system preserves speech intelligibility, speaker identity, and vocal expressiveness.

Conclusion: Practical neural speech communication is feasible on highly resource-constrained edge devices, demonstrating that the proposed approach enables real-time low-bitrate speech communication on low-power platforms.

Abstract: Low-bitrate speech communication on resource-constrained edge devices remains challenging due to stringent computational, memory, and bandwidth constraints. We present TinyCall, a lightweight neural audio codec designed for real-time speech communication on low-power platforms such as the ESP32 microcontroller and Raspberry Pi. The proposed system targets emergency communication and other bandwidth-limited scenarios while preserving speech intelligibility, speaker identity, and vocal expressiveness. To enable efficient deployment, we propose a minimal neural audio codec architecture together with a framework for converting a causally trained codec into a truly streamable codec through pseudo-lookahead decoding and decoder-input caching. We further replace conventional residual vector quantization (RVQ) with Residual Finite Scalar Quantization (RFSQ) to reduce inference complexity on edge processors and employ a progressive three-stage training strategy for stable optimization under latent quantization. An MFCC-based perceptual loss encourages preservation of speaker characteristics, including harmonic structure and vocal timbre. Experimental results demonstrate real-time operation on a Raspberry Pi 3 while achieving intelligible speech reconstruction at bitrates as low as 2.3 kbps. The proposed approach demonstrates that practical neural speech communication is feasible on highly resource-constrained edge devices.

</details>


### [161] [Synthesis and editing of multi-instrument audio mixtures using scalar-quantised latents with MIDI Span conditioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25546)
*Sungkyun Chang,Keshav Bhandari,Simon Dixon,Emmanouil Benetos*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: SpanSynth-Edit is a flow-matching model for MIDI-guided synthesis and editing of multi-instrument audio mixtures using low-frame-rate scalar-quantised latents. It encodes note lifecycles as MIDI Spans (unordered event sets with continuous attributes) pooled into conditioning vectors per audio frame, supporting timbre guidance via contextual audio and iterative editing through resynthesis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Music creation involves iterative refinement - changing selected musical details while retaining others. Current methods lack efficient support for such targeted editing while maintaining consistency in the rest of the mixture.

Method: Flow-matching model using: (1) MIDI Span encoding - instrument-labelled note lifecycles as unordered event sets with continuous-valued attributes, pooled into one conditioning vector per audio-latent frame; (2) Low-frame-rate scalar-quantised latents for efficiency; (3) Contextual audio for instrument-specific timbre guidance; (4) Editing by resynthesising target region from revised MIDI.

Result: Competitive performance on single- and multi-instrument benchmarks; demonstrates within-frame onset control; limitations identified in transcription-based note-adherence evaluation.

Conclusion: SpanSynth-Edit provides an effective approach for MIDI-guided synthesis and editing of multi-instrument audio, with the ability to make targeted changes while preserving other elements, though transcription-based evaluation metrics have limitations for assessing note adherence.

Abstract: Music creation often involves iterative refinement, changing selected musical details while retaining the rest. To support such refinement, we introduce SpanSynth-Edit, a flow-matching model for MIDI-guided synthesis and editing of multi-instrument audio mixtures using low-frame-rate scalar-quantised latents. MIDI Span encodes instrument-labelled note lifecycles as unordered event sets with continuous-valued attributes and pools each set into one conditioning vector per audio-latent frame. The model uses contextual audio for instrument-specific timbre guidance and supports editing by resynthesising the target region from revised MIDI. Experiments on single- and multi-instrument benchmarks show competitive performance and demonstrate within-frame onset control. We also discuss limitations of transcription-based note-adherence evaluation.

</details>


### [162] [NeuMark: Neural Codec Resynthesis-Robust Audio Watermarking in the Codec Latent Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25719)
*Annan Wu,Wen-Chin Huang,Tomoki Toda*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: NeuMark is a codec-latent audio watermarking framework that embeds watermark evidence into SpeechTokenizer acoustic tokens to resist neural-codec resynthesis attacks, using cross-attention to inject a 16-bit message across RVQ layers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional audio watermarking methods embed watermarks in waveform, timbre features, or latent representations, but modern neural codecs can remove watermarks through resynthesis using quantized acoustic representations, creating a new threat that existing methods cannot address.

Method: NeuMark uses cross-attention mechanism to inject a 16-bit message across residual vector quantization (RVQ) layers, distributing the watermark over codec-aligned latent structure. This approach embeds watermark evidence directly into SpeechTokenizer acoustic tokens.

Result: Experimental results show that NeuMark substantially improves robustness under neural-codec resynthesis while supporting both watermark detection and message recovery. The paper also analyzes the trade-off between reconstruction-referenced transparency and original-referenced robustness.

Conclusion: NeuMark provides a viable solution for embedding watermarks in neural codec latent spaces, achieving better robustness against resynthesis attacks compared to traditional methods while maintaining dual functionality of detection and message recovery.

Abstract: Audio watermarking is increasingly important for tracing generated speech. Several audio watermarking methods have been proposed to embed the watermark in various domains, such as waveform, timbre feature, or latent representations, for making the embedded watermark robust against traditional digital signal processing (DSP) attacks. On the other hand, modern neural codecs introduce a different threat from DSP attacks: they resynthesize speech through quantized acoustic representations and can remove the embedded watermark evi- dence that is not aligned with codec-preserved structure. In this paper, we propose NeuMark, a codec-latent audio watermarking framework that embeds watermark evidence into SpeechTok- enizer acoustic tokens to address this resynthesis threat. NeuMark uses cross-attention to inject a 16-bit message across residual vector quantization (RVQ) layers, distributing the watermark over codec-aligned latent structure. Experimental results show that NeuMark substantially improves robustness under neural- codec resynthesis while supporting both watermark detection and message recovery. We also analyze the trade-off between reconstruction-referenced transparency and original-referenced robustness.

</details>


### [163] [Boundary and Intra-Segment Learning for Partial Audio Deepfake Localization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25822)
*Zhe Ye,Xiangui Kang,Minhua Huang,Kai Wu,Kong Aik Lee,Chng Eng Siong*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: BISL proposes boundary and intra-segment learning for fine-grained partial audio deepfake localization, achieving 2.52% EER and 97.40% F1-score on PartialSpoof.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
