Motivation: Partial audio deepfakes manipulate only selected speech regions, making them difficult to localize. Existing methods focus on boundary positions but fail to model feature changes at authenticity transitions or explore internal characteristics of continuous bona fide and spoofed segments.

Method: BISL introduces two learning mechanisms: (1) Boundary learning to model feature differences between adjacent frames and distinguish authenticity transitions from general acoustic variations; (2) Intra-segment learning to capture overall characteristics of continuous bona fide/spoofed segments while enhancing feature consistency within each segment. The method jointly learns frame, boundary, and segment information for fine-grained localization.

Result: BISL achieves an EER of 2.52% and F1-score of 97.40% on PartialSpoof, outperforming compared methods. It maintains competitive performance on HAD and shows improved cross-dataset performance on LPS.

Conclusion: By jointly learning frame-level, boundary-level, and segment-level information, BISL enables more effective fine-grained partial audio deepfake localization. The approach successfully addresses both boundary detection and internal segment characterization challenges.

Abstract: Partial audio deepfakes manipulate only selected speech regions, making them difficult to be localized. Existing methods exploit boundary cues for partial deepfake localization, but primarily focus on identifying boundary positions rather than modeling the feature changes that characterize authenticity transitions. Meanwhile, the internal characteristics of continuous bona fide and spoofed segments remain underexplored. In this paper, we propose Boundary and Intra-Segment Learning (BISL), which introduces boundary learning to model feature differences between adjacent frames and distinguish authenticity transitions from general acoustic variations. In addition, intra-segment learning captures the overall characteristics of continuous bona fide and spoofed segments while enhancing feature consistency within each segment. By jointly learning frame, boundary, and segment information, BISL enables more effective fine-grained partial audio deepfake localization. Experiments on multiple localization benchmarks show that BISL achieves an EER of 2.52\% and an F1-score of 97.40\% on PartialSpoof, outperforming the compared methods, while maintaining competitive performance on HAD and improved cross-dataset performance on LPS. The code will be made publicly available upon acceptance.

</details>


### [164] [Latent Audio Watermarking for Robustness to Neural Codec Resynthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25830)
*Lovro Brulec,Sahil Karawade,Leonard Kinzinger*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper proposes using continuous neural codec latents (specifically EnCodec) as an embedding space for audio watermarks, which demonstrates superior robustness to neural codec resynthesis compared to existing waveform-domain methods like AudioSeal and WavMark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing waveform-domain audio watermarks are robust to many conventional distortions but degrade substantially under neural codec resynthesis. This work investigates whether continuous neural codec latents provide a more suitable embedding space for watermark embedding.

Method: A feedforward embedder maps a multi-bit payload to an additive latent perturbation, which is decoded through the unchanged codec decoder. The approach uses a restricted formulation built around frozen pretrained EnCodec, with optional codec-aware training.

Result: The latent watermark formulation degrades more gradually under repeated and low-bitrate EnCodec resynthesis compared to AudioSeal and WavMark. It transfers to unseen DAC (digital-to-analog converter), retains high detection under most waveform distortions, and shows substantial EnCodec robustness even without codec-resynthesis supervision. Learned perturbations are preserved more strongly through codec cycling than equal-norm random controls.

Conclusion: Continuous neural codec latents provide a promising embedding space for watermarks that remain robust to neural codec resynthesis. The inherent robustness emerges from the latent formulation itself and is further strengthened by codec-aware training.

Abstract: Existing waveform-domain audio watermarks are robust to many conventional distortions but can degrade substantially under neural codec resynthesis. We investigate whether continuous neural codec latents provide a more suitable embedding space using a restricted formulation built around frozen pretrained EnCodec. To test this, a feedforward embedder maps a multi-bit payload to an additive latent perturbation decoded through the unchanged codec decoder. Compared with AudioSeal and WavMark, our latent watermark formulation degrades more gradually under repeated and low-bitrate EnCodec resynthesis, transfers to unseen DAC, and retains high detection under most waveform distortions. Substantial EnCodec robustness emerges even without codec-resynthesis supervision, indicating that this behavior is inherent to our latent formulation and is further strengthened by codec-aware training. Learned perturbations are also preserved more strongly through codec cycling than equal-norm random controls, with preservation depending more on channel-specific allocation than temporal structure. End-to-end perceptual quality remains close to that of the frozen EnCodec reconstruction, indicating that much of the observed degradation originates from the codec carrier itself. Overall, these results show that continuous neural codec latents provide a promising embedding space for watermarks that remain robust to neural codec resynthesis.

</details>


### [165] [Challenges of Multi-Speaker Extraction for Real Conversational Speech Enhancement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25948)
*Robert Sutherland,Stefan Goetze,Jon Barker*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper addresses challenges in training target-speaker and multi-speaker speech extraction models, specifically the issues of excess silence in real conversations and mismatch between enrolment and target speech. The authors propose a new loss function that mitigates these problems, demonstrating improvements in STOI (0.55→0.60) and frequency-weighted segmental SNR (4.35→5.12).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current neural network approaches for speaker extraction are trained and evaluated using simulated datasets with balanced speech and enrolment samples. However, real multi-party conversations contain significantly more silence than speech, and enrolment samples often differ substantially from target speech. These mismatches impact model performance in real-world scenarios.

Method: The paper proposes a new loss function designed to mitigate the effects of excess silence during training. The impact of mismatch between enrolment speech and target speech is also explored through experiments.

Result: The proposed loss function improves STOI from 0.55 to 0.60 (9.1% relative improvement) and frequency-weighted segmental SNR from 4.35 to 5.12 (17.7% relative improvement).

Conclusion: The proposed loss function effectively addresses the silence imbalance problem in training speaker extraction models, leading to significant improvements in speech quality metrics. The work also highlights the importance of addressing enrolment-target speech mismatch in real-world applications.

Abstract: Target-speaker and multi-speaker extraction are techniques for extracting speech from a desired speaker or desired speakers in the presence of other speakers and/or noise. Neural network approaches for this task are often trained and evaluated using simulated datasets, with balanced amounts of target speech and speaker enrolment samples which closely match the target speech. However, in real multi-party conversations, participants are often silent for more time than they are speaking, and their enrolment speech samples can differ substantially from the target speech in the conversation. These factors can impact the training and evaluation of these techniques on recordings of real conversations. This work proposes a new loss function, which helps mitigate the effect of excess silence in training, improving STOI from 0.55 to 0.60, and frequency-weighted segmental SNR from 4.35 to 5.12. Additionally, the impact of the mismatch between the enrolment speech and target speech is explored.

</details>


### [166] [From Reliable Text to Real Voices: Trust-Aware Progressive Adaptation for Low-Resource TTS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25951)
*Jiayi Lu,Yizhong Geng,Jinghan Yang,Tianhan Jiang,Boxun An,Yingming Gao,Ya Li*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper addresses low-resource text-to-speech (TTS) adaptation by proposing trust-aware progressive adaptation, which combines synthetic and real speech while considering supervision order and pseudo-label reliability. The method uses transcript-agreement weighting between two ASR systems to filter noisy supervision, achieving improved content accuracy with high naturalness and competitive speaker similarity for Burmese and Lao languages.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Low-resource TTS adaptation is constrained by scarce paired data and costly manual transcription. Existing fixed-voice TTS systems provide accurate pronunciation but lack speaker diversity and have flat prosody. Real recordings offer natural prosody and diverse voices but contain ASR pseudo-label errors. The key insight is that supervision order significantly affects content accuracy and speaker similarity.

Method: The paper proposes trust-aware progressive adaptation with two stages: (1) Synthetic-to-real adaptation first establishes text-speech correspondences using synthetic speech, then (2) Restores reference-speaker control using real speech. Additionally, transcript-agreement weighting uses agreement between two fixed ASR systems as a proxy for pseudo-label reliability to limit noisy supervision. Experiments were conducted with FireRedTTS3 on Burmese and Lao, and OmniVoice on Burmese.

Result: The method achieved improved content accuracy while maintaining high naturalness and competitive speaker similarity on Burmese and Lao languages. The approach offers a practical path to zero-shot voice cloning in low-resource languages with less manual transcription.

Conclusion: Jointly considering supervision order and pseudo-label reliability when combining synthetic and real speech provides an effective solution for low-resource TTS adaptation. This approach reduces the need for manual transcription while enabling zero-shot voice cloning in low-resource languages.

Abstract: Low-resource text-to-speech (TTS) adaptation is constrained by scarce paired data and costly manual transcription. Existing fixed-voice TTS systems can provide relatively accurate pronunciation, but their synthetic speech offers limited speaker diversity and may exhibit flat prosody. Real recordings provide natural prosody and diverse voices, yet their automatic speech recognition (ASR) pseudo-labels may contain transcription errors. We find that supervision order affects content accuracy and speaker similarity. We propose trust-aware progressive adaptation: synthetic-to-real adaptation first establishes text-speech correspondences, then restores reference-speaker control using real speech. Transcript-agreement weighting uses agreement between two fixed ASR systems as a proxy for pseudo-label reliability to limit noisy supervision. Experiments with FireRedTTS3 on Burmese and Lao and OmniVoice on Burmese show improved content accuracy with high naturalness and competitive speaker similarity. Jointly considering supervision order and pseudo-label reliability when combining synthetic and real speech offers a practical path to zero-shot voice cloning in low-resource languages with less manual transcription. Audio demos are available at https://insiderx-pro.github.io/S2R-Adaptation-TTS/

</details>


### [167] [REVE: Efficient Hallucination Correction for Large Audio-Language Models via Reused Encoder States](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26028)
*Hongjin Song,Jiasheng Kuang,Xinyu Yang,Qiuyu Fang,Ziyu Wu,Guowu Tan,Xiang Xie*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: REVE is a lightweight method to verify whether audio events mentioned by large audio-language models are actually present in the input, by reusing encoder states already computed by the target model instead of running a second audio encoder.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large audio-language models can hallucinate and mention acoustic events absent from the input. Existing verification methods require a separate audio encoder and forward pass, which is computationally expensive.

Method: REVE uses two readouts: (1) one summarizes class scores across audio frames, (2) another uses pooled states from four consecutive frame intervals. Class-aware score fusion combines their outputs for verification without re-encoding the audio.

Result: On AudioSet, REVE removes 92.9% of label-unsupported mentions under faithful-mention recall constraint. It achieves comparable reduction to CED-Tiny and CED-Base with ~1/18 the latency of CED-Base. Effective on DESED mixtures and different target-model architectures.

Conclusion: Encoder-state reuse is an effective and efficient approach for verifying audio event mentions, achieving significant hallucination reduction with minimal computational overhead.

Abstract: Large audio-language models may mention acoustic events that are absent from the input. A separate audio event detector can verify these mentions, but doing so requires a second audio encoder and a separate forward pass. We propose Reused Encoder States for Verifying Events (REVE), a lightweight method that uses states already computed by the target model. One readout summarizes class scores across audio frames, while another uses pooled states from four consecutive frame intervals. Class-aware score fusion combines their outputs to verify generated event mentions without encoding the audio again. On AudioSet, REVE removes 92.9% of label-unsupported mentions under a faithful-mention recall constraint. With fewer added parameters and no second audio-encoding pass, REVE achieves a reduction comparable to those of CED-Tiny and CED-Base. Its complete verification latency is about 1/18 of the CED-Base path. Results on controlled DESED mixtures and different target-model architectures further confirm the effectiveness of encoder-state reuse.

</details>


### [168] [Benchmarking Open-Source Speech Emotion Recognition in Naturalistic Mandarin Spine Clinic Consultations: A Pilot Validation Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26054)
*Tsz Yuet Yeung,Zonglin He,Dong Chen,Huili Peng,Huiren Tao,Kenneth MC Cheung*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This study benchmarked three open-source speech emotion recognition (SER) models (emotion2vec+, SenseVoice, FunASR) against a researcher-consensus reference in naturalistic Mandarin spine-clinic consultations. Despite high inter-model agreement (90.8%), all models showed near-zero recall for minority emotions (Sad, Fear, Anger, Surprised), with majority-class accuracy metrics being misleading for clinical deployment readiness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Speech emotion recognition (SER) could enable passive affect monitoring in clinical encounters, but most systems are validated on acted laboratory speech rather than naturalistic clinical settings. This study aimed to benchmark SER models on real-world Mandarin outpatient consultations to assess their utility for clinical affect monitoring.

Method: Retrospective analysis of prospectively collected single-center recordings from spine-clinic consultations. Audio was loudness-normalized; 65 utterances (5-50 s; one per participant; 31 patients, 34 family members) were labeled by six calibrated annotators into six emotion categories (Happy, Sad, Fear, Anger, Neutral, Surprised). Consensus used majority vote with Fleiss kappa filtering and clinician adjudication for low-agreement segments. Metrics included unweighted accuracy (UA), macro-average per-class accuracy, class- and sample-level weighted accuracy (WA), and F1 with bootstrap 95% CIs.

Result: Labels were imbalanced (Neutral 58.5%); median Fleiss kappa was 0.230 (IQR 0.134-0.519). SenseVoice and FunASR achieved UA 61.5% (95% CI 49.2-73.8%), macro-average per-class accuracy 87.2%, class-level WA 92.5%, and sample-level WA 24.6%. emotion2vec+ yielded UA 55.4% (95% CI 42.5-67.7%) and macro-average per-class accuracy 85.1%, with class-level WA 90.6% and sample-level WA 24.2%. Despite high inter-model agreement (90.8%), all models had near-zero recall for Sad, Fear, Anger, and Surprised.

Conclusion: In naturalistic clinical settings with class imbalance, majority-class accuracy metrics are misleading. SER models poorly detect minority emotions against a noisy naturalistic reference. Clinical deployment readiness cannot be inferred from acted-corpus benchmarks without domain adaptation, multimodal modeling, stronger reference standards, and outcome validation.

Abstract: Speech emotion recognition (SER) may enable passive affect monitoring in clinical encounters, but most systems are validated on acted laboratory speech rather than naturalistic Mandarin outpatient consultations. We benchmarked three open-source SER models (emotion2vec+, SenseVoice, FunASR) against a researcher-consensus reference in naturalistic spine-clinic speech, assessing minority-state detection under class imbalance. In a retrospective analysis of prospectively collected single-center recordings, audio was loudness-normalized and only conversations among patients, family members, and clinicians were retained. Sixty-five utterances (5-50 s; one per participant; 31 patients, 34 family members) were labeled by six calibrated annotators into six categories (Happy, Sad, Fear, Anger, Neutral, Surprised). Consensus used majority vote with Fleiss kappa filtering and clinician adjudication for low-agreement segments. Metrics included unweighted accuracy (UA), macro-average per-class accuracy, class- and sample-level weighted accuracy (WA), and F1 with bootstrap 95% CIs. Labels were imbalanced (Neutral 58.5%); median Fleiss kappa was 0.230 (IQR 0.134-0.519). SenseVoice and FunASR achieved UA 61.5% (95% CI 49.2-73.8%), macro-average per-class accuracy 87.2%, class-level WA 92.5%, and sample-level WA 24.6%. emotion2vec+ yielded UA 55.4% (95% CI 42.5-67.7%) and macro-average per-class accuracy 85.1%, with class-level WA 90.6% and sample-level WA 24.2%. Despite high inter-model agreement (90.8%), all models had near-zero recall for Sad, Fear, Anger, and Surprised. In this pilot, majority-class accuracy was misleading: SER poorly detected minority emotions against a noisy naturalistic reference. Clinical deployment readiness cannot be inferred from acted-corpus benchmarks without domain adaptation, multimodal modeling, stronger reference standards, and outcome validation.

</details>


### [169] [SceneTTS-Bench: A Benchmark for Scene-Level TTS in Drama Dubbing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26255)
*Yizhong Geng,Yanliang Li,Jinghan Yang,Tianhan Jiang,Yingming Gao,Ya Li*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: SceneTTS-Bench is a benchmark for evaluating text-to-speech systems specifically for drama dubbing, addressing the gap that existing evaluation protocols only assess sentence-level performance while ignoring critical scene-level behaviors. The benchmark measures three dimensions: timbre consistency, emotional expressiveness, and rhythm coherence across 160 bilingual scenes with ~10,300 utterances.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current TTS evaluation protocols remain at sentence-level, leaving critical scene-level behaviors (timbre consistency across turns, emotional expressiveness in high-tension scenes, rhythm coherence in long-form synthesis) insufficiently measured. This limits the ability to assess TTS suitability for drama dubbing applications.

Method: The benchmark uses a backend-agnostic Canonical Intermediate Representation for fair cross-system comparison. Three automatic pipelines are employed: (1) Speaker Consistency Score for timbre-drift detection, (2) Under-Acting Ratio for under-acting identification, and (3) Rate Discontinuity Ratio for rate-discontinuity quantification. The corpus consists of 100 real-world drama scripts and 60 generated scripts totaling 160 bilingual scenes.

Result: Experiments on four TTS systems confirm that each system exhibits distinct weaknesses and that scene-level rankings diverge substantially from sentence-level metrics, demonstrating the importance of scene-level evaluation.

Conclusion: Scene-level TTS evaluation is essential for drama dubbing applications. The proposed SceneTTS-Bench benchmark reveals weaknesses that sentence-level metrics miss, and the framework demonstrates extensibility through synthetic data augmentation. Resources are publicly available.

Abstract: Text-to-speech systems are increasingly used for drama dubbing, yet evaluation protocols remain sentence-level, leaving critical scene-level behaviors insufficiently measured. We present SceneTTS-Bench, a benchmark that evaluates TTS along three dimensions: timbre consistency across character turns, emotional expressiveness on high-tension utterances, and rhythm coherence under segmented long-form synthesis. The corpus comprises real-world and generated drama scripts totaling 160 bilingual scenes and approximately 10,300 utterances, with real-world scripts serving as the primary source (100 scenes) and generated scripts as a supplementary source (60 scenes), demonstrating the framework's extensibility through synthetic data augmentation. A backend-agnostic Canonical Intermediate Representation ensures fair cross-system comparison. Three automatic pipelines produce per-utterance diagnostics: Speaker Consistency Score for timbre-drift detection, Under-Acting Ratio for under-acting identification, and Rate Discontinuity Ratio for rate-discontinuity quantification. Experiments on four TTS systems confirm that each system exhibits distinct weaknesses and that scene-level rankings diverge substantially from sentence-level metrics. Benchmark resources are publicly available at https://piedpiperg.github.io/scenetts-bench/ .

</details>


### [170] [MambaVoice: Lightweight Audiovisual Singing Voice Separation Via A Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26635)
*Adithi Shankar,Gopika Krishnan,Gloria Haro,Xavier Serra,Martín Rocamora*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: MambaVoice is a lightweight audiovisual framework using hybrid Mamba-Transformer architecture for singing voice separation from music videos. It achieves 14.18 dB SDR on Acappella with only 16.2M parameters, demonstrating that hybrid SSM-attention architectures can efficiently combine audio and visual cues for source separation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Isolating a target singing voice from music videos remains challenging, especially with multiple vocalists and dense instrumental accompaniment. Existing methods either lack visual guidance or require heavy computational resources.

Method: The model uses an attention-based band-split audio encoder and an ST-GCN for facial motion features, fused via multiplicative gating. A hybrid backbone combines Transformer self-attention with Selective State Space Models (SSMs) for efficient long-range temporal modeling with linear complexity.

Result: MambaVoice achieves 14.18 dB SDR on Acappella and strong cross-dataset performance on URSing, performing comparably to larger models while using only 16.2 million parameters. Perceptual studies confirm the objective improvements.

Conclusion: Hybrid SSM-attention architectures are effective for scalable, efficient audiovisual source separation and are well-suited as lightweight components in larger pipelines. The implementation is made publicly available.

Abstract: Isolating a target singing voice from a music video remains challenging, particularly in the presence of multiple vocalists and dense instrumental accompaniment. We propose MambaVoice, a lightweight audiovisual framework that leverages a hybrid Mamba--Transformer architecture for targeted singing voice separation. The model jointly encodes audio and visual streams using an attention-based band-split audio encoder and a spatio-temporal graph convolutional network (ST-GCN) for facial motion features. These modalities are fused through a multiplicative gating mechanism, enabling visual cues to selectively modulate audio representations. The fused features are processed by a hybrid backbone that combines Transformer self-attention with Selective State Space Models (SSMs), achieving efficient long-range temporal modeling with linear complexity. We evaluated MambaVoice on the Acappella and URSing datasets under challenging conditions, including mixtures with interfering singers. At 16.2 million parameters, the model demonstrates comparable performance, achieving 14.18 dB SDR on Acappella and strong cross-dataset performance on URSing, comparable to larger models at a fraction of the parameter count. These findings highlight the effectiveness of hybrid SSM--attention architectures for scalable, efficient audiovisual source separation, suggesting they are well-suited as lightweight components within larger pipelines. We conduct a perceptual study that further supports our improvements in objective metrics. We provide our implementation online.

</details>


<div id='cs.MM'></div>

# cs.MM [[Back]](#toc)

### [171] [CogenPVG: Cognitive-Enhanced Reflective Multi-Agent Framework for Persuasive Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25821)
*Yuntian Xiao,Shoulong Zhang,Wenfeng Song,Yan Wang,Yi Chen,Shuai Li*

Main category: cs.MM

TL;DR: CogenPVG is a novel Cognitive-Enhanced reflective multi-agent framework for Persuasive Video Generation (PVG). It decomposes the generation process into four sequential stages (argument reasoning, storyboard planning, asset creation, post-editing), each with generator-critic agent pairs guided by the Elaboration Likelihood Model (ELM) psychological theory. The central route uses critical thinking theory for logical reasoning, while the peripheral route uses heuristics for multimodal asset optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Persuasive video generation is valuable but under-explored. While multimodal content generation has advanced significantly, creating AI-empowered videos with substantial persuasiveness comparable to human-made content remains challenging. Existing works focus mainly on commercial purposes, leaving general persuasive topics unaddressed.

Method: CogenPVG employs a four-stage pipeline imitating human video producers: (1) argument reasoning with critical thinking theory for cognitive enhancement via ELM's central route; (2) storyboard planning; (3) asset creation; (4) post-editing. Each stage uses generator-critic agent pairs with reflective refinement grounded in ELM theory - central route for logical reasoning, peripheral route for heuristic-based multimodal optimization.

Result: Extensive experiments and comprehensive analysis demonstrate that CogenPVG achieves the best persuasion performance, proving the effectiveness of the proposed multi-agent framework for the PVG task. It is the first work focused on general persuasive topics rather than being confined to commercial purposes.

Conclusion: The cognitive-enhanced multi-agent framework successfully generates persuasive videos by integrating psychological theory (ELM) into the generation process. The combination of central route (logical reasoning) and peripheral route (heuristic-based optimization) through reflective generator-critic pairs enables effective persuasion for general topics, establishing a new benchmark in PVG research.

Abstract: Persuasive video generation (PVG) is a valuable yet under-explored research topic. Despite the significant advances in multimodal content generation, AI-empowered automated creation of human-made-like videos with substantial persuasiveness remains a formidable challenge. In this paper, we propose CogenPVG, a novel Cognitive-Enhanced reflective multi-agent framework tailored for Persuasive Video Generation task. Given the topic and stance from the user, we decouple the sophisticated generation process into four sequential stages: argument reasoning, storyboard planning, asset creation, and post-editing, imitating the workflow of human video producers. To ensure high persuasiveness, each stage is equipped with a pair of generator and critic agents, following a reflective refinement scheme grounded in a solid psychological theory of persuasion, the Elaboration Likelihood Model (ELM). In the argument reasoning stage, we generate highly logical and credible reasoning thoughts under the guidance of critical thinking theory, enabling cognitive enhancement via the central route of the ELM. For the other three stages, we generate and optimize multimodal assets, assembling them into a persuasive video guided by theories of heuristics, as the peripheral route of the ELM. To the best of our knowledge, CogenPVG is the first work focused on general persuasive topics, without being confined to commercial purposes. Extensive experiments and comprehensive analysis demonstrate that our framework achieves the best persuasion performance, thereby proving the effectiveness of our proposed multi-agent framework for the PVG task.

</details>


### [172] [TV-AudioRemover: Joint Text-Visual Guided Sound Removal with Multi-Task Hard-Mixture Curriculum](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25864)
*Xinyue Guo,Jianxuan Yang,Daiguo Zhou,Jiagao Hu,Yuxuan Chen,Fei Wang,Jian Luan*

Main category: cs.MM

TL;DR: TV-AudioRemover is a framework that removes sound associated with visually removed objects from video audio, using both the edited video and text instructions as guidance. It addresses the audio-visual inconsistency problem where object removal in video leaves persistent acoustic traces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Visual object removal in videos leaves acoustic traces that cause audio-visual inconsistency. Existing approaches are limited: video inpainting models work only on pixels, while audio editing models rely on text-only guidance which lacks the semantic grounding and temporal synchronization provided by multimodal control.

Method: The method proposes a text-visual guided sound removal framework combining: (1) a pipeline to construct million-scale training dataset of single-object audio-visual aligned samples; (2) architectural augmentations including task tokens, generalizable instruction modeling, and modality-specific global guidance; (3) multi-task training for task-role comprehension; (4) hard-mixture curriculum using semantically similar acoustic mixtures for fine-grained source discrimination during fine-tuning.

Result: The method achieves state-of-the-art performance on both subjective and objective metrics, as demonstrated on the proposed AV-Remove-Bench benchmark.

Conclusion: The paper presents a novel multimodal approach for sound removal that effectively leverages both visual context and natural language instructions. The proposed AV-Remove-Bench benchmark and evaluation protocol provide standardized assessment for this task. The key innovation is the combination of visual and textual guidance for audio editing, which outperforms single-modal approaches.

Abstract: Visual object removal can eliminate a target from video frames, yet its acoustic trace persists in the soundtrack, causing obvious audio-visual inconsistency. Existing video inpainting models operate solely on pixels, while audio editing models, especially for the sound removal task, are typically driven by text and therefore rely on limited single-modal control, which is less effective than multimodal guidance that provides stronger semantic grounding and temporal synchronization cues. In this paper, we present Text-Visual Guided Sound Removal (TV-AudioRemover), a target sound removal framework that leverages the visually edited video together with a natural-language instruction to suppress the sound associated with the removed visual object from the original audio mixture. To acquire high-quality training data, we devise a pipeline to construct a million-scale dataset of single-object audio-visual aligned samples, from which we synthesize mixture-target pairs customized for model training. To effectively leverage visual context and follow instruction intent, we augment the model architecture with task tokens, generalizable instruction modeling, and modality-specific global guidance. We further adopt multi-task training to strengthen task-role comprehension, and employ a hard-mixture curriculum that leverages semantically similar acoustic mixtures during fine-tuning to enhance fine-grained source discrimination. To support evaluation, we present AV-Remove-Bench, a comprehensive audio-visual object removal benchmark, along with dedicated objective metrics and an MLLM-based evaluation protocol. Experiments demonstrate that our method achieves state-of-the-art performance on both subjective and objective metrics. Project page: https://yjx-research.github.io/TV-AudioRemover/.

</details>


### [173] [KeyBound: Keyed and Host-Bound Learned Audio Watermarking for Speech Provenance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26235)
*Bangshuo Zhu,Yuxin Cao,Weifei Jin,Fusen Guo,Huadong Mo,Jingling Xue,Wei Song*

Main category: cs.MM

TL;DR: KeyBound is a learned audio watermark for synthetic speech attribution that combines a secret key (for access control) with a host-aware carrier (for anti-transferability), achieving high detection accuracy even under severe distortions like spectral denoising while remaining robust against re-synthesis attacks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current learned audio watermarks are evaluated only against a fixed catalog of signal distortions (noise, compression, filtering, resampling), which is insufficient for provenance verification. A proper watermark for evidence of origin should: (1) not be readable by unauthorized parties, (2) not be transferable to unrelated audio, and (3) not vanish when re-synthesized by modern generative models.

Method: KeyBound uses two key ingredients: (1) a secret key that masks the payload, and (2) a host-conditioned carrier modulated by a frozen spectral representation of the host audio. It includes a key-independent presence head for detection by any party, while only key holders can read the attribution. The system is designed so that wrong-key decode achieves verification with probability at most 2.1×10⁻³.

Result: On LibriSpeech: KeyBound achieves 1.00 detection accuracy and 0.98 bit accuracy under a spectral denoiser that defeats all baselines. It decodes at chance without the correct key and rejects transplanted carriers. Detection transfers to held-out DAC and BigVGAN re-synthesis, though exact payload recovery degrades.

Conclusion: Speech provenance should be formulated as a keyed, host-bound attribution problem rather than merely recovering a payload under a predetermined catalog of signal distortions. This approach provides stronger guarantees for attributing synthetic speech to its source.

Abstract: Audio watermarking is a proactive route to attributing synthetic speech to its source. Learned audio watermarks are typically judged by payload recovery after a fixed catalog of signal distortions such as noise, compression, filtering, and resampling. That test is necessary but not sufficient for provenance. A mark offered as evidence of origin should not be readable by an unauthorized party, should not be transferable to unrelated audio, and should not vanish when the recording is re-synthesized by a modern generative model. We present KeyBound, a learned audio watermark that restores the two ingredients classical watermarking supplied and learned schemes set aside, a secret key and a host-aware carrier. KeyBound masks the payload with a secret key and embeds the masked bits through a carrier modulated by a frozen spectral representation of the host, so the key governs payload access while the host-conditioned carrier resists direct transplantation. A key-independent presence head lets any party detect a mark, whereas only a key holder reads its attribution, and under the single-sample uniformity assumption a wrong-key decode clears our verification rule with probability at most $2.1\times10^{-3}$. On LibriSpeech against WavMark, AudioSeal, and Timbre, KeyBound holds 1.00 detection accuracy and 0.98 bit accuracy under a spectral denoiser that costs every baseline its detection, decodes at chance without the key, and rejects transplanted carriers. Detection further transfers to held-out DAC and BigVGAN re-synthesis, though exact payload recovery degrades. Speech provenance is thus better posed as a keyed, host-bound attribution problem than as the recovery of a payload under a catalog of signal distortions fixed in advance.

</details>


### [174] [ROAM-ASD: Robust Open-World Active Speaker Detection with Flexible Multimodal Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26648)
*Pu Wang,Yujun Wang,Hugo Van hamme*

Main category: cs.MM

TL;DR: ROAM-ASD is a robust audiovisual framework for active speaker detection that jointly models audio, full-face, and fine-grained mouth representations using unified joint self-attention with modality-agnostic query tokens. It achieves state-of-the-art performance across five benchmarks and handles missing observations through modality dropout.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Active speaker detection requires reliable association between visible faces and acoustic speech, but existing systems degrade under challenging domains or incomplete observations (missing input streams).

Method: ROAM-ASD uses a unified joint self-attention mechanism that processes all input streams (audio, full-face, mouth) together with modality-agnostic query tokens, enabling direct interaction among available modalities. Modality dropout is employed to improve robustness when input streams are unavailable.

Result: ROAM-ASD achieves state-of-the-art results on five benchmarks: 98.8% mAP on WASD, 87.9% on UniTalk, 96.5% on AVA, 99.3% on ASW, and 98.2% on Talkies, improving over previous best systems by 5.1, 4.7, 0.9, 1.0, and 2.1 mAP points respectively. It also shows improved zero-shot cross-dataset generalization and robustness to missing observations.

Conclusion: The proposed ROAM-ASD framework successfully addresses domain generalization and incomplete observation challenges in active speaker detection through multi-stream joint attention and modality dropout, achieving consistent performance improvements across diverse datasets.

Abstract: Active speaker detection (ASD) requires reliable association between visible faces and acoustic speech, yet existing systems often degrade under challenging domains or incomplete observations. We introduce ROAM-ASD, a robust audiovisual framework that jointly models audio, full-face, and fine-grained mouth representations. A unified joint self-attention mechanism processes all input streams together with modality-agnostic query tokens, enabling direct interaction among available modality inputs. Modality dropout further improves robustness when input streams are unavailable. ROAM-ASD achieves state-of-the-art performance across five ASD benchmarks: 98.8% mAP on WASD, 87.9% on UniTalk, 96.5% on AVA, 99.3% on ASW, and 98.2% on Talkies, improving over previous best systems by 5.1, 4.7, 0.9, 1.0, and 2.1 mAP points, respectively. ROAM-ASD also substantially improves zero-shot cross-dataset generalization and remains robust to missing observations.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [175] [Do Synthetic Personas Predict Real Audience Response? A Sim-to-Real Study Where a No-Persona Baseline Beats Persona-Based Copy Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25010)
*Alexandre Cristovão Maiorano*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether using LLM-generated "synthetic personas" improves prediction of audience engagement compared to a simple zero-shot approach. Using the Upworthy Research Archive as ground truth, the study finds that persona conditioning actually degrades predictive validity—asking the model directly (no-persona) outperforms the ten-persona panel by a substantial margin.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Marketers increasingly use LLMs as "synthetic personas" to predict audience reactions before publishing content. Despite claims that profile-conditioned LLMs mimic human responses, no empirical validation exists against real behavioral data. This study addresses that gap by testing whether synthetic personas actually improve prediction accuracy against real-world engagement metrics.

Method: The study conducted a sim-to-real validity study using the Upworthy Research Archive (thousands of headline A/B tests with actual click-through rates). They compared: (1) a ten-persona panel grounded in real audience demographics vs. (2) a no-persona zero-shot baseline that simply asks the model how likely a typical reader is to click. Since most A/B tests lacked statistically significant winners, analysis focused on the reliable subset (n=399). Results were replicated across three independent Upworthy splits and validated on a different-domain news dataset, using multiple models (three Gemini tiers and OpenAI GPT-4.1).

Result: The no-persona baseline significantly outperformed the persona panel: Kendall τ = 0.361 (medium effect) vs. 0.084; top-1 accuracy 49.2% vs. 34.6%. These confidence intervals were non-overlapping. The result replicated across three Upworthy splits, held on a different-domain news dataset, and was robust to seed, prompt phrasing, and model choice (across Gemini tiers and GPT-4.1).

Conclusion: For predicting aggregate engagement, a plain LLM ranker outperforms persona simulation. Synthetic personas are not merely weak predictors—they are worse than not using them. Direct questioning taps an accurate population-level prior, while forcing the model to role-play specific personas injects bias and noise. The takeaway challenges the prevailing assumption that persona conditioning improves LLM predictions.

Abstract: Marketers increasingly use large language models (LLMs) as "synthetic personas" to predict how an audience will react to a piece of copy before it ships, encouraged by evidence that profile-conditioned LLMs mimic human samples. But is that prediction actually valid against real behaviour - and does the persona machinery help? We present a sim-to-real validity study using the Upworthy Research Archive - thousands of headline A/B tests on shared real traffic, with measured click-through - as held-out ground truth. We compare a ten-persona panel, grounded in the real audience's demographics, against a no-persona zero-shot baseline that simply asks the model how likely a typical reader is to click. Two findings stand out. First, ground-truth reliability is the binding constraint: most A/B tests have no statistically distinguishable winner, so validity can only be measured on the reliable subset (n = 399). Second, and counter to the persona-simulation premise, persona conditioning degrades predictive validity: the no-persona baseline ranks variants markedly better (Kendall τ = 0.361, a medium effect; top-1 accuracy 49.2%) than the persona panel (τ = 0.084; top-1 34.6%), with non-overlapping confidence intervals. Asking the model directly taps an accurate population-level prior; forcing it to role-play specific personas injects bias and noise. The result replicates across three independent Upworthy splits, holds in direction on a different-domain news dataset, and is robust to seed, prompt phrasing, and model choice - across three Gemini tiers and a different model family (OpenAI gpt-4.1, significant paired gap). The takeaway: for predicting aggregate engagement, a plain LLM ranker beats persona simulation - synthetic personas are not merely a weak predictor, they are worse than not using them. All numbers regenerate from a public, artifact-first replication package.

</details>


### [176] [Do Existing Preconditioners Improve Biomedical Tabular Foundation Learning? An Empirical Study on TabPFN Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25013)
*M. Sajid,Pinki Khatun,M. Tanveer*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates five AdamW-based preconditioning strategies for fine-tuning TabPFN v2.5 on 59 biomedical datasets. The original AdamW optimizer outperforms curvature-aware preconditioners, suggesting the need for biomedical-aware optimization approaches tailored for healthcare tabular foundation models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tabular foundation models like TabPFN show strong potential for biomedical data analysis, but the impact of optimization and preconditioning strategies on biomedical fine-tuning remains unexplored. This work addresses this gap by systematically evaluating different preconditioning approaches.

Method: Comprehensive empirical investigation of five AdamW-based preconditioning strategies for fine-tuning TabPFN v2.5 across 59 biomedical datasets spanning Alzheimer's disease, breast cancer, schizophrenia, significant memory concern (SMC), KEEL biomedical datasets, and UCI biomedical benchmarks. Evaluation considers predictive performance, computational efficiency, and statistical significance analysis.

Result: The original AdamW optimizer consistently achieves the best overall performance and statistical ranking, while existing curvature-aware preconditioners fail to provide reliable improvements across diverse biomedical learning scenarios.

Conclusion: Generic preconditioning approaches may not adequately capture the optimization characteristics of biomedical tabular learning. This motivates the development of biomedical-aware preconditioners specifically tailored for healthcare-oriented tabular foundation models.

Abstract: Tabular foundation models have recently shown strong potential for structured biomedical data analysis. Among them, TabPFN has emerged as an effective approach for low-data tabular classification tasks. However, the impact of optimization and preconditioning strategies on biomedical fine-tuning remains largely unexplored. In this work, we present a comprehensive empirical investigation of five AdamW-based preconditioning strategies for fine-tuning TabPFN v2.5 on 59 biomedical datasets spanning Alzheimer's disease, breast cancer, schizophrenia, significant memory concern (SMC), KEEL biomedical datasets, and UCI biomedical benchmarks. The evaluation considers predictive performance, computational efficiency, and statistical significance analysis. Experimental results demonstrate that the original AdamW optimizer consistently achieves the best overall performance and statistical ranking, while existing curvature-aware preconditioners fail to provide reliable improvements across diverse biomedical learning scenarios. The findings suggest that generic preconditioning approaches may not adequately capture the optimization characteristics of biomedical tabular learning, motivating the development of biomedical-aware preconditioners specifically tailored for healthcare-oriented tabular foundation models.

</details>


### [177] [4DGS-JEPA: Temporally Compositional Joint-Embedding Prediction for Dynamic Gaussian Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25036)
*Yongchao Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: 4DGS-JEPA proposes a Gaussian-native joint-embedding predictive architecture for causal multi-horizon prediction over dynamic 3D scenes represented as Gaussian splatting. It uses hierarchical representations (scene/motion-group/Gaussian-level) with a horizon-conditioned transition operator, combined with temporal composition principles and hybrid correspondence mechanisms for identity tracking.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Dynamic Gaussian Splatting approaches are optimized for reconstruction, future-state generation, or rendering rather than learning reusable predictive dynamics. There is a need for a predictive model that can learn reusable dynamics from dynamic Gaussian scenes.

Method: The model uses: (1) Hierarchical scene-, motion-group-, and Gaussian-level representation; (2) Horizon-conditioned transition operator supporting direct prediction and recursive rollout; (3) Temporal composition principle where different transition paths to the same endpoint produce compatible states; (4) Endpoint and multi-horizon path supervision; (5) Selective geometry decoder with geometry-level composition; (6) Hybrid correspondence combining persistent canonical identity with residual optimal-transport matching under reordering and topology change.

Result: Three controlled experiments demonstrate: (1) Temporal composition reduces latent path dependence while retaining predictive accuracy; (2) Geometry-level composition improves consistency of decoded motion; (3) Hybrid correspondence preserves reliable identity while remaining robust when correspondence becomes ambiguous.

Conclusion: 4DGS-JEPA provides a predictive, temporally compositional formulation of dynamic Gaussian worlds, enabling reusable predictive dynamics for dynamic 3D scenes.

Abstract: Dynamic Gaussian Splatting provides an explicit representation of evolving 3D scenes, but existing approaches are primarily optimized for reconstruction, future-state generation, or rendering rather than for learning reusable predictive dynamics. We propose 4DGS-JEPA, a Gaussian-native joint-embedding predictive architecture for causal multi-horizon prediction over dynamic Gaussian scenes. The model uses a hierarchical scene-, motion-group-, and Gaussian-level representation together with a horizon-conditioned transition operator that supports both direct prediction and recursive rollout. Its central principle is temporal composition: different chronological transition paths reaching the same future endpoint should produce compatible predictive states. Endpoint and multi-horizon path supervision anchor these predictions to future target embeddings, while a selective geometry decoder and geometry-level composition ground the learned dynamics in consistent group motion and Gaussian geometry without requiring complete future appearance reconstruction. We further introduce a hybrid correspondence mechanism that combines persistent canonical identity with residual optimal-transport matching under reordering and topology change. We characterize zero-loss path agreement and finite-error rollout accumulation theoretically. Three controlled experiments provide mechanism-level evidence that temporal composition reduces latent path dependence while retaining predictive accuracy, geometry-level composition improves consistency of decoded motion, and hybrid correspondence preserves reliable identity while remaining robust when correspondence becomes ambiguous. Together, 4DGS-JEPA provides a predictive, temporally compositional formulation of dynamic Gaussian worlds.

</details>


### [178] [VideoX-Qwen: Data-Centric Instruction-Based Video Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26015)
*JJiahang Li,Dingbao Shao,Xinyu Chen,Song Wu,Jiang Lin,Duo Li,Yuhang Liu,Jiaxin Hu,Shengrong Gu,Ying Tai,Zili Yi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VideoX-Qwen presents an integrated data-construction and model-training framework for general instruction-based video editing, producing 1.2 million paired video editing records and developing a unified Qwen-Wan editor that achieves state-of-the-art results on 9 of 11 metrics compared to UniVideo and Kling O1.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Progress in general-purpose video editing requires both large-scale paired supervision and effective adaptation of video-generation backbones to instruction-driven editing. Unlike video generation, video editing must preserve unrelated subjects, scene structure, motion, and temporal continuity while executing requested transformations.

Method: 1) Data Construction: Scalable production pipeline organizing specialized generation and understanding models into complementary routes for addition, removal, replacement, and attribute editing, followed by quality screening and instruction enrichment. 2) Model Training: Unified Qwen-Wan editor combining multimodal semantic conditioning with dense source-video latent guidance, using progressive image-video training strategy to align multimodal instruction interface, adapt video generator to source-conditioned editing, and refine output quality with high-resolution data.

Result: Produced over 1.2 million directional video-editing records (400,000+ per major task group) with 89% automatic acceptance rate. In 100-example comparison with UniVideo and Kling O1, VideoX-Qwen achieves best mean result on nine of eleven metrics including instruction following, editing quality, content preservation, structural/perceptual similarity, and video-distribution quality.

Conclusion: The large-scale data-production system and unified training framework provide a practical foundation for more capable instruction-driven video editing, demonstrating the effectiveness of combining scalable data construction with progressive training strategies for general video editing tasks.

Abstract: Progress in general-purpose video editing depends on constructing large-scale paired supervision and effectively adapting video-generation backbones to instruction-driven editing. Unlike video generation, video editing must execute a requested transformation while preserving unrelated subjects, scene structure, motion, and temporal continuity. We present VideoX-Qwen, an integrated data-construction and model-training framework for general instruction-based video editing. Our scalable production pipeline organizes specialized generation and understanding models into complementary routes for addition, removal, replacement, and attribute editing, followed by quality screening and instruction enrichment. It produces more than 1.2 million directional video-editing records, including over 400,000 records in each major task group, with an automatic acceptance rate of 89%. The resulting corpus provides broad and structured coverage of common editing operations through a unified source-instruction-target interface. We further develop a unified Qwen-Wan editor that combines multimodal semantic conditioning with dense source-video latent guidance. A progressive image-video training strategy aligns the multimodal instruction interface, adapts the video generator to source-conditioned editing, and refines output quality with selected high-resolution data. In a 100-example comparison with UniVideo and Kling O1, VideoX-Qwen achieves the best mean result on nine of eleven reported metrics, including instruction following, editing quality, content preservation, structural and perceptual similarity, and video-distribution quality. Together, the large-scale data-production system and unified training framework provide a practical foundation for more capable instruction-driven video editing.

</details>


### [179] [An Accurate and Interpretable Hyper Graph Neural Network for GBM Survival Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25088)
*Mushahid Intesum*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a multi-modal interpretable survival prediction framework for glioblastoma multiforme (GBM) that combines sheaf hypergraph neural networks, concept bottleneck layers, and EST regularization to achieve both high accuracy and interpretability without the typical trade-off between them.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current GBM survival prediction models face a trade-off between accuracy and interpretability - performant models lack transparency while interpretable models have degraded predictive power. Additionally, existing methods underutilize multi-modal MRI and clinical metadata. The authors argue this trade-off is not inherent and can be addressed through graph neural networks with structured representations.

Method: The framework integrates three key components: (1) Sheaf hypergraph neural network capturing higher-order tissue patch relationships through directional, asymmetric message passing; (2) Concept bottleneck layer compressing representations into clinically grounded concepts for ante-hoc interpretability; (3) Extension sufficiency test (EST) regularizer penalizing unfaithful explanations during training. Clinical and genomic features are incorporated through gated fusion.

Result: Evaluated on 593 patients from UPenn-GBM dataset under 5-fold cross-validation, the framework achieves a concordance index of 0.643 with the lowest fold-level variance (std = 0.015) among all compared models.

Conclusion: This is the first work to unify sheaf hypergraph convolution, concept bottleneck supervision, and EST regularization for interpretable survival prediction from brain MRI. The approach demonstrates that accuracy and interpretability can be achieved simultaneously without compromising either objective.

Abstract: Survival prediction for glioblastoma multiforme (GBM) demands models that are both accurate and interpretable, yet existing approaches treat these objectives as com- peting, where performant models sacrifice transparency, while interpretable models accept degraded predictive power. We argue that this trade-off is not inherent. Graph neural net- works offer a structural foundation for extracting interpretable, explainable representations without compromising discriminative ability. Furthermore, current methods typically rely on a single imaging modality, underutilizing the complementary information available across multi-modal MRI and clinical metadata. We propose a multi-modal framework that inte- grates three components to address both objectives simultaneously: (1) a sheaf hypergraph neural network that captures higher-order relationships among tissue patches through direc- tional, asymmetric message passing; (2) a concept bottleneck layer that compresses learned representations into clinically grounded concepts, enforcing ante-hoc interpretability; and (3) an extension sufficiency test (EST) regularizer that penalizes unfaithful explanations during training, ensuring that model explanations genuinely reflect the internal decision process. Clinical and genomic features are incorporated through gated fusion, preserving the dominant prognostic signal of molecular markers while retaining concept-level traceabil- ity. Evaluated on 593 patients from the UPenn-GBM dataset under 5-fold cross-validation, our framework achieves a concordance index of 0.643 with the lowest fold-level variance among all compared models (std = 0.015). To our knowledge, this is the first work to unify sheaf hypergraph convolution, concept bottleneck supervision, and EST regularization for interpretable survival prediction from brain MRI

</details>


### [180] [Ovis-Embedding: Pushing the Frontiers of Universal Omni-Modal Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25165)
*Ovis-Embedding Team*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Ovis-Embedding is a state-of-the-art omni-modal embedding model that uses a shared multimodal backbone to encode text, image, video, and audio in a common representation space, achieving SOTA performance on multiple benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome modality fragmentation in embedding models and advance universal embedding models for any-to-any retrieval by using unified omni-modal training instead of separate modality towers.

Method: Three key advances: (1) Native omni-modal initialization using pretrained Qwen-omni model with low-rank adaptation via contrastive training; (2) Data-centric omni-modal training with a broad high-quality corpus and homogeneous-source sampling for task-consistent batches; (3) Embedding-specific optimization using focal loss for hard examples and similarity-based Embedding Distillation from complementary experts, with low-rank feature decomposition at inference for compact embeddings.

Result: Ovis-Embedding family achieves state-of-the-art performance on MMEB-v3, MMEB-v2, MVEB, MAEB, and RTEB benchmarks, demonstrating effectiveness across text, image, video, and audio modalities.

Conclusion: Unified omni-modal training can overcome modality fragmentation and effectively advance universal embedding models capable of any-to-any retrieval across all modalities.

Abstract: In this report, we introduce \textbf{Ovis-Embedding}, a state-of-the-art omni-modal embedding family built on native integration of text, image, video, and audio. Instead of assembling separate modality towers, Ovis-Embedding uses a shared multimodal backbone to encode different modalities in a common representation space. Specifically, we make \textbf{three key advances}: (1) \textbf{native omni-modal initialization}: we adopt a pretrained Qwen-omni model as the embedding backbone and adapt it through contrastive training with low-rank initialization; (2) \textbf{data-centric omni-modal training}: we construct a broad, high-quality corpus spanning text, images, video, audio, and interleaved multimodal data. To improve data efficiency, we introduce homogeneous-source sampling to form task-consistent batches with informative in-batch negatives; and (3) \textbf{embedding-specific training and inference optimization}: we use focal loss to emphasize hard examples and similarity-based Embedding Distillation to transfer fine-grained similarity structure from complementary experts. At inference time, low-rank feature decomposition enables compact embeddings with flexible dimensionality and minimal performance loss. Empirical evaluations show that the \textbf{Ovis-Embedding} family achieves state-of-the-art performance on \textbf{MMEB-v3}, \textbf{MMEB-v2}, \textbf{MVEB}, \textbf{MAEB}, and \textbf{RTEB}, demonstrating its effectiveness across text, image, video, and audio modalities. These results highlight the potential of unified omni-modal training to overcome modality fragmentation and advance universal embedding models for any-to-any retrieval.

</details>


### [181] [X-Planner: Event-Structured Task Planning for Embodied Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25187)
*Howard Lu,Shalfun Li,Porter Pan,Cris,Lumen,Cyril,Eric Hu,Lily Li,Maeve Zhang,Robert Wang,KZ Zheng,Viggo Chen,Tim Ding,Regsis Cheng,YJ Xiao,Kian,Hai Lin,Alan Song,Elise Ma,Gody Li,Victor Yao,Yohann Tang,Ingrid Yu,Jason He,James Wang,Ryan Yu,Ping Yang,Chris Pan,Vincent Chen,Roy Gan,Hao Wang,Qian Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: X-Planner is a planning front-end for Vision-Language-Action systems that addresses implicit planning and coarse supervision issues. It introduces hierarchical planning data with source-dependent annotation depth and event-structured plan forms (discrete and latent interfaces). The system uses Staircase Decoding for continuous chain-of-thought states and achieves second-place performance on BERTScore-F1 and Overall score in offline evaluation, while outperforming baselines in real-robot experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern VLA systems leave task planning implicit, and existing CoT planners rely on coarse task-level annotations or serialize long reasoning traces token-by-token. There is a need for better supervision and representation of embodied reasoning in long-horizon manipulation tasks.

Method: X-Planner uses a shared VLM backbone with two interfaces: (1) a discrete interface emitting interpretable event states, and (2) a latent interface using Staircase Decoding to relay continuous CoT states across staggered Transformer depths. Planning data combines Ego, UMI, and teleoperation with hierarchy granularity and source-dependent annotation depth. Takeover-time annotations and human-designed failures supervise error recognition. A frozen latent-to-text reconstruction provides semantic anchoring.

Result: In offline two-step planning evaluation, X-Planner ranked second among four evaluated models on both BERTScore-F1 and judge-based Overall score. In real-robot experiments, it outperformed the evaluated baselines.

Conclusion: X-Planner demonstrates that hierarchical planning data with source-dependent annotations and event-structured plan forms (discrete and latent) can effectively improve planning-text quality and downstream execution in embodied AI tasks. The approach characterizes the relationship between planning quality and robotic execution performance.

Abstract: Task planning bridges high-level instructions and executable behavior in long-horizon manipulation, yet modern Vision-Language-Action (VLA) systems often leave this intermediate structure implicit. Existing chain-of-thought (CoT) planners also tend to rely on coarse task-level annotations or serialize long reasoning traces token by token. We present X-Planner, a planning front-end that addresses both the supervision and representation of embodied reasoning. Our planning data combine Ego, UMI, and teleoperation under a hierarchy granularity with source-dependent annotation depth. Takeover-time annotations and human-designed failures supervise ongoing error recognition. On the model side, a shared VLM backbone exposes two event-structured plan forms: a discrete interface that emits interpretable event states and a latent interface that relays continuous CoT states across staggered Transformer depths through Staircase Decoding. A frozen latent-to-text reconstruction objective provides a semantic anchor for the latent representation. Offline two-step planning evaluation places X-Planner second among four evaluated models on both BERTScore-F1 and a judge-based Overall score. In real-robot experiments, respectively, outperforming the evaluated baselines. These results characterize planning-text quality and downstream execution.

</details>


### [182] [Lean Pool: An AI-Maintained Archive of Formalized Mathematics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25199)
*Vasily Ilin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Lean Pool is an AI-driven repository system for formalized mathematics that is autonomously grown, maintained, and optimized by AI agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The abstract does not explicitly state the motivation, but the system appears to address the challenge of building and maintaining large-scale repositories of formalized mathematical knowledge, likely to accelerate mathematical discovery and formal verification efforts.

Method: The method involves using AI agents to autonomously grow (add new content), maintain (ensure correctness and consistency), and optimize (improve structure and efficiency) the repository of formalized mathematics.

Result: The result is a self-sustaining repository of formalized mathematics (Lean Pool) that is continuously improved by AI agents without heavy human intervention.

Conclusion: The abstract suggests that AI agents can effectively manage and develop a formalized mathematics repository, potentially enabling large-scale formalization projects that would be impractical manually.

Abstract: Lean Pool is a repository of formalized mathematics. It is grown, maintained and optimized by AI agents.

</details>


### [183] [The AI Neuroscientist: An Interactive Agentic Interface for Neuroimaging Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25254)
*Aakash Patel,Panos Ketonis,Shreya Saxena,Smita Krishnaswamy,David van Dijk*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AI Neuroscientist is a language agent that integrates large language models with neuroimaging tools, enabling researchers to perform quality control, modeling, and visualization on fNIRS data through natural language queries, providing an accessible alternative to scripted pipelines for non-computational researchers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Analyzing neuroimaging data requires specialized coding and statistical expertise, which limits accessibility for researchers without computational backgrounds.

Method: The system integrates a large language model (LLM) with a neuroimaging toolset to perform quality control, modeling, and visualization, allowing researchers to query data quality and specify analysis parameters directly in natural language.

Result: Demonstrated capabilities using functional near-infrared spectroscopy (fNIRS) data; evaluated on a custom fNIRS benchmarking suite against general-purpose LLM agents with code sandboxes.

Conclusion: Provides a transparent and interactive alternative to conventional scripted pipelines for small-scale data exploration. Future extensions will generalize the architecture to additional modalities (fMRI) and expand the benchmarking suite to additional fNIRS tasks.

Abstract: Analyzing neuroimaging data requires specialized coding and statistical expertise, which limits accessibility for researchers without computational backgrounds. We present the AI Neuroscientist, a language agent for interactive data exploration. The system integrates a large language model (LLM) with a neuroimaging toolset to perform quality control, modeling, and visualization. This allows researchers to query data quality and specify analysis parameters directly in natural language, providing a transparent and interactive alternative to conventional scripted pipelines for small-scale data exploration. We demonstrate these capabilities using functional near-infrared spectroscopy (fNIRS) data, and evaluate the agent on a custom fNIRS benchmarking suite against general-purpose LLM agents with code sandboxes. Future extensions will generalize the architecture to additional modalities, including functional magnetic resonance imaging (fMRI) data, and expand the benchmarking suite to additional fNIRS tasks.

</details>


### [184] [MedGate-Fusion: Integrating First-Encounter Semantic Narratives and Physiological Biomarkers for Prospective Stroke Risk Stratification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25272)
*Hemn Khdr,Mohammad Noaeen,Karim Keshavjee,Aziz Guergachi,Zahra Shakeri*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MedGate-Fusion is a multi-modal deep learning model that combines transformer-based embeddings of clinical narratives with ten routine biomarkers to predict five-year stroke risk in primary care patients. The model was trained on 102,736 patient records from Canadian primary care electronic health records.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prospective stroke risk stratification in primary care is challenging because early risk signals are distributed across routine biomarkers and unstructured clinical narratives. Existing methods may not effectively leverage both data types, and diagnostic mentions in clinical notes could introduce target leakage.

Method: MedGate-Fusion uses a multi-modal gated architecture that integrates: (1) transformer-based embeddings of first-encounter clinical narratives, and (2) ten routinely recorded risk markers. Dictionary-based redaction was applied to remove stroke-related terms from notes prior to semantic encoding to prevent target leakage.

Result: From 808,921 encounter-level observations, a first-encounter cohort of 102,736 unique patient records was constructed with non-empty narratives and sufficient data for five-year stroke outcome evaluation.

Conclusion: The study presents a novel multi-modal approach for stroke risk prediction that combines unstructured clinical text with structured biomarkers, while addressing target leakage through dictionary-based redaction. This enables more accurate prospective stroke risk stratification in primary care settings.

Abstract: Prospective stroke risk stratification in primary care is challenging because early risk signals are distributed across routine biomarkers and unstructured clinical narratives. We propose MedGate-Fusion, a multi-modal gated architecture that integrates transformer-based embeddings of first-encounter narratives with ten routinely recorded risk markers. We used electronic medical record data from the Canadian Primary Care Sentinel Surveillance Network (CPCSSN). Starting from 808,921 encounter-level observations, we constructed a first-encounter cohort and retained 102,736 unique patient records with non-empty narratives and sufficient data to evaluate a five-year stroke outcome. To reduce explicit target leakage from diagnostic mentions in notes, we applied dictionary-based redaction of stroke-related terms prior to semantic encoding.

</details>


### [185] [When LLM Agents Fail to Read the Room: ReAdapt for Relational Social Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25284)
*Jianzhe Lin,Xiaolin Li,Yunda Liu,Fei Wang,Jubin Chheda*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses a critical limitation in LLM agents: their inability to leverage relational evidence when making social decisions. The authors identify that standard agent loops fail to explicitly represent how new relational evidence should revise the agent's social hypothesis, leading to surface-obvious choices that diverge from relationship-grounded decisions. They propose ReAdapt, which augments the ReAct loop with an explicit structured social state capturing goal, belief, relationship, norm, and disclosure, along with a typed Adapt step that updates this state and emits policy operations before choosing actions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The core motivation is that social decisions (e.g., reacting to posts, reaching out to contacts) depend more on latent relationship dynamics (tie strength, reciprocity, mutual connections) than on content salience. Standard LLM agents lack explicit representation of how relational evidence should revise their social hypotheses, making them prone to surface-level choices when relational and content cues diverge. This represents a fundamental gap in current LLM agent architectures for social reasoning tasks.

Method: The authors introduce a relationship-reasoning benchmark with 500 synthetic social worlds containing friendships, follows, reaction histories, and feeds, yielding 1,000 queries across two tasks: (1) reaction selection and (2) warm introduction (finding the best bridge to a target person). They propose ReAdapt (Relationship-Adaptive Agent with Policy-driven sTate), which augments the ReAct loop with an explicit structured social state z = (G, B, R, N, D) capturing goal, belief, relationship, norm, and disclosure. After each tool observation, ReAdapt runs a typed Adapt step that updates this state and emits a policy operation (continue, switch, abandon, or clarify) before choosing the next action.

Result: With Gemini-3-Flash on a stratified subset of n = 150 queries per task, ReAdapt improves warm-introduction accuracy from 37% to 51% (+14 points) and reaction-selection accuracy from 69% to 77% (+8 points). Oracle regret drops from 0.260 to 0.152 (warm introduction) and from 0.095 to 0.053 (reaction selection), respectively. The benchmark construction ensures that the surface-obvious candidate differs from the relationship-grounded oracle in about 53% of queries, forming an overturn subset where relational evidence must revise initially plausible choices.

Conclusion: The results demonstrate that explicit relational-state adaptation helps LLM agents turn retrieved social evidence into revised decisions. By holding the model, tools, and environments fixed, the study provides strong evidence that the proposed structured social state and adaptive policy mechanism effectively address the failure mode where agents default to surface-obvious choices. This work opens avenues for more sophisticated social reasoning in LLM agents, particularly in tasks where relational dynamics outweigh content-based cues.

Abstract: A social agent's most basic decisions (should I react to this post? who should I reach out to?) are not purely content problems. The right action often hinges on the latent relationship between people -- tie strength, reciprocity, mutual connections -- rather than on which content is most salient. Standard LLM agent loops do not explicitly represent how new relational evidence should revise the agent's current social hypothesis, leaving them prone to surface-obvious choices when relational and content cues diverge. We formalize this failure mode with a relationship-reasoning benchmark: 500 synthetic social worlds with friendships, follows, reaction histories, and feeds, yielding 1,000 queries over two tasks, reaction selection and warm introduction (finding the best bridge to a target person). By construction, the surface-obvious candidate differs from the relationship-grounded oracle in about 53% of queries, forming an overturn subset where the agent must use relational evidence to revise an initially plausible choice. We propose ReAdapt (Relationship-Adaptive Agent with Policy-driven sTate), which augments the ReAct loop with an explicit structured social state z = (G, B, R, N, D) capturing goal, belief, relationship, norm, and disclosure. After each tool observation, ReAdapt runs a typed Adapt step that updates this state and emits a policy operation (continue, switch, abandon, or clarify) before choosing the next action. With Gemini-3-Flash on a stratified subset of n = 150 queries per task, ReAdapt improves warm-introduction accuracy from 37% to 51% (+14 points) and reaction-selection accuracy from 69% to 77% (+8 points). Oracle regret drops from 0.260 to 0.152 and from 0.095 to 0.053, respectively. Holding the model, tools, and environments fixed, these results suggest that explicit relational-state adaptation helps LLM agents turn retrieved social evidence into revised decisions.

</details>


### [186] [Attention as a Routing Graph: Live Circuit Extraction from a Single Forward Pass](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25285)
*Ash Manvi,Samreena Tajreen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a cheap method to extract "circuits" (important attention paths) from language models by treating attention as a routing map from a single forward pass, then testing if those routes actually matter through ablation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Finding circuits in language models typically requires many careful interventions (ablations), which is computationally expensive. The authors seek a simpler, cheaper alternative.

Method: Treat attention as a routing map from one forward pass, keep small set of routes pointing toward the answer, and test if those routes matter via ablation. Evaluated on 100 prompts per cell across GPT-2 Small, GPT-2 Medium, and Pythia-410M using paired gap tests and bootstrap confidence intervals.

Result: On induction and IOI tasks (where the "right" circuit is known), ablating extracted edges hurts much more than ablating random sets of the same size. The extract step costs one forward pass; a head-by-head patch sweep costs about two orders of magnitude more.

Conclusion: The method provides a cheap sketch that carries real causal signal on known tasks, with clear failure modes when it does not work. Not claiming complete circuit atlas, but a practical approach for circuit discovery.

Abstract: Finding circuits in language models usually means running many careful interventions. We try something simpler: treat attention as a routing map from one forward pass, keep a small set of routes that point toward the answer, and ask whether those routes actually matter.
