  They often do. On induction and IOI (tasks where the "right" circuit is already known), ablating our extracted edges hurts the model much more than ablating a random set of the same size. We evaluate n=100 prompts per cell on GPT-2 Small, GPT-2 Medium, and Pythia-410M, with paired gap tests and bootstrap confidence intervals. The extract step costs one forward; a head-by-head patch sweep costs about two orders of magnitude more.
  We are not claiming a complete circuit atlas. We are claiming a cheap sketch that carries real causal signal on known tasks, with clear failure modes when it does not. Code and evaluation artifacts are at https://github.com/Aquinf03/live-circuit-routing.

</details>


### [187] [When Big Data Becomes a Curse: Spatial Heterogeneity and the Limits of Learning from Passive Acoustic Monitoring Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26125)
*Gabriel Spadon,Wayne Renaud,Priyanka Aravindan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper analyzes 908,072 AIS-labeled Passive Acoustic Monitoring recordings from 38 deployments to evaluate model transferability across stations, seasons, and hydrophone configurations. The study compares random-window validation versus station-grouped holdout validation approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The researchers aimed to understand how well acoustic monitoring models transfer across different deployment stations, seasons, and hydrophone configurations, and to determine whether standard random-window validation appropriately represents generalization to unseen stations.

Method: The analysis used 908,072 AIS-labeled 679-second recordings from 38 deployments across 20 Atlantic Canadian station identifiers and 21 receiver positions. They employed bidirectional cross-season transfer over 18 station identifiers observed in both seasons, along with repeated station-grouped holdout validation and comparison with random-window diagnostic approaches.

Result: Station identity prediction achieved 15.9% (AIS-contact) and 16.4% (no-AIS-contact) balanced accuracy. Hydrophone model prediction achieved 74.1% and 85.6% balanced accuracy but was confounded with season. Station-grouped holdout yielded ROC-AUC of 0.612 (95% CI: 0.582-0.648), versus 0.661 for random-window diagnostic. The paired difference was 0.049 (0.040-0.056). Removing raw energy changes decreased ROC-AUC from 0.612 to 0.603.

Conclusion: Random-window validation significantly overstates transfer performance to unseen station identifiers. The findings support implementing dependence-aware validation protocols and broader independent spatial sampling. Spatial-expert models remain a hypothesis rather than an established solution. The study highlights the importance of accounting for deployment-level dependencies in acoustic monitoring validation.

Abstract: Passive Acoustic Monitoring produces large archives whose recordings are clustered by deployment, season, station identifier, and acquisition configuration. We analyze 908,072 AIS-labeled 679-second recordings from 38 deployments, 20 Atlantic Canadian station identifiers, and 21 receiver positions. The AIS-contact prior varies by more than 200-fold, and per-deployment screening distributions require local interpretation. Bidirectional cross-season transfer over the 18 station identifiers observed in both seasons predicts station identity above the 5.56% uniform-chance level, with balanced accuracy of 15.9% for AIS-contact and 16.4% for no-AIS-contact recordings. The same descriptors predict the two hydrophone models at 74.1% and 85.6% balanced accuracy, respectively, but hydrophone model is strongly confounded with season and other deployment-level acquisition differences. On a retrospectively screened and capped benchmark of 54m507 recordings, repeated station-grouped holdout yields an ROC-AUC of 0.612 with a station-bootstrap 95% interval of 0.582 to 0.648, compared with 0.661 under a random-window diagnostic. Their paired difference is 0.049 (0.040 to 0.056). Removing raw energy changes unseen-station ROC-AUC from 0.612 to 0.603, while an exploratory training-station scale analysis is non-monotonic. These results show that random-window validation overstates transfer to unseen station identifiers in this corpus. They support dependence-aware validation and broader independent spatial sampling, while spatial-expert models remain a hypothesis rather than an established remedy.

</details>


### [188] [Learned Enterprise Data Comprehension: Compression and Routing for Data Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25286)
*Ethan Torres,Eric Mills*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces "latent equivalence learning" to improve structured-data agents in enterprise settings by separating persistent task-relevant identities from their dataset-specific realizations, using Gaussian prototypes and soft-membership profiles to enable efficient query-conditioned evidence routing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current agentic systems use markdown-style memory to preserve discovered information, but this obscures the natural division of labor: agents excel at semantic reasoning while learned systems are better at predicting recurring structure. The authors aim to bridge this gap by creating a framework that separates identity representation from dataset-specific realizations.

Method: The framework introduces: (1) support-realized Gaussian prototypes that learn how identities are expressed in specific data environments, (2) soft-membership profiles to retain distinctions lost under hard assignment, (3) a learned query-prototype system representing recurring evidential requirements, and (4) a learned compatibility function mapping queries into the persistent identity structure. This identity-factorized, query-conditioned routing materializes relevant dataset-specific evidence for downstream reasoning.

Result: On the Data Agent Benchmark (54 queries across 12 heterogeneous datasets): achieved 94.67% dataset-macro stratified Pass@1 over five trials, with 258/270 successful raw query attempts, compared to 55.51% for Claude Opus 4.6 reference agent, ranking first among 40 leaderboard entries.

Conclusion: The latent equivalence learning framework successfully bridges the gap between agentic semantic reasoning and learned structural prediction, enabling agents to operate over organized evidential states rather than reconstructing cross-schema structure at every query. This represents a significant advancement in structured-data agent capabilities.

Abstract: Structured-data agents in enterprise settings must reason over complex data environments whose relevant evidence is distributed across schemas, relationships, policies, and recurring business roles. Modern agentic systems often address this burden through reusable markdown-style memory or skill files that preserve previously discovered information for later queries, reducing the need to rediscover the same structure repeatedly. This is useful, but it obscures a natural division of labor: agents are well suited to semantic reasoning, while learned systems are well suited to predicting and organizing recurring structure. We introduce latent equivalence learning to bridge this gap. The framework separates persistent task-relevant identities from their dataset-relative realizations. In our realization, supporting and opposing evidence shape support-realized Gaussian prototypes that learn how those identities are expressed in a particular data environment, while soft-membership profiles retain distinctions lost under a hard assignment. A separate learned query-prototype system represents recurring evidential requirements and maps them through a learned compatibility function into the same persistent identity structure. This identity-factorized, query-conditioned routing materializes the relevant dataset-specific evidence for downstream reasoning, allowing the agent to operate over an already organized evidential state rather than reconstructing cross-schema structure at every query. On the Data Agent Benchmark, spanning 54 queries across 12 heterogeneous datasets, our full implementation achieves 94.67% dataset-macro stratified Pass@1 over five complete trials and 258/270 successful raw query attempts, compared with 55.51% for the benchmark's Claude Opus 4.6 reference agent, ranking first among 40 leaderboard entries at submission.

</details>


### [189] [Making Agents More Consistent: Skills Should Form Habits for Repeat Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25299)
*Travis Weber,Rohit Taneja*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses inconsistency in AI agents performing repeated tasks. The authors propose "skill habit formation" - where agents create deterministic "habits" from their successful execution history that run as scripts instead of re-reasoning. On text-to-SQL tasks, habit-formed variants achieved 100% consistency (vs 26-62% for reasoning arms) while using 14-56% fewer tokens, becoming net positive after 7-53 reuses. The trade-off is 2.6% accuracy loss on natural paraphrases and 26% near boundary cases, but deterministic errors enable auditability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI agents are inconsistent on repeated work (38-74% disagreement rates) and wasteful (95%+ of output re-deriving known plans). Buyers, auditors, and regulators require consistency. The authors seek to make agents deterministic and auditable while reducing computational waste.

Method: Skill habit formation: (1) Agent mines execution history for candidate skills - deterministic variants that compete against the incumbent; (2) Candidate declares its input space region; (3) Four gates of ascending cost admit candidates; (4) Central gate tests candidate's execution trace against retained reference within tolerance measured from reference's run-to-run variability; (5) Common case runs as script, rest falls through to reasoning.

Result: On text-to-SQL: habit-formed variant reproduced on all 456 repeated dispatches (vs 11-26/42 for reasoning arms). Used 14-56% fewer tokens. Net positive after 7-53 reuses. Guard admitted work it should have deferred on 2.6% of natural paraphrases and 26% near boundary. 11/13 failures invisible to trace-conformance gate. Separating routing from parameter extraction raised end-to-end accuracy from 0.888 to 0.952 at 43% cost.

Conclusion: Skill habit formation achieves perfect consistency while reducing token usage, but at the cost of accuracy on edge cases (2.6-26% failure rates). Deterministic errors are as reliable as good habits - this is the price of auditability. The approach is non-inferior to reasoning arms (p<0.0001) and becomes economically beneficial after moderate reuse.

Abstract: On repeated work, agents are inconsistent. We ran 42 tasks three times each and found that, depending on the model, 38% to 74% returned answers that did not agree. Consistency is what a buyer, an auditor, or a regulator requires, and agents do not have it. They are wasteful too: 95.3% to 97.2% of what an agent generates goes to re-deriving a plan the system already knows.
  We propose skill habit formation. An agent mines its own execution history for candidate skills, deterministic variants that compete against the incumbent rather than replacing it. A candidate declares the region of input space it claims, so the common case runs as a script and the rest falls through to reasoning. Four gates of ascending cost admit candidates; the central one tests a candidate's execution trace against a retained reference, within a tolerance measured from that reference's own run-to-run variability.
  On text-to-SQL, three of four reasoning arms reproduced their own output on 11 to 13 of 42 repeated questions and the fourth on 26 of 42, while a habit-formed variant reproduced on all 456 dispatches we repeated and was non-inferior to every arm it replaced (p<0.0001). It also used 14% to 56% fewer tokens, turning net positive after 7 to 53 reuses.
  We measured what this costs in accuracy. The guard admitted work it should have deferred on 2.6% of natural paraphrases and 26% of inputs near its boundary, and 11 of 13 such failures were invisible to the trace-conformance gate at any threshold. Deterministic errors repeat exactly: a bad habit is as reliable as a good one, and that is the price of the property that makes the system auditable. Separating routing from parameter extraction raised end-to-end accuracy from 0.888 to 0.952 at 43% of the cost.

</details>


### [190] [Potential for Enhanced Learning in Machine Learning Classes by Using Wiki LLM Indexing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25303)
*Brian Wright*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper compares two knowledge representations (vector RAG vs. LLM-compiled wiki) for grounding large language models as course-specific tutors in machine learning. The wiki approach outperformed retrieval on cross-unit concept linking questions while maintaining full source citation traceability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work showed fixed retrieval strategies were suboptimal for grounding LLMs in course materials. The authors question whether corpus structure at ingest time matters more than retrieval quantity at query time, seeking a better approach for ML courses that require explicit concept linking and traceability.

Method: Controlled head-to-head comparison over identical classroom corpus (Foundations of Machine Learning): (A) vector RAG (best prior configuration) and (B) LLM-compiled wiki synthesizing corpus into linked concept pages with cross-references and source citations. Evaluated 59 questions across four categories (single-fact recall, cross-unit concept linking, synthesis/explanation, currency post-revision) using LLM judge against human-authored rubric.

Result: Single-fact questions: similar performance (9.33 vs 9.96/10). Cross-unit linking: wiki significantly outperformed (9.93 vs 8.14). Wiki achieved 100% grounding in cited sources vs 64% for retrieval. Wiki citations enable traceback to source lectures.

Conclusion: Wiki-based structure supports foundational elements of best practice for ML courses, providing superior concept linking and source traceability. Instructors should consider wiki-based structure for AI-supported learning in ML courses, though further testing is needed.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly deployed as course-specific tutors, but their usefulness depends on grounding in vetted instructional materials that are often revised mid-semester. Our prior work built a multimodal retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) system over an authentic machine learning course corpus (Foundations of Machine Learning) and found that retrieval improved contextual grounding, but that fixed retrieval strategies were suboptimal. That motivates a different question: whether how a corpus is structured at ingest time matters more than how much is retrieved at query time. We present a controlled head-to-head comparison of two knowledge representations over an identical classroom corpus: (A) vector RAG, replicating the best-performing configuration from our prior study, and (B) an LLM-compiled wiki (Karpathy framework), in which the corpus is synthesized at ingest into linked concept pages with explicit cross-references and citations back to source materials. We evaluate 59 questions spanning single-fact recall, cross-unit concept linking, synthesis and explanation, and currency after a syllabus revision, scored by an LLM judge against a human-authored rubric. Both representations answered single-fact questions about equally well (9.33 vs. 9.96 of 10), but diverged sharply on questions requiring links across course units. The compiled wiki remained accurate and grounded (9.93; 100% grounded in cited sources), while retrieval scored lower and was markedly less grounded (8.14; 64%). The wiki's citations let students and instructors trace any claim back to the lecture that introduced it, adding a layer of dynamic retrieval that machine learning courses require. While further testing is needed, instructors using AI to support learning in ML courses should consider wiki-based structure for its potential to support foundational elements of best practice.

</details>


### [191] [Clarification Is Not Correction: LLMs Fail to Let Go](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25337)
*Jianzhe Lin,Xiaolin Li,Fei Wang,Robert Douglas,Rajeshkumar Golani,Jubin Chheda*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper identifies "early posterior collapse" as a fundamental failure mode in dialogue systems where models commit to a single interpretation of ambiguous user intent early in conversation, then treat later clarification as contextual refinement rather than correction. This leads to persistent errors even when the final dialogue contains equivalent information presented in a different order.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors challenge the prevailing assumption that dialogue failures stem primarily from memory limitations (context length, lossy summaries, forgotten constraints). They argue this framing misses a deeper issue: models don't forget—they commit too early to interpretations before ambiguity is resolved, and later clarifying information cannot invalidate this committed state.

Method: Controlled dialogue experiments across three domains (writing, planning, coding) using Gemini-2.5-Pro and Gemini-2.5-Flash. Thousands of trials tested whether information order affects outcomes despite equivalent final task-relevant content. Evaluated standard prompting strategies and memory techniques (summaries, chain-of-thought) as potential fixes.

Result: Information order significantly affects outcomes—the same information presented in different sequences yields different results even when final dialogue contains equivalent task-relevant content. Later clarification is treated as refinement of stale task state rather than correction. Coding tasks show exceptional vulnerability due to early assumptions embedding in structured artifacts (interfaces, control flow). Standard interventions (summaries, chain-of-thought) fail reliably: summaries can actually collapse ambiguity further, and CoT reduces explicit reasoning errors without improving actual task success.

Conclusion: The paper argues for shifting research focus from "retaining more context" to "preserving uncertainty." Proposed design principles: (1) hold tentative hypotheses while ambiguity persists, (2) ask before executing when high-impact ambiguity exists, (3) rebuild from revised state when later evidence invalidates earlier interpretation. Early posterior collapse is presented as a fundamental architectural/research problem rather than a simple prompting fix.

Abstract: Dialogue failures in language models are usually framed as memory failures: context too long, summaries lossy, a constraint forgotten. We argue this misses a deeper problem: in many conversations the model does not forget, it commits too early. An ambiguous early turn collapses into a single hidden interpretation, and later clarification is filtered through that commitment. We call this early posterior collapse: unresolved user intent collapsing into a committed task state before ambiguity is resolved. We study it with controlled dialogue tasks in writing, planning, and coding using Gemini-2.5-Pro and Gemini-2.5-Flash. Across thousands of trials, the same information in different orders yields different outcomes, even when the final dialogue contains equivalent task-relevant information. This order effect suggests later clarification is treated as extra context rather than a corrective signal: it refines a stale task state without invalidating it. Coding tasks are especially vulnerable, suggesting early assumptions get embedded in structured artifacts such as interfaces and control flow. Standard prompting and memory strategies do not reliably help: summaries can collapse ambiguity, and chain-of-thought can reduce explicit wrong commitment in reasoning traces without improving final task success. These findings motivate uncertainty-preserving state management. If assistants cannot let go of early interpretations, robustness cannot rely on post hoc correction alone; it must keep ambiguous early turns from hardening into one task state. Assistants should hold tentative hypotheses while ambiguity remains, ask before executing when high-impact ambiguity persists, and rebuild from a revised state when later evidence invalidates an earlier reading. Rather than one prompting fix, we aim to redirect research for interactive LLMs from retaining more context toward preserving uncertainty.

</details>


### [192] [From Decorative to Load-Bearing: Task Difficulty Shapes the Causal Role of Chain-of-Thought](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25366)
*Renee Jia,Di Mu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces "continuation-based causal testing" to measure how load-bearing Chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning is for final answers. The key finding is that CoT monitoring faces a structural dilemma: on easy tasks, models bypass their reasoning (CoT carries little signal); on hard tasks, errors propagate through corrupted reasoning steps before any monitor can intervene. This creates a fundamental problem for CoT-based AI safety oversight.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Chain-of-thought monitoring is only meaningful if written reasoning causally constrains the answer. Current methods don't distinguish between mechanistic faithfulness (how reasoning is computed) and behavioral load-bearingness (whether reasoning actually determines the answer). The authors seek to measure this behavioral notion directly.

Method: Continuation-based causal testing: an ablation-patch intervention that perturbs one reasoning step, truncates the chain, and forces the model to continue from the corrupted prefix. This measures how much the CoT "loads" on the final answer. Experiments across Gemma-2-9B-IT, Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, and DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B on GSM8K, MMLU, and BIG-Bench Hard. Includes a 2x2 analysis, variance partition over 28,584 continuations, judge-sensitivity analysis, and blind two-annotator study (n=500). Also tests linear probes on hidden states and additive activation steering.

Result: CoT load-bearingness tracks model-relative task difficulty: (1) On easy tasks, models silently bypass their own reasoning; (2) On hard tasks, they follow corrupted steps and propagate errors. Error propagation rises 16x from GSM8K to BBH multistep arithmetic. Variance partition attributes 98.8% of explained deviance to task difficulty versus only 0.8% to perturbation type. Reasoning-specific RL suppresses error propagation. Error-propagation vs. non-propagation labels are invariant to judge prompt with perfect inter-annotator agreement (Cohen's kappa = 1.00). Linear probes can separate behavioral modes (silent bypass, self-correction, error propagation), but additive activation steering provides limited causal control—flipping only ~25% of error-propagation cases.

Conclusion: There is a fundamental structural problem with CoT-based oversight and AI safety monitoring: where the reasoning trace is easy to read (easy tasks), it carries little signal; where it matters (hard tasks), errors propagate before a monitor can intervene. Behavioral mode is readable but not reliably controllable. This gradient creates an inherent limitation for CoT-based safety mechanisms.

Abstract: Chain-of-thought (CoT) monitoring is only meaningful if written reasoning causally constrains the answer. We introduce continuation-based causal testing, an ablation-patch intervention that perturbs one reasoning step, truncates the chain, and forces the model to continue from the corrupted prefix. It measures how load-bearing a CoT is for the final answer, a behavioral notion distinct from mechanistic faithfulness. Across Gemma-2-9B-IT, Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, and DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B on GSM8K, MMLU, and BIG-Bench Hard, CoT load-bearingness tracks model-relative task difficulty: on easy tasks models silently bypass their own reasoning; on hard tasks they follow corrupted steps and propagate errors. A matched 2x2 analysis shows task difficulty dominates perturbation type: error propagation rises 16x from GSM8K to BBH multistep arithmetic, and a variance partition over 28,584 continuations attributes 98.8% of explained deviance to task difficulty versus 0.8% to perturbation type. Reasoning-specific RL suppresses error propagation and compresses the gradient. A four-variant judge-sensitivity analysis and blind two-annotator study (n=500) show the error-propagation vs. non-propagation label is invariant to judge prompt, with perfect inter-annotator agreement (Cohen's kappa = 1.00). This gradient creates a structural problem for CoT-based oversight and AI safety monitoring: where the trace is easy to read it carries little signal, and where it matters errors propagate before a monitor can intervene. Linear probes on hidden states separate silent bypass, self-correction, and error propagation, but additive activation steering provides limited causal control, flipping only about 25% of error-propagation cases at best. Behavioral mode is readable but not reliably controllable.

</details>


### [193] [Robust Failure, Conservative Repair: Textual Knowledge Distillation from Cross-Model Failures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25400)
*Andrew Ren,Haokun Liu,Chenhao Tan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses instability in failure-based textual knowledge distillation, where knowledge transferred from a source model's errors may not generalize well to other models. The authors propose RFCR (Robust Failure, Conservative Repair), which derives rules from failures shared across multiple models, sharpens rule application boundaries using boundary cases, and abstains when no useful rule is found. On a 400-item BIG-Bench Hard task set, RFCR improved baseline cheat sheet performance from 68.50% to 71.25% (+2.75 pp).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Failure-based textual knowledge distillation can transfer useful knowledge from a source model's errors to other models, but this transfer is unstable. Rule atoms (standalone rules injected at inference time) can encode transferable task knowledge or model-specific reasoning patches that may confuse other models. Additionally, injected rules can be misapplied to unrelated cases, causing incorrect answer flips. A preliminary experiment showed that rule distillation from a single model's failures underperforms baseline cheat sheets on non-source model families.

Method: The paper proposes Robust Failure, Conservative Repair (RFCR), a textual distillation procedure with three key components: (1) deriving rules from failures shared across multiple models (rather than a single model), (2) sharpening rule application boundaries using boundary cases, and (3) abstaining when no useful rule is found. This approach builds on a pipeline that distills training examples into task-specific cheat sheets.

Result: On a 400-item BIG-Bench Hard task set, RFCR improved baseline cheat sheets from 68.50% to 71.25%, representing a +2.75 percentage point improvement with 95% CI [+1.25, +4.50]. Notably, this improvement occurred without performance degradation on previously correct cases. Ablations and cross-model diagnostics confirmed that accuracy gains come from both new knowledge injection and strict rule-application control.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates that failure-based knowledge distillation can be stabilized by focusing on failures shared across multiple models rather than single-model failures. The conservative approach of sharpening rule boundaries and abstaining when uncertain prevents performance degradation while enabling meaningful improvements. This work provides a pathway for more reliable transfer of failure-derived knowledge across different model families.

Abstract: Failure-based textual knowledge distillation aims to discover gaps in a model's knowledge by examining its task errors. The distilled knowledge can be useful for the reasoning of both this model ("source model") and other models. However, this transfer of knowledge may not be stable. We define a rule atom to be a standalone rule injected into a model's textual input at inference time. A rule atom can encode transferable task knowledge or model-specific reasoning patches that can confuse other models. Also, the injected rule atoms can be misapplied to unrelated cases, causing the model to incorrectly flip its answer based on irrelevant information. Building on a pipeline that distills training examples into task-specific cheat sheets that aid model reasoning, we examine when failure-derived rules can improve these cheat sheets. Our early experiment shows rule distillation from a single model's failures underperforms the baseline cheat sheet on non-source model families. This motivates Robust Failure, Conservative Repair (RFCR), a textual distillation procedure that derives rules from failures shared across models, sharpens their application boundaries using boundary cases, and abstains when no useful rule is found. On a 400-item BIG-Bench Hard task set, RFCR improves the baseline cheat sheets from 68.50% to 71.25% (+2.75 pp; 95% CI [+1.25,+4.50]) without performance degradation on previously correct cases. Ablations and cross-model diagnostics support that accuracy gains come from both new knowledge injection and strict rule-application control.

</details>


### [194] [Efficient Iterative Retrieval with Heterogeneous Batching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25405)
*Dohyun Park,Hubertus Franke,Daniel G. Waddington,Swaminathan Sundararaman,Yongjoo Park*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Orthrus is a serving system that improves GPU utilization and throughput for information retrieval by combining embedding and generative models in a unified inference loop with heterogeneous batching, achieving 1.28x-4.52x throughput improvement and up to 55.8% latency reduction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current serving systems suffer from low throughput and poor GPU utilization because they execute embedding and generative models in isolation. Coarse-grained partitioning (dedicating GPUs to specific tasks) fails to adapt to dynamic workloads and creates computational "bubbles".

Method: Orthrus addresses these challenges through two key techniques: (1) chunked embedding with incremental pooling to unify embedding and generation workloads with conflicting computational patterns, and (2) workload-aware batch composition adjustment to optimize batch composition for high performance.

Result: Evaluated on four A100 GPUs, Orthrus achieves 1.28x--4.52x higher throughput on controlled workloads and up to 55.8% lower end-to-end p99 latency on an iterative-RAG benchmark, relative to baseline deployments.

Conclusion: The proposed system successfully enables heterogeneous batching within a unified inference loop, effectively addressing the computational conflicts between embedding and generation workloads while significantly improving throughput and reducing latency.

Abstract: Modern information retrieval increasingly employs both embedding and generative models to handle complex queries. However, current serving systems suffer from low throughput and poor GPU utilization because they execute these models in isolation. Coarse-grained partitioning, such as dedicating GPUs to specific tasks, fails to adapt to dynamic workloads and creates computational "bubbles". To address these, we present Orthrus, a serving system that performs heterogeneous batching within a unified inference loop. The primary challenge lies in unifying embedding and generation workloads with conflicting computational patterns while optimizing batch composition for high performance. Orthrus addresses these challenges through chunked embedding with incremental pooling and by adjusting batch composition in a workload-aware manner. Evaluation on four A100 GPUs shows that, relative to baseline deployments, Orthrus achieves 1.28$\times$--4.52$\times$ higher throughput on controlled workloads and up to 55.8% lower end-to-end p99 latency on an iterative-RAG benchmark. We release our code at https://github.com/illinoisdata/Orthrus .

</details>


### [195] [From Offline Proxies to Online Decisions: A Layered Engagement Evaluation Framework for Conversational AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25408)
*Xuanyi Li,Vaskar Nath,Hossein Amirkhani,Jay Li,Alex Deng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes using offline proxy signals to predict online A/B experiment outcomes for conversational AI systems, addressing the limitation that live experiments are costly in traffic and time. The authors develop a three-step alignment framework and validate it on 489 offline-online contrasts from 27 experiments, achieving 81.1% F1 on the primary test set compared to 34.3% for raw classifier scores.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Online A/B experiments are the gold standard for user engagement decisions in conversational AI, but traffic and time constraints limit how many changes can be tested. There is a need for offline signals that can predict experiment outcomes without requiring live user exposure, enabling better prioritization of candidates before allocating scarce experiment traffic.

Method: The authors propose a "composite" construction with three alignment steps: (1) behavioral label to product outcome, (2) learned classifier to candidate-assistant behavior, and (3) aggregated offline signal to experiment effect. They also contribute a diagnosis checklist and an evaluation protocol using interval-aware decision agreement (comparing confidence intervals rather than point estimates) and within-experiment ranking. The instantiation includes: an evaluation suite for scoring candidate behavior, an engagement classifier trained to predict session/prompt-level engagement, and a calibration layer mapping sample-level score differences to online model-level engagement deltas.

Result: On the primary test set of 113 contrasts from eight experiments run after the map was frozen, the composite achieves 81.1% F1, compared to 34.3% for the raw classifier score. The composite makes no wrong-direction calls, while the raw score makes 31 such errors. The evaluation spans 489 paired offline-online contrasts from 27 experiments on a deployed multi-turn assistant, covering model checkpoints to system-prompt tuning. All offline predictions were computed before their corresponding experiments ran to prevent overfitting.

Conclusion: The evidence supports using the composite offline signal to prioritize candidates before allocating scarce experiment traffic. This approach has been deployed for selecting among training checkpoints and tuning system prompts, demonstrating practical utility in real-world conversational AI development.

Abstract: Online A/B experiments are the decision standard for user engagement, but traffic and readout time limit how many conversational-AI changes can be tested. We ask whether an offline signal designed to be computable without treatment-arm user exposure agrees with the outcomes of those experiments. We contribute a reusable construction and diagnosis checklist that treats an offline proxy as a chain of three alignments: behavioral label to product outcome, learned classifier to candidate-assistant behavior, and aggregated offline signal to experiment effect. A companion evaluation protocol audits the whole composite by interval-aware decision agreement, which compares offline and online confidence intervals instead of point estimates, and by within-experiment ranking. The instantiation we evaluate comprises a fixed evaluation suite on which candidate behavior is scored, an engagement classifier trained to predict session/prompt level engagements, and a calibration layer mapping sample-level score differences to online model-level engagement deltas. We then report the audit: 489 paired offline-online contrasts (one candidate arm against its control) from 27 experiments on a deployed multi-turn assistant, spanning model checkpoints to system-prompt tuning. Our primary test uses the 113 contrasts from eight experiments that ran after the map was frozen: on these the composite reaches 81.1% F1, against 34.3% for the raw classifier score it is built on, and makes no wrong-direction calls where that raw score makes 31. Every offline prediction was computed before its experiment ran to prevent overfitting. The evidence supports using the composite to prioritize candidates before scarce experiment traffic is allocated---in our deployment of the experiment, selecting among training checkpoints and tuning system prompts.

</details>


### [196] [ZeroGate: Trust-Preserving Fast Paths for Governed AI Agent Runtimes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25443)
*Zexun Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ZeroGate is an authorization system that separates exact-action approval from durable local admission by using signed short-lived ActionPasses and a trusted runtime adapter to reconstruct final actions. The system couples nonce consumption, quota updates, and admission receipts in SQLite transactions. Evaluation across 4800 cloud attempts shows prepared execution (9.8-11.4ms p95) is faster at dispatch than synchronous execution (25-334ms), but the complete lifecycle is longer in prepared mode—indicating the boundary improvement is not a net speedup.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Moving authorization earlier in a workflow can shorten an agent's dispatch boundary without removing authorization work, but it introduces the challenge of handling actions whose payload, authority, or relevant state may change between approval and execution. The paper seeks to address this revalidation problem while maintaining security guarantees.

Method: ZeroGate uses a two-phase approach: (1) an issuer signs a short-lived ActionPass for the initial action, and (2) a trusted runtime adapter reconstructs the final action before a local gate checks its binding and consumes its nonce. A SQLite transaction atomically couples nonce consumption, applicable quota updates, and an admission receipt. The authors prove a conditional decision-preservation proposition: successful local admission implies a specified synchronous policy would authorize the same action at the admission point, given certain conditions (sound approval, current policy dependencies, faithful observations, atomic consumption).

Result: The evaluation involved three components: authored semantic fixtures, controlled concurrency and crash experiments, and an Azure Blob study comparing synchronous versus prepared execution. Across 4800 cloud attempts, prepared worker-admission-to-dispatch p95 latency ranged from 9.802 to 11.374 ms, versus 25.018 to 334.000 ms synchronously across tested concurrency levels. However, prepared mean complete lifecycle is longer at every level—the boundary improvement does not translate to net speedup.

Conclusion: The contribution is NOT a new cryptographic primitive or universal performance frontier. Instead, it provides: (1) an explicit revalidation contract that formalizes the conditions under which early authorization remains valid, (2) a durable reference boundary separating approval from admission, and (3) an auditable comparison demonstrating where authorization cost is paid. The key insight is that while moving authorization earlier can reduce dispatch latency, the total lifecycle cost may increase, so the trade-off must be evaluated based on specific system requirements.

Abstract: Moving authorization earlier can shorten an agent's dispatch boundary without removing authorization work. It can also admit an action whose payload, authority, or relevant state has changed. ZeroGate separates exact-action approval from durable local admission: an issuer signs a short-lived ActionPass, and a trusted runtime adapter reconstructs the final action before a local gate checks its binding and consumes its nonce. A SQLite transaction couples nonce consumption, applicable quota updates, and an admission receipt. We state a conditional decision-preservation proposition: successful local admission implies that a specified synchronous policy would authorize the same action at the admission point, provided approval is sound, all policy dependencies are represented and current, observations are faithful, and consumption is atomic. The implementation alone establishes neither current-world freshness nor exactly-once remote effects. Evaluation separates authored semantic fixtures, controlled concurrency and crash experiments, and an Azure Blob study comparing synchronous and prepared execution through the same issuer and gate. Both modes mint an exact-action pass; lifecycle latency includes preparation and prepared-batch dwell. Across 4800 cloud attempts, prepared worker-admission-to-dispatch p95 ranges from 9.802 to 11.374 ms, versus 25.018 to 334.000 ms synchronously, across the tested concurrency levels. Prepared mean complete lifecycle is longer at every level: the boundary improvement is not a net speedup. The contribution is an explicit revalidation contract, a durable reference boundary, and an auditable comparison of where authorization cost is paid, not a new cryptographic primitive or a universal performance frontier.

</details>


### [197] [Rollout Efficiency in Reinforcement Learning for Reasoning Large Language Models: A Taxonomy and Future Directions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25463)
*Niloofar Gholipour,Marcos Assuncao,Gursimran Singh,Timothy Yu,Rajkumar Buyya,Julien Gascon-Samson,Zhenan Fan,Yong Zhang,Xiaojie Xu,Yaqiang Yao,Xiaolong Bai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This survey provides a systematic taxonomy of recent research on rollout efficiency for reasoning-oriented reinforcement learning, classifying approaches from mechanism and bottleneck perspectives, and identifying future research directions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reasoning-oriented RL enables LLMs to solve complex multi-step tasks but faces substantial training costs due to rollout (trajectory generation), making efficient rollout mechanisms critical for maintaining data quality while reducing costs.

Method: Systematic taxonomy classification from both mechanism and bottleneck perspectives, analyzing how different technique families address distinct sources of rollout inefficiency.

Result: A comprehensive classification of approaches, analysis of combination opportunities and potential conflicts, identification of evaluation gaps, and discussion of open challenges.

Conclusion: The survey identifies gaps in efficiency gain reporting and discusses future research directions for improving rollout efficiency in reasoning-oriented RL systems.

Abstract: Reasoning-oriented reinforcement learning enables large language models to solve mathematical, coding, and other multi-step tasks, but shifts a substantial portion of the training cost to rollout, where trajectories are generated for policy updates. Efficient rollout mechanisms are therefore essential to reduce this cost while maintaining the freshness, consistency, and statistical validity of training data. This survey provides a systematic taxonomy of recent research on rollout efficiency for reasoning-oriented reinforcement learning, classifying existing approaches from both mechanism and bottleneck perspectives. Based on this taxonomy, we analyze how different technique families address distinct sources of rollout inefficiency, examine opportunities and potential conflicts for combining them, identify gaps in the evaluation and reporting of efficiency gains, and discuss open challenges and future research directions.

</details>


### [198] [Real-Time Hand Gesture Recognition for OpenXR Using Transformer-Based Machine Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25466)
*Salar Rezayani,Russell Butler*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This study proposes a transformer-based hand gesture recognition system using OpenXR hand-tracking data in Unity, achieving improved classification accuracy by capturing temporal dependencies in hand joint positions and wrist rotation angles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hand gesture recognition is crucial for intuitive human-computer interaction in gaming, VR, and robotics applications, but existing systems struggle with robustness across varying hand orientations and sizes.

Method: The approach uses transformer-based machine learning models trained on positional data of hand joints and wrist rotation angles captured through the OpenXR standard in Unity, leveraging transformers' sequential modeling capabilities to capture temporal dependencies within short gesture windows.

Result: The system demonstrates significant improvement in gesture classification accuracy and robustness across different hand orientations and sizes.

Conclusion: The transformer-based approach successfully improves hand gesture recognition. Future work will focus on detecting movement flow and transitions between gestures to create a more comprehensive gesture recognition system.

Abstract: Hand gesture recognition is a key component in human-computer interaction (HCI), enabling intuitive interfaces for applications in gaming, virtual reality (VR), robotics, and more. This study integrates transformer-based machine-learning models for real-time hand gesture recognition, using hand-tracking data captured through the OpenXR standard in Unity. We leverage positional data of hand joints and wrist rotation angles to train a custom gesture recognition system. By utilizing the sequential modeling capabilities of transformers, the system captures temporal dependencies within short gesture windows and classifies gestures robustly across hand orientations and sizes. The results show a significant improvement in gesture classification accuracy. Building on this, we outline how the approach can be extended toward detecting the flow of movement, i.e., the transitions between gestures, as future work.

</details>


### [199] [ShowTellArena: Evaluating Business Workflow Understanding from Demonstrations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25467)
*David Garg,Ritobrata Sarkar,Ehsan Azarnasab,Siddhartha Borah*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ShowTellArena is a benchmark protocol and public dataset for evaluating AI agents' comprehension of narrated business demonstrations. It contains 50 business workflow tasks with recordings, screenshots, narration, fixture seeds, and 502 questions across domains like finance, hiring, procurement, customer decisions, inventory, and logistics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The core problem is how to evaluate what an AI agent has understood from a teaching demonstration, similar to how one would teach a colleague by showing work and explaining decisions along the way.

Method: The benchmark holds business scenarios and quizzes fixed while allowing each product to capture lessons through its own teaching interface. Questions test operational rules, boundaries, exceptions, and errors in proposed automations. The study analyzed 218 selected pilot attempts across 39 workflow cases, including 28 cases attempted by all three evaluated systems.

Result: Exploratory results revealed both answer errors and failures to complete the teaching experience. The release exposed verification gaps and the pilot's uneven coverage, exclusions, and grading provenance.

Conclusion: The contribution is an inspectable dataset and assessment workflow that others can extend. The selected pilot is not a controlled product ranking, indicating this is a foundational release for future research rather than a competitive evaluation.

Abstract: We often teach a colleague by showing the work and explaining the decisions as we go. How can we check what an agent understood from the same lesson? We introduce ShowTellArena, a benchmark protocol and public dataset for comprehension after narrated business demonstrations. The v1.0 release contains 50 business workflow tasks, with recordings, screenshots, narration, fixture seeds, and 502 questions. Tasks span finance, hiring, procurement, customer decisions, inventory, and logistics. The protocol holds the business scenario and quiz fixed while allowing each product to capture the lesson through its own teaching interface. Questions test operational rules, boundaries, exceptions, and errors in proposed automations. We analyze 218 selected pilot attempts across 39 workflow cases, including 28 cases attempted by all three evaluated systems. These exploratory results expose both answer errors and failures to complete the teaching experience. We describe the release's verification gaps and the pilot's uneven coverage, exclusions, and grading provenance. The contribution is an inspectable dataset and assessment workflow that others can extend; the selected pilot is not a controlled product ranking.

</details>


### [200] [RAG-NAROK: Retrieval-Aware Knowledge Corpus Poisoning in RAG with Source-specific Refutation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25469)
*Abdullahil Kafi,Alvi Ataur Khalil*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RAG-NAROK is a novel adversarial attack framework that exploits the transparency of Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) systems by generating query-specific refutation documents that target and devalue the legitimate sources retrieved for a given query, steering LLM outputs toward attacker-chosen answers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing knowledge-base poisoning attacks are fundamentally static and blind to the competitive documentary landscape in the retrieval context. The authors aim to address this limitation by introducing an adaptive attack that exploits RAG pipeline transparency to specifically target retrieved sources.

Method: RAG-NAROK operates in two phases: (1) it extracts legitimate source identities from the RAG pipeline's transparency features, then (2) generates "Anchor-Specific Refutation documents" that explicitly name and devalue retrieved sources while leveraging recency and authority biases to steer generation toward a target answer.

Result: RAG-NAROK significantly outperforms static poisoning baselines across diverse domains, demonstrating the effectiveness of query-adaptive adversarial attacks in RAG systems.

Conclusion: The attack reveals a fundamental tension between RAG transparency and AI security - the very features that make RAG systems verifiable and auditable also create new adversarial surfaces that can be exploited to manipulate model outputs.

Abstract: Retrieval augmented generation (RAG) systems have emerged as the dominant architecture for grounding large language model (LLM) outputs in verifiable external knowledge, yet their structural reliance on a dynamic retrieval pipeline introduces a largely unexplored class of adversarial vulnerability. Existing knowledge-base poisoning attacks are fundamentally static. Adversarial documents are pre-computed and injected without any awareness of what the victim system will actually retrieve for a given query, leaving the attack blind to the competitive documentary landscape that surrounds its payload in the generator's context window. Unlike traditional static poisoning attacks that are blind to the retrieved context, we introduce RAG-NAROK (Retrieval-Anchored Generation Negation And Response Quality Collapse), a RAG attack framework that adapts to the query text. RAG-NAROK exploits the transparency inherent in RAG pipeline to first extract the legitimate source identities, then generate Anchor-Specific Refutation documents that explicitly name and devalue retrieved sources while leveraging recency and authority biases to steer the text generation toward a target answer. Our results demonstrate that RAG-NAROK significantly outperforms static baselines across diverse domains, revealing a fundamental tension between RAG transparency and AI security.

</details>


### [201] [Spectra: A Rules-Driven LLM Pipeline for Automated KYC Document Processing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25474)
*Miray Wahib,Ethan Tran,Rea Mourad,Mira Muti,Nikita Dvornik*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents Spectra, an AI-assisted document processing platform for automating KYC (Know Your Client) onboarding in capital markets. The system combines a structured rules engine with LLM-based agents to classify documents, extract structured data, and validate compliance against regulatory policies. By consolidating a traditional four-party process into two parties and eliminating sequential handoffs, the system significantly reduces onboarding time and human effort.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: KYC onboarding in capital markets is currently a manual, time-intensive process requiring analysts to classify documents, extract structured data from heterogeneous sources, and validate compliance against complex regulatory policies. This process takes multiple weeks per client due to sequential handoffs between multiple parties, creating significant operational inefficiencies.

Method: The authors propose a restructured workflow that consolidates the traditional four-party process into two parties that share most of the work and can be automated together, eliminating intermediate handoffs. The Spectra platform combines: 1) a structured rules engine that encodes compliance policy as a queryable database for focused context injection, and 2) LLM-based classification, extraction, and validation agents. Rather than a single monolithic prompt, document processing is decomposed into isolated, auditable stages, each optimized independently and traceable to specific policy clauses.

Result: On real KYC documents, Spectra achieves 100% classification accuracy and 89.4% extraction accuracy. The human review burden dropped by 96%.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates that KYC onboarding processes contain repeatable components well-suited to AI automation. The combination of a rules engine with LLM-based agents, along with workflow consolidation, can dramatically reduce both processing time and human effort while maintaining high accuracy in document classification and data extraction.

Abstract: Know Your Client (KYC) onboarding in capital markets requires analysts to manually classify documents, extract structured data from heterogeneous sources, and validate compliance against complex regulatory policies. This process requires significant analyst time per client, with end-to-end onboarding often stretching to multiple weeks due to sequential handoffs. In this work, we analyze an on-boarding process and find that it comprises repeatable components well-suited to AI automation. We therefore propose a restructured workflow to be amenable to automation: we consolidate the traditional four-party process into two parties that share most of the work and can be automated together, eliminating intermediate handoffs that compound delays. To automate the remaining steps, we introduce Spectra, an AI-assisted document processing platform that combines a structured rules engine with LLM-based classification, extraction, and validation agents. The rules engine encodes compliance policy as a queryable database, enabling focused context injection that reduces token usage while improving extraction precision. Rather than a single monolithic prompt, the system decomposes document processing into isolated, auditable stages, each optimized independently and traceable to specific policy clauses. In evaluation on real KYC documents, Spectra achieves 100% classification accuracy and 89.4% extraction accuracy. Human review burden dropped by 96%.

</details>


### [202] [Queer inclusion in speech datasets: An audit and taxonomy of practical tensions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25491)
*Brooklyn Sheppard,Anaelia Ovalle,Adina Williams,Levent Sagun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper audits speech datasets for LGBTQIA+ representation, finding extremely low queer speaker inclusion (0-1.4%), and develops a taxonomy of tensions between conventional dataset collection methods and participatory approaches needed for marginalized communities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The researchers aim to address the lack of queer voices in speech technology datasets, which creates exclusion and potential bias in speech AI systems. They use the queer community as a case study to understand broader challenges in collecting speech data from marginalized populations.

Method: The researchers conducted an audit of six diverse speech datasets to measure queer representation, then compared these with two datasets specifically created by, for, and with the queer community. Through this comparison, they identified tensions between standard AI speech data collection practices and participatory approaches.

Result: Queer representation in the six audited datasets is critically low (0-1.4% of speakers), making robust disparity measurement impossible. The study also identified multiple tensions between conventional speech dataset collection customs and values emphasized in participatory research with marginalized communities.

Conclusion: The paper provides a taxonomy describing the tensions between standard speech dataset collection practices and participatory approaches, highlighting the need for more inclusive and community-engaged data collection methodologies in AI and speech technology research.

Abstract: In this paper, we examine speech datasets for their inclusion of LGBTQIA+, or queer, voices and provide a taxonomy of tensions to better understand why there is a lack of such voices in current speech technology datasets. Through an audit of six diverse speech datasets, we find that measurable queer representation is low (0-1.4% of speakers) - insufficient for robust disparity measurement. We take this community as a case study to consider what challenges and tensions are associated with collecting speech data from marginalized communities. For comparison, we audit an additional two datasets from the speech sciences that were created by, for, and with the queer community. We note that many customs in speech dataset collection efforts in AI and speech technology research may conflict with values emphasized in participatory approaches with marginalized communities, and provide a taxonomy describing these tensions.

</details>


### [203] [FIRE: Failure-Informed Runtime Engineering for Reliable Language-Model Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26048)
*Nikita Agarwal,Nivedit Jain*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Runtime policies (targeted natural-language instructions and action denials applied at failure-prone states) significantly improve reliability of language-model agents without changing model weights or prompts. On Terminal-Bench 2.1, policies increased repeated success (pass^2) by 3.4-9.2 percentage points across three GPT-5.6 tiers, with Sol reaching 73.6%. A five-arm randomized experiment confirmed the mechanism works through intended corrective behavior (22/24 attempts) versus generic alternatives (max 14/24).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language-model agents often reach working solutions but fail to consistently deliver them. The authors investigate whether targeted interventions at failure-prone states can improve reliability without modifying the underlying model or user prompts.

Method: 1) Applied runtime policies (natural-language instructions/action denials) at states preceding observed failures across 87-task Terminal-Bench 2.1 suite with two attempts per task. 2) Conducted five-arm randomized experiment comparing real policies against: no policy, timing-matched sham, and generic verification/reconsideration.

Result: Across all three GPT-5.6 tiers (Luna/Terra/Sol), runtime policies increased pass^2: 50.6→54.0%, 55.2→60.9%, 64.4→73.6%. Sol's best-of-two improved by 1.2 points while repeated success rose by 9.2 points, showing policies convert reachable solutions into dependable delivery. Policy-guided Terra (71.4%) outperformed unassisted Sol (64.3%) at half the cost. Randomized experiment showed real policies achieved 61% on eligible tasks vs 39% without policy, 36% sham, and 39-43% for generic alternatives. Intended corrective behavior appeared in 22/24 coded policy attempts vs max 14 in other arms.

Conclusion: Runtime policies represent a practical reliability layer that substantially improves repeatability of capabilities agents already possess, without requiring model changes or prompt modifications. This demonstrates how engineering around models can unlock dependability for real-world use cases.

Abstract: Language-model agents often reach a working solution and then fail to consistently deliver it. We study runtime policies: targeted natural-language instructions and action denials applied by the agent harness at states that preceded observed failures, without changing model weights or the user prompt. With this, keeping capability constant, we observe a meaningful unlock in delivered reliability. Across the complete 87-task Terminal-Bench 2.1 suite, with two attempts per task, policies increase repeated success (pass^2) in all three GPT-5.6 tiers: 50.6% to 54.0% for Luna, 55.2% to 60.9% for Terra, and 64.4% to 73.6% for Sol. Sol's best-of-two success changes by 1.2 points while repeated success rises by 9.2, showing that policies chiefly convert reachable solutions into dependable delivery. We further cover 14 tasks under Terra's frozen portfolio. Policy-guided Terra reaches 71.4%, compared with 64.3% for unassisted Sol, at about half the cost, demonstrating how engineering around models could unlock dependability for a use case. To isolate the mechanism we run a randomized five-arm experiment: real policies reach 61% on eligible tasks, versus 39% without a policy, 36% with a timing-matched sham, and 39 to 43% with generic verification or reconsideration. The intended corrective behavior appears in 22 of 24 coded policy attempts, against at most 14 in any other arm. Runtime policies are therefore a practical reliability layer: they make capabilities an agent already possesses substantially more repeatable.

</details>


### [204] [Towards participatory speech dataset curation: A queer case study and conceptual framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25496)
*Brooklyn Sheppard,Anaelia Ovalle,Adina Williams,Levent Sagun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a participatory framework for speech dataset creation focused on marginalized communities, using the LGBTQIA+ community as a case study. The framework addresses concerns about AI harms (such as 'gaydar' technologies) by involving communities in the data curation process itself.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The LGBTQIA+ community has documented concerns about AI and has experienced harms from technologies like 'gaydar' that purportedly identify individuals as queer. Existing speech data collection methods are unsuitable for engaging with queer speakers, necessitating a new participatory approach.

Method: The authors conducted a review of: (1) common speech data collection practices and their limitations for queer speakers, (2) previous participatory AI efforts with queer community engagement, and (3) participatory speech data collection efforts for other marginalized communities. From this review, they developed a conceptual framework drawing on co-design and knowledge sharing principles.

Result: A conceptual framework for participatory speech data curation comprising four overlapping and two-way processes: (1) defining a community, (2) project formulation, (3) modes of participation, and (4) personal autonomy. The framework is designed for and with marginalized communities.

Conclusion: The framework provides a foundation for ethical speech data collection that centers community agency, autonomy, and co-design principles, aiming to prevent AI harms while empowering marginalized communities in the data creation process.

Abstract: In this paper, we motivate the need for a participatory speech dataset creation framework through a case study of the LGBTQIA+, or queer, community - a community with documented concerns about AI and reported harms, including attempts to develop 'gaydar' technologies that purportedly identify individuals as queer. We review common speech data collection practices, why these methods may be unsuitable for engaging with queer speakers, and discuss previous efforts in participatory AI with queer community engagement, as well as participatory endeavours specific to speech data collection for other marginalized communities. From this review, we develop a conceptual framework for participatory speech data curation by, for, and with marginalized communities drawing on insights from co-design and knowledge sharing. We propose a framework comprising overlapping and two-way processes of defining a community, project formulation, modes of participation, and personal autonomy.

</details>


### [205] [Recursive self-improvement of AI research agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26457)
*Dhruv Srikanth,Bingchen Zhao,Dixing Xu,Yuxiang Wu,Zhengyao Jiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AIDE^2 is a system where an AI research agent recursively improves its own code by proposing changes, benchmarking them on R&D tasks, and retaining the best performers. In an 8-day autonomous run, it discovered seven successive improvements (search policy, memory mechanisms) that generalized to four held-out benchmarks and reduced reward hacking from 55% to 32%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation addresses the diminishing returns trend in AI R&D spending. The authors seek to create a self-improvement loop where AI research agents can optimize their own code, potentially counteracting this trend and increasing research efficiency.

Method: AIDE^2 implements a recursive self-improvement loop: (1) the agent proposes changes to its own code, (2) modified versions are benchmarked on a suite of AI R&D tasks, (3) changes performing best on hidden evaluations are retained. This creates an iterative process where each accepted rewrite becomes the agent edited in the next round.

Result: During an autonomous 8-day run, AIDE^2 discovered seven successive improvements including a new search policy and memory mechanisms that compress/manage growing context. These gains generalized to four held-out benchmarks (ML engineering, heuristic algorithm engineering, physics-based weather forecasting). The strongest discovered agent matches or exceeds a human-engineered production research agent on FML-Bench. Additionally, reward hacking rate dropped from 55% to 32% (7pp below human agent), despite not being explicitly optimized.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates that an AI research agent can improve its own research efficiency through recursive self-improvement, and these gains transfer to tasks and domains the loop never encountered. This suggests recursive self-improvement is a viable path for increasing AI research productivity, with the bonus of reduced reward hacking as an emergent property.

Abstract: AI agents are beginning to automate research and development across the AI stack, from improving training efficiency to optimizing inference. A natural next step is to improve the research efficiency of the agents themselves. When an AI research agent's own code is the object of optimization, each accepted rewrite becomes the agent that the next round edits. We refer to this loop as recursive self-improvement. Its significance lies in a long-standing trend, in which increased cumulative spending on R&D yields diminishing returns. Sustained self-improvement offers a way to counter this trend. We present AIDE^2, a system that implements this loop for a frontier AI research agent. It proposes changes to its own code, benchmarks modified versions of itself on a suite of AI R&D tasks, and keeps the changes that perform best on hidden evaluations. In an autonomous 8-day run, AIDE^2 discovered seven successive improvements, ranging from a new search policy to memory mechanisms that compress and manage the agent's growing context. These gains generalize to four held-out benchmarks spanning machine learning engineering, heuristic algorithm engineering, and physics-based weather forecasting, the last of which is out of distribution from the selection tasks. On all four, the strongest discovered agent matches or exceeds a human-engineered production research agent that ranks among the strongest on FML-Bench. On a separate held-out task family, the discovered agents also exhibit reduced reward hacking, a property the loop never explicitly optimized for: the rate falls from 55% to 32% during the run, 7 percentage points below the human-engineered agent. Together, these results show that an AI research agent can improve its own research efficiency through recursive self-improvement, and that these gains transfer to tasks and domains the loop never encountered.

</details>


### [206] [SMTB: Fast Structure-Mapping with Tight Bounds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25508)
*Daniel Weitekamp,Christopher MacLellan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces SMTB (Structure-Mapping with Tight Bounds), a new structure-mapping algorithm that is 5-15x faster than the traditional SME and 50% better at finding mappings in large nested domains. It is part of the CRE (Cognitive Rule Engine) project, which provides a flexible multi-language framework with Python interface to C++ implementations of cognitive algorithms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to address limitations in the Structure-Mapping Engine (SME), which biases higher-order correspondences in tree-like predicate logic. This bias can cause failures in certain situations. The authors aimed to create an algorithm that maximizes relational connectivity without privileging higher-order relations, allowing it to work effectively over arbitrary relational graphs.

Method: The authors developed SMTB, which maximizes relational connectivity without privileging higher-order relations—unlike SME. SMTB is integrated into the broader CRE project, providing a flexible multi-language-compatible framework with Python interface to C++ implementations of core cognitive algorithms including pattern matching, planning, and structure-mapping.

Result: SMTB achieves 5-15x speed improvement over SME and approximately 50% better performance at finding mappings in large nested domains. Evaluation was conducted over 5845 domain pairs from the SME corpus. SMTB also avoids failure modes that SME encounters in certain situations.

Conclusion: SMTB successfully addresses limitations of SME by working equally well over arbitrary relational graphs and tree-like domains of nested predicate logic. The CRE project provides accessible tools for cognitive systems research. The algorithm is particularly advantageous when privileging "higher-orderness" in structure-mapping would cause issues.

Abstract: Structure-mapping forms analogies by aligning systems of relationally connected elements based on shared structure instead of surface features. We introduce a new structure-mapping algorithm: Structure-Mapping with Tight Bounds (SMTB) that is 5--15x faster than the structure-mapping engine (SME) and about 50\% better at finding mappings in large nested domains. SMTB is part of the broader Cognitive Rule Engine (CRE) project, a flexible multi-language-compatible framework with an accessible Python interface to state-of-the-art C++ implementations of core algorithms commonly used in cognitive systems such as pattern matching, planning, and structure-mapping. CRE and SMTB are designed to work with a wide range of representation choices. Unlike SME, which biases higher-order correspondences in tree-like predicate logic, SMTB maximizes relational connectivity without privileging higher-order relations. This allows SMTB to work just as well over arbitrary relational graphs as it does in tree-like domains of nested predicate logic. We discuss situations where privileging "higher-orderness" in structure-mapping can cause issues, and illustrate how SMTB avoids failure modes that SME would encounter in these situations. We also provide an evaluation comparing SMTB to SME v4 over 5845 domain pairs from the SME corpus.

</details>


### [207] [Grow the Harness, Not the Context: From Strategy-Free Scaffolds to Reusable Specialist Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26760)
*Laizhen Li,Jiarui Li,Juanjuan Zhao,Kejiang Ye,Ye Li,Cheng-zhong Xu,Xitong Gao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Growing Harness is a failure-guided training paradigm that transforms recurring control decisions into reusable executable code, reducing LLM calls by 76.0-91.8% while maintaining high success rates across different model scales (4B-120B parameters).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents handle streams of related tasks, but standard harnesses repeatedly ask the model to reconstruct the same control decisions inside each task's context, wasting computational resources on repetitive control logic rather than task-specific semantic reasoning.

Method: 1) Start with a strategy-free scaffold exposing fixed model and tool interfaces but encoding no task-solving controller
2) Use function-level execution traces to localize each failure to a bounded code surface
3) An optimizer repairs a window of failures jointly
4) Apply a success-first held-out gate to roll back repair sequences that harm prior capability
5) Accumulate accepted edits in one shared harness, allowing control structure to emerge from task feedback

Result: Achieved highest mean success in 5/6 benchmark-model settings; trails best by 0.7 pp in the sixth. Reduced LLM calls by 76.0-91.8% and deployed-agent inference cost by 74.4-98.6%. On WebArena-Verified, success remains 44.7-45.3% across model scales while Tool-Calling falls to 6.7% with 4B model.

Conclusion: Persistent program growth can move recurring control out of model context and into low-cost code, yielding reusable specialist agents that remain effective with smaller deployment models. This approach enables efficient LLM usage by reserving model calls for task-specific semantic reasoning while encoding reusable control logic in code.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents often handle streams of related tasks, yet standard harnesses repeatedly ask the model to reconstruct the same control decisions inside each task's context. We study whether task feedback can instead turn recurring control into reusable executable code, while reserving LLM calls for task-specific semantic reasoning. We introduce Growing Harness, a failure-guided training paradigm that learns the agent harness itself from a strategy-free scaffold that exposes fixed model and tool interfaces but encodes no task-solving controller. Function-level execution traces localize each failure to a bounded code surface, an optimizer repairs a window of failures jointly, and a success-first held-out gate rolls back repair sequences that harm prior capability. Accepted edits accumulate in one shared harness, allowing its control structure to emerge from task feedback. Across BrowseComp-Plus and WebArena-Verified with three deployment models from 4B to 120B parameters, Growing Harness achieves the highest mean success in five of six benchmark-model settings and trails the best mean by 0.7 pp. in the sixth. Relative to a Tool-Calling agent, it reduces LLM calls by 76.0-91.8% and deployed-agent inference cost by 74.4-98.6%. On WebArena-Verified, its success remains 44.7-45.3% across model scales, whereas Tool-Calling falls to 6.7% with the 4B model. Ablations show that trace-local edits, joint repair, and gate-based rollback each improve final success. These results show that persistent program growth can move recurring control out of model context and into low-cost code, yielding reusable specialist agents that remain effective with smaller deployment models.

</details>


### [208] [Weakly Supervised Quantum Error Mitigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25555)
*Seyed Mohamad Ali Tousi,G. N. DeSouza*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes an unsupervised quantum error mitigation method that uses cheap, unreliable signals from circuit structure and hardware calibration instead of expensive ideal outputs from noiseless simulation. It uses 16 heuristic labeling functions and a probabilistic label model to estimate per-qubit error probabilities, which are then used to mitigate the measured distribution via the inverse readout channel.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Supervised quantum error mitigation requires ideal outputs from noiseless simulation, whose cost grows exponentially with system size - exactly when mitigation is needed most. The authors ask whether cheap, unreliable signals can replace these expensive ideal labels.

Method: The method assembles 16 heuristic labeling functions (stabilizer/parity constraints, relaxation/readout characteristics, local depth, gate counts, neighboring activity), reconciles their disagreements with a probabilistic label model, and reads the resulting per-qubit error probabilities as a readout channel whose inverse mitigates the measured distribution. No ideal output enters the training path.

Result: On 147,000 five-qubit circuits executed on two IBM devices (Algiers and Hanoi), the method removes 24.3% and 28.8% of the KL divergence to the ideal distribution, respectively. This outperforms the strongest analytical baseline (15.4% and 21.5%). Supervised neural models remain stronger where ideal labels exist.

Conclusion: The proposed unsupervised method successfully mitigates quantum errors without requiring ideal outputs, achieving better performance than analytical baselines on the tested devices. It provides a viable solution for the regime where ideal labels cannot be computed, though supervised methods remain superior when ideal labels are available.

Abstract: Supervised approaches to quantum error mitigation learn a map from noisy circuit outputs to ideal ones, and therefore require the ideal outputs. Producing those ideal outputs demands noiseless classical simulation, whose cost grows exponentially with system size, so supervision is unavailable in exactly the regime where mitigation matters most. We ask whether cheap, individually unreliable signals drawn from circuit structure and hardware calibration can take the place of ideal labels. We assemble sixteen heuristic labeling functions (stabilizer and parity constraints, relaxation and readout characteristics, local depth, gate counts, and neighboring activity), reconcile their disagreements with a probabilistic label model, and read the resulting per-qubit error probabilities as a readout channel whose inverse mitigates the measured distribution. No ideal output enters the training path. On $147{,}000$ five-qubit circuits executed on two IBM devices, the method removes $24.3\%$ (Algiers) and $28.8\%$ (Hanoi) of the Kullback-Leibler divergence to the ideal distribution, against $15.4\%$ and $21.5\%$ for the strongest published analytical baseline, a margin that holds on both devices and lies far outside its bootstrap interval.
