Supervised neural models trained on ideal distributions remain stronger where such labels exist, and we quantify that gap rather than setting it aside; the method's claim is to the regime where they do not, since the labels they require cannot be computed for the circuits mitigation is needed for. The codes will be released shortly.

</details>


### [209] [SWE-Serve: Benchmarking Agentic Engineering For Production Inference Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26777)
*Jennifer Williams,Dave Farris,Jeff Farris,Jiantao Jiao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SWE-Serve is a benchmark with 53 repository-grounded tasks for evaluating agents on production inference engineering tasks, derived from SGLang changes. It spans six inference engineering families, runs on CPU or H100 GPU, and uses hidden functional/regression tests including E2E serving tests. The best configuration achieves 75% mean pass@1 but reveals a substantial gap between local task completion and production correctness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks inadequately cover production inference engineering: repository-level software engineering benchmarks don't target inference, general terminal-agent benchmarks include few inference tasks, and dedicated inference benchmarks focus on isolated kernel generation rather than repository-scale production feature implementation.

Method: SWE-Serve provides 53 tasks derived from recent production changes to SGLang, spanning six inference engineering families. Tasks execute on CPU or single H100 GPU, evaluated with hidden functional and regression tests, including E2E serving tests and calibrated performance gates. Uses executable no-op and oracle controls, adversarial verifier review, and closed-book execution for evaluation integrity. Tests across 11 models and 31 model-effort configurations.

Result: Best configuration achieves 75% mean pass@1. On 19 tasks with E2E coverage, E2E tests reject roughly one-third of patches that pass every other test (45.9% under verifier vs 69.4% with E2E tests excluded from scoring). Pass rate increases for each model's best-performing configuration.

Conclusion: SWE-Serve exposes a substantial gap between completing tasks locally and achieving production correctness. By making the production correctness gap directly measurable, SWE-Serve enables the field to track whether future agents move beyond completing tasks locally to achieving production correctness.

Abstract: We introduce SWE-Serve, a benchmark for evaluating agents on production inference engineering tasks. Implementing an inference feature can require coordinating multiple changes across the serving stack, including model support, runtime execution, and public APIs. Existing benchmarks provide limited coverage of production inference engineering: repository-level software engineering benchmarks do not target inference, while general terminal-agent benchmarks include only a few inference tasks. Dedicated inference benchmarks, meanwhile, focus primarily on isolated kernel generation or performance optimization rather than repository-scale production feature implementation. SWE-Serve provides 53 repository-grounded tasks derived from recent production changes to SGLang, spanning six inference engineering families. Each task executes on either CPU or a single GPU (H100) and is evaluated with hidden functional and regression tests, including, where applicable, end-to-end (E2E) serving tests and calibrated performance gates. Executable no-op and oracle controls, adversarial verifier review, and closed-book execution support task validity and evaluation integrity. Across 11 models and 31 model-effort configurations, the best-performing configuration achieves 75% mean pass@1. SWE-Serve exposes a substantial gap between completing tasks locally and achieving production correctness. On 19 tasks with end-to-end coverage, model-serving E2E tests reject roughly one-third of patches that pass every other test (45.9% under the verifier versus 69.4% with E2E tests excluded from scoring), with pass rate increasing for each model's best-performing configuration. By making the production correctness gap directly measurable, SWE-Serve enables the field to track whether future agents move beyond completing tasks locally to achieving production correctness.

</details>


### [210] [Recovering Agentic Sovereignty: Mitigating the Consensus Paradox via Contrastive Epistemic Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25570)
*Dahlia Shehata,Ming Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces Contrastive Epistemic Decoding (CED), a zero-shot inference method that mitigates LLM sycophancy (conformity to adversarial consensus) by using dual forward-passes on a single architecture, achieving up to 33% reduction in cognitive loafing and 30.75% accuracy recovery across multiple benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models exhibit parametric vulnerability to adversarial swarm consensus, meaning they tend to conform to popular but potentially incorrect opinions—termed "sycophancy." This undermines model reliability and truthfulness.

Method: CED uses a dual forward-pass on a single architecture (instead of relying on a weaker secondary model like standard Contrastive Decoding). It introduces: (1) an asymmetric, zero-bounded probability clamp, and (2) a discrete top-k truncation mask. These mechanisms mathematically suppress toxic consensus tokens while avoiding grammatical collapse.

Result: Evaluated on 7,200 paired trajectories across GAIA, SWE-bench, and Multi-Challenge benchmarks using Gemma-2 (9B), Llama-3.1 (8B), and Mistral v0.3 (7B). CED reduces cognitive loafing by up to 33.00% absolute and achieves up to 30.75% accuracy recovery. Different models show distinct behaviors: Gemma-2 exhibits passive task-focus, while Llama-3.1 actively refutes simulated swarm consensus.

Conclusion: CED successfully decouples compliance from capability without fine-tuning, neutralizing architectural and positional biases. It provides a practical, inference-time intervention that preserves model capabilities while improving robustness to consensus bias.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) exhibit a parametric vulnerability to adversarial swarm consensus. To mitigate this sycophancy, we introduce Contrastive Epistemic Decoding (CED), a zero-shot inference intervention. Unlike standard Contrastive Decoding (CD) which relies on a weaker secondary model, CED utilizes a dual forward-pass on a single architecture to isolate conformity bias. By introducing a novel asymmetric, zero-bounded probability clamp and discrete top-k truncation mask, CED mathematically suppresses toxic consensus tokens without causing grammatical collapse. Evaluated across 7,200 paired trajectories on complex benchmarks (GAIA, SWE-bench, Multi-Challenge) using Gemma-2 (9B), Llama-3.1 (8B), and Mistral v0.3 (7B), CED successfully neutralizes architectural and positional biases. By reducing cognitive loafing by up to 33.00% absolute, CED drives significant performance gains, yielding up to a 30.75% accuracy recovery. Regaining sovereignty induces distinct architectural behaviors---passive task-focus in Gemma-2 and active refutation of the simulated swarm in Llama-3.1---showing CED decouples compliance from capability without fine-tuning.

</details>


### [211] [CliffCompaction: Cost-Efficient Compaction for Long-Horizon Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26779)
*Trang Nguyen,Eulrang Cho,Bingqing Chen,Tim Dettmers*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CliffCompaction is an autocompaction technique that reduces computational costs by up to 50% while maintaining or improving performance on complex agent tasks. It works by truncating/dropping content without rephrasing, preventing context drift, and enables efficient test-time scaling for long-context agent problems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agents working on complex problems require millions of tokens of context, but limited context windows necessitate compaction across sessions. Existing approaches may introduce context drift through rephrasing, and the cost-performance trade-off for test-time scaling needs improvement.

Method: CliffCompaction uses a scaffold-agnostic API-proxy implementation that only truncates or drops content without rephrasing. Each compaction pass operates only on original content, and prior compacted output is discarded—never compacting a compaction.

Result: Reduces cost by up to 50% under bounded context. Adds over 10 percentage points on Terminal-Bench for less than cost of two full-context runs. Under parallel test-time scaling, Kimi K2.6 matches Opus 4.7 and exceeds Opus 4.6 and GPT-5.3 Codex at lower cost. On KernelBench: achieves 2.23× speedup after 200 steps and 3.58× after 400 steps, surpassing specialized search algorithms and trained agents.

Conclusion: CliffCompaction enables efficient continual learning over sessions exceeding a million tokens through faithful compaction (truncation only, no rephrasing). It provides a general-purpose solution with significant cost savings while maintaining or improving performance, making test-time scaling more cost-effective.

Abstract: Agents often work on complex problems that require millions of tokens of context, which necessitates compacting across sessions due to limited context windows. We develop CliffCompaction, an autocompaction technique that reduces cost by up to 50% under a bounded context while maintaining or improving performance on Terminal-Bench and achieving new levels of efficiency for test-time scaling and state-of-the-art results on KernelBench. The per-rollout savings of CliffCompaction make the performance--cost trade-off of test-time scaling more efficient, adding over 10 percentage points on Terminal-Bench for less than the cost of two full-context runs. Under parallel test-time scaling, CliffCompaction lets Kimi K2.6 match Opus 4.7, and exceed Opus 4.6 and GPT-5.3 Codex at lower cost. The key to CliffCompaction's effectiveness is that it keeps compacted information faithful by only truncating or dropping content, never rephrasing or rewriting it. We never compact a compaction---each pass operates only on original content, and prior compacted output is discarded, preventing context drift from accumulating. These properties sustain continual learning over sessions exceeding a million tokens: on KernelBench, CliffCompaction reaches CUDA kernel speedups of $2.23\times$ after 200 steps and $3.58\times$ after 400 steps, surpassing specialized search algorithms and trained agents despite being a general-purpose compaction technique. We open-source a scaffold-agnostic API-proxy implementation of CliffCompaction usable with Claude Code, Codex and other harnesses.

</details>


### [212] [A Behavioral Trait Leaks into Preferences: Diagnosing Trait Interference in LLM User Simulators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25572)
*Chaehyun Kim,Sein Kim,Hongseok Kang,Chanyoung Park*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses a critical flaw in LLM-based user simulators for recommender evaluation: the intended independence between user preference attributes and behavioral activity traits collapses during simulation, leading to biased evaluation results. The authors propose PQA (Page-level Quality Anchoring), which uses personalized anchors to help users proactively exit low-quality pages, preserving the activity trait's role in modulating browsing depth within preference-conforming pages.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based user simulators aim to bridge the offline-online gap in recommender evaluation by emulating users with specific traits. However, the authors discovered that the two key traits (preference attributes determining what users engage with, and behavioral activity trait governing browsing duration) become interdependent during simulation, causing two major failures: trait interference (where amplified activity distorts preference boundaries) and evaluation invalidity (where satisfaction scores inflate with activity-driven page counts despite taste mismatches).

Method: The authors propose PQA (Page-level Quality Anchoring), a page-level quality anchoring method that uses a personalized anchor reflecting each user's intrinsic preference standard. This anchor guides the simulator by assessing whether a page meets the user's standard before further browsing, enabling proactive exits from low-quality pages while allowing the activity trait to modulate browsing depth within preference-conforming pages.

Result: Experiments demonstrate that PQA mitigates trait interference and improves the reliability of LLM-based simulator evaluation under activity shifts. The method successfully preserves the intended role of the activity trait while reducing bias in evaluation.

Conclusion: The paper reveals a fundamental flaw in LLM-based user simulator design and provides a practical solution through PQA. By using personalized anchors to separate preference satisfaction from browsing activity, the method enables more accurate recommender evaluation that is less susceptible to trait distribution biases.

Abstract: LLM-based user simulators aim to bridge the offline-online gap in recommender evaluation by emulating users through injected traits, where preference attributes determine what a user engages with and a behavioral activity trait governs how long they browse. However, we show this intended trait independence collapses during simulation, causing two failures: (i) Trait Interference, where amplified activity distorts preference boundaries and forces interactions with mismatched items to sustain browsing, and (ii) Evaluation Invalidity, where satisfaction scores inflate with activity-driven page counts despite taste mismatches, biasing evaluation toward trait distributions rather than recommender performance. To resolve this, we propose PQA, a page-level quality anchoring method that guides simulators using a personalized anchor reflecting each user's intrinsic preference standard. By assessing whether a page meets this standard before further browsing, PQA enables proactive exits from low-quality pages, letting the activity trait retain its intended role of modulating browsing depth within preference-conforming pages. Experiments show PQA mitigates trait interference and improves the reliability of LLM-based simulator evaluation under activity shifts. Our code is available at https://github.com/chaehyun1/PQA

</details>


### [213] [Direct Optimization of Generators for Search in Automated Theorem Proving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25575)
*Adam Ousherovitch,Ambuj Tewari*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper extends Compute-Aligned Training (CAT) to tree search settings in Automated Theorem Proving with LLMs. It introduces search-aware losses and a search-agnostic uniform-allocation (UA) loss that weights per-tactic cross-entropy gradients. Both approaches outperform cross-entropy on a Lean benchmark across six search strategies, with gains scaling with test-time compute.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cross entropy loss is suboptimal for LLMs deployed in tree search for theorem proving. Extending alignment to tree search is challenging because proof discovery depends on exploration and recovery through off-trace states that supervised demonstrations don't reveal.

Method: The authors (1) abstract policy-guided search to derive tractable trace-supported losses, (2) introduce a search-agnostic UA loss that accounts for budget without specifying search strategy, (3) both methods induce scalar weights on per-tactic cross-entropy gradients.

Result: On a Lean benchmark, both approaches achieve higher observed proof-success rates than cross-entropy across six search strategies. A single shared UA adapter shows strong results. Budget sweeps show larger gains at 16 than at 256 expansions.

Conclusion: The proposed search-aware losses and UA loss outperform cross-entropy baseline, with CAT scaling with test-time compute. The work addresses the misalignment between training objective (cross-entropy) and deployment scenario (tree search) in ATP.

Abstract: Fine-tuned Large Language Models (LLMs) significantly advance Automated Theorem Proving (ATP), but are often deployed as guiding policies within tree search rather than for single-attempt generation. Recent work shows cross entropy is suboptimal for an LLM used in flat search strategies such as aggregation or filtering and that work has developed new loss functions to correct this misalignment. Extending this alignment to tree search is more challenging: proof discovery depends on exploration and recovery through off-trace states that supervised demonstrations do not reveal. We extend Compute-Aligned Training (CAT) to this setting through an abstraction of policy-guided search, deriving tractable, trace-supported losses. Alongside these search-aware losses, we introduce a search-agnostic uniform-allocation (UA) loss that accounts for the budget without specifying the specific search. Both induce scalar weights on per-tactic cross-entropy gradients. We characterize how off-trace behavior affects the search-aware weights, including conditions for vanishing approximation error at large budgets. On a Lean benchmark, both approaches achieve higher observed proof-success rates than cross-entropy across six search strategies, with strong results from a single shared UA adapter. Budget sweeps show larger gains over cross-entropy at 16 than at 256 expansions, implying CAT scales with test time compute.

</details>


### [214] [Gaze responses to false-positive computer-aided detection prompts during colonoscopy: a paired-video and real-time eye-tracking study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25581)
*Te Luo,Yan Zhu,Peiyao Fu,Ruijie Yang,Xian Yang,Quanlin Li,Pinghong Zhou,Shuo Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: False-positive CADe prompts in colonoscopy frequently attract endoscopist gaze (48.6-65.2% of cases) with attention persisting 5-17x longer than prompt visibility, potentially diverting attention from actual lesions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether false-positive computer-aided detection (CADe) prompts divert endoscopists' attention during colonoscopy, as the attentional impact of individual prompts remains unclear.

Method: Two complementary studies: (1) Retrospective paired-video experiment with 5 endoscopists (3 senior, 2 novice) viewing 60 colonoscopy videos with/without CADe; (2) Prospective study recording gaze during 42 real-time CADe-assisted colonoscopies by 9 senior endoscopists. False-positive prompts (outside lesion windows) were classified as artifact events and gaze attraction/attention occupation was quantified using event-locked eye tracking.

Result: False-positive prompts attracted gaze in 48.6% (68/140) retrospective and 65.2% (533/817) prospective events. Median attention occupation lasted 1000-1100ms vs prompt durations of 33-267ms, representing 5.15-17.55-fold time amplification. Visible artifact prompts drew gaze closer to prompted regions than unassisted references. Lesion gaze recognition remained high (98.0% vs 99.0%), and first gaze entry occurred 147.8ms earlier with CADe.

Conclusion: False-positive CADe prompts frequently capture gaze in both controlled and real-time clinical settings, with attention persisting beyond prompt visibility. These findings support considering prompt-related attentional burden in CADe evaluation and design.

Abstract: False-positive computer-aided detection (CADe) prompts may divert endoscopists' attention during colonoscopy, yet the attentional impact of individual prompts remains unclear. We used event-locked eye tracking to quantify gaze attraction and attention occupation in complementary retrospective and prospective studies. In a retrospective paired-video experiment, 3 senior and 2 novice endoscopists viewed 60 colonoscopy videos with and without CADe. The prospective study recorded gaze during 42 real-time CADe-assisted colonoscopies performed by 9 senior endoscopists. Screened CADe prompts outside expert-annotated lesion windows were classified as false-positive artifact events. False-positive prompts attracted gaze in 48.6% (68/140) of retrospective observations and 65.2% (533/817) of prospective events. Among attraction events with complete recovery, median attention occupation lasted 1000 ms in the retrospective study and 1100 ms in the prospective study. Corresponding median prompt durations were 33 ms and 267 ms, with median time amplifications of 17.55-fold and 5.15-fold, respectively. In paired retrospective comparisons, visible artifact prompts drew gaze closer to the prompted region than did the same-coordinate unassisted reference. Secondary retrospective analyses showed high lesion gaze recognition without and with CADe (98.0% versus 99.0%). First gaze entry into lesion regions occurred 147.8 ms earlier with CADe. Across controlled and real-time clinical settings, false-positive CADe prompts frequently captured gaze, with attention persisting beyond prompt visibility. These findings support considering prompt-related attentional burden in CADe evaluation and design.

</details>


### [215] [Transformer Heads Looking for Order](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25588)
*Jasper van Doornmalen,Alexander Kozachinskiy,Corinna Mathwieser,Tomasz Steifer,Felipe Urrutia,José Verschae,Przemysław Andrzej Wałȩga*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proves that checking whether a sequence of bits is ordered cannot be solved by a 1-head 1-layer transformer but can be solved by a 2-head 1-layer transformer, under the model assumption that transformers include an output MLP.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Unlike similar previous results, this work accounts for the output MLP component in transformer architectures, which was not considered in prior analyses.

Method: Theoretical analysis comparing the computational capabilities of 1-head versus 2-head transformers under the transformer model with output MLP.

Result: A 1-head 1-layer transformer is insufficient to check if a sequence of bits is ordered, while a 2-head 1-layer transformer can successfully perform this task.

Conclusion: At least 2 heads are required in a 1-layer transformer to solve the bit ordering problem when the model includes an output MLP.

Abstract: In this note, we show that the problem of checking, whether a sequence of bits is ordered, is not doable by 1-head 1-layer transformers but is doable by a 2-head 1-layer transformer. Unlike similar previous results, our results assume the model where transformers have an output MLP.

</details>


### [216] [Evaluating Coding Agents on Kernel Exploit Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25591)
*Junyoung Jang,Gwanhyun Lee,Hwiwon Lee,Kyuheon Kim,Jongseong Kim,Jinho Jung,Lingming Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: KEX-bench is a benchmark for evaluating coding agents on exploit primitive generation against real OS kernels, containing 45 task instances across 40 Linux and Windows CVEs covering various vulnerability types. Without reference PoC, agents solve 5.0% Windows and 56.0% Linux tasks; with reference PoC, they solve 68.9% of all tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While coding agents now find real vulnerabilities in production software, existing bug discovery benchmarks do not measure whether agents can construct exploit primitives. This research addresses this gap by creating a benchmark to evaluate agents' ability to build exploit primitives from kernel vulnerabilities.

Method: KEX-bench contains 45 task instances across 40 Linux and Windows CVEs, covering five exploit primitive types: kernel address leak, instruction-pointer control, heap read, heap write, and arbitrary address write. Each task runs in an isolated virtual machine with controlled tools and uses a deterministic verifier to check primitive-specific success. The evaluation tests state-of-the-art coding agents paired with frontier and open-weight models under fixed tool-call budgets.

Result: Without a reference proof of concept (PoC), the strongest configuration solves 1 of 20 Windows tasks (5.0%) and 14 of 25 Linux tasks (56.0%). With a reference PoC, the strongest configuration solves 31 of 45 tasks (68.9%). The results reveal a significant gap where agents can reach kernel crashes but fail to shape kernel state into exploit primitives.

Conclusion: There exists a substantial gap in coding agents' ability to construct exploit primitives, even when they can trigger kernel crashes. The benchmark highlights the need for further research in AI-assisted exploitation. KEX-bench is released for reproducible research on AI-assisted exploitation.

Abstract: Coding agents now find real vulnerabilities in production software. However, bug discovery results do not measure whether agents can construct exploit primitives. We introduce KEX-bench, a benchmark for evaluating coding agents on exploit primitive generation against real operating-system kernels. KEX-bench contains 45 task instances across 40 Linux and Windows CVEs, covering kernel address leak, instruction-pointer control, heap read, heap write, and arbitrary address write. Each task runs in an isolated virtual machine, exposes controlled tools, and uses a deterministic verifier to check primitive-specific success. We evaluate state-of-the-art coding agents paired with frontier and open-weight models under fixed tool-call budgets. Without a reference proof of concept (PoC), the strongest configuration solves 1 of 20 Windows tasks (5.0%) and 14 of 25 Linux tasks (56.0%). With a reference PoC, the strongest configuration solves 31 of 45 tasks (68.9%). This highlights the gap where agents reach kernel crashes but fail to shape kernel state into exploit primitives. We release KEX-bench for reproducible research on AI-assisted exploitation at https://kex-bench.github.io.

</details>


### [217] [ArticleMiner: Ontology-Guided Knowledge Graph Construction from Scientific Publications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25607)
*Md Abrar Jahin,Craig A. Knoblock,Jay Pujara*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ArticleMiner is a framework that extracts scientific facts from papers and supplementary files by combining multiple parsers and language models, with domain-specific task modules that define canonical names, surface forms, derivation rules, and RDF bindings. It was evaluated on 163 papers across four domains (drug-discovery chemistry, materials science, machine learning, mineral geochemistry) and outperformed LLM few-shot baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current semantic table interpretation methods assume clean tables are already available, while publication-level extraction systems are typically single-domain. The challenge is that table structure alone doesn't capture the scientific meaning—headers, captions, units, and field conventions are essential. There's a need for a flexible, multi-domain approach that can extract scientific facts from both papers and supplementary files.

Method: The authors developed a "middle path" approach using ArticleMiner framework with four key components: (1) A shared process that reads papers and supplementary files, (2) Multiple parsers and a language model that gather evidence, (3) Evidence reconciliation mechanism, (4) Bounded human-authored task modules for each domain that define canonical names, surface form mappings, derivation rules, validity constraints, identity keys, and RDF bindings. Four task modules were built for drug-discovery chemistry, materials science, machine learning, and mineral geochemistry.

Result: Evaluation on 163 papers (including a new geochemistry benchmark with expert-curated ground truth) showed ArticleMiner outperformed same-LLM few-shot baselines on all four tasks in point estimates. Uncertainty existed on the two smaller benchmarks. The geochemistry comparison included supplementary files, demonstrating the improvement wasn't solely from domain guidance.

Conclusion: ArticleMiner provides a reusable framework for scientific fact extraction across multiple domains by combining parsers, language models, and domain-specific task modules. The modular design allows flexibility while maintaining domain accuracy. The approach shows promise for systematic literature mining, though further validation on larger benchmarks would strengthen the findings.

Abstract: Scientific papers keep much of their quantitative content in tables and supplementary files, where a number means something only through its header, caption, unit, analytical method, and the conventions of its field. Recovering the rows and columns of a table is therefore not the same as recovering the scientific fact it reports. Most semantic table-interpretation methods assume that a clean table is already available and subsequently map its cells or columns to ontology terms, whereas most publication-level extraction systems are designed for a single domain. We study a middle path: a shared process that reads a paper and its supplementary files, gathers evidence from several parsers and a language model, and reconciles that evidence, while a bounded human-authored task module for each task supplies the domain meaning. The module lists the canonical names the graph may use, the surface forms that map to them, a small set of derivation rules and validity constraints, an identity key, and the bindings used to write RDF. It defines what a task is allowed to emit; it does not try to list every convention of a field. We build four such modules (for drug-discovery chemistry, materials science, machine learning, and mineral geochemistry) in the ArticleMiner framework, and evaluate them on 163 papers, including a new geochemistry benchmark with expert-curated ground truth. In comparisons against a same-LLM few-shot baseline, the point estimates favor ArticleMiner on all four tasks, with uncertainty on the two smaller benchmarks. The geochemistry comparison also includes access to supplementary files, so its improvement cannot be attributed to domain guidance alone.

</details>


### [218] [Reasoning-Preserving Fine-Tuning of Post-RL LLMs with Null-Basis LoRA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25618)
*Wenzhi Fang,Nicholas Tzou,Lazar Valkov,Srinivas Chappidi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: NB-LoRA is a parameter-efficient fine-tuning method that preserves reasoning capabilities in post-RL LLMs by reparameterizing LoRA updates through an approximate null basis constructed from reasoning activations, maintaining near pre-fine-tuning reasoning accuracy while matching standard LoRA adaptation performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Adapting post-RL models to new knowledge domains via supervised fine-tuning (SFT) severely overwrites acquired reasoning capabilities. Existing solutions like experience replay, specialized initialization, or gradient projection provide limited preservation or incur substantial training overhead.

Method: 1) Analyze reasoning activations and discover they concentrate in low-dimensional subspaces with substantial null-space capacity; 2) Estimate approximate null spaces from a modest number of examples; 3) Formulate reasoning retention as a layer-wise hidden-state preservation constraint; 4) Construct a fixed approximate null basis from reasoning activations; 5) Reparameterize LoRA updates through this basis to enforce preservation constraint throughout fine-tuning.

Result: NB-LoRA matches standard LoRA in adaptation performance, maintains reasoning accuracy near pre-fine-tuning levels, and generalizes this preservation to held-out reasoning benchmarks across multiple RL-trained LLMs and diverse downstream tasks.

Conclusion: Reasoning capabilities can be preserved during fine-tuning by enforcing updates through a null basis derived from reasoning activations, providing an effective parameter-efficient solution for adapting post-RL LLMs without losing their acquired reasoning abilities.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL)-based post-training has become an effective approach for eliciting reasoning capabilities in large language models (LLMs). However, adapting post-RL models to new knowledge domains or behaviors through subsequent supervised fine-tuning (SFT) can severely overwrite these capabilities. Existing approaches mitigate such forgetting through experience replay, specialized initialization, or constrained optimization using gradient projection, but either provide limited preservation or incur substantial training overhead. Our analysis shows that reasoning activations concentrate in low-dimensional subspaces, leaving substantial null-space capacity for adaptation, and that the corresponding approximate null spaces can be reliably estimated from a modest number of examples. Motivated by these observations, we propose Null-Basis Low-Rank Adaptation (NB-LoRA), a parameter-efficient method for adapting post-RL LLMs while preserving their acquired reasoning ability. We formulate reasoning retention as a layer-wise hidden-state preservation constraint and construct a fixed approximate null basis from reasoning activations. LoRA updates are then reparameterized through this basis, enforcing the preservation constraint throughout fine-tuning. Extensive experiments across multiple RL-trained LLMs and diverse downstream tasks show that NB-LoRA matches standard LoRA in adaptation performance, maintains reasoning accuracy near pre-fine-tuning levels, and generalizes this preservation to held-out reasoning benchmarks.

</details>


### [219] [ChatT2: An Adaptive Framework for Developing a Large Language Model-Based Agent for Natural Product Domain Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25620)
*Yihan Wang,Qiandi Gao,Yihui Zhuang,Liangjun Ge,Heqian Zhang,Jiaquan Huang,Zhiwei Qin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ChatT2 is an LLM-based multiagent system designed to help novices and experts explore bacterial type II polyketides, a therapeutically important class of natural products, by integrating retrieval-augmented generation with bioinformatics and cheminformatics tools.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scientific investigations into microbial natural products are challenging for novices due to the complexity of microbial systems, biochemical diversity, technical skill requirements, and the demands of bioinformatics and data analysis. Existing general LLMs struggle with limited, specialized corpora and complex biological information.

Method: ChatT2 was developed within an autonomous multiagent framework consisting of three components: (1) a mentor that uses chain-of-thought prompting to refine user intent, (2) an executor that synthesizes multimodal information via retrieval-augmented generation and integrates bioinformatics and cheminformatics tools, and (3) an evaluator that assesses output richness and accuracy.

Result: The system addresses the challenges faced by general LLMs in understanding specialized corpora and complex biological information, providing both experts and novices with a valuable tool for exploring natural products.

Conclusion: ChatT2 represents a promising solution for democratizing access to microbial natural product research, particularly for bacterial type II polyketides, with a webserver available at https://chatt2.site/#/chat.

Abstract: Scientific investigations into microbial natural products (NPs) present significant challenges for novices, largely due to the complexity of microbial systems, biochemical diversity, technical skill requirements, and the demands of bioinformatics and data analysis processes. To address these issues, we introduce ChatT2, a large language model (LLM)-based agent that is specifically tailored to the unique characteristics of bacterial type II polyketides. These polyketides form a structurally distinct and therapeutically important NP family. ChatT2 was developed within an autonomous multiagent framework composed of a mentor, an executor, and an evaluator, each with defined responsibilities. The mentor acts as an intermediary between ChatT2 and the user, utilizing chain-of-thought prompting to refine the intent of the user. Under the guidance of the mentor, the executor synthesizes multimodal information via retrieval-augmented generation techniques and seamlessly integrates bioinformatics and cheminformatics tools. The evaluator ultimately assesses the output of the executor to ensure the richness and accuracy of the retrieved information. Our research highlights how ChatT2, designed with this multiagent framework, addresses the challenges faced by general LLMs in terms of understanding limited, specialized corpora and complex biological information and provides both experts and novices with a valuable tool for exploring various NPs of interest. The ChatT2 webserver can be accessed at https://chatt2.site/#/chat.

</details>


### [220] [Ladders of Thought: A Self-Evolving Curriculum of Progressively Simplified Reasoning Traces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25643)
*Minghui Liu,Thomas Magelinski,Dehao Yuan,Qi Yu,Furong Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LoT (Ladders-of-Thought) is a framework that improves reasoning in small-to-mid-scale LLMs by combining progressive question rewrites with a self-evolving curriculum. It automatically generates easier variants of reasoning problems, organizes them by difficulty, and uses a bandit scheduler for adaptive training allocation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Small and mid-scale LLMs struggle with reasoning tasks even when using knowledge distillation (KD). The authors aim to address this brittleness by creating a framework that helps smaller models develop stronger reasoning capabilities through progressive learning.

Method: LoT employs three key components: (1) automatic generation of semantically faithful but easier variants of reasoning problems, (2) difficulty bucketing using step-based measures, and (3) a self-evolving bandit scheduler for adaptive training allocation across difficulty levels.

Result: LoT consistently outperforms KD across 1-8B models. On arithmetic tasks: +32pp on AddSub, +25pp on SVAMP. On in-domain test splits: +2-8pp improvements. On multi-hop reasoning: +16pp on QASC, +25pp on StrategyQA. It also converges faster than staged curricula.

Conclusion: Progressive question rewrites combined with adaptive curricula provide a simple yet effective approach for strengthening reasoning capabilities in smaller LLMs, with significant improvements across math and multi-hop reasoning domains.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) excel at reasoning when scaled to hundreds of billions of parameters, but small- and mid-scale models remain brittle reasoners even with knowledge distillation (KD). We present Ladders-of-Thought (LoT), a framework that improves reasoning by combining progressive question rewrites with a self-evolving curriculum. LoT automatically generates semantically faithful but easier variants of reasoning problems, organizes them into difficulty buckets using step-based measures, and employs a self-evolving bandit scheduler to allocate training adaptively. Evaluated on two reasoning domains, math and multi-hop reasoning, across 1-8B models from different families, LoT consistently improves over KD. It delivers large gains on arithmetic tasks (e.g., +32 percentage points on AddSub, +25pp on SVAMP), +2-8pp improvements on in-domain test splits, and strong though dataset-dependent benefits on multi-hop reasoning (e.g., +16pp on QASC, +25pp on StrategyQA). LoT also converges faster than staged curricula, highlighting the value of adaptive progression. These results show that progressive rewrites coupled with adaptive curricula provide a simple yet effective recipe for strengthening reasoning in smaller LLMs.

</details>


### [221] [Testing-Driven Reliability Audit of Trajectory-Based Early Outcome Prediction for LLM Agents: Target-Specific Calibration Transfer Persists Within a Single Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25647)
*YanZe Cao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates calibration transfer errors in early-outcome prediction systems for agent evaluation. Using SWE-bench trajectories and a dual-head prediction pipeline, researchers tested whether predictors calibrated on one agent generalize to other agents. While most agent/predictor combinations showed acceptable transfer, two specific combinations (gpt-5-mini/SUCCESS and claude-opus-4.6/FAILURE) exhibited persistent calibration-transfer errors, suggesting target-specific rather than broad systematic failures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the problem of expensive agent evaluation by enabling early termination of runs once outcomes become predictable. However, calibration is at risk when predictors are applied to agents they weren't trained on. The study investigates whether such transfer failures are widespread across agent systems or concentrated in specific combinations.

Method: The researchers conducted: (1) leave-one-agent-out calibration audit, (2) shared-predictor leave-two-agents-out control, (3) oracle prior correction, and (4) a robustness battery including training cohorts, task resampling, task halves, jackknife, and threshold variations. Fixed-scaffold TerminalBench analysis served as a pre-registered boundary test. They used public SWE-bench Verified trajectories and a frozen dual-head early-outcome prediction pipeline.

Result: Broad same-predictor pairwise heterogeneity was not supported: median corrected-gap differences were 0.0180 (SUCCESS head, 45 pairs) and 0.0385 (FAILURE head, 35 pairs), failing to meet the pre-registered heterogeneity criterion. However, two specific combinations showed persistent calibration-transfer errors: gpt-5-mini/SUCCESS (median corrected gap 0.1377) and claude-opus-4.6/FAILURE (median corrected gap 0.1107), with no sign reversal under frozen controls. TerminalBench failed to establish cross-benchmark replication.

Conclusion: A strong target-specific calibration-transfer error can exist within one frozen environment, but evidence does not establish that such errors are intrinsic to the model or general across benchmarks. The failures appear to be specific to particular agent/head combinations rather than systemic across the board.

Abstract: Predicting early outcomes based on trajectory can decrease the expenses associated with agent evaluation by terminating a run once the outcome becomes sufficiently predictable, assuming that the predictor's confidence is properly calibrated. Calibration is at risk when a predictor is applied to an agent on which it was never trained, but it is not known whether such transfer failures are broad across agent systems or concentrated in specific target agent/head combinations. Using public SWE-bench Verified trajectories and a frozen dual-head early-outcome prediction pipeline, we ran a leave-one-agent-out calibration audit, a shared-predictor leave-two-agents-out control, oracle prior correction, and a robustness battery over training cohorts, task resampling, task halves, jackknife, and thresholds. Fixed-scaffold TerminalBench analysis served as a pre-registered boundary test. Broad same-predictor pairwise heterogeneity was not supported; the median pairwise corrected-gap differences were 0.0180 (SUCCESS head, 45 pairs) and 0.0385 (FAILURE head, 35 pairs), and the pre-registered heterogeneity criterion was not met on either head. Two specific combinations, gpt-5-mini/SUCCESS and claude-opus-4.6/FAILURE, showed persistent calibration-transfer errors (median corrected gaps 0.1377 and 0.1107) without a sign reversal under any frozen control. TerminalBench did not establish cross-benchmark replication: the success target produced zero decisions (INDETERMINATE), and the failure target did not satisfy the pre-registered persistence criterion. Therefore, a strong target-specific calibration-transfer error can exist within one frozen environment, but the evidence does not establish that the error is intrinsic to the model or general across benchmarks.

</details>


### [222] [Seeing Is Not Perceiving: When Synthetic Consumers Can and Cannot Pretest Visual Marketing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25677)
*Yi-Lin Tsai,Yung-Hsiu,Lai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether generative AI agents can serve as synthetic consumers to pretest visual marketing assets (logos, packaging, ads). The study stress-tests this assumption across six canonical visual marketing experiments, manipulating model generation (GPT-4o-mini vs GPT-5.4-mini) and input format (plain text vs JSON). Results show that while all configurations passed manipulation checks, none reproduced more than two human effects, with most being nonsignificant. In-context learning improves but does not fully solve the problem—heterogeneity remains severely understated. The paper proposes a governance protocol (Calibrate, Intervene, Deploy) for responsible use of synthetic consumers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The marketing field increasingly uses generative AI as synthetic consumers to pretest visual assets at lower costs. However, the fundamental assumption that AI models can perceive consumer meaning from visual cues remains largely untested. This research seeks to validate or challenge this assumption through empirical stress-testing.

Method: The study conducted six canonical visual marketing experiments, manipulating two key levers: (1) model generation (GPT-4o-mini vs. GPT-5.4-mini) and (2) input format (plain text vs. JSON). They tested whether these configurations could reproduce established human effects from the literature, using manipulation checks to ensure valid experimental conditions.

Result: All configurations passed manipulation checks, but none reproduced more than two of six human effects—most effects were nonsignificant, with one significant reversal of the human pattern. In-context learning (providing conceptual or empirical evidence) steered responses toward human effects but had limits: even successful configurations reproduced less than half of the natural spread of human responses, severely understating consumer heterogeneity.

Conclusion: AI models cannot yet reliably substitute for human consumers in visual marketing pretesting. While in-context learning helps, synthetic consumers fail to capture consumer heterogeneity. The paper proposes a governance protocol (Calibrate, Intervene, Deploy) to guide when synthetic consumers can responsibly screen creatives versus when human panels remain necessary.

Abstract: Marketers now deploy generative AI agents as synthetic consumers to pretest visual assets such as logos, packaging, and advertising at a fraction of human-panel cost. However, this procedure assumes that a model seeing a visual cue can also perceive its consumer meaning, which is largely untested. We stress-test the assumption using six canonical visual marketing experiments, varying the two levers managers control: model generation (GPT-4o-mini vs. GPT-5.4-mini) and input format (plain text vs. JSON). Every resulting configuration passed the manipulation checks; however, none of the configurations reproduced more than two of the six human effects, and the remainder were nonsignificant. The one exception was a significant reversal of the human pattern. Providing conceptual or empirical evidence through in-context learning steers average responses toward the human effect. Yet steering has a limit: even when it succeeds, a configuration reproduces less than half of the natural spread of human responses and so understates consumer heterogeneity. We integrate these results into an AI governance protocol (Calibrate, Intervene, Deploy) that delineates when synthetic consumers can responsibly screen creatives and when human panels remain necessary.

</details>


### [223] [Toolcompass: Guiding Tool Trialing, Not Suppressing It](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25678)
*Junlin Fang,Chong Zhang,Do Nguyen-Thanh,Xiaogang Xu,Zhen Fang,Sean Du*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ToolCompass is a post-training framework that helps LLM agents generalize from trained tools to unseen tools by organizing tool-call representations according to shared functions. It uses von Mises-Fisher distributions to model function classes, reducing intra-function variation while increasing inter-function separation, enabling better exploration of unfamiliar tools without excessive trials.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents must generalize from tools seen during training to unseen tools at deployment. The key challenge is tool trialing: excessive trials waste interaction budget, while selective trials enable exploration of unfamiliar tools. Existing outcome-based post-training leaves wasteful trials unguided, while turn-level supervision may suppress necessary exploration.

Method: ToolCompass models each function class as a von Mises-Fisher distribution and jointly reduces intra-function variation across domains and increases inter-function separation. This structure transfers experience from seen tools to functionally similar unseen tools, directing exploration away from unrelated alternatives. The framework requires no ground-truth call traces or unseen-tool access and incurs no inference overhead.

Result: Experiments on AppWorld and FTRL show consistent gains across GRPO, RFT, and DMPO. ToolCompass improves AppWorld OOD task success by up to 10.71 percentage points over vanilla post-training and performs best among competitive baselines on both benchmarks.

Conclusion: ToolCompass provides an effective solution for tool generalization in LLM agents by organizing tool-call representations by function using von Mises-Fisher distributions. The approach achieves significant improvements (up to 10.71pp) on out-of-distribution tasks while requiring no additional inference overhead or ground-truth data.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents must generalize from tools seen during training to unseen tools at deployment. A key challenge is tool trialing, i.e., excessive trials waste the interaction budget, whereas selective trials enable exploration of unfamiliar tools. Existing outcome-based post-training leaves wasteful trials unguided, while turn-level supervision may suppress necessary exploration. We introduce ToolCompass, a post-training framework that guides tool trialing by organizing tool-call representations according to shared functions. Specifically, ToolCompass models each function class as a von Mises--Fisher distribution and jointly reduces intra-function variation across domains and increases inter-function separation. This structure transfers experience from seen tools to functionally similar unseen tools, directing exploration away from unrelated alternatives. ToolCompass requires no ground-truth call traces or unseen-tool access and incurs no inference overhead. Experiments on AppWorld and FTRL show consistent gains across GRPO, RFT, and DMPO. improves AppWorld OOD task success by up to 10.71 percentage points over vanilla post-training and performs best among competitive baselines on both benchmarks.

</details>


### [224] [How Strongly Should Task State Influence an LLM Agent?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25686)
*Chenyu Zhang,Wonbin Kweon,Jiawei Han*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates how task state information reaches an LLM agent (displayed, told, or enforced) and finds that each method's effectiveness depends on model capabilities, task characteristics, and whether failures are state-deprecable. An unverified ledger the agent writes itself beats an accurate checklist shown to it, directives help proportionally to model obedience, and enforcement works only when failures are state-decidable and frequent.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon assigned work requires LLM agents to track task state (completed, blocked, cancelled, or open steps). Existing systems either keep state as text in prompts or enforce it through modules, but no one has isolated how much reliability comes from each approach—showing, telling, or enforcing state.

Method: The authors fixed task rules, model, and paired episodes, then varied how strongly task state reaches the agent across four modalities: (1) raw transcript, (2) exact checklist, (3) per-turn directives from a state machine compiled from briefs and advanced only by execution receipts, or (4) an enforcement gate that refuses state-violating actions. Every episode was scored by exact payload matching against dynamic ground truth. Experiments covered three models, two reasoning regimes, and two domains (τ²-bench airline policy and PM-Bench).

Result: Four key findings hold without per-turn reasoning: (1) displaying accurate state is unreliable, (2) an unverified ledger the agent writes itself beats an accurate checklist it is shown, (3) directives help in proportion to the model's obedience, and (4) enforcement needs no obedience but is bounded by the correctness of its state and by the matcher mapping requests to steps. On τ²-bench, a gate raised a 235B agent's pass¹ from 0.39 to 0.54 but changed nothing for a 35B agent. On PM-Bench (where acting turns on recognizing a cue rather than state), showing the record is best—matching or beating both gates and reversing the ledger-over-checklist finding—and enforcing the matcher's judgement drops a 35B agent below its raw transcript. Per-turn reasoning at 235B compresses separations without repairing text rungs.

Conclusion: Enforcement pays when failures are state-decidable and frequent, but hurts when the gate's judgement is wrong. The effectiveness of each approach depends on the domain: when acting depends on recognizing cues rather than state, displaying the record works best; when failures are state-decidable, enforcement helps. The choice between showing, telling, or enforcing task state should be guided by whether failures are state-decidable and how frequently they occur.

Abstract: Long-horizon assigned work requires an LLM agent to track the state of a task: which steps are done, blocked, cancelled, or open to repetition. Agent systems either keep this state as text in the prompt and rely on the model to read that text, or move the state into a module that enforces it, and each system is evaluated as a whole, so no one knows how much reliability comes from the state being shown, told, or enforced. We fix the task rules, the model, and paired episodes and vary how strongly task state reaches the agent: a raw transcript, an exact checklist, per-turn directives from a state machine compiled from the brief and advanced only by execution receipts, or an enforcement gate on that machine that refuses state-violating actions; every episode is scored by exact payload matching against dynamic ground truth. Across three models, two reasoning regimes, and two domains, four findings hold without per-turn reasoning: displaying accurate state is unreliable, an unverified ledger the agent writes itself beats an accurate checklist it is shown, directives help in proportion to the model's obedience, and enforcement needs no obedience but is bounded by the correctness of its state and by the matcher that maps requests to steps; per-turn reasoning at a 235B agent compresses these separations without repairing the text rungs. The same gate, compiled from $τ^2$-bench's airline policy, raises a 235B agent's pass$^1$ from 0.39 to 0.54 and changes nothing for a 35B agent that rarely violates the policy; on PM-Bench, where acting turns on recognizing a cue rather than on state, showing the record is the best rung--matching or beating both gates and reversing the ledger-over-checklist finding--and enforcing the matcher's judgement drops a 35B agent below its raw transcript. Enforcement pays when failures are state-decidable and frequent, and hurts when the gate's judgement is wrong.

</details>


### [225] [TCMaster: Confidence-Aware Querying and Workload-Guided Physical Design for Multi-Source Traditional Chinese Medicine Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25712)
*Zheng Chen,Yuzhu Li,Haoxuan Li,Zhongde Zhang,Lianshun Jin,Peiwu Qin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TCMaster is a property-graph query system for Traditional Chinese Medicine knowledge graphs that enables confidence-aware traversal and workload-guided physical optimization. It integrates multiple data sources into a KG with ~221K entities and 723K edges, featuring confidence annotations, path ranking policies, and physical design optimizations (direction selection, bitmaps, materialized shortcuts) that significantly improve query performance and QA accuracy on Neo4j.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-source knowledge graphs need query mechanisms that expose reliability (confidence) and exploit domain structure. Existing KG query systems lack mechanisms to handle confidence metadata and domain-specific optimizations, particularly for complex domains like Traditional Chinese Medicine where data quality varies and domain structure can be leveraged for performance.

Method: TCMaster integrates pharmacopoeias, prescriptions, molecular databases, and LLM-extracted micro-semantics into a unified KG. It implements: (1) Edge annotation with provenance-level confidence; (2) Cypher query rewriting with confidence predicates; (3) Multi-hop path ranking under PRODUCT, MIN, or weighted-average policies; (4) Physical design optimizations including ontology skew exploitation through direction selection, herb-attribute bitmaps, and materialized shortcut edges.

Result: On Neo4j: direction selection improves attribute lookup by 1.47x; shortcuts accelerate high-fanout target counting by 4.42x; confidence filtering removes 39.3% of low-quality heterogeneous paths; KG retrieval improves TCMbench QA accuracy by 20.0 percentage points.

Conclusion: TCMaster demonstrates that confidence-aware querying and domain-specific physical design optimizations can significantly improve both query performance and downstream QA accuracy for domain-specific knowledge graphs. The combination of confidence filtering and workload-guided optimizations provides substantial gains over baseline Neo4j deployments.

Abstract: Multi-source knowledge graphs (KGs) need query mechanisms that expose reliability and exploit domain structure. This paper presents TCMaster, a property-graph query substrate for confidence-aware traversal and workload-guided physical design over Traditional Chinese Medicine KGs. TCMaster integrates pharmacopoeias, prescriptions, molecular databases, and LLM-extracted micro-semantics into a KG with approximately 221K entities and 723K base edges. It annotates edges with provenance-level confidence, rewrites Cypher queries with confidence predicates, ranks multi-hop paths under PRODUCT, MIN, or weighted-average policies, and uses ontology skew through direction selection, herb-attribute bitmaps, and materialized shortcut edges. On Neo4j, direction selection improves attribute lookup by a factor of 1.47, shortcuts accelerate high-fanout target counting by a factor of 4.42, confidence filtering removes 39.3 percent of low-quality heterogeneous paths, and KG retrieval improves TCMbench QA accuracy by 20.0 percentage points.

</details>


### [226] [LingLan: An Advancing Traditional Chinese Medicine Diagnosis LLM with Multimodal Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25715)
*Zheng Chen,Zhicheng Du,Haoxuan Li,Yingshan Liang,Peiwu Qin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses the challenge of integrating AI into Traditional Chinese Medicine (TCM) by proposing a Unification Framework for Multimodal Data (UFMD) that standardizes tongue and pulse images into structured clinical descriptions, and a TCM-specific large language model (LingLan-14B) that emulates the I-AOI-P diagnostic workflow. The approach achieves 103.5% relative improvement in diagnostic accuracy and up to 82% F1-score.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The integration of AI into TCM has been slow due to TCM's reliance on holistic, subjective diagnostic methods (Inspection, Auscultation and Olfaction, Inquiry, and Palpation - I-AOI-P) that are difficult to align with quantitative, standardized medical systems.

Method: The authors propose: (1) UFMD - a framework that automatically processes tongue and pulse images into structured, clinically standard descriptions, integrating multi-source diagnostic information into unified digital records of the I-AOI-P process; (2) LingLan-14B - a TCM-specific large language model fine-tuned via supervised learning to emulate the diagnostic logic and workflow of the I-AOI-P process.

Result: The method achieves a relative improvement of 103.5% over the baseline (62.72% vs. 30.82%) and reaches an F1-score of up to 82%.

Conclusion: The proposed UFMD and LingLan-14B model significantly enhance diagnostic accuracy in TCM by bridging the gap between traditional holistic diagnostic methods and modern AI-powered quantitative analysis.

Abstract: Though artificial intelligence (AI) increasingly transforms modern medicine, its integration into Traditional Chinese Medicine (TCM) has been relatively slow, primarily due to TCM's reliance on holistic, subjective diagnostic methods---namely Inspection, Auscultation and Olfaction, Inquiry, and Palpation(I-AOI-P)---which are difficult to align with quantitative, standardized medical systems. In this work, we introduce a Unification Framework for Multimodal Data (UFMD), which automatically processes tongue and pulse images into structured, clinically standard descriptions, integrating multi-source diagnostic information into a unified digital record of I-AOI-P process. Building on this structured data, we create LingLan-14B, a TCM-specific large language model fine-tuned via supervised learning to emulate the diagnostic logic and workflow of I-AOI-P process. Experimental results show that our method significantly enhances diagnostic accuracy, achieving a relative improvement of 103.5% over the baseline (62.72% vs. 30.82%) and reaching an F1-score of up to 82%.

</details>


### [227] [OmniFysics-Nano-V2 Technical Report: Understanding the Physical World Across Modalities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25738)
*Yizhou Liu,Jinghang Han,Kaixiang Qiu,Qi He,Minghao Han,Yue Jiang,Xujia Chen,Wei Zou,Shunli Wang,Lihua Zhang,Dingkang Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OmniFysics-Nano-V2 is a compact omni-modal model that combines vision, audio, speech, and text inputs with a shared reasoning framework. It addresses two key limitations in existing omni-modal models: (1) lack of explicit physical supervision, and (2) homogeneous training objectives. The model uses a dual-branch physics-aware data pipeline and a two-stage GRPO curriculum to achieve leading results on 17 of 21 benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current omni-modal models are trained primarily on semantic descriptions and general-purpose objectives, which leaves physical attributes, interaction states, and causal mechanisms incompletely specified. Simply adding more modalities doesn't provide the supervision needed to connect observations with the physical structure of the world. This gap limits AI systems' ability to understand the physical world.

Method: 1) Dual-branch physics-aware data pipeline: (a) grounds salient objects in structured physical attributes, (b) aligns visual changes with acoustic events, intermediate responses, and interaction outcomes. 2) Two-stage Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) curriculum: progresses from general task correctness to fine-grained physical perceptual reasoning. 3) Reinforcement-learning prompts curated by reward diversity.

Result: Leading results on 17 of 21 benchmarks against SOTA omni-modal models, across multimodal, audio-visual, and physical reasoning benchmarks. The model improves physical-world understanding while preserving broad omni-modal competence.

Conclusion: OmniFysics-Nano-V2 successfully integrates physical-world perception with omni-modal capabilities through physics-aware data pipeline and curriculum-based RL training. It represents a significant step toward next-generation Physical AI by equipping AI systems with both omni-modal understanding and physical reasoning capabilities.

Abstract: Omni-modal models have expanded multimodal interaction across vision, audio, speech, and language. However, their training is predominantly organized around semantic descriptions and general-purpose objectives, leaving physical attributes, interaction states, and causal mechanisms only partially specified. This gap is not simply a matter of modality coverage: adding more modalities does not by itself provide the supervision needed to connect observations with the physical structure of the world. We present OmniFysics-Nano-V2, a compact omni-modal model for physical-world perception and understanding. The model supports image, video, audio, speech, and text inputs within a shared reasoning framework, together with text and speech generation. To address the lack of explicit physical supervision, we construct a dual-branch physics-aware data pipeline that grounds salient objects in structured physical attributes and aligns visual changes with acoustic events, intermediate responses, and interaction outcomes. To address homogeneous training objectives, we curate reinforcement-learning prompts by reward diversity and adopt a two-stage Group Relative Policy Optimization curriculum that progresses from general task correctness to fine-grained physical perceptual reasoning. Experiments across multimodal, audio-visual, and physical reasoning benchmarks show that the proposed data and training strategy improves physical-world understanding while preserving broad omni-modal competence. The proposed model achieves leading result on 17 of 21 benchmarks against SOTA omni-modal models. By equipping AI systems with both omni-modal and physical-world perception capabilities, OmniFysics-Nano-V2 is poised to become a cornerstone of next-generation Physical AI.

</details>


### [228] [The Limits of Simulated Societies: How Post-Training and Survey Fine-Tuning Erase Cross-Cultural Variance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25760)
*Rojin Ziaei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper develops a diagnostic framework to evaluate LLMs as human population simulators, measuring not just point accuracy but also dispersion retention (dr ratio). Testing 11 zero-shot and 5 fine-tuned models on 10,000 WVS respondent-question pairs across 12 countries reveals that alignment training causes "consensus collapse" - compressing opinion spread toward group stereotypes while only marginally improving accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM evaluations in computational social science focus on average response accuracy, missing critical aspects of opinion diversity within real human populations. The researchers aimed to fill this gap by developing a framework that captures both accuracy and dispersion retention.

Method: The framework measures point accuracy alongside dispersion retention (dr = predicted std / human std). Evaluation used 10,000 respondent-question pairs from the World Values Survey across 12 countries and 6 continents. They tested 11 zero-shot models and 5 fine-tuned variants using SFT, DPO, and GRPO methods, tracking the post-training trajectory from Llama 3.1 70B base to Tulu 3 checkpoints.

Result: Supervised instruction tuning halves the opinion spread (dr: 1.22 → 0.59) with only +0.9 point accuracy gain. Later alignment stages (DPO, GRPO) fail to restore diversity. A WEIRD vs non-WEIRD gap emerges: the most accurate model (Tulu 3 70B-DPO, 57.9%) maintains dr=0.50 overall but only 0.11 for Nigeria versus 0.70-0.87 for WEIRD countries. Raising temperature to 1.0, GRPO with distribution-shaped rewards, and mixing with unaligned priors all fail to recover dispersion, especially for non-WEIRD populations.

Conclusion: Point accuracy alone is insufficient for evaluating LLM human simulators. Current post-training methodologies trade opinion diversity for consensus, with alignment systematically compressing output variance. This bias disproportionately affects non-WEIRD populations, suggesting fundamental limitations in using LLMs as diverse human population simulators without new approaches to preserve opinion spread.

Abstract: Using large language models (LLMs) to simulate diverse human populations has the potential to transform many aspects of computational social science, yet many evaluations score the average response rather than the spread of opinion within real groups. Here, we develop a diagnostic framework that measures point accuracy alongside dispersion retention, the ratio of predicted to human standard deviation ($\dr$), on 10{,}000 respondent--question pairs from the World Values Survey (WVS) spanning twelve countries and six continents. We evaluate eleven zero-shot language models and five variants fine-tuned on WVS data with SFT, DPO, and GRPO. We identify a failure mode we term \textit{consensus collapse}, where alignment training compresses outputs toward one stereotype per group. Along the post-training trajectory from the Llama~3.1 70B base to the Tulu~3 checkpoints, the first stage, supervised instruction tuning, removes half of the spread with minimal accuracy gain ($\dr$ 1.22 to 0.59; accuracy $+0.9$ points), the later stages do not restore it, and a gap opens between WEIRD and non-WEIRD countries that survey fine-tuning then deepens while pursuing higher point accuracy. The most accurate model (Tulu~3 70B-DPO fine-tuned on WVS, 57.9\%) keeps half the human spread overall ($\dr = 0.50$) and 11\% of it for Nigeria, against 0.70--0.87 for WEIRD countries. Raising the sampling temperature to 1.0 leaves the Wasserstein-1 distance ($\wone$) to human distributions unchanged for both fine-tuned DPO models, and GRPO on Qwen~3.5 9B does not restore the spread under either an accuracy reward or a distribution-shaped reward. Mixing the aligned model with an unaligned prior raises $\dr$ from 0.51 to 0.62 on a held-out split but leaves Nigeria at 0.36. Point accuracy alone therefore misjudges these simulators, and current post-training trades diversity for consensus.

</details>


### [229] [Neurosymbolic Action Model Learning under Partial Observability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25766)
*Adem Kikaj,Lennert De Smet,Giuseppe Marra,Luc De Raedt*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: NeSyAM is a novel neurosymbolic approach for learning action models from partially observable visual data, addressing the limitation of existing methods that require complete, fully observable traces. The approach also includes a unified variational framework for theoretical analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current action model learning approaches require complete traces of fully observable images, which is unrealistic in real-world scenarios where observations may be missing or incomplete. This limits the applicability of existing neurosymbolic methods to practical problems where partial observability is common.

Method: The paper proposes NeSyAM, a neurosymbolic modeling paradigm that learns action models under partial observability. It also presents a unified variational framework for theoretically analyzing and comparing different approaches.

Result: NeSyAM was tested on six visual planning domains across three observation regimes, consistently recovering relevant parts of the true action model even under partial observability.

Conclusion: The proposed NeSyAM approach successfully enables action model learning under partial observability, demonstrating consistent performance across various domains and observation conditions. The unified variational framework provides theoretical grounding for understanding the limitations of existing methods.

Abstract: AI planning studies how an agent can reach a goal by executing a sequence of actions. To plan correctly, the agent needs an action model describing when each action can be executed and how it changes the world. Constructing such models by hand requires domain expertise, and can be costly and error-prone. Action models can instead be learned from available data using existing neurosymbolic approaches, but they currently assume access to complete traces of fully observable images . These approaches fail to learn action models under partial observability where some of the images might not be present or are not fully informative of the current state of the world. Hence, this paper proposes NeSyAM, a novel neurosymbolic modeling paradigm for action model learning under partial observability. In addition, the paper presents a unified variational framework for theoretically analysing the limitations of existing methods compared to our proposed approach. NeSyAM is then tested extensively on six visual planning domains and three observation regimes to show it consistently recovers relevant parts of the true action model under partial observability.

</details>


### [230] [Towards Omni-dimensional GUI Agent Navigation with Masked Trajectory Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25769)
*Yan Zhang,Pei Fu,Daiqing Wu,Huawen Shen,Ruoceng Zhang,Shaojie Zhang,Jiahui Yang,Yu Zhou,Can Ma,Zhenbo Luo,Jian Luan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MaP (Masked Trajectory Prediction) is a unified framework for multi-task GUI navigation that addresses optimization inconsistency and data heterogeneity by modeling multi-turn interactions as trajectories and using component masking/prediction for training, combined with a role-aware adapter for dynamic token routing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GUI navigation requires complex skills including step-wise decision-making, state-action alignment, and long-horizon planning. While mixing these tasks seems intuitive, direct combination suffers from inconsistent optimization objectives and profound data heterogeneity, creating a severe bottleneck.

Method: 1) Model multi-turn GUI interactions as trajectories
2) Define training objectives through component masking and prediction
3) Shift optimization from task-specific marginal distributions to a consistent objective
4) Design a role-aware adapter learning module that dynamically routes each token to specialized representation spaces to handle data heterogeneity

Result: Extensive experiments on five representative GUI navigation benchmarks demonstrate that MaP effectively mitigates gradient conflicts and significantly outperforms direct mixture training.

Conclusion: MaP establishes a robust paradigm for multi-task GUI navigation by successfully harmonizing divergent GUI navigation tasks through unified training objectives and role-aware adapter learning.

Abstract: Graphical User Interface (GUI) Agents autonomously interact with software to fulfill user requests, where GUI navigation stands out as the most critical and challenging capability. Mastering this capability demands a complex synergy of step-wise decision-making, state-action alignment, and long-horizon planning. While directly mixing these corresponding navigation tasks seems intuitive to simultaneously acquire these skills, such a direct combination is severely bottlenecked by inconsistent optimization objectives and profound data heterogeneity. To overcome these barriers, we propose the MaP (stands for ``\textbf{M}asked Tr\textbf{a}jectory \textbf{P}rediction''), a unified framework that seamlessly harmonizes divergent GUI navigation tasks. By modeling multi-turn GUI interactions as a trajectory and defining training objectives through component masking and prediction, MaP shifts the optimization from task-specific marginal distributions to a consistent objective. Furthermore, to handle the data heterogeneity across multiple navigation tasks, we design a role-aware adapter learning module that dynamically routes each token to a specialized representation space. Extensive experiments on five representative GUI navigation benchmarks demonstrate that MaP effectively mitigates gradient conflicts and significantly outperforms the direct mixture training, establishing a robust paradigm for multi-task GUI navigation.

</details>


### [231] [The Tasteful Agent: Measuring and Improving Taste in Long-Horizon Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25804)
*Wenbo Pan,Zhichao Liu,Shujie Liu,Jingying Zeng,Chin-Yew Lin,Xianfeng Tang,Yan Lu,Qi He,Xiaohua Jia*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Taste-Bench is a benchmark that evaluates LLM agents' decision-making quality ("taste") at critical forks in long-horizon tasks, automatically constructed from agent trajectories without human annotation. The best model achieves only 59.7% accuracy, and taste can be improved through distillation training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks measure end-to-end success of agents on long-horizon tasks but fail to evaluate the quality of intermediate decisions (hypotheses to test, implementations to build on) that determine the final outcome. This capability, termed "taste," was previously unmeasurable.

Method: Automatic mining of "decision forks" from: (1) parallel attempts at the same task, and (2) detours within a single trajectory. Each fork presents multiple directions where one leads to better outcomes, evaluated without seeing downstream consequences.

Result: Best model achieves only 59.7% accuracy on Taste-Bench. Forks where deciding evidence appears later in trajectories are significantly harder for all models. Surprisingly, larger reasoning budget does not improve accuracy. However, taste can be trained via distillation—a teacher model that has seen outcomes trains a student that improves on unseen tasks and end-to-end SWE-bench Pro success.

Conclusion: Taste is a measurable and trainable capability in LLM agents. The benchmark reveals current limitations in decision-making quality, and distillation offers a promising approach to improve agent taste, leading to better outcomes on complex engineering and research tasks.

Abstract: LLM agents increasingly work on long-horizon tasks, and the decisions they make along the way, such as which hypothesis to test or which implementation to build on, determine the outcome of the whole run. Making these decisions well is becoming a key capability for both engineering and research agents. We refer to the ability to make good long-horizon decisions as the taste of an agent. While existing benchmarks measure the end-to-end success of agents on long-horizon tasks, none of them measures the taste of an agent. To address this problem, we build Taste-Bench, a benchmark of taste questions constructed automatically from trajectories that agents produced in engineering and research tasks. Each question presents a decision fork, a point in a trajectory where multiple directions are available and one of them leads to a better outcome, and the evaluated model chooses among these directions without seeing what happens after the fork. We mine these forks automatically from parallel attempts at the same task and from detours inside a single trajectory, without needing human annotation. We evaluate frontier models on Taste-Bench and find that the best model answers only 59.7% of the questions correctly. We further find that forks whose deciding evidence appears later in the trajectory are much harder for every model, and that a larger reasoning budget does not improve the accuracy. Finally, we show that taste can be trained.
