We distill the judgment of a teacher that has seen the outcome into a student model, and the student makes better decisions on unseen tasks and improves end-to-end success on held-out SWE-bench Pro tasks.

</details>


### [232] [When Are Aggregate Agent Traces Diagnosable? Traffic-Governed Interpretation and Calibrated Abstention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25806)
*Peiying Zhu,Sidi Chang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a bounded rule for interpreting aggregate agent behavior in hotel-pricing simulations. It uses a two-gate approach (reference-map gate and matched runtime gate) to establish exposure before scoring change, abstaining when traces cannot support claims. The method achieved 76.4% reference admission, zero false admissions (meeting 0.20 criterion), and significant improvements in negative log likelihood (29.3% reduction).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Runtime traces can appear transparent, but a closed-loop policy determines which states are visited and which failures become visible. Faults may leave no aggregate trace when the policy rarely visits affected cells. The authors seek to address the diagnosability decision preceding scoring or localization, particularly when entry into aggregate-only fault interpretation is required.

Method: The study uses a simulated hotel-pricing agent mapping time, inventory, and market state to discrete price actions under varying demand regimes. A two-gate diagnostic approach is employed: (1) Reference-map gate requiring repeated clean-policy support; (2) Matched runtime gate requiring joint support in clean and current streams. Signal analysis occurs only after both gates pass. False admission is calibrated on a disjoint clean stream at the physical-component level, with detection modeled by affected clean traffic rather than nominal cell coverage.

Result: In a frozen one-shot heldout, 55/72 (76.4%) regime-component units were reference-admitted (representing 20 physical components); 54/55 passed matched runtime admission while the rejected unit abstained. False admission was 0/20, with a one-sided exact 95% upper bound of 0.1391, meeting the frozen 0.20 criterion. Across 540 repeated unit-arm rows, affected clean traffic reduced negative log likelihood by 29.3% (gain of 0.1264 nats per row, 95% CI [0.0593, 0.1918]). Adding mask family and interaction improved log loss by 0.0015 nats per row (upper bound 0.0066), below the 0.01 practical-sufficiency margin. Development audit showed exact minimum hitting set and greedy selection chose identical supports in 12/12 scenarios.

Conclusion: The result is a bounded rule for interpreting aggregate agent behavior: first establish exposure, then score change, and abstain when the trace cannot support the claim. This approach provides a principled framework for fault diagnosis in closed-loop policy systems where aggregate traces may be insufficient for reliable interpretation.

Abstract: Runtime traces can appear transparent, but a closed-loop policy determines which states are visited and which failures become visible. We study a simulated hotel-pricing agent mapping time, inventory, and market state to discrete price actions under varying demand regimes. A fault may leave no aggregate trace when the policy rarely visits affected cells. We treat entry into aggregate-only fault interpretation as a diagnosability decision preceding scoring or localization. A reference-map gate requires repeated clean-policy support; a matched runtime gate then requires joint support in clean and current streams. Signal analysis occurs only after both pass. We calibrate false admission on a disjoint clean stream at the physical-component level and model detection by affected clean traffic rather than nominal cell coverage. In a frozen one-shot heldout, 55/72 (76.4%) regime-component units were reference-admitted, representing 20 physical components; 54/55 passed matched runtime admission, while the rejected unit abstained. Stable false admission was 0/20, with a one-sided exact 95% upper bound of 0.1391, meeting the frozen 0.20 criterion. Across 540 repeated unit-arm rows nested in those 20 clusters, affected clean traffic reduced negative log likelihood by 29.3% relative to cell coverage, a gain of 0.1264 nats per row (cluster-bootstrap 95% interval [0.0593, 0.1918]). Adding mask family and its interaction improved log loss by 0.0015 nats per row (one-sided upper bound 0.0066), below the frozen 0.01 practical-sufficiency margin. A development audit found that exact minimum hitting set and greedy selection chose identical supports in 12/12 scenarios because singleton evidence had resolved the conflicts. The result is a bounded rule for interpreting aggregate agent behavior: first establish exposure, then score change, and abstain when the trace cannot support the claim.

</details>


### [233] [Optimizing the Score, Losing Sight of the Task: Reward Hacking Across Weights, Selection, and Prompts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25848)
*Vansh Wahi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper develops a comparative framework for analyzing "reward hacking" across three optimization methods in language model systems: weight updates, output selection, and persistent prompt modifications. The authors argue that higher evaluation scores don't necessarily mean better performance, because optimizers can exploit evaluator weaknesses rather than improving actual task capability. The framework includes formal bounds on evaluator disagreement and a capacity ordering for policy classes, while demonstrating why distance alone cannot universally rank vulnerability. The work maps defenses across substrates and emphasizes that reliable improvement requires controlling accessible failure modes and preserving independent evidence of task quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The core problem motivating this research is that optimization in language model systems can achieve higher evaluation scores without actually improving task performance. This occurs because optimizers can exploit "mistakes" or limitations in the evaluator (reward hacking), rather than genuinely improving capabilities. The authors seek to understand how different optimization approaches (weights, selection, text) differ in their vulnerability to this failure mode and which defenses transfer across these substrates.

Method: The authors develop a comparative framework that builds on the Proxy Compression Hypothesis and prior research on inference-time and in-context reward hacking. They formalize: (1) a distance-dependent upper bound on evaluator disagreement, and (2) a capacity ordering for nested policy classes. They use both theoretical analysis and a finite-output illustration to demonstrate how the location of scoring defects affects which optimization method favors which behavior. They also map representative defenses across the three substrates.

Result: The key results include: (1) a formal demonstration that distance alone cannot establish a universal ranking of vulnerability across optimization methods - meaning it's not possible to simply say one method is always more or less vulnerable to reward hacking; (2) an exact finite-output illustration showing how the location of a scoring defect changes which behavior each optimization method favors; (3) a mapping of which defense mechanisms transfer directly across substrates versus which offer only functional analogies; (4) special focus on persistent prompts - which are inspectable but whose behavioral effects may be hard to anticipate from small textual changes.

Conclusion: The authors conclude that reliable improvement in language model systems depends on: (1) controlling accessible failure modes, and (2) preserving evidence of task quality independent of the score being optimized. The framework connects optimization choices to verification requirements, suggesting that the choice of optimization method has implications for what verification mechanisms are needed to ensure genuine progress rather than score exploitation.

Abstract: A higher evaluation score does not always mean a better language model system. When optimization exploits an evaluator's mistakes, measured progress can conceal unchanged or deteriorating task performance. This failure can arise through parameter updates, selection among generated outputs, or revisions to persistent prompts. We develop a comparative framework for reward hacking across these three optimization substrates: weights, selection, and text. Building on the Proxy Compression Hypothesis and research on inference-time and in-context reward hacking, we examine how reachable behavior, optimization budgets, and persistent adaptation shape exposure to proxy error. We formalize a distance-dependent upper bound on evaluator disagreement and a capacity ordering for nested policy classes, then show why distance alone cannot establish a universal ranking of vulnerability. An exact finite-output illustration demonstrates how the location of a scoring defect changes the behavior favored by each method. We also map representative defenses across substrates, identifying which mechanisms transfer directly and which offer only functional analogies. Persistent prompts receive particular attention: their contents are inspectable, but the behavior induced by a small textual change may be difficult to anticipate. The formal analysis, numerical illustration, and published evidence together provide a basis for comparing optimization methods and identifying the conditions under which their defenses transfer. The resulting framework connects optimization choices to verification requirements: reliable improvement depends on controlling accessible failure modes and preserving evidence of task quality independent of the score being optimized.

</details>


### [234] [Prediction Is Not Detection: Evaluating Pre-Recognition Claims in Longitudinal Clinical AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25852)
*Jing Yang,Long R. Jiao,Xiujun Cai,Zongjiu Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses methodological issues in evaluating clinical AI systems for early disease detection. Current evaluation approaches can artificially inflate performance metrics and lead time by conflating recognition-mediated care process signals with true early detection. The authors propose using interval-censored pre-recognition transitions, an independent reference standard, and a prespecified recognition proxy to properly assess whether AI systems detect conditions before human clinical recognition.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to establish proper methodology for evaluating longitudinal clinical AI systems for early detection. The authors observe that existing event-based evaluations can treat recognition-mediated care process signals as "shortcuts" and recognition-dependent endpoints as reference standards, which inflates apparent performance and lead time while undermining cross-center transportability. This creates a gap between demonstrating prognosis versus actual detection before recognition.

Method: The paper proposes three key methodological components: (1) defining an interval-censored pre-recognition transition to properly model the time before clinical recognition, (2) establishing an independent as-of reference standard that is not contaminated by the detection system being evaluated, and (3) using a prespecified recognition proxy to make detection claims testable.

Result: The abstract presents a methodological framework rather than empirical results. The key contribution is a proposed framework for making detection claims testable in clinical AI evaluation, addressing the methodological limitations of current approaches.

Conclusion: The conclusion establishes that proper early detection evaluation requires distinguishing between prognosis and pre-recognition detection. The proposed interval-censored framework with independent reference standards provides a way to make valid claims about AI detection before clinical recognition, which is essential for clinically useful early detection systems.

Abstract: Clinically useful early detection requires validated pre-recognition lead time. Yet event-based evaluations of longitudinal clinical AI can treat recognition-mediated care-process signals as shortcuts and recognition-dependent endpoints as reference standards, inflating apparent performance and lead time while undermining cross-center transport. Such results may serve prognosis without establishing detection before recognition. We define an interval-censored pre-recognition transition, an independent as-of reference standard, and a prespecified recognition proxy to make the claim testable.

</details>


### [235] [AgenticSizing: A Large Language Model-based Multi-Agent Framework for Analog Circuit Sizing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25873)
*Yijia Hao,Pratibha Verma,Dongxu Guo,Cristian Sestito,Michael O'Boyle,Christos-Savvas Bouganis,Themis Prodromakis*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A multi-agent LLM framework for complex analog circuit sizing that mimics expert design team collaboration by decomposing circuit topology, extracting design knowledge, and coordinating role-specialized sizing agents. Validated on 8 circuits up to 55 transistors and 60 variables, achieving 60% success rate on LDO where classical optimizers failed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Analog circuit sizing is challenging due to large design space, strong trade-offs, and increasing complexity. Existing LLM-based methods lack explicit circuit-topology understanding and are mainly evaluated on simple blocks. Classical optimizers fail on complex circuits like LDO.

Method: 1) Analyze circuit topology and decompose netlist into functional blocks/substructures. 2) Extract lightweight design knowledge for reuse. 3) Use a planner to coordinate multiple role-specialized sizing agents for global optimization. Workflow mimics expert analog design team collaboration.

Result: Validated on 8 circuits (largest: 55 transistors, 60 sizing variables). For LDO benchmark: 60% success rate with 83 average iterations, where classical optimizers failed. Ablation studies show topology understanding, design-knowledge infusion, and agent specialization provide complementary benefits.

Conclusion: The multi-agent LLM framework successfully handles complex analog circuit sizing with topology-aware decomposition and role-specialized agents. It outperforms classical optimizers on challenging benchmarks like LDO, with interpretable and simulation-driven optimization. Source code available for reproducibility.

Abstract: Analog circuit sizing remains a challenging and time-consuming task due to the large design space, strong performance trade-offs, and increasing circuit complexity in scaled technologies. Although recent large language model (LLM)-based methods show promise in improving sample efficiency and interpretability, existing approaches often lack explicit circuit-topology understanding and are mainly evaluated on relatively simple analog building blocks. This paper presents a multi-agent LLM-based framework for complex analog circuit sizing. The proposed framework first analyzes the circuit topology and decomposes the netlist into functional blocks and substructures. It also extracts lightweight design knowledge for reuse. Based on the extracted topology and knowledge, a planner coordinates multiple role-specialized sizing agents to update design variables and achieve global performance specifications. This workflow mimics the collaborative process of an expert analog design team and provides a structured, interpretable, and simulation-driven optimization procedure. The framework was validated on eight circuits, with the largest design containing up to 55 transistors and 60 sizing variables. Notably, for the LDO benchmark, the proposed method achieved a 60\% success rate with an average of 83 iterations, where classical optimizers failed to find feasible solutions. Further, ablation studies demonstrate that topology understanding, design-knowledge infusion, and agent specialization provide complementary benefits. The source code is available to support reproducibility.

</details>


### [236] [CausalLoss-Fin: Attributing Financial-Agent Loss to Decisions and Infrastructure Faults](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25960)
*Abhishek Sharma*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses the problem of attribution in payment exception handling: existing methods incorrectly blame agent decisions for infrastructure failures (like dropped messages). The authors propose a benchmark-based approach that decomposes losses into infrastructure effects, policy differentials, and reference residuals using Shapley values, revealing that agent-only baselines misattribute 100% of infrastructure failures and charge $114,383.40 to agents incorrectly.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current agent-step attribution methods charge every dollar of loss to agent decisions, even when infrastructure faults (like dropped settlement messages) are the real cause. These methods intervene on agent actions but don't expose infrastructure faults as intervenable variables, leading to systematic misattribution.

Method: The authors use a benchmark with explicit, replayable fault processes. They decompose episodes into named, individually repairable messages and intervene on both agent choices and infrastructure. A "telescoping identity" splits any policy's loss into three components: infrastructure effect, policy differential against the best implementable policy, and reference-policy residual. Shapley value decomposition divides the infrastructure effect into signed allocations over individual messages. They evaluate 545 planted episodes across 3 deterministic programmatic policies.

Result: Agent-only baselines (lacking infrastructure variables) misfile 100% of infrastructure episodes and incorrectly charge $114,383.40 to agents. Repairing what the baseline names recovers 0.0% of available loss; repairing a minimal sufficient set recovers 100.0%. Scoring messages individually is not merely imprecise: 27.8% (95% CI: 23.3-32.3%) of episodes do not decompose additively.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates that attribution methods ignoring infrastructure variables systematically misattribute infrastructure failures to agent decisions. This has significant practical implications for evaluating agent performance in payment systems. The prevalence figures are specific to this generator, not field rates. The approach is limited to deterministic programmatic policies, limiting external validity to stochastic agents.

Abstract: When an agent handling a payment exception loses money, the agent-step attribution methods this paper compares against will name one of its actions. They will do so even when a settlement message was dropped and the agent never had a chance: they intervene on agent actions and do not expose infrastructure faults as intervenable variables, so every dollar they explain is charged to a decision. We take a benchmark whose fault process is explicit and replayable, decompose each episode's realised delivery schedule into named, individually repairable messages, and intervene on both the agent's choices and the infrastructure's. A telescoping identity splits any policy's loss exactly three ways: an infrastructure effect, a policy differential against the best implementable policy, and a reference-policy residual. Two of the three can be negative, so none is a share; Shapley then divides the first into signed allocations over individual messages. One result is structural and needs no corpus: an agent-only baseline identifies no infrastructure cause, because its model contains no variable that could name one. What 545 planted episodes across 3 policies measure is the size of that consequence. It misfiles 100% of infrastructure episodes and charges $114,383.40 to the agent. Repairing what it names recovers 0.0% of the available loss; repairing a minimal sufficient set recovers 100.0%. Scoring messages one at a time is not merely imprecise: 27.8% (95% CI: 23.3--32.3%) of episodes do not decompose additively. We evaluate deterministic programmatic policies rather than language-model agents, which is what makes replay exact and which limits external validity to stochastic agents. The prevalence figures are properties of this generator, not field rates.

</details>


### [237] [CQ4OE: A benchmark for assessing LLM-assisted ontology generation from competency questions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26029)
*Jiayi Li,Ziyuan Wang,Daniel Garijo,María Poveda-Villalón*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CQ4OE is a benchmark for systematically evaluating LLM-based ontology generation from Competency Questions (CQs), addressing limitations in current evaluation methods by providing CQ-driven gold ontologies with explicit provenance and two complementary evaluation tasks (CQ2Term and CQ2Onto).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ontology generation from CQs is central but labor-intensive in Ontology Engineering. Current LLM evaluations are fragmented due to heterogeneous task formulations, lack of fine-grained CQ provenance, conflated metrics, and reference ontologies not designed around evaluation CQs.

Method: The authors created CQ4OE with: (1) CQ-driven gold OWL ontologies with explicit provenance linking each CQ to required classes, properties, and axioms; (2) Two evaluation tasks - CQ2Term (term-level evaluation over 99 CQs) and CQ2Onto (ontology-level evaluation over 118 CQs including hierarchy, property modeling, and axiom-level structure); (3) Experiments with nine LLMs using zero-shot, iterative, and multi-agent generation strategies.

Result: Experiments show that LLMs recover explicit vocabulary terms more reliably than creating ontologies, with particular challenges in property modeling, hierarchy construction, and axiom generation.

Conclusion: LLMs struggle with structural and logical ontology construction compared to vocabulary extraction, highlighting the need for improved strategies in ontology-level generation tasks.

Abstract: Ontology generation from Competency Questions (CQs) is a central yet labor-intensive phase of Ontology Engineering. While large language models (LLMs) offer promising automation capabilities, current evaluations remain fragmented. Task formulations are heterogeneous, gold standards often lack fine-grained CQ provenance, metrics conflate lexical overlap with structural and logical adequacy, and reference ontologies are not always explicitly designed around the evaluation CQs. Here, we address these limitations with CQ4OE, a benchmark for the systematic and reproducible evaluation of LLM-based ontology generation from CQs. For each ontology in the benchmark, we build a CQ-driven gold OWL ontology with explicit provenance linking each CQ to the classes, properties, and axioms required to answer it. From this resource, we define two complementary evaluation tasks. CQ2Term supports term-level evaluation of CQ-specific class and property prediction over 99 CQs, and CQ2Onto supports ontology-level evaluation over 118 CQs, including hierarchy, property modeling, and axiom-level structure. We demonstrate CQ4OE with experiments using nine LLMs under zero-shot, iterative, and multi-agent generation strategies, showing that LLMs recover explicit vocabulary terms more reliably than creating ontologies, particularly in property modeling, hierarchy construction, and axiom generation.

</details>


### [238] [Canonical locks that encode part-whole hierarchies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26046)
*Rajat Modi,Yogesh Singh Rawat*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes "canonical locks," a geometric primitive for encoding part-whole hierarchies in neural networks using higher-dimensional vectors (d ≥ 4) and relative phase differences. The approach uses bottom-up and top-down neural fields that interact to achieve thermal equilibrium, with symmetry-breaking dynamics connected to mental rotation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current methods flatten tree structures into sequences for autoregressive sequence-to-sequence models, which works for NLP parse trees but fails for images. There is a need for a representation that can encode part-whole hierarchies for visual data.

Method: The method models parts/wholes as higher-dimensional vectors (d ≥ 4) and encodes information in relative phase differences. The network consists of positionally-bound bottom-up and top-down neural fields that drive each other to thermal equilibrium. Symmetry-breaking depends on the angular arrangement of parts/wholes on a disk/ring in higher dimensions.

Result: The paper demonstrates the existence of symmetrical configurations in the network. The computational iterations required to break these symmetries depend on the angle between parts/wholes arranged on a disk (or ring) in higher dimensions.

Conclusion: The proposed canonical locks framework appears to have connections to the psychological phenomenon of mental rotation, suggesting a potential bridge between neural network representations and cognitive processes.

Abstract: One of the challenges in representational learning is how to encode part-whole hierarchies in a neural net. Prior works rely on flattening tree-like structures into string-like sequences and training a sequence-to-sequence model via autoregression. While such a representation works for parse-trees in NLP, it is not entirely clear how to make it work for images. Thus, we propose a geometric primitive called canonical locks. The key idea is that parts/wholes can be modelled as higher-dimensional vectors ($d \geq 4$), and information can be encoded in their relative phase differences.
  Inductively, the net consists of positionally-bound bottom-up and top-down neural fields, which drive each other to achieve a state of thermal equilibrium. Additionally, we show the existence of a few symmetrical configurations in the net. The computational iterations taken to break these symmetries depend on the angle between parts/wholes arranged on a disk (or more precisely a ring) in higher dimensions. It also appears to have connections to the psychological phenomenon of mental rotation.

</details>


### [239] [Adversarial Course-of-Action Generation: Game-Theoretic Multi-Agent Algorithms for COA matching & COA generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26059)
*Natan Vidra,Alina Kapanova,Arun Kanhai,Spurthi Setty*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: COA-Bench is a benchmark for evaluating course-of-action generation policies in adversarial planning scenarios through self-play. It represents COAs as typed action chains with conditional branches, uses synthetic quality scores, BLUE-vs-RED advantage metrics, Nash-gap distance, and doctrinal coherence heuristics. The benchmark includes 50 synthetic scenarios across five operational templates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The problem of COA generation is inherently distributed: systems must propose structured candidate actions, evaluate them against adversarial responses, and maintain tactical coherence under changing conditions. Existing approaches lack a standardized offline benchmark for comparison, making it difficult to evaluate and reproduce different COA generation policies.

Method: The authors implement COA-Bench as a typed action chain representation with conditional branches. They evaluate two approaches: (1) a sampled best-response policy drawing eight RED candidates, and (2) a two-stage multi-agent council with five BLUE proposer agents, RED-team adjudication, and critique-driven revision. Metrics include synthetic COA quality scores, BLUE-vs-RED advantage scores, Nash-gap distance, and FM 3-0-inspired doctrinal coherence heuristics.

Result: The sampled best-response policy reduced BLUE advantage from .516 to .485 and BLUE wargame win rate from .920 to .820. The two-stage multi-agent council achieved .509 BLUE advantage and .820 BLUE win rate. The authors also identified and fixed a benchmark-design issue where scenario framing was stored as metadata but had no effect on generated COA content.

Conclusion: COA-Bench provides an inspectable evaluation harness for comparing COA generation policies. The preliminary benchmark evidence suggests that adversarial testing (RED candidates) significantly impacts BLUE performance. The artifact is not operational and uses no real, classified, or human-subject data. The work offers lessons for building auditable agentic planning artifacts, though the benchmark is described as "small" and "offline," indicating limited scope.

Abstract: Course-of-action (COA) generation is a distributed planning problem: a system must propose structured candidate actions, evaluate them against an adversarial response, and surface options that remain tactically coherent under changing conditions. We present COA-Bench, a small offline benchmark and reproducibility artifact for comparing COA generation policies through self-play. Following the BattleCOA terminology, we reserve COA matching for asset-effect matching and COA generation for course-of-action generation; the present artifact does not implement either DecisionFunction directly. Instead, it represents COAs as typed action chains with conditional branches, assigns a synthetic COA quality score, compares opposing COAs with a BLUE-vs-RED advantage score and Nash-gap distance, and scores doctrinal coherence with an FM 3-0-inspired heuristic rubric. Across 50 synthetic scenarios spanning five operational templates, a sampled best-response policy that draws eight RED candidates reduces BLUE advantage from .516 to .485 and BLUE wargame win rate from .920 to .820; a two-stage multi-agent council with five BLUE proposer agents, RED-team adjudication, and critique-driven revision obtains .509 BLUE advantage and .820 BLUE win rate. We also identify and fix a benchmark-design issue in which scenario framing was stored as metadata but had no effect on generated COA content. COA-Bench is not an operational battle-management system and uses no real, classified, proprietary, or human-subject data. The contribution is an inspectable evaluation harness, preliminary benchmark evidence, and lessons for building auditable agentic planning artifacts.

</details>


### [240] [ChainUQ: Reasoning Consistency-Aware Uncertainty Quantification for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26060)
*Dahai Yu,Rongchao Xu,Lin Jiang,Ximiao Li,Guang Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ChainUQ is a reasoning consistency-aware uncertainty quantification framework for LLMs that addresses limitations in token-level probability approaches by using alignment-aware confidence estimation and reasoning-chain consistency calibration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs exhibit impressive reasoning capabilities but response-level confidence remains unreliable when intermediate claims conflict with final conclusions. Current UQ approaches fail to capture logical inconsistencies within reasoning chains, relying only on token-level probabilities and lacking mechanisms to dynamically calibrate confidence using structural logic.

Method: ChainUQ consists of two components: (1) An alignment-aware lightweight UQ module that estimates raw intrinsic model confidence from frozen features aligned to the final conclusion; (2) A reasoning consistency-aware calibrator that refines this score using reasoning-chain consistency evidence.

Result: Evaluations across diverse in-distribution and out-of-distribution benchmarks show ChainUQ consistently improves response-level uncertainty estimation, achieving an average 3.1% relative gain in AUROC and up to 45.0% relative reduction in ECE, with direct transferability to new settings without additional fine-tuning.

Conclusion: ChainUQ effectively addresses the challenge of capturing logical inconsistencies in LLM reasoning chains, providing more reliable uncertainty quantification that generalizes across different settings.

Abstract: While large language models (LLMs) exhibit impressive reasoning capabilities, response-level confidence may remain unreliable when intermediate claims conflict with the final conclusion. Therefore, effective uncertainty quantification (UQ) is required to capture logical inconsistencies within the reasoning chain, not just the correctness of the final output. Current approaches have two major limitations: (1) their reliance on token-level probabilities fails to capture reasoning consistency, and (2) they lack mechanisms to dynamically calibrate confidence using the structural logic of the generated chain. To advance existing research, we introduce ChainUQ, a reasoning consistency-aware uncertainty quantification framework for LLMs. ChainUQ consists of two key technical components: an alignment-aware lightweight UQ module that estimates a raw intrinsic model confidence score from frozen features aligned to the final conclusion, and a reasoning consistency-aware calibrator that refines this score using reasoning-chain consistency evidence. Evaluations across diverse in-distribution and out-of-distribution benchmarks show that ChainUQ consistently improves response-level uncertainty estimation, achieving an average 3.1% relative gain in AUROC and up to 45.0% relative reduction in ECE, and can be directly transferred to new settings without additional fine-tuning.

</details>


### [241] [RankCert: When Can Simulated Learners Safely Select an AI Tutor? Robust Decision Certification Under Structural Uncertainty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26069)
*Nizam Kadir*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces RankCert, a method to certify tutoring policies in simulation-based tutor selection when multiple adequacy criteria unanimously support the same candidate. The method was evaluated on 1,280 held-out settings using EdNet-KT1 data, finding that certification occurred in only 3.75% of settings, with RankCert abstaining in all ambiguous, misspecified, and structural-conflict scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Simulation-based tutor selection can be unstable when different but predictively adequate learner models imply different policy rankings. This creates uncertainty in which tutoring policy to select, necessitating a certification method that only recommends a policy when multiple criteria consistently support it.

Method: RankCert certifies one of eight equal-budget tutoring policies only when six criteria unanimously support the same candidate: model-averaged utility, probability-best, posterior regret, cross-domain rank, family coverage, and leave-one-domain-out and leave-one-visible-family-out averages. Evaluation used a licensed EdNet-KT1 derivative with 5,000 learners and 590,056 responses across 1,280 frozen held-out settings spanning five oracle families.

Result: Minimum-domain mean pairwise top-1 agreement was 0.272917 (95% CI [0.253646, 0.293229]), indicating substantial structural disagreement. Cohort-noise variance decreased from n=30 to n=300, but structural family share remained nonzero. RankCert reduced total held-out decision loss by 0.006605 normalized-outcome units (95% CI [0.004859, 0.008407]). However, at comparable coverage, it did not reduce selective risk relative to a confidence-gated point certificate (difference -0.000213; 95% CI [-0.003238, 0.002384]; Holm p=0.929654). Certification occurred in only 3.75% of settings, exclusively in stable scenarios.

Conclusion: RankCert provides conservative decision certification under declared utility and uncertainty sets, abstaining when there is ambiguity, model misspecification, or structural conflict. The "safe" designation applies only to benchmark-scoped decisions; no human-learning, causal, deployment-effectiveness, or general-safety claims are made. The low certification rate (3.75%) reflects the difficulty of achieving consensus across multiple adequacy criteria in tutor selection.

Abstract: Simulation-based tutor selection can be unstable when predictively adequate learner models imply different policy rankings. RankCert certifies one of eight equal-budget tutoring policies only when model-averaged utility, probability-best, posterior regret, cross-domain rank, family coverage, and leave-one-domain-out and leave-one-visible-family-out averages support the same candidate; otherwise it abstains. We evaluated RankCert in 1,280 frozen held-out settings spanning five rotating held-out oracle families, 64 scenarios per family, and four cohort sizes. Calibration used a licensed, de-identified EdNet-KT1 derivative with 5,000 learners and 590,056 retained responses; all five family representatives passed the frozen adequacy gate. Minimum-domain mean pairwise top-1 agreement was 0.272917 (95% CI [0.253646, 0.293229]), showing substantial structural disagreement. Cohort-noise variance decreased from n = 30 to n = 300, while the structural family share remained nonzero. RankCert reduced total held-out decision loss relative to full-coverage point selection by 0.006605 normalized-outcome units (95% CI [0.004859, 0.008407]). At comparable coverage, however, it did not reduce selective risk relative to a confidence-gated point certificate (difference -0.000213; 95% CI [-0.003238, 0.002384]; Holm p = 0.929654). Certification occurred in 3.75% of settings and only in stable scenarios; RankCert abstained in every ambiguous, misspecified, and structural-conflict setting. "Safe" denotes only benchmark-scoped decision certification under the declared utility and uncertainty set; no human-learning, causal, deployment-effectiveness, or general-safety claim is made.

</details>


### [242] [Selection-Invariant Communication Compilers for Privacy-Aware Multi-Agent LLM Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26076)
*Jinghan Xu,Longze Fan,Zeyuan Wang,Xinjin Li,Hankai Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper identifies "selection-channel leakage" in multi-agent workflows, where private state can be inferred from choices among semantically valid message realizations after authorization. The authors propose SICC (Selection-Invariant Communication Compiler), which constrains the post-authorization representation kernel to prevent this leakage while maintaining protocol utility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-agent workflows exchange intermediate messages that can reveal private state through selection-channel leakage - even when final output is safe, the choice among semantically valid realizations can create an inference channel after authorization fixes what may be released.

Method: The authors introduce SICC, which constrains the post-authorization representation kernel rather than prescribing templates. Any deterministic or independently public-randomized generator satisfying the invariant is valid. Requirement-indexed canonical forms serve as one auditable implementation. The approach combines authorization, public-only form generation, and a dependency-safe utility gate.

Result: Across 132 AgentLeak communication replays and 100 executable LangGraph tasks, deterministic SICC retains complete protocol utility without a positive excess-gain signal. Independent public randomization preserves the same result in AgentLeak and 480 controlled cases.

Conclusion: The compositional communication-layer guarantee ensures that authorization, public-only form generation, and dependency-safe utility gate make the emitted transcript reveal no information beyond the complete authorized view. Private-state-aware selection remains vulnerable after surface-disjoint and length-matched controls, but SICC effectively prevents this leakage while maintaining utility.

Abstract: Structured multi-agent workflows exchange intermediate messages whose content and form can reveal private state even when the final output is safe. We identify selection-channel leakage: after authorization fixes what may be released, a private-state-aware choice among semantically valid realizations creates an additional inference channel. We introduce the selection-invariant communication compiler(SICC), which constrains this post-authorization representation kernel rather than prescribing templates. Any deterministic or independently public-randomized generator satisfying the invariant is valid; requirement-indexed canonical forms are one auditable implementation. We prove a compositional communication-layer guarantee: authorization, public-only form generation, and a dependency-safe utility gate make the emitted transcript reveal no information beyond the complete authorized view. Private-state-aware selection remains vulnerable after surface-disjoint and length-matched controls. Across 132 AgentLeak communication replays and 100 executable LangGraph tasks, deterministic SICC retains complete protocol utility without a positive excess-gain signal; independent public randomization preserves the same result in AgentLeak and 480 controlled cases.

</details>


### [243] [The Architect, the Adversary, and the Judge: Closed-Loop Generation of Standards-Aligned Assessment Items at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26087)
*Wenhui Chen,Ziyao Lin,Jianlin Chen,Peiji Long,Chi Man Vong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CLAIM is a production pipeline for K-12 assessment-item generation using a two-stage generate-then-attack protocol with adversarial review, bi-directional few-shot conditioning, and a knowledge dictionary of 44,844 error-correction rules. It achieves 97.8% pass rate but reveals significant judge-relative quality assessment and a fundamental asymmetry: multiple-choice items reach 98%+ accuracy while fill-in-the-blank items plateau at 82.8-96.7%, attributed to the open-set boundary determination problem that autoregressive decoders cannot solve.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses the challenge of automated K-12 assessment item generation at production scale, while critically examining what the high pass rate actually certifies. The authors investigate whether the quality metrics are robust across different evaluators and item types.

Method: The pipeline uses: (1) Two-stage generate-then-attack protocol where the model drafts as "curriculum architect" then reviews as adversarial reviewer; (2) Bi-directional few-shot conditioning on accepted and rejected items with evaluator diagnosis; (3) Knowledge dictionary of 44,844 error-correction rules mined from feedback and retrieved per standard and item type. Tested on 43,227 items across 755 Common Core ELA standards, 3 item types, and 10 LLMs. Production run of 9,074 items evaluated by expert evaluator, then re-scored by external judges from other vendors.

Result: The pipeline achieves 97.8% expert-evaluator pass rate on production run. However, re-scoring by external judges reveals: (1) format ordering is reproduced but larger open-set deficit than deployed evaluator; (2) weak accept/reject agreement at production prevalence (kappa ≈ 0.13); (3) judges agree poorly with each other. Key asymmetry: multiple-choice and multiple-select reach 98%+ under static rules, while fill-in-the-blank is capability-tiered (82.8-96.7%) and plateaus under prompt optimization, with error mass shifting between answer-key over-inclusion and omission. When evaluator is distilled, fail-recall rises from 8% to 63% while F1 saturates at 0.25.

Conclusion: Quality assessment is judge-relative with no student-response data. The fundamental asymmetry between item types stems from open-set boundary determination - a task autoregressive decoders are structurally ill-equipped to solve. The high pass rate does not certify absolute quality but rather reflects the evaluator's specific boundary criteria.

Abstract: We present CLAIM, a production pipeline for K-12 assessment-item generation coupling a two-stage generate-then-attack protocol (the model drafts as a "curriculum architect", then re-enters the same conversation as a hostile adversarial reviewer), bi-directional few-shot conditioning on accepted and rejected items, the latter carrying the evaluator's diagnosis, and a knowledge dictionary of 44,844 error-correction rules mined from that feedback and retrieved per standard and item type. Across 43,227 scored items over 755 Common Core ELA standards, three item types, and ten LLMs, the pipeline reaches a 97.8% expert-evaluator pass rate on a 9,074-item production run. We then ask what that rate certifies. Re-scoring a stratified sample with three judges from other vendors, blind to the deployed verdict, reproduces the format ordering under every judge and recovers a larger open-set deficit than the deployed evaluator does; but agreement on the accept/reject binary is weak at production prevalence (kappa about 0.13), and the judges agree with each other no better. The level is therefore judge-relative, and with no student-response data our quality evidence is evaluator-judged throughout. The corpus also exposes a robust asymmetry. Multiple-choice and multiple-select generation saturate at 98% or above for both frontier models under a dozen static rules, whereas fill-in-the-blank generation is capability-tiered (82.8-96.7% across five models under a matched rule set, standards, and judge) and plateaus under prompt-only optimization, with error mass shifting between answer-key over-inclusion and omission as rules accumulate. We analyze this as open-set boundary determination, a task autoregressive decoders are structurally ill-equipped to solve, and show the asymmetry recurring when the evaluator itself is distilled: fail-recall rises from 8% to 63% while F1 saturates at 0.25.

</details>


### [244] [FusionMMT: A Unified Multimodal and Multitask Learning Framework for Nuclear Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26095)
*Qiang Chen,Xiao Wang,Qingquan Yang,Hao Si,Zikang Yan,Meiwen Chen,Guosheng Xu,Jin Tang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents FusionMMT, the first unified multimodal multitask framework for intelligent tokamak plasma diagnostics, built on the EAST-VTD640 dataset containing 640 EAST shots with vision and time-series diagnostics for three tasks: disruption prediction, ELM recognition, and H98 regression.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing intelligent diagnostic methods for tokamak fusion plasmas are limited to either multimodal single-task or unimodal multitask learning. A unified multimodal multitask learning framework for high-performance, long-pulse, steady-state fusion operation remains underexplored.

Method: FusionMMT employs multi-scale, time-aware, and variable-aware modeling to handle heterogeneous sampling rates and high computational costs. It combines task-adaptive multimodal fusion with progressive multitask optimization to learn shared and task-specific representations while mitigating cross-task conflicts and optimization imbalance.

Result: Extensive experiments on EAST-VTD640 show that FusionMMT outperforms representative multimodal multitask methods across disruption prediction, ELM recognition, and H98 regression tasks.

Conclusion: FusionMMT successfully provides a unified solution for multimodal multitask tokamak plasma diagnostics, effectively addressing the challenges of heterogeneous data and cross-task optimization conflicts.

Abstract: With the growing global demand for energy, nuclear fusion has emerged as a promising direction for future clean energy. Tokamaks represent one of the leading approaches to magnetic-confinement fusion. Achieving high-performance, long-pulse, and steady-state operation requires effective diagnosis of plasma states. However, existing intelligent diagnostic methods are largely limited to either multimodal single-task or unimodal multitask learning, while a unified multimodal multitask learning framework remains underexplored. To address this gap, we construct EAST-VTD640, a multimodal multitask dataset that integrates vision and time-series diagnostics from 640 EAST shots for disruption prediction, edge-localized mode (ELM) recognition, and H98 regression. On this basis, we present FusionMMT, the first unified multimodal multitask framework for intelligent tokamak plasma diagnostics. FusionMMT employs multi-scale, time-aware, and variable-aware modeling to handle heterogeneous sampling rates and the high computational cost of high-frequency sequences. It further combines task-adaptive multimodal fusion with progressive multitask optimization to learn shared and task-specific representations while mitigating cross-task conflicts and optimization imbalance. Extensive experiments on EAST-VTD640 show that FusionMMT outperforms representative multimodal multitask methods across disruption prediction, ELM recognition, and H98 regression. The source code will be released on https://github.com/Event-AHU/OpenFusion

</details>


### [245] [Neoadjuvant chemotherapy response prediction using pretreatment diffusion and contrast-enhanced magnetic resonance imaging with clinical variables](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26105)
*Pablo García Marcos,Paula Puerta González,Guillermo Lorenzo,Héctor Gómez,Covadonga del Camino,Adán Rodríguez,Ignacio Peláez,Angel Rio-Alvarez,Víctor M. González*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A deep learning model combining apparent diffusion coefficient (ADC) maps, dynamic contrast-enhanced MRI (DCE-MRI), and HR/HER2 subtype achieves an AUC of 0.86 for predicting pathological complete response in breast cancer patients before neoadjuvant chemotherapy, using the ACRIN 6698/I-SPY2 multicenter dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prediction of pathological complete response before neoadjuvant chemotherapy may facilitate more tailored therapeutic planning for breast cancer patients.

Method: Deep learning model using EfficientNet-B0 pretrained encoders for image feature extraction with late fusion clinical information. Evaluated multiple clinical variables (age, race, histological type, HR/HER2 subtype, SBR grade, maximum diameter); only HR/HER2 subtype improved AUC and was retained. Stratified five-fold cross-validation was used.

Result: Standalone ADC maps achieved mean AUC 0.79; DCE-MRI achieved 0.74. Adding HR/HER2 subtype improved performance to 0.83 and 0.81, respectively. Final configuration using both imaging modalities and HR/HER2 subtype achieved AUC of 0.86.

Conclusion: Results support pretreatment multimodal learning for response prediction, although external validation is required before clinical use.

Abstract: Prediction of pathological complete response before neoadjuvant chemotherapy may facilitate more tailored therapeutic planning for breast cancer patients. This work proposes a deep-learning model for pretreatment data only, combining apparent diffusion coefficient maps, dynamic contrast-enhanced magnetic resonance imaging, and clinical variables. The study uses the public ACRIN 6698/I-SPY2 multicenter dataset. The architecture employs EfficientNet-B0 pretrained encoders for image feature extraction and late fusion with clinical information. Multiple clinical variables were evaluated, including age, race, histological type, HR/HER2 subtype, SBR grade, and maximum diameter. Only HR/HER2 subtype improved the average area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUC) and was retained in the final model. Using stratified five-fold cross-validation, standalone apparent diffusion coefficient maps achieved a mean AUC of 0.79, whereas dynamic contrast-enhanced magnetic resonance imaging achieved 0.74. Adding HR/HER2 subtype improved performance to 0.83 and 0.81, respectively. The final configuration, using both imaging modalities and HR/HER2 subtype, achieved an AUC of 0.86. These results support pretreatment multimodal learning for response prediction, although external validation is required before clinical use.

</details>


### [246] [Early Prediction of Pathological Complete Response to Neoadjuvant Chemotherapy Using Temporal Deep Learning on DWI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26106)
*Pablo García Marcos,Md. Tarequl Islam,Paula Puerta González,Guillermo Lorenzo,Héctor Gómez,Covadonga del Camino,Angel Rio-Alvarez,Víctor M. González*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A deep learning framework using EfficientNet with diffusion-weighted MRI (DW-MRI) at baseline and after one neoadjuvant chemotherapy cycle achieves AUC of 0.90 for early prediction of pathological complete response (pCR) in breast cancer, enabling timely treatment adaptation without gadolinium or extensive clinical data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Early identification of non-responders to neoadjuvant chemotherapy (NACT) is crucial for timely treatment adaptation in breast cancer. Existing predictive models rely on multiparametric MRI, late treatment time points, or extensive clinical data, limiting their applicability in clinical practice.

Method: EfficientNet-based temporal deep learning model fed with cropped tumor-centered patches from diffusion-weighted MRI (DW-MRI) acquired at baseline and after the first NACT cycle. The model directly learns tumor shape and local tissue characteristics without explicit radiomic feature engineering. Training performed with 10-fold cross-validation.

Result: The model achieved an area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUC) of 0.90 for pCR prediction after one treatment cycle. The approach provides actionable information early, avoids gadolinium administration, and reduces dependence on heterogeneous clinical data.

Conclusion: DW-MRI-based deep learning on tumor-centered patches constitutes a minimally invasive, clinically deployable strategy for early pCR prediction. It supports earlier escalation or de-escalation of NACT, facilitates protocol standardization, enables multi-centre deployment, and allows privacy-preserving data sharing, with direct implications for personalized treatment adaptation in neoadjuvant breast cancer therapy.

Abstract: Early identification of non-responders to neoadjuvant chemotherapy (NACT) is crucial for timely treatment adaptation in breast cancer. However, many existing predictive models rely on multiparametric magnetic resonance imaging (MRI), late treatment time points, or extensive clinical data, which limits their applicability. This study proposes a deep learning framework for early prediction of pathological complete response (pCR) using only diffusion-weighted MRI (DW-MRI) acquired at baseline and after the first NACT cycle. This framework feeds cropped tumor-centered patches to an EfficientNet-based temporal model that directly learns tumor shape and local tissue characteristics without explicit radiomic feature engineering. The model, trained with 10-fold cross-validation, achieved an area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUC) of 0.90 for pCR prediction after one cycle, providing actionable information after a single treatment cycle while avoiding gadolinium administration and reducing dependence on heterogeneous clinical data. By focusing on the baseline-to-first-cycle window instead of later stages, the approach supports earlier escalation or de-escalation of NACT, and its exclusive reliance on DW-MRI facilitates protocol standardization, multi-centre deployment and privacy-preserving data sharing. These results demonstrate that DW-MRI-based deep learning on tumor-centered patches constitutes a minimally invasive, clinically deployable strategy for early pCR prediction, with direct implications for personalized treatment adaptation in neoadjuvant breast cancer therapy.

</details>


### [247] [DTOC: Dynamic Tool Output Compression for Adaptive Context Management in AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26121)
*Abhay Chaturvedi,Shreya Bhattacharya,Rashmika Gopalkrishnan,Peter van der Putten*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: As agent capabilities have grown, practical limitations increasingly stem from constrained context windows rather than model capacity. Common strategies, such as truncation, heuristic aging, and lossy summarization, may discard useful information or introduce hallucination risk. To address these challenges, we propose Dynamic Tool Output Compression (DTOC), a framework for scalable context management in LLM-based agents that models context updates as explicit and reversible operations within the agent reasoning loop. DTOC retains full tool outputs in external memory while inserting compact placeholders into the active context, enabling selective reconstruction when needed. We formalize the DTOC mechanism, integrate it into a ReAct-style agent architecture, and provide a production-oriented implementation supporting on-demand restoration of compressed outputs. Experiments on DeepSWE reveal model-dependent effects: for responsive models (Sonnet 4.6, GPT-5.4), DTOC reduces input tokens (10.3 and 12.7%) and agent steps (2.4 and 32.3%), while increasing solve rates (2.5 and 1.5 times higher) and lowering cost per solved task (3 and 3.5 times lower cost per solved task). For the other models results are more mixed, with GPT-5.5 doubling solve rate and halving cost, but no impact on solve rate and negative impact on cost for the other models. Ablation results show reversibility is critical: disable-only compression variants degraded performance, while full DTOC recovered baseline accuracy at substantially lower context cost. These findings indicate that explicit, reversible context management can improve the efficiency of long-horizon agent reasoning without degrading task performance.

</details>
